Study Number I PLAIN ENGLISH FOR TRUST BUSINESS 

ΟΝ NE of the things that has helped to keep the trust business shrouded in mystery and to make people dread to talk over their affairs with trustmen and thereby less disposed to patronize trust institutions has been the frequent and needless use of unfamiliar, unintelligible, or even displeasing terms in talking with and in writing to people on trust matters.¹ 

The every-day vocabulary of many trustmen includes many such terms. These terms may be classified into two groups: (1) terms that are objectionable simply because they are unfamiliar or unintelligible to people and (2) terms that are objectionable because to many persons they are displeasing on account of unpleasant connotations. There is a good deal of overlapping in the two groups. Some terms, such as "corpus," are both unfamiliar and displeasing. Some terms, such as "life tenant," are objectionable because they are used indiscriminately and hence sometimes inaccurately. 

1 During recent years, notable progress has been made in simplifying terminology for both the law of trusts and the trust business. Professor Austin W. Scott, as reporter of the Restatement of the Law of Trusts and also as author of Scott on Trusts, has done much to clarify and simplify the terminology in which the law of trusts is stated. As to his policy on the point, he says: "An exact terminology is very necessary for a clear understanding of the law and practices governing the administration of trusts. It is important that lawyers and trustmen, if they are to understand each other, should use the same words and in the same sense. As draftsmen of the Restatement of Trusts it was our policy, as far as possible, to use ordinary English words and to use them with a single meaning. I believe that loose use of language leads to loose thinking."

In the preparation of the textbook Trust Business for the American Institute of Banking, the Committee on Trust Education of the Trust Division of the American Bankers Association and the editors used ordinary English wherever it was possible to do so. Whenever it was necessary to use a Latin, French, technical, or otherwise unfamiliar term, it was defined in simple English. Bankers игр

STUDIES IN TRUST BUSINESS UNFAMILIAR Terms 

The following terms, commonly used in connection with trust business, are objectionable mainly because they are unfamiliar. 

Directly opposite each is a substitute term which has been chosen out of a number of suggestions as perhaps having the most to commend it for common usage.

PLAIN ENGLISH FOR TRUST BUSINESS 

3 

Administrator cum Testamento Annexo 

To the average person "cum testamento annexo" is wholly meaningless, and so is its literal translation, "with the will annexed." A suggested substitute "administrator under the will" would be acceptable but for the fact that it leaves the impression that the administrator is named in the will, whereas he is appointed by the court. Another suggested substitute “administrator appointed to execute the will," is undesirable only because the words, "execute the will," might not be clear to the average person. A third and acceptable suggested substitute administrator appointed by the court to carry out the terms of the will makes it clear that the administrator is appointed by the court and is not named in the will. 

Administrator de Bonis Non

The term "de bonis non" is meaningless to the average person, as shown by the following true story. The administrator of the estate of a colored man in Tennessee died before the estate was settled. 

Members of the dead man's family asked the attorney for the estate what was to be done next. The attorney answered that the court would have to appoint an administrator "de bonis non." Thereupon one member of the family was heard to inquire, "What does he mean?" and another, to reply, "I don't know unless he means that there ain't goin' to be no bonus." The literal translation of "de bonis non," "of the goods not," is wholly meaningless. Other substitutes which have been suggested are "administrator supplementary as to the unsettled part of the estate," "administrator of estate not yet administered" and "administrator to finish the settlement of the estate." The words "unsettled part of the estate" may convey the idea that a part of the estate is in confusion or controversy. The most acceptable term that has been suggested is administrator appointed by the court to complete the settlement of the estate. 

Administrator cum Testamento Annexo de Bonis Non 

This compound term is as objectionable as the preceding one. A substitute which would be better understood by the average person or be much easier to explain would be: administrator appointed by the court to complete the settlement of the estate under the terms of the will.

4 

STUDIES IN TRUST BUSINESS 

Cestui or Cestui que Trust

There would seem to be no justification for using the old French term, “cestui" or "cestui que trust," which literally means "one who trusts," when the term beneficiary, which has the same meaning, has such a pleasing connotation and seems to be universally acceptable.² Corpus

The term "corpus" (which literally means "body") is objectionable not only because it is unusual and unfamiliar to the average person but also, as indicated above, because it frequently has an unpleasant connotation. Many a trust customer beneficiary, especially if it is soon after a death in the family, associates the term corpus with corpse and inwardly shudders when he or she first hears the term "corpus" employed by the trust officer in connection with the estate.

The only other substitute that has been suggested is "capital.” The average person understands the meaning of both terms "principal" and "capital." The term principal already is imbedded so deeply in the literature of both the law of trusts and of the trust business³ that it would be inadvisable to try now to dislodge it and substitute the term "capital."

Wherever any technical or foreign-language term is used, it must first be stated, then translated into English, and then explained, thus making "three bites of a cherry" instead of one or two bites, which would be sufficient if a self-explanatory term were used. 

Corporate Fiduciary

When Trust Business, the two-volume textbook of the American Institute of Banking, was being written, the Chairman of the Committee on Trust Education of the Trust Division, American Bankers Association, which had in charge the preparation of the text, asked the Executive Committee of the Division whether the term "fiduciary" or the term "trust" should be used. The Executive Committee 

2 See Scott, Austin Wakefield, The Law of Trusts, sec. 3.2, p. 38; Little Brown and

Company, Boston, 1939 (hereafter cited by the shorter form, Scott on Trusts). 

3 Bogert, George Gleason, The Law of Trusts and Trustees, Vernon Law Book

Company, Kansas City, Missouri, and West Publishing Company, St. Paul, Minn., 1935 (hereafter cited by the shorter form Bogert on Trusts and Trustees).

recommended unanimously that the term "trust" be used in place of "fiduciary" wherever it was feasible to do so. 

There are three good reasons for preferring "trust" to "fiduciary."

It is the simpler and better understood term. It ties in better with "trust business" and "trust service." It is more accurate, since "fiduciary" covers a great many relationships, such as attorney at law and client, principal and agent, partners, director and stockholder, that are foreign to the trust business.*

American Institute of Banking, and the American Bankers Association all have given the term "trust" preference over "fiduciary." Accordingly, "trust institution" is preferred to "corporate fiduciary" and "trust association," to "corporate fiduciary association.”

In 1933, the Executive Committee of the Trust Division, in drafting the Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, after considering objections to, and suggested substitutions for, the term trust institution, finally came back to it as being the best term. Hence the statement was entitled, A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions. In the joint Statement of Policies of the American Bar 

Association and the American Bankers Association Trust Division trust institution is the term used.

It is advisable to use the term "corporate executor" or "corporate trustee" in cases where a distinction is to be made between individual executors and trustees and institutional executors and trustees. But to use these terms generally in the place of "bank" 

4 Scott on Trusts, sec. 2.5, p. 33. 

Any of 5 The term trust institution has been objected to on the ground that it sounds too much like a public establishment: an asylum, hospital, orphanage, or school. 

Other substitutes that have been suggested are "bank," "trust company," "trust corporation," "trust bank" and "corporate executor" or "corporate trustee.' these terms would be inaccurate for the following reasons. Of the 17,000 banks in the United States, only about 2,800 (not counting branches) are engaged in the trust business. Of the 2,800, 1,315 do not have the word "trust" or any other word of like import in their corporate titles to indicate that they are engaged in the trust business. A large number of "trust companies" are not engaged in the trust business but in a banking or loaning business. "Trust corporation" is a British term which has a strict statutory meaning. "Trust bank," of which, by corporate title, there is only one in the United States, would fit the bank that has a trust department but not the trust company that does not have a banking department. Besides, the term "trust bank" might be objectionable as indicating that the bank was specializing in the trust business to the subordination or exclusion of commercial or savings banking.

or "trust company" or "institution" is to confuse trust service for individuals with trust service for corporations. 

Many trustmen, lawyers, and laymen have caused confusion in the minds of the public by using the term "corporate" in a dual sense. 

By "corporate trustee" they usually mean a corporation engaged in the trust business, either personal or corporate. By the term "corporate trust" they almost invariably mean a trust for a corporation in connection with a bond or note issue. The term "corporate trustee❞ would best be reserved for a trustee under a corporate bond or note issue.

Life Tenant 

The term "life tenant" as applied to a beneficiary of a trust is objectionable both because it is an unfamiliar term to the average person and because it frequently is used inaccurately. A person cannot be a life tenant of personal property in those jurisdictionsstill in the majority — that keep the distinction between real property and personal property. Life tenant means the one who owns real property during his or somebody else's lifetime and which at his or the other person's death passes to somebody else. He has a life estate in the real property. Yet the term "life tenant” frequently is used indiscriminately to apply to beneficiaries of trusts having equitable interests in either real property or personal property or in both, and, worse still, to those having interests for years or for indeterminate periods, as well as to those having interests for life. If the beneficiary of a trust has a life estate in real property, it is correct to speak of him as a "life tenant" and the term should be applied to him exclusively. Present beneficiary has the most in its favor as a substitute for "life tenant."

Among three discarded suggested substitutes, (1) "direct beneficiary" is objectionable because it implies that only the person who is receiving present benefits is a direct beneficiary, whereas others whose possession and enjoyment are only postponed also may be direct beneficiaries; (2) “life beneficiary" is a proper term where it refers to one who is a beneficiary for life but not where it refers to one who is only a beneficiary for a term of years or for any period, definite or indefinite, less than a life; (3) "income beneficiary" is a proper term where the beneficiary is to receive only income as distinguished from principal. In an increasing proportion of modern

trusts, the beneficiary is entitled to receive stipulated payments or distributions regardless of whether in the technical sense they are income or principal. In such cases, "income beneficiary" is not an appropriate term. There would seem to be little or no objection to "immediate beneficiary," but present beneficiary appears to have the most in its favor.

Remainderman 

The same objections that apply to the term "life tenant" apply to the term "remainderman." The term is unfamiliar to the average person and it is often used inaccurately in those jurisdictions that retain the distinction between real property and personal property. 

If the trust property is real property and if the beneficiary is a remainderman in the legal sense of the term he may be known as a “remainderman” and the term should be reserved for his special use. Otherwise the term ultimate beneficiary seems the preferable substitute for "remainderman" as applied to the final beneficiary of a trust.

Other suggested substitutes were discarded for the following reasons: "Principal beneficiary” in conversation may be understood to mean the preferred beneficiary or the one who is to receive the greater benefits from the trust. "Future beneficiary" is objectionable because there may be a series of "future beneficiaries" before the distribution of the property and the termination of the trust. For example, A (father), creates a trust to pay the income to B (wife) for life, then to C (daughter) for life, and after B's and C's death to deliver the estate, discharged of the trust, to C's children, if any, and, if none, to D (an institution). C, C's children, and D all would be "future beneficiaries." Intermediate as well as final beneficiaries may be "future beneficiaries." So, the term "future beneficiaries" appears to be too indefinite. "Remainder beneficiary" has a good deal in its favor and, but for the possible confusion with "remainderman," would not be objectionable. 

Testamentary Trust 

Trust under will is preferable to "testamentary trust" only for the sake of simplicity. Once there was a distinction between a will and 

See Roseberry, L. H., Tomorrow's Trusts, The Annuity Type, Trust Bulletin, March 1941, p. 17; Bardt, Harry M., Why Principal and Income, Ibid., p. 24.

a testament. "Will" referred to the disposal of real property and "testament" to the disposal of personal property. Then it was proper to speak of a person's "will and testament." This distinction obtains no longer anywhere in the common law world. "Will" covers all kinds of property. Consequently the terms "will and testament" and "testamentary trust" are vestiges of a bygone age. 

Trust Indenture

Technically, an indenture is a contract under seal. Formerly, in executing a deed (which is a type of indenture) in counterparts, it was written with both parts on one sheet. The parts were "indented" by being cut asunder in a notched or waved line. Hence the instrument was called an "indenture."

The modern trust instrument covering personal property need not be under seal. In most jurisdictions the agreement need not be, although it always should be, in writing. Consequently the technical or historical reason for applying the term "trust indenture" to personal property no longer exists. Likewise the term "deed of trust" and "trust deed" when applied to personal trusts frequently are used inaccurately. A deed is an instrument of conveyance under seal and to the layman connotes real property. The instrument creating a living trust, as just stated, need not be under seal and often does not cover real property.

The only other substitute that has been suggested is “trust instrument." The objection to this term is that it is too general. A will with trusts, a declaration of trust, or a court order may a "trust instrument." So the term "trust instrument" should be reserved as an inclusive term for all kinds of documents creating trusts. 

A practical reason now exists for preferring some other term than "trust indenture" when referring to personal trusts. The Trust 

Indenture Act of 1939 almost preempts the term "indenture" for corporate trust instruments. It will be well, therefore, to observe this distinction by referring to personal trust instruments as trust agreements and to corporate trust instruments as trust indentures. 

Trust Inter Vivos 

The term "trust inter vivos" (literally, "between or among the living") is objectionable solely because of its unfamiliarity to laymen. Among substitutes discarded are "voluntary trust,” “immediate

trust," "trust under agreement," and "agreement trust." The term "voluntary" emphasizes the voluntariness of the act of creating the trust. "Immediate" suggests the immediate effectiveness of the trust (not awaiting the death of the creator, as in a trust under will.) "Under agreement" and "agreement" suggest the contractual feature of the trust (based upon an agreement between the creator and the trustee). There could be no technical objection to "trust under agreement," except that perhaps it might not include a trust created by declaration of trust. It is in harmony with "trust under will." 

It is not out of harmony with official trust literature." "Agreement trust" has not gained currency and, unless or until it does, will sound stilted and artificial.

The substitute, living trust, although not ideal, is chosen because it emphasizes the fact that this type of trust becomes operative during the creator's lifetime and not (as in the case of a trust under will) only after his death. It is the term long employed in official trust literature. Its use in trust circles has been accepted as a fact by Scott. It has been adopted by Bogert.10 

Uniform or Composite or Commingled Trust Fund 

The terms "uniform," "composite," and "commingled” have been employed indiscriminately to describe trust funds invested collectively. The term common trust fund, on the other hand, has a strictly technical meaning.¹ 

11 Whereas all common trust funds are composite or commingled funds, not all composite or commingled funds, by any means, are common trust funds. 

Yet, when one speaks of a "uniform" or "composite" or "commingled" trust fund, one ordinarily is thinking of a common trust fund in the technical sense. It is highly desirable, therefore, that the term common trust fund be employed only in its technical sense and 

7 See Trust Business, Vol. 1, p. 26; The American Institute of Banking, New York, 1935 (hereafter cited by the shorter form Trusts I) where the only difference (which is immaterial) is that it is referred to as "trust by contract." 

8 Trust Division, The Graduate School of Banking, American Institute of Banking and American Bankers Association. 

9 Scott on Trusts, sec. 17. 

10 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, secs. 1, 106, 123, 1037, 1038, 1039, 1040, 1041, 1042, 1043, 1044, and 1047. See also 18 Ohio Law Review 353. 

11 Federal Revenue Act of 1936 as amended by the Revenue Act of 1938, sec. 169; 

Regulation F-Trust Powers of National Banks.

that, when one means common trust fund, one should not use a lay term such as “uniform," "composite,” or “commingled.” "12 The word "common" in the term common trust fund has been criticised. Some trustmen say that people associate it with the words cheap, ordinary, second-rate, and inferior. However, as applied to trust funds invested collectively the term "common" has been in general use in the English-speaking countries since 1870. New 

Zealand has had a "common" fund so-called since 1870, Australia since 1918, and the United States since 1930. Common trust fund is the term employed in the Federal Revenue Act, in the Regulations of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, and in several state statutes. To undertake now to uproot the term would be about as futile as to try to do so in the case of "common law," "common stock," and "common sense."

Other Unfamiliar Terms 

There are other terms employed in the trust business which are unfamiliar to most laymen but for which substitutes are not suggested here because the terms are seldom heard outside of particular localities, for example, "committee," or are found mostly in legal documents or heard in conversations with lawyers, such as "per capita," "per stirpes," and "letters testamentary." The mere listing of the terms should challenge trustmen to find substitutes that the public will understand. These terms are: administrator pendente lite, ancillary administrator or ancillary executor, caveat, committee (for an incompetent), decree (order of court), dry trust, escheat, escrow, et al, et ano, exculpatory clause, guardian ad litem, intestate, inventory value, letters ad colligendum, letters testamentary, naked trust, non-legals (applied to investments), passive trust, pecuniary legacy, per capita, per stirpes, probate, proctor, (attorney, counsel), publication vs. creditors, publication vs. heirs, publication nisi, recordation (as applied to trust indentures), residuary estate, scrivener (draftsman of wills and trust agreements), and testate. 

Investment Terms

Trust investment terminology teems with unfamiliar terms. For example, trust investment officers use such terms as: unfavorably situated marketwise, a coverage for charges, a 3.05% basis, net of 

12 Trusts and Estates, January 1940, p. 57.

II amortization, an equity lien, a secular uptrend, cyclical swings, an income producer, a stock return of 4% (when speaking of bonds), margin in the gross, carried down to net, a holding company obligation, a marginal producer, yield to first call date, current yield, yield to maturity, giving weight to the secular rather than the cyclical trends, a lien possessing equity characteristics, and equities. 

The average person, even though a trust customer or beneficiary and supposed to be familiar with trust terminology, has little conception of the meaning of such investment terms. It is the combination of simple words into unfamiliar terms that is confusing. For example, a person may know the meaning of every word in the term "a lien possessing equity characteristics," yet be utterly in the dark as to the meaning of the term itself. 

It would be very helpful to the trust business if trust investment officers would work out a simpler trust investment terminology to be employed in conversation and correspondence with customers, beneficiaries, and all other laymen.

TERMS WITH DISPLEASING CONNOTATIONS 

Terms that are objectionable mainly because they have displeasing connotations and therefore tend to arouse hostility, together with suggested substitutes, are the following: 

Displeasing Term 

Suggested Substitute 

Attorney account

Agency Client Customer 

Encroach upon or invade 

Use principal principal Fees

Prospect 

Compensation or charges 

Prospective customer Shop 

Bank or institution or company

Attorney Account 

The average person is confused by the term, "attorney account,” and thinks it has something to do with lawsuits and court-houses.

To him the term has an unpleasant connotation. As used by some trust institutions, it means a type of agency account. If used at all, it should be "attorney in fact account," for the reason that an attorney at law is a person (lawyer) authorized to prosecute and defend actions in a court of law, whereas an attorney in fact is one who acts for another outside of court and usually is a layman. It would be better to refer to such an account as a managing agency, a custody account, or a safekeeping account, as the case may be. Client

A trust institution is engaged in a business and not in a profession.13 Persons who do business with a trust institution are customers, not clients. A client, technically defined, is "one in whose interest a lawyer acts as by giving advice, appearing in court, or conducting a case."14 It connotes an intimate, personal, professional relationship between a lawyer and the person he represents. When a trustman speaks of those who do business with his institution, as "clients," he tends to leave the impression that he thinks of them as his clients instead of as customers of the trust institution. 

When a trust institution in one breath asserts its right to advertise¹5 because it is engaged in a business and in the next breath speaks of those who do business with it as clients (knowing that "client" connotes a professional relationship and that it is unethical for professional men to advertise) it puts itself in the untenable position of trying to blow both hot and cold with the same breath—of being in a business which is a profession and at the same time one that ethically can advertise. It is not surprising, then, that a trustman's reference to the customers of a bank or trust company as "clients" or, worse, as "his clients" arouses the hostility of lawyers and tends to make them suspect that trust institutions may be practicing law. In 

1938, the Executive Committee of the Trust Division, taking cognizance of the foregoing facts, recommended that those who do business with trust institutions thereafter be referred to as customers and not as "clients.”16 

13 Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, Art. VI, sec. 1. 

14 Funk and Wagnalls Company Dictionary. 

15 Ibid. see also joint Statement of Policies of the American Bar Association and the American Bankers Association Trust Division, September 27, 1941, sec. 4. 

16 See Trust Bulletin, April 1938, p. 15.

It is recognized that customer is not an ideal term, since it is a common term for dealings in goods, not for rendering services. It is defined as "one who purchases goods or a supply for any current need for another; a purchaser; a buyer." "Patron," which has been suggested as a possible substitute for “client," is not satisfactory because it conveys the idea of benefaction a patron of music or sculpture, as "one who protects, fosters, countenances or supports some person or thing." Nevertheless, customer is the best substitute for "client" that has been suggested. 

Encroach upon or Invade Principal

Until very recently it has been customary for trustmen to speak or write of a trustee's authority to "encroach upon" or to "invade" principal, and the terms are found in many wills and trust agreements. The words "encroach" and "invade" both have a sinister connotation. To encroach is "to invade partially or insidiously and appropriate the possessions or province of another." To invade is "to enter with an armed force with hostile intent."18 To speak of "encroaching upon" or "invading" principal is to create and leave the impression that for some extraordinary reason the trustee is being authorized to do what under normal conditions would be wrong, would not be tolerated, would not even be thought of as being within the province of a trustee. 

The attitude against using principal is a vestige of the time when the prime function of a trustee was to keep the principal intact at all costs, when the principal had to be preserved even though the present beneficiaries starved in the meantime. The dominant philosophy at the present time is that in most cases the present beneficiary is the preferred beneficiary, that he or she is to be served well even though the ultimate beneficiary receive less than the original amount of the trust. "To consume" and "to make payments out of" have been suggested as substitutes for the term "encroach upon" or "invade," but have been objected to for the following reasons: "To consume” has an unpleasant inference. "To make payments out of" connotes paying out cash, whereas the trustee may see fit to make certain dis- 

17 Funk and Wagnalls Company Dictionary. 

18 Ibid.

tributions in kind. The term "to use" is preferred to either of the other suggestions, notwithstanding the fact that it might conceivably leave the impression that the use of principal is in the nature of an advancement or loan. Fees

Objection to the indiscriminate use of the term “fees” is mainly because of its inaccuracy. It is applied indiscriminately to all sorts of compensation — to fees proper, to commissions, and to allowances for service. A fee is a fixed amount. A trustee may name a minimum amount, $25, for example, which it charges annually for handling an account; this is a fee. A commission, on the other hand, is a percentage of the amount or value of the subject matter. The annual charge for handling a trust account frequently is a percentage of the gross income, and hence is a commission. Most of the compensation of trust institutions is either commissions or "commissionlike arrangements," to use Mayo Shattuck's term.19.

The term "fees," used indiscriminately, has an implication to some lawyers that trust institutions, like themselves, are receiving fees for professional services rendered. It would seem better to use the terms "fees" and "commissions" accurately where they apply and to use either compensation or charges where fees, commissions, and other types of compensation are meant to be included in one term. 

In Great Britain the corresponding term is "renumeration," but in the United States the word "renumeration" is not even indexed in Bogert, Scott or Shattuck. Compensation may not be an ideal term in every respect but it is an accepted American term; it is used in the Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions20 and in all official trust literature. A good American term is charges, which is equally acceptable and has the merit of being simple and brief. Prospect The term "prospect" applied to a person is not found in all dictionaries. If it were, this would not make the word palatable. People do not like to be addressed or referred to or even thought of as 19 Shattuck, Mayo Adams, A Trustee's Handbook. Augustus Peabody Loring. 5th edition, Little Brown & Company, Boston, Mass. 1940. (hereafter cited by the shorter form, Shattuck Revision) sec. 91, p. 233. 

20 Art. V.

"prospects." It sounds too much like "suspect" and gives them a creepy feeling.

Sensing this resentment of the term, the Committee on Trust Education of the Trust Division, when preparing the American Institute of Banking textbook Trust Business, substituted the term prospective customer. Although a cumbersome term and not ideal, it is better than "prospect" and is the only substitute so far suggested. Shop

A good many trustmen have fallen into the habit of speaking of their bank or trust company as a "shop." "In our shop," they say, "we do thus and so." It may be that they say "shop" because they think it sounds informal, intimate, and sociable. 

These men may be surprised to know that the very people upon whom they wish especially to make a good impression find the term "shop" applied to a trust institution distinctly unpleasant. People like to associate dignity, convention, and a degree of formality with the institution that is to settle their estate or administer their trusts and to look after the welfare of their beneficiaries. They think that it is belittling the bank or trust company they are considering for their executor and trustee to refer to it as a “shop." 

In deference to this quite understandable sensitiveness, it would be better for trustmen to resume the habit of referring to the trust institution as bank or institution or company and to say, "In our bank (or institution or company) we do thus and so." 

TRUST DEPARTMENT PERSONNEL NOMENCLATURE 

21 Certain terms used to designate persons employed in the trust department also have an effect, either good or bad, upon both the customer relations and the public relations of trust institutions.² 

In recognition of this fact, in 1940, the Executive Committee of the Trust Division named a special Committee on Trust Department Personnel Nomenclature. In April 1941 the report of this committee was approved by the Executive Committee and was thereby made an official statement. The report recommends, among other changes, the use of the terms trust administrator for the manager of trust 21 For a discussion of proper titles for trust officials, see editorial in Trusts and 

Estates, December 1939, p. 640. Noted on page 19, note 28.

accounts, and trust representative or estate consultant for those engaged in promotional work, and the elimination of the term "assistant" as a prefix to vice-president or trust officer. 

Trust Administrator

Heretofore the person in the trust department who deals directly in person and by correspondence with customers, beneficiaries, and third parties with respect to an account has been called variously trust officer, personal trust officer, associate trust officer, vice trust officer, vice-president, junior vice-president, assistant vice-president, trust administrator, and trust administrative officer.22

By adopting the Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, trust institutions have committed themselves as follows to bring beneficiaries into direct contact with administrative or senior officers: "It should be the policy of trust institutions that all personal trusts should be under the direct supervision of and that beneficiaries should be brought into direct contact with the administrative or senior officers of the trust department.' 23 The Trust Division now recommends that the persons who handle trust accounts be known in their personal dealings with customers, beneficiaries, and others as trust administrators. This does not mean that "trust administrator" should be anyone's official title. For example, one may be vice-president of the bank or trust company and still be known as "administrator" of a particular trust account. 

There has been some objection to the term trust administrator because "administrator" is being used in a dual sense in the same institution. The institution is the administrator of an estate and some individual in the institution is the administrator of an account. "Account executive" has been suggested, but this also leads to confusion. The head of the trust department is an "executive" officer; the person who handles an account is an account “executive.” 'Account supervisor" is another term that has much to commend it. In smaller institutions, which have only one "trust officer," there 

22 Directory of Trust Institutions, Trustmen, and Trust Associations, 1939, p. 12. (Published by Trust Division, American Bankers Association, New York, hereafter cited by the shorter form Directory.) 

23 Art. III, sec. I.

would be no point in calling him trust administrator, for "trust officer" is fully as descriptive a term. So, it is mainly for the larger institutions, where there are not enough "trust officers" to handle al! the accounts, that the term trust administrator is recommended. 

Estate Consultant or Trust Representative 

A similar confusion has existed in the nomenclature of the men engaged in promotional work for the trust department. These men have borne many different titles, some descriptive of their functions and some not at all descriptive. Among the descriptive titles have been estate analyst, estate consultant, new business solicitor, new business man, new business representative, solicitor, special representative, trust adviser, trust representative, and trust solicitor. The non-descriptive titles have run the whole gamut of corporate titles - such as vice-president, assistant vice-president, trust officer, assistant trust officer, with a preference for "assistant" — for example, assistant secretary, assistant treasurer, assistant trust officer.24 

The Trust Division now recommends that the term trust representative or estate consultant be used to designate the person whose main function is promotion through personal representation. 

The term trust representative has been suggested as a substitute for "solicitor" or "new business man." It has merit to recommend it. 

In Trust Business, Volume II, the American Institute of Banking textbook,25 "personal representative" is the term employed. In the 

Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions also the term used is "personal representative."26 The only objection to the word "representative" as applied to promotional work is that it does not indicate the nature of the representation. It may leave the impression that the person is a general representative of the trust institution, and in a sense every member of the staff is a trust representative. 

The term estate consultant has several things in its favor. First, it is descriptive of the function. In principle the person engaged in promotional work for a trust institution is an adviser and consultant rather than a salesman. “Trust business is so individual and distinctive that the customer cannot always obtain from printed matter 

24 Directory, supra, p. 12. 

25 Pp. 360-380. 

26 Art. VI, sec. 2.

all he wishes to know about the protection and management the trust institution will give his estate and the services it will render his beneficiaries."27 Second, the term is attractive to people in that it implies specialized, confidential advice on the business (not legal) aspects of estate problems and plans. Third, it should be both acceptable and attractive to lawyers in that it avoids the odious suggestion of solicitation and carries the suggestion that the trust institution has available for consultation with them and their clients a representative who is a specialist in the practical phases of estate planning. 

As between the two terms trust representative and estate consultant, the committee expressed no preference but commended both of them.

In furtherance of this general idea, it has been suggested that every trust department, large or small, well may designate one or more persons attached to the trust department trust representative or estate consultant regardless of their other title. A person may be designated as trust representative or estate consultant in his promotional work although he may be vice president, trust officer, secretary, treasurer, or have some other title. An assistant trust officer, assistant secretary or assistant treasurer, without change of official title, may be designated as trust representative or estate consultant and by the latter designation may be known in all his promotional activity.

The double designation of persons is a common practice in American banks and trust companies, one designating the corporate and the other the functional position-for example, vice-president and trust officer, or vice-president and cashier. Acting in one capacity, he is a vice-president; in another, a trust officer or cashier. So, the naming of everyone who handles accounts trust administrator and everyone who engages in promotional work trust representative or estate consultant is neither a leveling up nor a leveling down process in staff organization. Assistant

The National Public Opinion Survey, made for the Trust Divi sion, American Bankers Association, by Elmo Roper in 1940, brought 

27 lbid.

1 to light the fact that a substantial number of lawyers and others say that their main criticism of trust institutions is that small trust accounts are not given the individual attention of senior officers but are liable to be turned over to minor assistants. The frequent use of the term "assistant," as applied to officers with whom trust customers, beneficiaries and lawyers deal, tends to give some basis for this criticism.

Several of the medium-sized institutions are eliminating the word "assistant" from the term "assistant trust officer" and calling all the staff members who handle accounts and deal with customers and beneficiaries "trust officers."

The Trust Division recommends that the term "assistant" as applied to the office of vice-president or trust officer, except in positions where the officer does not deal personally with the public, be eliminated.28 Trustman

In September 1941 the members of the Executive Committee of the Trust Division approved the term trustman to designate a person whose occupation is rendering trust service in the employment of a trust institution. It is a general term which includes "trust officer,” "vice-president in charge of trusts," "head of trust department," and anyone else whose occupation is rendering trust service through a trust institution. To avoid confusion it should not be applied to an individual fiduciary or to a person in his employment. It is not a substitute for "trust officer," which is a functional title, or for "vice-president" or any other corporate title. Trustman is an occupational name just as "banker" is one.

CONCLUSIONS 

This Study deals almost exclusively with the possible effect of the terminology of trust business upon the customer relations and the public relations of trust institutions. It is not an attempt in the slightest degree to reform the terminology of the law of estates and trusts. Any such reform as may be needed is the exclusive function of the lawyers themselves. It is assumed that lawyers will continue to employ the technical language of their profession. It is for them 

28 Trust Bulletin, July-August 1941, p. 17.

alone to decide how far they will carry their technical terminology over into their conversation and correspondence with their clients and with the public. The President of the American Bar Association for 1941-1942 has expressed himself heartily in favor of plain English for trust law also.29 Nor is it an attempt to reform the technical terminology of trustmen. It is assumed that in their conversation and correspondence with one another they will continue to use as many technical terms as they please. This study is only an attempt to call the attention of trustmen to the bad effect that unfamiliar, displeasing or unintelligible terminology may have upon the customer relations and the public relations of trust institutions. 

Few of the substitutes suggested for unfamiliar or displeasing terms are ideal, or the ones that would be suggested if one had a clean sheet to write upon. All that can be claimed for any term is that it seems to be the best so far suggested. It is better to agree upon and use a second-best term, if it is a descriptive term the meaning of which is generally understood, than to continue the use of a variety of terms. Meanwhile, search for the ideal term may be continued. 

No matter how accurate, literally and technically, a term may be, if it does not tend to promote good relations, a substitute that does promote good relations should be sought until it is found. 

The modern trustman must be tri-lingual. In his dealings with lawyers he must understand the language of the law of estates and trusts. In his dealings with fellow trustmen he must know and use the technical language of his and their occupation. In his dealings with customers and beneficiaries he must not only know, understand and use the language of trust business but also must be able to explain estate and trust matters in language that the layman himself knows, understands, and uses. 

Another thing that earnestly and constantly should be striven for is greater uniformity in the terminology of trust functions. If trustmen are to understand one another and if other people are to understand them, trustmen generally must use words to describe trust functions in the same sense and with a single meaning. "Agency," "managing agency," or "custody account," for example, should have one and the same meaning in every part of the country 

29 Armstrong, Walter P., A Practicing Lawyer Looks at Legal Education, The 

American Law School Review, December 1940, p. 775, at pp. 777-778; see also New 

York Times, October 4, 1941.

for trustmen, for trust customers and beneficiaries, and for lawyers and other advisers. It would be well for trust institutions generally to adopt the terminology of trust functions used in Trust Business, the American Institute of Banking textbook. 

Naturally people are suspicious of what they do not understand.

This applies to every phase of the trust business-to services, to positions, and to functions. The Trust Division, no doubt, had this human trait in mind when it declared in its Statement of Policies for the Acceptance of Trust Business, "Explanations to prospective customers should be complete and understandable."30

This is what the author of this Study had in mind when he said at the 1941 Mid-Winter Trust Conference:

Instead of talking about our own business we ought to tell people their own basic needs for trust service; tell them what trust service means to them and their family and friends and business; tell them what trust services the trust institution has to offer them; tell them what the trust institution only can do for them or what it can for them better than anyone else can do. We ought to tell the people all these things; tell all of them over and over, line upon line, precept upon precept, here a little, there a little. And, above all, we ought to tell them in ABC language, not in ABA³¹ language. 32 31 Trustmen have everything to gain and nothing to lose by making a zealous, continuous effort to simplify the terminology of trust business until all trust terms are expressed in plain English and until trust service is understood, accepted, and spoken well of in the homes of common men.

SECOND SERIES, NUMBER TWO 

December Why 

Trust Institutions

Exist by 

The Graduate School of Banking 

American Bankers Association 1941 

SUPPLEMENT TO THE TRUST BULLETIN 

Published by 

The Trust Division AMERICAN BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

22 East 40 Street New York, N. Y.

COPYRIGHT 1941 BY AMERICAN BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 2  

TRU *RUST institutions will have an assured place in the economic order of the future to the extent that their services meet basic needs of people. This Study presents the results of the author's research to find out why trust institutions came into being, why they still exist, and what basic human needs they serve. 

Wherever English Common Law Prevails 

Trust institutions¹ are found in practically every country in which the English common law is the prevailing legal system, including, for example, Burma, Ceylon, Hong Kong, Trinidad, and Tobago, as well as England, Australia, New Zealand, the Union of South Africa, Canada, and the United States of America. They have not been developed to an appreciable extent in any of the civil law countries² except Japan. There they represent a recent development and are modelled after English and American institutions. 

1 In A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions trust institutions are defined as "corporations engaged in trust business under authority of law" (Art I, sec. 1) and trust business as "the business of settling estates, administering trusts and performing agencies in all appropriate cases for individuals; partnerships; associations; business corporations; public, educational, social, recreational, and charitable institutions; and units of government." (Art. I, sec. 2.)

Throughout these Studies the term "trust service" is employed as a general term to include all types of service of a fiduciary character rendered by a trust institution. 

2 Civil law countries include all the civilized countries except the common law countries constituting the British Empire and Commonwealth of Nations and the United States of America. The results of the author's study, travel, and correspondence with people in the countries of Central Europe and of Central and South America between the years 1927 and 1937 indicate that prior to the outbreak of the present war there was a growing demand on the part of the people of the leading civil law countries for trust institutions. In some of these countries in Germany and Italy, for instance- -a few trust institutions already had been established.

In Response to Demand of People 

According to published statements and the author's research trust institutions have come into existence in response to the demand of people rather than upon the initiative of promoters. Discussing why banks in England entered the trust business, C. H. Barker in his book, Banks as Trustees, says: The Public Trustee began functioning in 1908, and almost immediately a desire sprang up among the public that their own particular bank should provide similar facilities to those furnished by the Public Trustee. . . . It, therefore, came in course of time that the banks received applications from their own customers asking why did they not undertake this work. The demand for such facilities became so great that as early as 1909 ... some of the banks intimated to their customers that they were prepared to undertake executor and trustee business. . . . During the year 

1909, and the year immediately following, all the big banks, by one method or the other, established their trustee business, and later on the smaller banks . . . followed suit. . . . It is true to say that this was an instance of the supply following the demand. It was not a case of the banks seeking another side to their business in order to bring grist to their own particular mill. 

The English life insurance companies also, as evidenced by the following statement in the Investors' Chronicle of London in its issue of September 10, 1937, went into the trust business in response to the wishes of their policyholders:

In the first instance assurance companies sought powers to act as trustees in response to the wishes of policyholders who had created provision for dependents by means of assurance with the companies and who desired some service that would ensure the money so provided being well administered for the benefit of their dependents after their decease.

There appear to be three reasons why trust institutions are found wherever the English common law prevails and why they have come in response to the demand of people. First, the principal trust functions-executorship, trusteeship, and guardianship—are distinctive developments of the English common law. Second, the proper discharge of these functions, particularly under present-day economic conditions, calls for specialization by anyone serving as executor, trustee, or guardian. Third, trust institutions, as corporations possessing certain distinctive attributes, are in an advantageous position to render these specialized services. 

3 Barker, C. H., Banks as Trustees, pp. 9-13: Sir Isaac Pitman & Sons, Ltd., London, 1933.

TRUST FUNCTIONS DEVELOPMENTS OF COMMON LAW 

The English common law has created a demand for trust institutions in the following ways: (1) by elevating the position of the office of executor, (2) by developing the trust and creating the office of trustee, and (3) by placing guardianship under court appointment and supervision.

Position of Executor Elevated 

The first step of the English common law by which the position of the office of executor was elevated was to give property owners freedom to dispose of their property by will. 

Only in English-speaking countries is there broad freedom of disposition of property by will. Except for the wife's dower and the husband's curtesy, except for provision for afterborn children, and except for some limitation upon gifts to charity, a person is free to dispose of his entire estate by will. In the civil law countries, on the other hand, a person can dispose of only a comparatively small part of his estate by will. Most of his estate, under the rules of descent and distribution, goes by operation of law to his "forced heirs," who are his wife, children, and next-of-kin. A will is therefore a much more important document in the common law countries than it is in the civil law countries. 

The second step in elevating the position of executor was by placing him under court appointment and supervision. Executorship began as an office of the church and the bishop himself sometimes served as the executor. In common law countries, although the office has been in process of development over a period of 800 years, the complete jurisdiction of the state* over the office has been recognized only since 1802. Today in every common law country the executor, although named in the will, is appointed by the court and is under its supervision. The executor is a right arm of the court in carrying out the terms of the will and, therefore, occupies a position of much importance.

In the civil law countries many estates, both large and small, are wound up and distributed without the intervention of the court. 

4 Trusts I, p. 460-461.

Many civil law wills do not name an executor. The "forced heirs" accept legacies as charges on their inheritances and themselves carry out the terms of the will without the aid of an executor or of the court. Hence, in these countries executorship is a comparatively unimportant office.

Development of Trust 

5 The trust is a distinctive product of the Anglo-American common law system. Wherever it exists in a civil law country it has been grafted as an alien branch onto a native trunk. The trust has been so grafted onto civil law systems in Quebec, Panama, Mexico, Puerto Rico, Louisiana, and Japan. Substitutes for the trust have been evolved in still other countries; but, at best, they are only substitutes and not the trust in the common law meaning of the term. 

The trust and the trustee have come into being simultaneously, for there must be a trustee for every trust. Thus, the trustee, also a distinctive product of the common law, was until recently an exclusive possession of English-speaking people. 

Guardian under Appointment and Supervision of Court In the common law countries the unit of society is the individual; in the civil law countries the unit is the family.' In dealing with minors and incompetent persons in common law countries the state takes the initiative in looking after its dependent citizens; whereas, in civil law countries, the family does so. In England and the United 

States, for example, the state, through its court, appoints the guardian of a minor or incompetent person, and the guardian is at all 

* Restatement of the Law of Trusts, p. 1; American Law Institute Publishers, 

St. Paul, 1935 (hereafter cited by the shorter form, Restatement of Trusts). See also 

Scott on Trusts, pp. 1-28 and Maitland, Selected Essays (1936), p. 129.

Scott quotes Maitland as saying to his students: "If we were asked what is the greatest and most distinctive achievement performed by Englishmen in the field of jurisprudence, I cannot think that we should have any better answer to give than this, namely, the development from century to century of the trust idea." • Lepaulle, Pierre, Civil Law Substitutes for Trusts, Yale Law Journal, Vol. 36, pp. 1126-1147. 

7 Maine, Sir Henry Sumner, Ancient Law, pp. 142-202; Oxford University Press, London, 1931.

times under the supervision of and accountable to the court. The guardian may be recommended by the father or mother or by other members of the family but it is the court that makes the appointment. In France, which is a typical civil law country, on the other hand, the parent has the first right to appoint the guardian. If the father or mother fails to make the appointment, the family council both makes it and supervises the administration of the guardianship. 

The family council normally is composed of six blood relativesthree from the father's side and three from the mother's side, or all the brothers and sisters if more than six. A local justice of the peace serves as convener and chairman of the council but votes only in case of a tie. Guardianship is much more of a business relationship in the common law countries than in the civil law countries. 

Thus the English common law paved the way for the coming of the trust institution. It made the will a document of vast importance and the trust "one of the most flexible of the instruments in the armory of our jurisprudence."

TRUST SERVICE A BUSINESS FOR SPECIALISTS 

The growing demands upon the offices of executor, trustee, and guardian, together with the increasing complexity of the economic order, have created the need for specialization in the discharge of these functions. In turn, service as executor, trustee, or guardian has become the work of specialists who may make it a business. The occupation of settling estates and administering trusteeships and guardianships is now fully recognized as being a business and not a profession. The Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, adopted by the American Bankers Association Trust Division in 1933, declares that trust business is the business of settling estates, administering trusts, and performing agencies.10 The joint Statement of Policies of the American Bar Association and the American Bankers Association Trust Division refers to a trust institution as 

8 Cachard, Henry, The French Civil Code, Revised Edition, Book I, Title X, pp. 133-155; The Lecram Press, Paris, 1930. 

9 Scott, Austin Wakeman, Fifty Years of Trusts, Harvard Law Review, November 

1936, p. 60 at p. 76. 

10 Art. I, sec. 2.

qualified and authorized by law as a legitimate business enterprise.

¹¹ 

Increasing Duties of Executor and Administrator 

If serving as executor under a will or as administrator of an estate meant today, as it once meant, scarcely more than assembling and safeguarding the assets, which were mostly tangible personal property, paying the debts and administration expenses, and distributing the balance, the service still might be rendered acceptably by a layman inexperienced in the settlement of estates- by a member of the family, a kinsman, a friend, or a business associate. The present-day bookkeeping, accounting, investment, tax, and other highly technical requirements have greatly increased the duties of executors and administrators. One chart of duties in settling an estate shows the executor charged with 16 estate duties and the administrator with 25 and both charged with 5 additional duties connected with beneficiaries.¹² For the proper settlement and distribution of an estate, a layman, if unguided and unaided, frequently finds himself in a maze of problems and difficulties in performing duties as executor or administrator. 

Increasing Duties of Trustee and Guardian

Similarly, if serving as trustee involved, as it once did, only holding and safeguarding the trust property and at the proper time turning it over to its ultimate owner with no tax or investment requirements to meet, trusteeship still might be the work of a layman inexperienced in the administration of estates.13 A chart of 

11 Art. IV. 

12 Trusts I, p. 81. 

13 Augustus Peabody Loring, in his Trustee's Handbook, said, "The original theory of the trust did not contemplate a trustee's doing anything but holding and taking care of the property; hence the trustee's duties were restricted largely to safekeeping and conservation. At the present day, the usual object of a trust is to settle property in the hands of persons of good business ability to manage it for the benefit of others not possessed of such ability; hence the trustee's duties are extended to those of management." (4th ed., p. 76; Little Brown & Company, Boston, 1928). Mayo

Shattuck, in his revision of Loring's Handbook, added, "Whatever a careful man would do to safeguard and manage his own property, the trustee should do for the trust property. . . . All privileges and incidents of legal ownership fall upon the ...

the duties of a trustee in administering a personal trust lists 32 duties with respect to the trust property and 24 with respect to the beneficiaries.¹ 14 Also there has been a marked increase in the number, variety, and complexity of the duties of a guardian, involving troublesome accounting, investment, tax, and other problems.

There were specialists in the settlement of estates and in the administration of trusteeships and guardianships long before there were corporations chartered and licensed to engage in the trust business. These accounting, tax, and investment problems and other complexities under the present economic order have only accentuated the need for specialization in this field. 

The original specialists were mostly lawyers. In some common law countries accountants are specialists in this field.¹5 In England the family solicitor and in the United States the family lawyer are the most generally accepted specialists in this field. In Boston this specialization went further than anywhere else in this country and materialized in the "Boston Trustee" or the professional individual trustee¹6 of whom Augustus Peabody Loring himself was a shining example. Few of these individual specialists have engaged in the trust business to the exclusion of their profession. The fact that these men, in order to handle trust business, had to make themselves specialists, even while practicing law, is proof of the demand for specialization in serving as executor, trustee, and guardian. 

Specialization by individuals set the stage for the entrance of the trust institution. In England, for example, the first corporation that offered trust service was organized by certain London solicitors. 

In South Africa, one of the present trust institutions was organized trustee. In short, the duties, powers and liabilities of the trustees are primary, not secondary, and are full, as befits an owner of property." (Shattuck Revision, Sec. 17, pp. 59-61.) 

14 Trusts I, p. 198.
