16 Holbrook, Donald, The Boston Trustee, Marshall Jones Company, Boston, 1937; 

Saltonstall, Leverett, and Carroll, Frederick A., Is There a Field in the United States for the Professional Individual Trustee?, Trust Bulletin, August 1938, pp. 27-28, and Trust Companies, August 1938, pp. 136-140.

primarily to take over the trust business already in the hands of certain individual accountants." The individual professional trustee -the individual specialist-is the father of the corporate specialist the trust institution.

SPECIAL ATTRIBUTES OF TRUST INSTITUTIONS 

As pointed out at the beginning of this Study, throughout the common law world trust institutions were called into existence because they possessed certain attributes which were essential for or advantageous to the rendering of trust service. 

During the school year 1940-41 the students majoring in trusts in The Graduate School of Banking were asked to state what they would say were the basic needs for trust institutions if, as the head of a trust department, they were asked the following question by an advertising man: "I am preparing a series of six newspaper advertisements on the basic needs for the services of a trust institution in this community. Please give me the theme and main points of each of these advertisements." The answers made by the 89 trustmen, representing every section of the country, are summarized, with some amplification, in the discussion that follows. The answers represent a fair cross-section of national opinion as to the basic needs for trust institutions.

Twelve things that people desire of executors, trustees, and guardians, which trust institutions are especially qualified to offer, are these: continuous existence, continuous capacity, financial responsibility, responsiveness to obligations, specialization, collective information, group judgment, impartiality, confidentiality, adaptability, accessibility, and governmental supervision.

In presenting these special attributes of trust institutions, care has been taken not to exaggerate them. The standards for trust advertising in the Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions of the Trust Division and in the joint Statement of Policies of the American Bar Association and the American Bankers Association Trust

Division have been observed in making this Study. The Statement of Principles declares, "Its (a trust institution's) advertisements should not overstate or overemphasize the qualifications of the trust 

17 Trust Business in Common Law Countries, p. 434.

institutions. ... There should be no reflection, expressed or implied, upon individuals." 18 The Statement of Policies declares, "A trust institution has an inherent right to advertise its trust services in appropriate ways. Its advertisement should be dignified and the qualifications of the institution should not be overstated or overemphasized." 19

Continuous Existence 

Many years may elapse between the date a will is made and the date it is offered for probate. A trust established for individuals may continue well over into the third generation and a charitable trust may continue in perpetuity. A guardianship of the property of a minor will continue until he is of legal age and a guardianship of the property of an incompetent person usually continues during his life. A trust indenture may cover bonds that will not mature until 

50 or 75 years after the date of issue, throughout all of which period a trustee under the bond issue must be prepared to function continuously.20 Hence the need for the continuous existence of executors, trustees, and guardians.

In meeting this need, trust institutions have the advantage of being corporations with long-term or perpetual charters. There are at least four trust institutions in the United States well beyond the first century of their continuous existence and several others are approaching the century mark.

Corporations sometimes lose or change their identity, which may cause a break in the continuity of their existence. For trust institutions this contingency is dealt with in one or more of four ways. 

First, in the event that a national bank changes its identity by consolidating with another national bank or with a state bank or changes its name, Regulation F-Trust Powers of National Banks21 

18 Art VI, sec. I. 

19 Art. IV. 

20 This Study does not include a discussion of corporate trust and agency services. 

In the United States the corporate form of business organization has created the demand for the services of trust institutions as trustee and agent in connection with bond and stock issues. However, this would be the subject matter of another Study. 

21 Secs. 3-5.

-provides for the continuance of trust service in existing accounts. 

Second, the statutes of many states provide for the continuance of trust service in the event of the consolidation of two or more state banks or trust companies. Third, in case of the liquidation of a trust institution, the court will appoint an administrator to complete the settlement of an estate and will, if need be, appoint a successor trustee or guardian to complete the administration of the trusteeship or guardianship. Fourth, most modern trust instruments, under which trust institutions are named, provide for the possibility of loss or change of identity of the institution. On this point the following is a typical provision:

If trustee shall, during the term of this trust, transfer its trust business to any other corporation authorized to do trust business, by merger, consolidation, or reorganization or be converted into a national banking association authorized to do trust business, such transferee corporation or such association and its successors shall thereupon without further transfer, substitution, act, or deed become subrogated and succeed as trustee hereunder, vested with all the trust estate, rights, obligations, powers, discretions, immunities, privileges, and compensations herein conferred upon This is supplementary to and not in limitation of any law on the subject that now exists or that may be hereafter enacted. 

Although a trust institution, because it is an organization of human beings, cannot guarantee continuity of existence, it does give assurance that it will employ every possible aid of organization. statute, and regulation in providing continuity of trust service. 

Continuous Capacity 

The person who makes a will or creates a trust desires all possible assurance that his executor or trustee will have continuous capacity throughout the entire period of the service, even though it extends through several generations. 

Members of the staff of a trust institution are, of course, subject to all human ailments. No trust institution can assure a customer that the person who is its trust officer the day the will is made will be its trust officer in full possession of all his faculties the day the will is probated. However, the trust institution can assure a customer that, as long as it remains in the trust business, it will provide continuous capacity, although not of any given individual, through the normal processes of staff successions, replacements, and additions.

Financial Responsibility 

For his peace of mind, as well as for the protection of his beneficiaries, a testator or settlor wants all possible assurance of the financial responsibility of his executor, trustee, or the guardian of the property of his children.

The trust institution, with its large capital and resources and with the special legal safeguards thrown around trust property, particularly uninvested funds, is especially qualified to give this assurance. 

In most states the legislature has recognized the financial responsibility of trust institutions by relieving them of the requirement to offer surety on their bond. The reason for this exemption is that the financial responsibility of the trust institution itself is regarded by the legislature as ample. 

Responsiveness to Obligations

A person making a will or creating a trust desires of his executor and trustee not only financial responsibility but also responsiveness to obligations. The two terms mean different things. The financial responsibility of an executor, trustee, or guardian refers to his or his surety's solvency. If he is financially responsible, then, by an appropriate action at law or proceeding in equity, he can be made to answer for his obligations. The responsiveness of an executor, trustee or guardian to his obligations refers to his inclination, disposition and readiness to answer for his obligations. Responsibility is a matter of the letter, responsiveness, of the spirit of accountability. In its practical effect upon beneficiaries of estates and trusts, there may be a vast difference between an executor's or trustee's ability, on the one hand, and his readiness and willingness, on the other, to discharge his obligations.

A trust institution, when engaging in the business of settling estates and administering trusteeships and guardianships, realizes that it can continue in business and prosper only as it satisfies its customers and their beneficiaries with the services it renders. It stakes its reputation upon discharging its obligations properly and promptly. 

By experience it has learned the duties, powers, responsibilities, and

liabilities of an executor, trustee, and guardian. For these reasons it is not disposed to cavil, quibble, or delay in discharging obligations once they are established.

Specialization 

As pointed out in the first part of this Study, trust service in the present economic order is the work of a group of specialists such as bookkeepers, accountants, tax men, investment men, real property men, and appraisers. Whether associated with one another or not, they should all be specialists in their respective fields if they are to meet the present-day requirements placed upon executors, trusteees, and guardians.

The volume of business of the trust institution and the number and variety of its accounts justify the trust institution in building up and maintaining a staff of specialists and give the members of the staff opportunity by experience to become all the more specialists in their respective fields.

In pointing out that the members of the staff of a modern trust institution are a group of specialists, it is not claimed that they are experts or that they are infallible in their work or inerrant in their judgment. They are human beings selected because they were regarded as possessing special qualifications and aptitude for the work. 

A person of average intelligence and ability, dealing with similar problems day after day, gradually acquires a degree of proficiency in the solution of those problems and in time becomes a specialist in his field. Suffice it to say that specialists in trust service are only selected human beings who have become specialists in their field by concentration, study, and experience.

The number of specialists or the degree of specialization need not be the same in every trust institution. A small trust institution, with a staff of only three or four persons, cannot afford to have a full-time accountant, tax man, investment man, and real property man. But even in the smallest trust institution the trust officer of the one-man trust department becomes a specialist in each of these fields as compared with a person who is not devoting his whole time to trust work.

An analogy is to be found in the profession of law or medicine. 

The general practitioner - the country lawyer or the country doctor - may not be a specialist compared with lawyers or doctors who devote their whole time to one line of practice; but he is, distinctly, a specialist in every phase of law or medicine as compared with laymen.

Collective Information 

A person seeking trust service under present-day conditions needs for his estate or trust not only the information of specialists but the organization, integration, and application of this information for the benefit of his particular estate or trust. His executor and trustee can render the best service, he thinks, not by consulting outside specialists separately but by obtaining their advice and knowledge collectively as members of the staff in the same organization. 

The trust institution is especially organized and equipped to satisfy this need. Visualize what actually is taking place in many a trust institution every day when an estate is in process of settlement by the trust institution as executor. The trust officers and division heads are sitting in conference around a table. A bookkeeping problem arises; the head bookkeeper is expected to have or to find the answer. An accounting problem arises; this is referred to an accountant. A tax problem arises; this is for the tax man. An investment problem arises; this is referred to the trust investment officer. So it is that every problem that arises is referred, formally or informally, to the person on the staff who is the specialist in that field. In this way the trust institution as executor is able to bring to bear upon the problems of the estate not only the independent information of its individual specialists but also the collective information of its group of specialists, each of them testing and perhaps refining his information in the crucible of discussion and sometimes of argument over the problems of a particular estate. 

While the exact procedure just described may not obtain in all trust institutions, every trust institution has some method of applying the collective information of specialists to its estate and trust problems.

Group Judgment 

A person who needs an executor or trustee wants one that will exercise sound judgment. He also wants that judgment to be group judgment—the composite judgment of several persons based upon varied knowledge and experience drawn from many sources. 

Trust institutions undertake to supply group judgment largely through the medium of trust committees of officers and directors. 

The specialists discussed in the preceding section are the technicians. 

They find and digest the facts and relate them to the point at issue. 

Technical facts supplied by the specialists are complemented by the general information of the officers and often of the directors. 

Responsibility for action based upon all the facts and information rests upon the latter group.

Illustrative of the extent to which a trust institution is required to apply group judgment in arriving at its decisions in connection with trust business are the requirements of Regulation F of the 

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System-Trust Powers of National Banks. Regulation F applies to all national banks with a trust department. It requires that the acceptance of trust accounts must be approved by the board or by a committee appointed by the board. The closing out or relinquishment of an account must be ratified or approved by the board or by a committee. Investments must be made, retained, or disposed of only with the approval of a committee of "capable and experienced officers or directors." Once during each period of twelve months the trust department must be audited by, or under the supervision of, a committee of directors, exclusive of active officers. Trust property and trust funds must be reviewed annually by a committee.

The members, from the board of directors, of trust department committees usually represent the principal businesses and professions in the community. The committee is frequently made up of a lawyer, a banker, a merchant, a manufacturer, a transportation man, and an investment man. In one institution, director members of the trust committee comprise the executive officer of a life insurance company in charge of investments, an engineer, a lawyer, and an executive of a fire insurance company in charge of investments. In a manufacturing center there would be one or more manufacturers

on the committee; in a rural community, one or more farmers. The officers and directors of a trust institution, drawing upon the knowledge and experience gained in their businesses and professions, supply the group judgment on which the decisions they are called upon to make, as discussed above, are based. Impartiality

A settlor or testator desires that his executor or trustee shall be impartial in his dealings with present beneficiaries. As between or among beneficiaries of the same class or as between present beneficiaries and ultimate beneficiaries as classes, a person by express provision in the instrument may authorize his executor or trustee to favor one or more beneficiaries over others or one class at the expense of the other class. But, even in the face of such a provision, he wants his executor or trustee to keep prejudice or partiality out of all his dealings with beneficiaries.

In desiring impartiality a person is only looking for what the law requires. Court after court has declared that it is the duty of an executor, trustee, or guardian to deal impartially as between the several beneficiaries.22 The difficulty with the court, however, is not in making the requirement but in exacting its fulfillment. 

A trust institution is especially qualified to meet this requirement. 

It usually comes into the executorship, trusteeship, or guardianship without preconceived prejudices or partialities on the part of the members of its staff. In the course of a day it usually deals with a number of estates and trusts, each with its own set of beneficiaries. 

It does not act through one person alone. Thus the effect of partiality, should it exist in any individual officer to any degree, is reduced to the minimum in the meeting of minds of the group as a whole and it usually disappears entirely from the final decision. 

In some common law countries an attempt has been made to legislate impartiality. In British India, for example, a statute provides that, where there are more beneficiaries than one, the trustee is bound to be impartial and must not execute the trust for the 

22 Scott on Trusts, sec. 183, pp. 970-972.

advantage of one at the expense of another.23 To try to legislate impartiality is almost as futile as to try to legislate morals. The main safeguard against partiality lies, not in legislation, but in the fairminded attitude of those who by experience have realized the necessity for impartiality in dealing with beneficiaries. Confidentiality

A person wants his estate and trust affairs to be kept confidential. 

Although, as Professor Scott points out, there is a distinction between a fiduciary relation and a confidential relation, it is true, nevertheless, that people expect the relation between an executor, trustee, or guardian and the testator, settlor, or beneficiary to be confidential as well as fiduciary. A confidential relation, he says, exists between two persons when one has gained the confidence of the other and purports to act or advise with the other's interest in mind.24 Gaining the confidence of another and acting and advising with his interest in mind is the very essence of all trust service. 

From the day a person enters the employment of a trust institution to the end of his employment there, he has impressed upon him that whatever goes on inside a trust department with respect to an account, a beneficiary, or any trust matter is strictly confidential. The confidentiality of the dealings between a trust officer or any member of the trust department staff and a trust customer, a prospective customer, a trust beneficiary, or any person who transacts business with the trust department is understood by the entire staff to be as inviolable as that of the relations between a lawyer and his client or between a doctor and his patient. 

In some countries the members of the staff of a trust institution are required to subscribe to an oath or affirmation of secrecy. In 

Tasmania, for example, members of the staff, directors, auditors, and agents of some trust institutions are required to make the fol lowing Declaration of Secrecy: 

of do hereby solemnly and sincerely declare that I will truly and faithfully according to the best of my skill and ability perform the duties imposed upon me in my capacity as a member of the staff of the and 23 Indian Trusts Act II of 1882, sec. 17; quoted in Trust Business in Common Law 

Countries, p. 489. 

24 Scott on Trusts, sec. 2.5, p. 34.

that in the performance of my said duties I will hold myself bound not to divulge or communicate any matter or thing coming to my knowledge appertaining to the business of the or to the affairs of any estate under administration in such office or in relation to the private affairs of any person concerned therein except as may be authorized by law to a person directly interested and entitled to information or for the purpose of assisting to carry out the powers and function of the and my own proper duties; and I make this solemn declaration by virtue of Section 132 of the Evidence Act, 1910.25 

In the United States, although a formal declaration of secrecy may not be required, confidentiality is one of the qualities expected of trust institutions. In turn, trust institutions, in their Statement of 

Principles, declare that a trust department is a distinctly private institution in its relations with its customers." Adaptability 26 Adaptability is still another quality that people look for in the one who is to handle their estate. By adaptability here is meant the ability to meet all the estate requirements of a person, not merely just one or only a few of them.

For the proper settlement and administration of his estate a person may need, among others, the services of the following: (1) an executor, (2) a trustee under will, (3) a trustee under one or more living trust agreements, (4) a trustee under one or more insurance trust agreements, and (5) an agent to represent him in one or more capacities. He has only one estate to be settled or administered, and many of the services connected with its distribution or administration are related to and affected by one another. 

There are some advantages in having the same person or the same corporation serve his estate in all these capacities. As people sometimes express it, they prefer to do all their trust business under one roof and with one set of people. 

Adaptability is one of the outstanding characteristics of trust institutions. They possess adaptability with respect to the number and variety of the services they render, the size of estates they handle, the characteristics of beneficiaries they serve, and the special needs of the trade areas in which they operate. 

Most trust institutions have general trust authority- that is, the 

25 Trust Business in Common Law Countries, p. 392. 

26 Art. VII, sec. I.

authority to render any and every recognized service needed for the administration and settlement of estates. A composite list of the trust functions of trust institutions in the United States contains 

80 different items.27

Trust institutions handle small estates as well as large ones, some difficult and some easy. The prospect of the constantly diminishing size of estates is likely soon to bring this aspect of adaptability into still greater prominence. Through the common trust fund and through further operating economies trust institutions may be able to administer small accounts relatively as economically and profitably as they now administer large accounts. 

Trust institutions serve abnormal as well as normal beneficiaries. Some beneficiaries are difficult and some easy to deal with. 

Trust institutions must, of necessity, adapt their services to different kinds of beneficiaries and must serve them with understanding and sympathy.

The trust property in the hands of a trust institution represents a cross-section of the economic wealth of the community. Trust institutions must adapt their services to the special economic needs of the trade area. In agricultural areas they develop farm management services; in industrial centers, corporate trust and agency services. 

Accessibility 

For an executor, trustee, or guardian, a person needs both for himself and for his beneficiaries one that is accessible- that is, physically within reach and open for business at all reasonable hours of every business day. The affairs of an estate or the needs of a beneficiary do not run according to a calendar. 

A trust institution, by virtue of its location, its organization, and its method of operation, possesses this quality of accessibility. It usually is located centrally and prominently and its location is well known. Although one or more members of the staff may be ill, absent, or incapacitated on a given day, other members will be on hand to carry on. The trust institution is open for the transaction of trust business and the rendering of trust service during business 

27 Trust Business in Common Law Countries, pp. 552-557.

hours of any and every business day of the year. As regards personal services to beneficiaries, as distinguished from business services to the estate, the members of the staff, who usually are well-known members of the community, are accessible for a special need or an emergency at any hour of the day or night. 

Governmental Supervision

People desire that their executor or trustee shall be advised, guided and kept in the proper path at every step in the discharge of its duties. For trust institutions such guidance is accomplished by means of a system of governmental supervision."

Governmental supervision of trust institutions is a distinctive development of the United States. Supervision is exercised in four different, yet related, ways. First, the trust institution as a corporation must be chartered. Second, in most states it must also be licensed by some governmental authority to open a trust department and to carry on a trust business. Third, it must operate its trust department under and in accordance with regulations prescribed by governmental agencies, federal or state. Fourth, it is subject to periodic unannounced examination by trained examiners representing these governmental authorities.

The functions of governmental supervision of trust institutions may be summarized as follows: 

1. Formulating and promulgating through an authorized governmental authority (state or federal), rules and regulations for the administration of trust business; 

2. Examining trust institutions to see whether or not these rules and regulations are being complied with; 

3. Enforcing compliance with these rules and regulations in appropriate and effective ways; 

4. Determining whether the trust business is being conducted in accordance with law and sound policies and practices; and 

28 Ibid., pp. 600-601; Stephenson, Gilbert Thomas, Studies in Trust Business, p. 272; Research Council, American Bankers Association, New York, 1938 (hereafter cited by the shorter form, Stephenson's Studies).

5. Bringing about corrections where they are necessary or desirable.29

Of these five, the last two are the preventive and advisory functions of governmental supervision. The development of these two functions has been especially marked since 1933. The governmental examiners, in addition to carrying out their duties as auditors, often offer friendly suggestions which help trust institutions in carrying on their trust business in accordance with the law and the regulations and in accordance with sound policies and practices. 

CONCLUSIONS 

The two basic facts: (1) that trust institutions are co-extensive with the common law world and (2) that, as far as the author's research has revealed, they have come into being in response to the demand of the people, and not merely upon the initiative of promoters are indicative of their essentiality to people living in countries where the English common law prevails. 

In no respect is it claimed that trust institutions have a monopoly in meeting any of the twelve attributes of executors and trustees discussed in this Study, except governmental supervision. All that is claimed is that trust institutions are in an especially advantageous position to serve people with respect to their basic estate and trust needs.

An unimpeachable presentation and interpretation of the basic needs of people for trust institutions in connection with their estates and trusts are highly important at this particular juncture in the life of the nation. If trust institutions were only conveniences, their existence would be in constant jeopardy during a period of economic stress or strain, when excess cargo usually is thrown overboard. 

However, to the extent that trust institutions serve certain basic needs and serve them better than anyone else, one should have no misgiving about their permanency in the economic order. 

In this country their permanency is assured as long as the private ownership of property is recognized and safeguarded, as long as 

29 For further discussion of governmental supervision, see Johnson, William H. A.,

Trust Department Supervision, Trust Bulletin, July 1936, p. 6; Wyatt, Walter, Supervisory Policies of the Federal Reserve System, Trust Bulletin, February, 1936, p. 18; Neilan, Edwin P., Trust Examination, Research Council, American Bankers 

Association, New York, 1939; and Stephenson's Studies, pp. 271-285.

freedom of disposition of property by will is preserved, and as long as the trust remains one of the most cherished and flexible instruments in the armory of our jurisprudence. Trust institutions may be expected to last as long as democracy itself lasts, for they have indispensable and irreplaceable functions to perform. They are both rooted in and outgrowths of the democratic way of life.

SECOND SERIES, NUMBER THREE 

February

How Executive 

Officers and Directors 

Keep in Touch with

Trust Matters by 

The Graduate School of Banking 

American Bankers Association 1942 

SUPPLEMENT TO THE TRUST BULLETIN 

Published by 

The Trust Division AMERICAN BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

22 East 40 Street New York, N. Y.-

HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 3 HOW  KEEP IN TOUCH WITH TRUST MATTERS 

"AS S president of a bank and trust company, what is your greatest executive problem with respect to your trust department?"

The prompt answer to this question, which was given by the president of a large midwestern trust institution in 1938, first suggested the making of this Study as an attempt to find workable solutions of what this president called his greatest executive problem with respect to his trust department. His answer was, "How can I keep in touch with what is going on in the trust department without its taking too much of my time or interfering too much with its work."

This Study, as it has developed, has been enlarged to include not only the chief executive officer- the president or the chairman of the board or sometimes the executive vice president - but also other executive officers¹ and members of the board of directors of a trust institution. 

Problem Common to all Types of Trust Institutions 

How the executive officers and directors of a trust institution may keep in close touch with what is going on in the trust department is a nationwide problem that concerns the small as well as the large trust institution. For a particular trust institution the problem arises not so much from its size as from its plan of organization. 

1 The Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System defines an executive officer as one "who participates or has authority to participate in the operating management of the bank or any branch thereof." Regulation O, sec. 1 (b). 45

Larger institutions. In the largest trust department, with groups of divisions and sections, each necessarily operating almost independently of the others, and with several thousand trust accounts of all types and sizes, it is a stupendous problem for the head of the department to keep in close touch with even the most important things going on in his department. For the president, and for the executive officers in the banking and other departments, and for the directors to do so is even a greater problem. 

Smaller institutions. In the small trust department the head of the trust department frequently has many non-trust duties. He may be obliged to leave the daily routine work of the trust department largely to junior officers, upon whom he must depend to bring to him only such matters as require his personal attention. In the smaller institution, the other executive officers and the directors frequently must depend upon some one to help keep them in touch with trust matters.

Medium-sized institutions. The problem is perhaps less acute in the medium-sized trust institution than it is in either the very large or the very small institution. Here the head of the trust department usually has the whole trust department, including each division, section, and unit, under his direct supervision. The president is in daily contact with his associates and has the opportunity to know what is going on in all the departments. In the medium-sized institution it may be easier to arrange conferences and meetings by means of which the other executive officers and the dirctors may keep in touch with trust matters.

Scope of Study 

Recently the following questionnaire was submitted to the head of the trust department of each of 30 of the larger trust institutions of the country: 

1. How do you actually keep in touch with what is going on in your trust department? What reports (formal or informal) do you require of your associate officers or division heads? What meetings of trust officers or division heads do you hold for the purpose of keeping in touch with what is going on? 

2. How do you actually keep your president informed of what is

going on in your department? What reports do you or others make to him? What trust meetings does he attend?

The replies received in response to this questionnaire would convince anyone that there is no one generally accepted best way of keeping the president, the head of the trust department, and the other executive officers, in touch with trust matters; furthermore, that the way of keeping such officers in touch with trust matters is governed largely by the plans of organization of the particular trust department and of the trust institution. No institution has found the solution to the problem. Most of them, however, have found a solution more or less satisfactory to themselves. 

'HOW THE HEAD OF THE TRUST DEPARTMENT KEEPS IN TOUCH WITH TRUST MATTERS 

In general, there are six aids to the head of the trust department in keeping in touch with trust matters: (1) mail; (2) memorandums; (3) reports; (4) meetings; (5) minutes; (6) conversations. Mail

Both the incoming and outgoing mail of the trust department may serve a useful purpose in keeping the head of the department in touch with trust matters.

Incoming mail. In some of the smaller trust departments the head of the department has all incoming mail pass over his desk. 

Although he may not undertake to read it all every day, with the aid of his secretary he sees that it is routed to the proper persons in the trust department. In the largest trust departments this procedure is not practicable. Where, however, the procedure is practicable, it may be psychologically advantageous in that it lets the people in the department know that the head of the department is keeping up with the correspondence, or at least has an opportunity of doing so. It has the disadvantages of (1) imposing a rather heavy, detailed duty upon the head of the trust department at the beginning of the day and (2) delaying the work in the trust department. 

Outgoing mail. There are two ways by which the head of the trust department may use the outgoing mail as a means of keeping

од 

in touch with trust matters. One is by his signing or initialing all outgoing letters; the other, by having an extra carbon copy of outgoing letters placed on his desk daily. 

The main disadvantages of the head of the trust department's requiring all outgoing mail to be brought to him daily for signing or initialing are that it (1) may put an undue physical burden upon him and (2) may tend to relieve other persons in the department of assuming their share of responsibility. Many heads of smaller and even medium-sized trust departments think that the advantages of signing or initialing outgoing letters outweigh the disadvantages.

There are distinct advantages in the procedure by which the head of the trust department has an extra carbon copy of each outgoing letter placed on his desk daily. Since it is not a file copy, the filing will not be held up should the head of the trust department be delayed in reading and initialing the carbon copy. Some heads of the trust department value very highly the reading of these carbon copies. The head of one large trust department says: 

We have been making use of such a file for the past six years and I am entirely sold on the idea that this is an effective method of keeping everycae in touch with the affairs of the trust department. 

Instead of its being a burden to me, I actually look forward to reading the file as it enables me to keep a careful check on everything that is going on. It is not necessary to rush through it in the morning at the time one usually opens his mail, and no harm is done if it waits over until later in the day. 

However, if the department head, finding this procedure burdensome, should fall into the habit of thumbing the carbon copies quickly, initialing the top sheet, and letting them go without giving them more than a casual glance, the task would become a mere formality, might have a demoralizing effect upon the staff, and should be dispensed with.

Even in the largest trust departments, in which it would not be practicable for the head of the trust department daily to read the carbon copies of all outgoing mail, he may nevertheless call for them by divisions, sections, or units of the trust department and in this way make good use of these carbon copies as a means of helping him keep in touch with trust matters in the different divisions, sections, or units. Whatever the size or plan of organization of the trust department may be, the head of the department will

be wise frequently, if not daily, to call for and read the carbon copies of all or of a definite portion of the outgoing mail of the previous day.

Memorandums 

There are three different types of memorandums designed to keep the head of the trust department in touch with trust matters: the memorandum proper; the daily blotter; and the bulletin. 

Memorandum proper. The head of the trust department and every administrative officer and division head in the trust department should form the habit of making and keeping memorandums of all important trust matters. The actual method of doing this is largely a matter of training, experience, and habit. Some administrative officers and division heads are disposed to go to one extreme by making a memorandum of every little thing that takes place during the day, while others are disposed to go to the other extreme by making a memorandum of only what they at the moment regard as the most important matters and failing to make a memorandum of matters, apparently of little importance at the time, that turn out later to be of very great importance. 

One of the functions of the head of a trust department is to take up with his administrative officers and division heads, one by one, the matters on which he desires memorandums made and brought to his attention.

However, it is fully as easy to overdo as it is to underdo the making and keeping of memorandums. If it is overdone, the procedure sooner or later will break down under its own weight. 

Daily blotter. Several years ago one of the large trust departments of the Midwest adopted a daily blotter system. Each officer in charge of a division or sub-division of the trust department keeps a daily blotter covering his activities and those of his subordinates. 

Each officer's secretary keeps a record of all personal and telephone calls received during the day and keeps a record of important letters dictated. If anyone of the assistants or clerical force in a particular division or section handles an important matter, this matter is referred to the officer in charge of that division or section to be covered in his daily blotter. From these records, before the rush of the day's work begins, each officer dictates a brief summary of

the conversations and important letters of the day before. This summary is kept in blotter form and a carbon copy is placed each morning on the desk of the executive head of the trust department. 

By this means, in a few moments each morning, he can glance over the reports and keep fairly well in touch with the activities of the various divisions and sections. 

Bulletin. A very few of the largest trust departments issue bulletins. Some of these bulletins are confidential and naturally go only to those designated to receive them. Others contain general information and are available to the entire trust department staff. All of these bulletins come as a matter of course to the attention of the head of the trust department. It is doubtful that the bulletin is very useful in keeping the head of the trust department in touch with many trust matters except those of a general nature. 

Reports 

For keeping in touch with trust matters, the head of the trust department must rely to a great extent upon the formal reports made to him periodically or when called for by him. There is an almost endless variety of such reports. The following is a partial list of matters upon which reports are made, as indicated by the answers to the questionnaire: 

Wills received and withdrawn.

Trust accounts opened and closed (with reason for closing). 

Accounts to be presented to the courts. 

Real property transactions — sales, purchases, rentals, repairs. 

Suits pending or threatened. 

Cash balances awaiting investment or distribution. 

Bonds deposited to secure cash deposits. 

Overdrafts or advances. Budget. 

Personnel additions and resignations. 

Securities filed in vault. 

Securities ordered sold or bought. 

Estate and inheritance tax records. 

Brokers' transactions. 

Auditor's report on accounts. 

Fees, commissions, and allowances.

Opinions of counsel.

The budget report, which is one of the most interesting of these reports, although it is not always so named, is described as "a monthly report originating in the statistical department of the bank and covering each department." With respect to the trust department, the budget figures are shown for the current month, for the subsequent month, and for the corresponding month of the preceding year. Total figures to date are also shown for the current year and for the preceding year, as well as for the amount the department is over or under its budget and for the unexpended balance for the year.

The head of the trust department of a large trust institution, which operates a number of branch trust departments, gives the following outline and description of the reports made to him: 

1. By the branches: (a) Earnings; monthly and semi-annual.

These figures are broken down and tabulated in the head office to make possible, at a glance, four-year comparisons of earnings of the various branches and of the various classes of our trusts. (b) Expenses; monthly and semi-annual. 

Net earnings (exclusive of bank overhead) are tabulated in the head office, providing a four-year comparison of monthly net earnings of each branch. (c) Trusts closed and trusts opened; for the week.

These disclose the capacity in which the institution has acted or will act, the rate and amount of the annual charge, the character and estimated value of the assets, the reason for closing business, the source of new business, the name of attorney associated, and a brief statement of the trustee's duties. (d) Business opened and closed and on the books at the month's end; a monthly summary.

From these reports, tabulations of assets, collateral securities. number and classification of existing trusts, etc., are maintained in the head office. 

2. By the head-office service divisions: (a) Real Estate Division 

(b) Trust Analysis Division 

(c) Trust Deed Division 

(d) Trust Development Division 

There are current monthly and semi-monthly statistical reports.

3. By the Comptroller's Department: 

(a) Semi-annual tabulations showing number and salary-rate of trust department staff, segregated as among branches, among divisions, and among officers and employees.

Reports on trust matters are essential but they should not be so numerous or so detailed as to become burdensome alike to the head of the trust department and to the persons who make them. It is as easy and as harmful to overdo the making of reports as it is to overdo the preparation of memorandums.

Meetings Every well-organized trust department has various departmental committees. Many of these committees which have similar functions are known by different names. There are, for instance, the trust committee, the trust investment committee, the corporate trust committee, the trust officers' committee, the trust new business committee, and the probate division committee.

Attendance. Should the head of the trust department attend all department committee meetings? He should, as a matter of course, be ex officio a member of every department committee, and he should attend as many of the meetings as possible. In committee meetings, where trust matters are presented and discussed, the head of the trust department obtains more vivid impressions about these matters than he can obtain from written reports. Furthermore, in such meetings he has an opportunity to size up his junior officers and division heads. But if the head of a large trust department attended every department committee meeting it would be impossible for him effectively to perform all his other executive functions as head of the department.

Should the head of the trust department preside at the trust department committee meetings that he does attend? With few exceptions it would seem preferable for some associate officer to assume the responsibility for calling and conducting each meeting. 

This leaves the head of the trust department free to come and go as his other engagements require. It permits him to participate more actively in the discussions than he would or perhaps should do as chairman.

Minutes. The head of the trust department should give careful consideration to the kind and copiousness of the minutes to be made of the transactions in the departmental committee meetings. 

It is important that a minute be made and kept of every material discussion, motion, resolution, or recommendation of a departmental committee. Yet, if one is not on guard, in a short time the minutes may become so voluminous as to be practically useless. The head of the trust department of one of the larger eastern trust institutions says that the minutes of his trust management commitee in eight years have grown into a library of 150 volumes. The function of this particular committee, which is composed of five senior officers and meets at least twice a week, is to pass on all special matters and to decide whether or not the committee itself can handle them or must pass them on to the president or chairman of the board or to the directors' committee. This library of 150 volumes represents the minute-books of only one of the several department committees of this institution. Conversations

They are referred to as "conversations" rather than as "interviews" or "conferences" in order to emphasize their informality. 

After all, the best way for the head of the trust department to keep in touch with trust matters is to establish and maintain close, friendly, sympathetic contacts with those who, under his supervision, are settling the estates, administering the trusts, performing the agencies, and doing the other trust work. There is something lacking in any organization in which the time of the head of the department is taken up so completely with callers, correspondence, reports, or meetings that he does not have the time, energy, or disposition to talk frequently, freely, and understandingly with his associates, his junior officers, and his division heads.

The head of the trust department should teach the members of his staff what to bring and what not to bring to his personal attention. If he encourages or permits them to bring to him for decision all the daily routine matters that call for prompt action, he soon will lose himself in details and have no time or energy for executive functions. If, on the other hand, he raises a barrier between

himself and his associates, junior officers, and division heads, and still holds them responsible for results without making it easy or possible for them to talk over matters with him, he is putting himself in a position where he cannot keep in close enough touch with trust matters to enable him effectively to advise with his staff. 

It may be practicable and desirable for the head of the trust department to have a designated time each day for conversations with the heads of his several divisions—real property, security analysis, tax, probate, and others. But problems in these divisions do not await any designated hour to obtrude themselves. Therefore, the head of the trust department should endeavor to make all the people in his department feel that they are free to bring important matters to him at any hour of the day, whenever they can get his attention without disturbing a customer or other caller.

HOW THE PRESIDENT KEEPS IN TOUCH WITH TRUST MATTERS 

How the president may keep in close touch with trust matters is a much more difficult problem. The president has a multitude of other duties to perform. He may have had little experience in conducting a trust department. Yet the president usually is the chief executive officer of the institution and the trust department is an integral and coordinate part of the bank or trust company. Consequently, it is as much the function of the president to keep in touch with trust matters as it is to keep in touch with bank matters. 

The ways already discussed of keeping the head of the trust department in touch with trust matters are not appropriate in all respects for the president. For example, the president of a large bank or trust company naturally cannot be expected to read the incoming or the outgoing mail of the trust department or the carbon copies of outgoing letters and an occasional reading would afford little connected information. However, the value to the president of the reading file of incoming and outgoing mail, as far as it is practicable for him to do so, should not be discounted. The head of one large trust department says:

As for the value of the reading file to the banking executives, it is my experience that more attention is paid to it than you seem to think. I am frequently called upon for an explanation of the whys and wherefores of certain letters. This gives me an added opportunity to more fully acquaint the banking executives with our problems.

Trust department memorandums and bulletins are not particularly helpful to the president. The president of a large trust institution would not have time to read all the minutes of the trust department committees.

It follows that, if the president is to keep in touch with trust matters through memorandums, reports, meetings, and conversations, they must be specially designed for the purpose. Memorandums

It is important for the head of the trust department to prepare or to pass on to the president memorandums on trust matters that involve or that may involve the institution as a whole. There should be an understanding between the head of the trust department and the president as to the kind of matters that should be presented by memorandums.

Weekly blotter. The trust department which has a daily blotter for the head of the trust department, as already described, also has a system of weekly blotter for the president, that has been in operation about four years. The administrative head of the trust department prepares and gives to the president each Monday morning a brief weekly summary from the daily blotter reports of the various officers and division heads regarding the matters which he thinks may interest the president. The president says that he finds this summary very valuable in that it gives him an idea of what is going on in the trust department without the necessity of his consuming a great deal of time in lengthy conferences. 

Reports 

It is neither possible nor necessary for the president to read and digest every technical or detailed report made to the head of the trust department. It is a problem, however, where to draw the line. 

One test that will apply in most cases is whether the report deals with matters calling for action by the directors' committee or only by the officers' committee. If the former, then the president should see and have an opportunity to digest and understand the report before it is presented to the directors' committee. A president may

be in a very embarrassing position should some trust matter calling for action by the directors' committee or by the board be presented to the committee or to the board without his knowing enough about the matter to discuss it intelligently. 

Meetings 

In the larger trust institutions where it may be a practical impossibility for the president to attend all the trust department committee meetings, he should attend as many as possible. His doing so shows the people in the trust department that their president really is interested in what is going on in the department and gives the president an opportunity to size up the men who are working on the department committees.

In the smaller and medium-sized institutions it is practicable, as a rule, for the president to attend the meetings and take an active part in the work of the trust department committees. In fact, many presidents highly prize their attendance at these meetings both as a means of keeping up with what is going on in the trust department and as a means of being of real assistance in the conduct of the department. So also do the heads of the trust department highly evaluate the president's attendance. One head of the trust department says:

Our trust committee meets very frequently, and it is a rare occasion when either the president of the bank or one of the higher banking officials is not in attendance. 

In my opinion, the attendance of the banking executives at the trust committee meetings is probably the most important factor in securing complete cooperation between the trust committee and the banking department. 

Minutes. The president of a trust institution should, of course, be expected to read and digest the minutes on trust matters of the directors' committee meetings and of the board meetings. When a matter is important enough to come before a committee of the board or the board itself by report, minute, or in any other way, it is important enough to be brought to the attention of the president beforehand. He should be in a position to discuss the matter intelligently with his committee or with his board. He should not be expected to read and to digest all the trust department committee minutes, nor all the technical reports that come to the head of the trust department.

Instead of dumping into the president's lap a lot of trust department reports and minutes, the head of the trust department might have a digest made of all matters that should come to the attention of the president, the board, or a committee of the board. The complete reports and minutes themselves are, of course, available to the president at all times and, whenever he can do so, he should avail himself of the opportunity to read them in full. Conversations

Conversation is a valuable means of keeping the president in touch with trust matters. This means not only conversation between the president and the head of the trust department but also between the president and other members of the trust department staff. The head of the trust department should feel free to go to the president on any and all trust matters of major importance. For example, the president should be informed at once if any suit is entered or threatened for breach of trust. The president should be brought into the discussion of any matter of sizable proportion which is up for compromise or adjustment. Perhaps this is a workable test: Any matter that cannot be settled inside the department and, therefore, must be brought to the attention of the trust committee of the board of directors should be brought to the attention of the president beforehand.

The head of the trust department should know what matters to bring to the president for his decision or information, or for the joint decision of the president and the head of the trust department, or for reference to the trust committee or to the entire board. He should not, however, be going to the president throughout the day with minor trust problems that he himself should solve, or ask the president to make a decision on any trust matter without a complete understanding of all the facts, or put upon the president the responsibility for that decision when it is questioned. 

The head of the trust department is an executive officer. Regulation F-Trust Powers of National Banks²-provides that the trust department of a national bank shall be under the management and immediate supervision of an executive officer. The Board of Gover- 

2 Sec. 6 (d).

nors of the Federal Reserve System has stated specifically that it will be assumed that a trust officer is an executive officer unless the bylaws of the bank or a resolution of the bank provides that he is not authorized "to participate in the operating management of the bank and he does not actually participate therein."³ 

The head of the trust department should not be the only channel through which trust matters flow from the trust department to the president's office. If an associate senior officer, junior officer, or division head brings to the attention of the head of the trust department a matter about which the latter feels that he should consult the president, it is a good rule, subject to exceptions, for the head of the trust department, when he goes to see the president, to take with him the person who is presenting the problem. The latter should of course go direct to the president on such a matter only at the suggestion of the head of the trust department. 

This procedure serves two good purposes. First, it makes certain that the problem is presented to the president in the way the person who raised the problem would like to have it presented. Second, it gives the president the opportunity to size up the various men in the trust department who are holding positions of responsibility. 

Still further, it helps the morale in a trust department when the junior officers and the division heads feel that, if they raise a problem of real moment, they can discuss it freely with the head of the department and, if necessary, with the president himself. 

The head of one large trust department has the following to say on this point:

I am glad you mention the importance of other trust officers feeling free to talk directly with the president about what is going on in the trust department. I have found this very helpful, and both the president and I have encouraged it in every way possible.

The way between the trust department and the president's office should not be a one-way street. The president should not always wait for the head of his trust department to come to him with his problems. At times the president would do well to go in to see the head of the trust department and ask, in substance: "What is going on in the trust department? Have you any problems that are bother- 

3 Regulation O, sec. 1, (b).

ing you? What are your plans for building up the department? Are there any trust matters that you would like to talk over with me?" 

There are a few presidents, unfortunately, who almost never darken the door of the trust department, who send for the head of the trust department only when there is something the matter in that department. The head of one large trust department said: 

Our president visits the trust department on an average of twice a year. With a president who is not willing to give any appreciable amount of his time to the department it is impossible for us to keep him informed to any extent that will make him a valuable adviser for the department. 

Fortunately, there are many presidents of trust institutions who make a habit of visiting the trust department daily and having a brief talk with the head of the trust department. 

While up to this point the discussion has been confined to keeping the president in touch with trust matters related to business already on the books of the trust department, it should not be overlooked that if the president is in close touch with the trust department he can be of great help also in obtaining new trust business. 

It is especially helpful, for example, to keep him informed as to persons and corporations who are being solicited for new trust accounts. Only by so doing can the trust department gain the full benefit of the president's prestige and personal contacts in the community which the bank or trust company serves. 

How OTHER EXECUTIVE OFFICERS KEEP IN TOUCH WITH TRUST MATTERS 

How do executive officers in other departments of a large or even a medium-sized trust institution keep in touch with trust matters? 

The following aids to a vice-president in the banking department, for instance, have been suggested: (1) having special articles on trust subjects called to his attention, (2) serving on trust department committees, and (3) attending officers' meetings at which trust matters are discussed. 

Special Articles on Trust Subjects 

If the head of the trust department were simply to hand or send to the head of the banking department a current issue of The Trust

Bulletin or of Trusts and Estates or of some other trust publication without comment, the latter probably would lay the publication aside for future reading and never get around to read it. If, on the other hand, the head of the trust department were to select from the publication just one article that would be of interest to a banker and call it directly and pointedly to the attention of the head of the banking department and ask the latter to read it and say what he thought of it, the chances are that the bank officer would read, criticize, and comment on the article. The six articles referred to at the end of this Study are typical of trust articles that would be of special interest to bank officers.

For this aid to be effective, the head of the trust department must use great care to select articles which are not too lengthy or technical. He must first read them himself to make sure of their contents and not go by title only. He must supply them to the banking department officers frequently and regularly, without letting the procedure lapse into a routine formality. 

Trust Department Committees

According to the plans of organization of the institution and of the trust department, executive officers of other departments may either serve as active members of one or several of the trust department committees or, by general understanding, sit in on the committee meetings. In smaller institutions all the executive officers of other departments might serve as active members or attend the meetings. In larger institutions they might serve on or visit these committees under some system of rotation. In every trust institution, large or small, it might be arranged for every executive officer in every department, in course of time, to serve on all of the trust department committees or occasionally attend their meetings. 

It is an accepted policy of trust institutions that the trust department from time to time should utilize the knowledge and experience and obtain the points of view of the executive officers of other departments. The trust department, in turn, may offer attendance and participation in its own departmental committee meetings as an effective aid in keeping executive officers in other departments in touch with trust matters.

Officers' Meetings 

Most trust institutions, with varying degrees of regularity and formality, have officers' meetings at which all the executive officers of all departments come together to discuss the affairs of the institution as a whole and in a general way the affairs of each department. These officers' meetings give the head of the trust department an opportunity to fit the trust department into the picture of the institution by acquainting the other officers with and interesting them in the important things taking place in the trust department. 

The head of the trust department should select with much care the trust matters to be brought to the attention of, and discussed with, other executive officers. On the one hand, he must avoid tedious generalities or superficialities of which the other officers soon will tire; on the other hand, he must avoid technicalities with which officers outside the trust department are not familiar. A trust officer, who has a sense of proportion and of news values, can make what he has to say in the officers' meeting about trust matters one of the most interesting parts of the meeting. Trust matters teem with human interest.

In addition to the three aids just discussed in keeping other executive officers in touch with trust matters, conversation may be very effective. The head of the trust department, who over the years acquires the habit of talking trust matters over tactfully and in an interesting manner with his associate officers in other departments, not only will get a great deal of help from them in solving his own departmental problems but also will stimulate and cultivate their active interest in trust matters. 

Each of the foregoing suggestions casts the main responsibility for putting them into effect upon the head of the trust department. 

However, one presupposes throughout that the head of the trust department has the solid backing of his president in his efforts to reach and interest his associate officers in trust matters. Without this backing the head of the trust department is helpless; with it, he can make an important place for his department in the interest and understanding of the other officers.

HOW DIRECTORS KEEP IN TOUCH WITH TRUST MATTERS 

Since a trust department is an integral and coordinate part of the bank or trust company, directors share with officers responsibility for the proper execution of the trust business. It is fully as important, therefore, that directors keep in touch with trust matters as with other affairs of the bank or trust company. 

Trust institutions over the country report that they have found these five things helpful in keeping their directors in touch with trust matters: (1) Having the head of the trust department appear regularly before the board of directors and make a report on the trust department; (2) having the head of the trust department or one of the trust officers make a series of brief addresses to the board on trust subjects; (3) having directors serve as rotating members of trust department committees; (4) having the head of the trust department and his trust officers advise with directors informally on trust matters; and (5) having the head of the trust department comment to the board of directors upon the reports of examination of the trust department by the governmental agencies. 

Report on Trust Department 

An increasing number of trust institutions say that they have found that the regular appearance of the head of the trust department at the board meetings and his making both a statistical and a narrative report of trust department activities, serve to keep the directors in touch with trust matters and to stimulate their interest in the trust department. If the head of the trust department is a director, he would attend the meetings anyhow and speak for the trust department. If he is not a member of the board, still he may, upon request, appear before the board at the appropriate time, make his report for the trust department, and withdraw from the meeting. 

The written report would be largely statistical, containing current and comparable figures on such items as new trust accounts, trust assets, earnings, and expenses. In addition to presenting the statistical report and making comments upon the figures, the head 

4 See Stephenson's Studies, p. 125-143: Chapter Eight, A Director's Responsibilities and Liabilities with Respect to Trust Business.

of the trust department might inform the board of other matters of interest in the trust department transpiring since the last meeting. 

For example, if a director had been especially helpful in obtaining a new trust account, the head of the trust department might mention this fact without mentioning any names or violating any confidences. This might serve also as a stimulus to other directors in helping to obtain new trust business. The presence of the head of the trust department would enable the directors to ask him questions on trust matters and to have him comment upon any trust matter any director sees fit to raise.

Addresses on Trust Subjects 

A few years ago one of the largest trust institutions in a southern city had one of its trust officers appear before the board of directors regularly each month for a period of a year and at each meeting make a ten-minute address on some trust subject that the trust officer thought would be of special interest to directors. Each address was written out beforehand and, after delivery, a copy was sent to all directors, those who were absent when it was delivered as well as those who were present. In the course of a year every director had the opportunity of hearing or reading a dozen brief, succinct, carefully prepared addresses on what the trust officer regarded as appropriate trust subjects. This innovation in the procedure of the board meetings of this institution was regarded by the officers and directors alike as having been notably successful. 

During the 1941-42 session of The Graduate School of Banking, the students majoring in Trusts, as one of their extension problems, were requested to prepare for delivery to their board of directors a ten-minute address on how officers and directors can help best to build up the trust department. It would be interesting and possibly helpful to the directors of the institutions from which these students come, in keeping in touch with trust matters, if the answer of the student were made available in some way to the directors of his institution.

Rotating Members of Trust Department Committees 

The regular members of the trust department committees have the best opportunity of all the directors to keep in touch with trust

matters. If, however, the membership of the board is large and if the policy of the trust institution is to continue the personnel of the trust department committees year after year, the result may be that some of the directors seldom or never will have opportunity of coming in close touch with trust matters through membership on trust department committees.

Some trust institutions have solved this problem satisfactorily by appointing one or more directors to serve as rotating members of the trust department committees. The term of service of a rotating member may be three or six months or a year. The term of service should be long enough to give the rotating member a close-up picture of the trust department and its problems. Thus, through rotating membership, every director will come in touch with trust matters within a comparatively short time. Another advantage of having rotating members is that it gives the president or the chairman of the board an opportunity to discover new material for the regular members of the trust department committees. 

Advising with Directors

The directors of many trust institutions are in a position to be especially helpful to trust officers in dealing with various administrative trust problems that are not appropriate for presentation to any of the committees or that do not lend themselves to committee action. In the course of a day's work an active trust officer frequently encounters problems that he would like to talk over freely and informally with some person whose judgment he respects and in talking with whom he does not run the risk of violating confidences. They are not technical problems calling for knowledge of the technique of trust business. Instead, they are mostly problems related to service to beneficiaries calling for business experience, common sense, and knowledge of human nature. If the trust officer felt free from time to time to call upon one or another of the directors of his institution and talk over these problems, the trust officer would find such conversations very helpful to him and the directors he talks with would find themselves becoming more and more interested in trust matters.

A trust officer might hesitate to approach a director on such mat-

ters. This would be particularly true if he was not a member of the board or did not have frequent or close contact with the directors. 

In deference to this hesitancy, the director himself might take the initiative by stopping by the trust officer's desk from time to time and asking him if there are any trust matters he would like to talk over. It would not matter how the trust officer and the director were brought together-whether on the initative of the one or the other; but, if in some way such a relationship were established and continued, the result inevitably would be the stimulation of the interest of the director in trust matters. 

Reports of Examination of Trust Department 

Some trust institutions have used very effectively the reports of examination of the trust department by the governmental agencies as an additional means of keeping the directors in touch with trust matters. In these institutions the chairman of the board or the president has the head of the trust department select from the report points, whether commendatory or critical, of special interest to the directors and comment upon these points at the meeting of the board at which the report on the trust department is considered. 

The institutions adopting this procedure do not mean these comments by the head of the trust department to be a substitute for the director's reading or digesting the report for himself. The trustman's comment upon the report tends to make the directors feel all the more keenly that they and the general officers and the trust department staff have a community of interest in and responsibility for the proper conduct of the trust business.

CONCLUSIONS 

There is no one, generally accepted, universally applicable, best way of keeping the head of the trust department, the president, the other executive officers, and the directors in close touch with trust matters. Yet in one way or another they must be kept in close enough touch with trust matters to be able properly to perform their respective functions.

Whatever means or aids are adopted and used for this purpose, the institution must be on guard always against setting up machin-

ery that will fall under its own weight or through cumbersomeness. After all, the chief function of the head of the trust department is to serve trust customers; letters, memorandums, reports, minutes, meetings, and conversations are but means to this end. 

When the mechanics of trust business gets the upper hand of the head of the trust department and consumes too much of his time and attention, his service to customers is impaired. Likewise, the chief duty of the president is to run a safe and sound bank or trust company; blotters, memorandums, reports, minutes, and meetings are but means to this end. 

In developing such plans, what practical use can be made of this 

Study? If the president of a trust institution is not quite satisfied with the extent to which he or the officers or directors are being kept in touch with trust matters, he might name a special committee to prepare a recommended plan. To the head of the trust department might be delegated the task of mapping out and supervising the work of the committee. Using this Study as a starting point, the committee might be able to work out some simple, workable and yet economical, even though not ideal, plan. Lest the committee become wedded to some particular plan, its members should remind themselves time and again that no plan, though it looks ideal on paper, is good if it does not work, while any plan, however informal it may be, is good if it does work. 

 Davis, Chester R., What the Head of the Trust Department Expects of His President, Trust Bulletin, March 1939, p. 13.

Trust Department, Trust Bulletin, March 1939, p. 8.
