SECOND SERIES, NUMBER FOUR 

April Should 

Trust Institutions 

Have Broad Discretionary Powers? by 

The Graduate School of Banking 

American Bankers Association 1942 

SUPPLEMENT TO THE TRUST 

Published by 

The Trust Division 

AMERICAN 

BULLETIN BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

22 East 40 Street New York, N. Y.

..HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 4 SHOULD TRUST INSTITUTIONS HAVE ? 

SHOULD a trust institution as executor or trustee have broad discretionary powers? In the settlement of an estate or the administration of a trust, should it be reasonably free to do what it deems best under the circumstances, or should it be told what to do and what not to do?

This question of broad discretionary powers for trust institutions, always important, under war conditions is assuming vastly increased importance. Post-war conditions will increase its importance still more. It is being raised both by testators and settlors and by draftsmen of wills and trust agreements. It is being asked of trust institutions. What is their attitude toward having broad powers? 

Do they prefer to have wide discretion or to act under instruction? 

This Study attempts to answer these three questions: (1) What are regarded as broad discretionary powers for trust institutions? (2) What are the attitudes of testators and settlors, of draftsmen, and of trust institutions toward trust institutions' having these powers? and (3) Why should trust institutions have them? This

Study is limited to personal trusts and does not deal with corporate trusts.

BROAD DISCRETIONARY POWERS 

Power, as the term is used in this Study, means the authority a trust institution as executor or trustee may exercise or refrain from exercising.

Classification of Powers 

The powers of an executor or trustee, whether corporate or individual, are classified in several ways—such as express and implied; 67

mandatory and discretionary; under statute and under the terms of the trust; and administration, investment, and distribution. An express power is an authority which is vested in an executor or trustee in clear and unequivocal terms; and implied power is one not stated in so many words but only implied or inferred from the terms and purposes of the will or trust. A mandatory power is one which it is the positive duty of the executor or trustee to exercise at some time and in some manner; a discretionary power is one which the executor or trustee may or may not exercise as it in good faith deems best. This Study is confined to express discretionary powers.¹ 

Express discretionary powers, as the term is employed in this Study, are those permissive powers granted by statute or by the trust instrument. Although a court order creating a trust is a trust instrument, it has not been deemed advisable to include in this Study a discussion of powers granted by court order. Also, while there may be express discretionary powers under an oral trust, they too are ruled out of this discussion, for a trust institution seldom, if ever, would accept an oral trust.

In this Study express discretionary powers in statutes and trust instruments are grouped under three heads: (1) those relating to the general administration of the estate or trust, or administration powers; (2) those relating to the investment of trust funds, or investment powers; and (3) those relating to the delivery, payment, application, or distribution of the funds or other property (principal or income) of the estate or trust, or distribution powers. Investment powers are a type of administration powers but, because of their special importance, they are treated separately from other administration powers. 

Powers under Statutes

Within recent years there has been a growing tendency on the part of state legislatures to give executors and trustees, corporate and individual alike, broader discretionary powers. So far the tendency has been more marked in other common law countries than in the 

United States.

The English Trustee Act, 1925, the Trusts (Scotland) Act, 1921, the 

1 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 551, p. 1734; Scott on Trusts, sec. 186, p. 982; Shattuck Revision, sec. 28, p. 93.

New Zealand Public Trust Office Act, 1908 and amendments, and the Trustee Acts of the various Provinces of Canada give broad discretionary powers to executors and trustees. In case of the New Zealand statute, however, these powers are restricted to the Public Trustee.2 

In the United States the broadest discretionary powers by statute to be found are those contained in the Florida Uniform Trust 

Admistration Act (1937), the Louisiana Trust Estates Act (1938), and the Oklahoma Trust Act (1940). A proposed act which, although it has failed of passing so far, should be mentioned in any study of discretionary powers is the Trust Administration Act for Minnesota, revision of 1940.

List of Powers under Statutes 

The following is a composite list of the discretionary powers contained in the Florida, Louisiana, and Oklahoma acts, and in the proposed Minnesota act, arranged under the three heads: administration powers, investment powers, and distribution powers. It should be understood that this is a composite list and that all of these powers are not to be found in any one of the acts but that each of them is to be found in one or more of the acts. These acts apply alike to corporate executors and trustees and to individual executors and trustees. No attempt is made to note the refinements or the slight differences in wording of the powers contained in these acts or even to follow the exact wording of the act. Furthermore, there is nothing to indicate from which of the acts the particular power is taken. 

Administration powers: 

1. Develop real property - To exchange, re-exchange, subdivide, develop, improve, dedicate to public use, make or obtain vacations of public plats, and adjust boundaries; to partition real property and on exchange or partition to adjust differences in valuation by giving or receiving money or money's worth; and to dedicate easements to public use without consideration if deemed by the trustee to be for the best interests of the trust. 

2. Grant options and sell To grant options and sell real property, including undivided interests, at public auction or private sale, for cash or upon credit secured by a lien on the property sold or other property 

2 Trust Business in Common Law Countries, pp. 157, 238, 315, and 715.

deemed adequate by the trustee, whether the division is horizontal, vertical, or is made in any other way. 

3. Grant leases To grant leases of real property with or without option of purchase, with or without covenants as to erection of buildings or as to renewals, although the term of the lease, renewal, or option may extend beyond the term of the trust. 

4. Raze and erect party walls or buildings — To raze existing party walls or buildings and erect new ones, alone or jointly with adjacent owners. 

5. Make repairs and alterations - To make ordinary or extraordinary repairs or alterations on buildings or other structures. 

6. Effect insurance - To effect and keep in force fire, windstorm, rent, title, liability, casualty, or other insurance of any nature, form, or amount for the proper protection of the property and the ownership thereof. 

To abandon property deemed by the trustee to 

7. Abandon property· be burdensome or valueless. 

8. Make oil, gas, and mineral contracts To make such contracts pertaining to oil, gas, or other natural resources as are customary in the community where the real property held in trust is located. 

9. Pay taxes and assessments - To pay all taxes and assessments levied or assessed against the trust estate or the trustee. 

10. Vote stock-To vote stock at corporate meetings in person or by proxy, with or without power of substitution. 

11. Use nominee To hold stock in its own name or in the name of a nominee or to keep securities unregistered, with or without disclosing the fiduciary relationship. 

12. Renew or extend or exchange obligations — To renew or extend or participate in the renewal or extension of notes, bonds, or other evidences of indebtedness, or to exchange the same. 

13. Reduce interest rates—To agree to a reduction in rates of interest on notes, bonds, or other evidences of indebtedness, or to any other modification or change to any extent deemed advisable. 

14. Change guarantees — To agree to any modification or change in the security for or guaranty of notes, bonds, or other evidences of indebtedness in the manner and to the extent deemed advisable. 

15. Waive default - To waive any defaut in performance of a covenant or condition of bonds, notes, or certificates of indebtedness or in the security therefor; to carry the same past due; and to enforce penalties for defaults as the trustee deems advisable.

16. Foreclose, bid in, and retain or dispose of property- To foreclose, bid in, and retain or dispose of property acquired under foreclosure. 

17. Create reserves To create reserves out of income for depreciation, obsolescence, amortization, taxes, assessments, and other obligations; and to restore to income reserves not used. 

18. Borrow and mortgage or pledge - To borrow money and, as security, to mortgage or pledge property or future principal or income. 

19. Advance funds for estate To advance money for the protection of the trust or trust property or for expenses, losses, or liabilities, and to hold a lien on the trust property for such advances and interest thereon. Expdate 

20. Raise funds-To sell, convert, mortgage, call in, or encumber trust property to raise principal to carry out the terms of the trust. 

21. Successor trustee As successor trustee to exercise the powers, duties, and discretions of the original trustee. 

22. Surviving trustee As surviving trustee to exercise the powers, duties, and discretions of the original co-trustees. 

23. Majority vote As co-trustees to act upon a majority vote unless the instrument provides otherwise. 

24. Incur expenses - To incur expenses necessary or appropriate to carry out the purposes of the trust. 

25. Deposit trust funds with itself — As corporate executor or trustee to deposit trust funds in its own banking department if such deposits are secured by legal or authorized securities equal in value to the amount of the deposit. 

26. Execute papers -- To execute and deliver agreements, assignments, bills of sale, contracts, deeds, notes, powers of attorney, receipts, or other instruments deemed necessary or desirable for carrying out the powers of the trustee or incidental to the purpose of the trust. 

27. Employ attorneys and other representatives — At the expense of the estate or trust to employ attorneys, accountants, experts, agents, and brokers reasonably necessary in the administration of the trust estate and to rely on information or advice furnished by them. 

28. Collect, receive, and give receipts — To collect, receive, and receipt for principal, rents, issues, profits, and income of the trust estate. 

29. Compromise, contest, arbitrate, or settle claims—To compromise, contest, arbitrate, or otherwise settle claims in favor of or against the trust estate or the trustee. - - 30. Do all the necessary or appropriate acts — To do all other things in relation to the trust necessary or desirable for carrying out the powers of the trustee or the purposes of the trust.

Investment powers: 

1. Retain original investments - To hold, without liability other than that involved in holding property legal for investment of trust funds, property, whether or not permissible for investment of funds of that particular trust, received from or though the settlor or property lawfully substituted therefor. 

2. Pay calls on investments To pay calls, assessments, and other charges on trust investments. 

3. Exercise rights - To sell or exercise stock subscription or conversion rights, options, and privileges connected with trust investments; to make payments, advancements, or loans in connection therewith; and to retain securities resulting from the exercise of such rights, options, and privileges. 

4. Participate in reorganizations — To participate in foreclosures, reorganizations, incorporations, reincorporations, readjustments, consolidations, mergers, dissolutions, liquidations, pooling agreements, and voting trusts; to assent to corporate sales, leases, and encumbrances; to deposit securities with protective committees; and to serve on such committees. 

5. Make investments legal for trust - To dispose of, call in, change, and make investments legal for the trust. 

6. Invest in non-legals under authority in general terms — Under an authority in general terms to invest in such securities or in such manner as the trustee may deem or think best or fit or to the advantage of the estate or in good safe securities or in other terms conferring a discretion not expressly limited to property for investment of trust funds, to construe such an authority as not limiting the trustee to property legal for investment of trust funds. 

7. Rely upon information and opinions-In making trust investments to rely upon information and opinions deemed reliable as to the legality of the investment for the particular trust. 

8. Retain investments after they have lost their status as legal or authorized investments - To retain, without liability only for retaining, investments legal or authorized when received or acquired which, because of changed conditions, have become non-legal or unauthorized. 

9. Accept genuineness and proper execution of instruments - To accept and act on the genuineness or validity of execution of instruments representing investments made by the trustee in good faith and without negligence. 

10. Purchase residence for family - Where reasonably necessary for

33333 the maintenance of the surviving wife or minor child, to invest trust funds in real property to be used for a home for such beneficiary. 

Distribution powers: 

1. Distribute in kind- To make divisions, allotments, and distributions of principal to legatees, beneficiaries, distributees, and remaindermen wholly or partly in property in kind. 

2. Make payments direct to minor — To make payments of income direct to minor beneficiaries over the age of 18 years as though they were of full age. 

3. Apply or expend income for minor without intervention of guardian -When income is directed to be paid to a minor, to apply or expend such income for the minor either with or without the intervention of a guardian. 

4. Advance income -To advance income to or for beneficiaries and take a lien on their future benefits. 

5. Permit family to occupy residence held in trust — To permit real property held in trust to be occupied by the surviving spouse or minor child of the testator or settlor. 

6. Pay funeral expenses of beneficiary — To pay the funeral expenses of the beneficiary receiving benefits from the trust estate at the time of such beneficiary's death. 

7. Continue to make payments or distributions until actual knowledge or written notice - When the happening of an event, such as marriage, divorce, attainment of a certain age, performance of educational requirements, or death, would affect the distribution of income or principal, to act without liability for mistake of fact prior to actual knowledge or written notice of such fact. 

Powers under Trust Instruments

An analysis of several hundred wills and trust agreements from different parts of the country has revealed that they contain the following discretionary powers in addition to the above-mentioned powers under statutes.

For a discussion of these powers, see the author's Discretionary Powers of 

Trustees under Wills and Trust Agreements, The Graduate School of Banking, 

American Bankers Association, New York, 1937.

Administration powers: 

1. Sales to or loans from trust estate -As executor to make sales to or obtain loans from the living trust or insurance trust created by the testator during his lifetime. 

2. Purchases from or loans to general estate As trustee under a living trust or insurance trust created by the testator, to make purchases from or loans to the general estate. 

3. Receive additional property in trust To receive or decline to receive additional property in the trust from the settlor or from third parties. 

4. Carry on business 

To carry on any business in which the testator was engaged at the time of his death and to incorporate and to dispose of such business. 

5. Sole custody of securities—As co-executor or co-trustee to have sole custody of the securities, giving the other co-executors or co-trustees reasonable access to such securities. 

6. Determine what is principal and what income To determine whether a given receipt or collection shall be treated as principal or as income. 

7. Allocate or apportion expenses — To allocate an item of expense to principal or to income or to apportion it between principal and income. 

8. Allocate or apportion realized appreciation To allocate realized appreciation on trust property to principal or to income or to apportion it between principal and income. 

9. Amortize premiums and accumulate discounts To amortize the premium on securities purchased at a premium or not to do so or to accumulate the discount on securities purchased at a discount or not to do so as the trustee deems best. 

To 10. Allocate or apportion extraordinary or non-cash dividends allocate or apportion extraordinary or non-cash dividends to principal or to income or to apportion them between principal and income. 

11. Carry several trusts as one estate To hold the principal of the trust estate undivided and to manage, invest, and account for the several shares as a single trust estate. 

To exercise the powers 

12. Exercise powers as executor or trustee granted whether acting as executor or as trustee of the same estate.

Investment powers: 

1. Invest in non-legals — In a state that by statute has a legal list of trust investments, to invest and reinvest trust funds in property not included in the legal list.

Distribution powers: 

1. Determine distributees· To determine who are entitled to distributive shares of the estate to the extent that, in the absence of bad faith or negligence, the trustee is protected in making such distributions. 

2. Fix values for distribution in kind — To fix values for distribution in kind, the executor's or trustee's decision, in the absence of bad faith or negligence, to be final. 

3. Pay successive beneficiary - To pay the beneficiary entitled to the next successive estate dividends declared but not paid and interest or other income accrued but not received at the time of the termination of the prior estate. 

4. Accumulate income- Where and to the extent not prohibited by law, to withhold and accumulate income and pay it over later or capitalize it. 

5. Use principal for income beneficiary - To pay, expend, or apply principal for the income beneficiary, with or without limitation upon the amount or percentage of principal so used, with or without specification or limitation of the purposes for which principal may be used. 

6. Withhold principal-To postpone or withhold distribution in whole or in part to the principal beneficiary until the beneficiary has, in the judgment of the trustee, satisfied certain general or specific conditions. 

7. Determine when and how to pay or apply income · To determine when (whether periodically or irregularly, as and when received, as and when called for, as and when needed in the judgment of the trustee) and how (whether paid direct to the beneficiary or to the guardian or to parent or person standing in the place of parent or guardian) income is to be applied to or expended for the benefit of the income beneficiary. 

8. Determine when and how to distribute principal - To determine when (whether named conditions have been satisfied) and how (whether at once or in instalments, whether paid to or applied for the beneficiary) principal is to be paid to or distributed for the principal beneficiary. 

These two lists of administration powers-one under statutes and the other under trust instruments-constitute a fairly exhaustive outline

of general administration powers. But the comparatively short lists of investment powers and of distribution powers give one very little idea of the variation in the statement of these powers as they are found in trust instruments. For example, the power to invest in nonlegals may be narrow or broad, specific or general, definite or indefinite, and yet designed to accomplish the same objective— namely, to give the trustee broader investment powers than it otherwise would possess. Similarly, the power to use principal for an income beneficiary may be narrow or broad, general or specific, definite or indefinite, and yet designed to accomplish the same objective namely, to give the trustee the power in its discretion to have the income beneficiary receive the benefit of some of or all the principal in addition to receiving all the income. So, while the combined lists of administration powers may be used by testators and settlors and draftsmen of trust instruments as a check-list, the lists of investment and of distribution powers may be used, at best, only to direct attention to these two groups of powers. 

ATTITUDES TOWARD  

What is the prevailing attitude of testators and settlors and of draftsmen of wills and trust agreements toward giving trust institutions as executor and trustee broad discretionary powers? What is the attitude of trust institutions toward having these powers? 

This discussion of attitudes must be confined mainly to investment powers and distribution powers, for as yet no study of attitudes toward general administration powers has been made. It may be true that in most cases a person's attitude toward general administration powers will be the same as his attitude toward investment and distribution powers. If he is liberal in granting one group of powers, he may be so in respect to other groups and, if he is conservative regarding one, he may be the same regarding others. But there are too many exceptions to state this as a rule. Many creators of trusts, for one good reason or another, are disposed to be liberal in the business administration of the trust and yet conservative in the personal administration. In one part of the trust instrument they will grant broad discretionary powers of administration and investment and in another part strictly limit the powers of distribution, or vice versa.

Attitudes of Testators and Settlors and Draftsmen 

In this Study the attitudes of testators and settlors and of draftsmen toward broad discretionary powers are treated together because in practice they cannot be separated. When anyone, analyzing a will or trust agreement, finds that it gives the trust institution as executor or trustee broad discretionary powers of administration, of investment, or of distribution, one cannot tell from the instrument alone whose original idea it was to give the trust institution these powers. It is not safe to generalize on the point. One may be tempted to say that the draftsman, since he may have had more experience with the settlement of estates and administration of trusts than his client has had, knows better than his client what the distribution powers should be. One may be tempted also to say that regular, usual, normal powers, whether of administration, investment, or distribution, are likely to be the work of the draftsman, while irregular, unusual, or peculiar powers are likely to have been incorporated at the suggestion or insistence of the testator or settlor. As a matter of fact, the testator or settlor, drawing upon his business and investment knowledge and experience, may have suggested the broad administration and investment powers and the draftsman, drawing upon his observation and experience in advising on and working out the terms of wills and trust agreements for other clients, may have suggested the broad distribution powers. The safe course, therefore, is to treat as one the client's and the draftsman's attitudes. toward broad discretionary powers.

The attitude of testators, settlors, and draftsmen toward granting trust institutions broad investment powers is shown by trust instruments. According to a study made in 1940 by a committee of the 

Trust Company Section, Pennsylvania Bankers Association, in New

York City 70% of a group of trust instruments analyzed gave the trustee full discretion as to investments. This committee also reported that, according to the records of several trust institutions in 

Pennsylvania, prior to 1930, 69% and, since 1930, 78% of the trust instruments give the trustee full discretion as to investments. An independent study of several hundred trust instruments in one trust institution in Delaware showed that over 80% of them give the trustee full discretion as to investments. The Committee on Trust

Policies of the Trust Division, American Bankers Association, re-

cently reported that several replies it received to an inquiry show that from 70% to 80% of the trust instruments already in force vide broad discretionary powers as to investments.* pro- As far as broad investment powers are concerned, in states that operate under the Massachusetts rule or prudent investor's rule there is less need for giving the trust institution broad investment powers, for it has them already. In states that have either mandatory or permissive legal lists there is a growing tendency on the part of testators and settlors and draftsmen alike to give trust institutions broad investment powers.

The attitude of testators and settlors toward giving trust institutions broad distribution powers has been brought to light recently in the course of a study made for quite another purpose. The Trust 

Research Department of The Graduate School of Banking has been conducting a research to ascertain, if possible, whether in most cases in the mind of the testator or settlor, the court, or the trust committee the present or the ultimate beneficiary is the preferred beneficiary. The following question was submitted to a group of active trust officers in 50 cities in 23 states: At the present time, for which of the two is the average testator or settlor more concerned about providing: the present beneficiaries of his estate or the ultimate beneficiaries? Eighty-six of the 88 trust officers replying stated that in their opinion the testator or settlor is more concerned about providing for his present beneficiaries than for his ultimate beneficiaries. 

The significance of this answer is not the almost unanimous preference for present beneficiaries but the ground on which these trust officers based their answer. Many of them said that they based their answer upon the fact that in so many cases the trustee had been granted the power to use principal for the present beneficiaries. The power to use principal for present beneficiaries may be regarded as the most sweeping of all the discretionary distribution powers of trust institutions. If trust institutions have this power to the wide extent indicated by these replies, they probably have other powers of 

4 Trust Bulletin, October 1941, p. 13 at p. 20. 

5 Massachusetts, Connecticut, Kentucky, Michigan, Missouri, North Carolina, Rhode Island, Utah, West Virginia, and possibly Maine, and New Hampshire as to half the investments in a given trust.

distribution, many of which would be incidental to the basic power to use principal.

All the available evidence, incomplete and fragmentary as it is, is to the effect that settlors, testators, and draftsmen are disposed to an increasing degree to give trust institutions broad investment powers and distribution powers. Possibly one is justified in assuming that their attitude would be the same toward broad administration powers. 

Attitude of Trust Institutions

Three recent researches on a nationwide scale of the attitude of trust institutions toward having broad discretionary powers have been made one on investment powers alone and two on both investment and distribution powers. None of these researches specifically covered general administration powers. 

In 1940 the Trust Research Department of The Graduate School of Banking asked 100 senior executive officers, representing older, larger, and presumably more experienced trust institutions in every section of the country, each to state the policies of his own institution, where it is consulted by the testator or settlor or the draftsman, as to encouraging or discouraging the inclusion in the instrument of broad discretionary powers as to (1) investments and (2) use of principal for the present beneficiaries. In the summer of 1941, the Committee on Trust Policies of the Trust Division of the American Bankers Association asked the head of the trust department or the trust investment officer of 108 of the larger trust institutions of the country each to state his opinion as to the advisability of a more widespread adoption of the Massachusetts rule as to trust investments. While the question as stated makes no mention of investment powers, it is understood that an answer in favor of a more widespread adoption of the Massachusetts rule is tantamount to an answer in favor of giving trustees broad discretionary investment powers. In the fall of 1941, following a discussion of the subject at the 1941 resident session of The Graduate School of Banking, the students majoring in Trusts were asked, as one of their extension problems, to state what in their judgment should be the general policy of a trust institution with respect to encouraging the inclusion of broad discretionary powers (1) as to the investment of

trust funds and (2) as to the use of principal by or for present beneficiaries.

The results of the 1940 research of the Trust Research Department and of the 1941 research of the Committee on Trust Policies were combined and presented in a report of the Committee on Trust Policies, which report was approved by the Executive Committee of the Trust Division of the American Bankers Association, September 29, 1941, and ordered published. The report contains six recommendations, two of which are as follows:

That trust institutions encourage the inclusion in wills and trust agreements of broad investment powers.

That trust institutions encourage the inclusion in wills and trust agreements of broad powers of administration, with particular reference to use of principal for present beneficiaries. . .

The report concluded with this statement:

In the light of the foregoing it seems advisable that each trust institution consider the establishment of a policy on . the wider use of provisions giving the trustee broader discretionary powers. From this standpoint the recommendations which provide greater flexibility through broader powers are all the more important and urgent.6 ...

The answers of The Graduate School of Banking students majoring in Trusts, to the extension problem, were almost unanimous that the trust institution should encourage the inclusion of broad investment powers. They were overwhelmingly but not nearly so unanimously in favor of including broad powers as to the use of principal.

The attitude of trust institutions toward having broad discretionary powers, both as expressed officially by the Trust Division of the American Bankers Association and individually by representatives of trust institutions, is one of encouraging the inclusion in wills and trust agreements of broad discretionary powers of administration, of investment, and of distribution.

Why Trust Institutions Should Have Broad Discretionary Powers 

From the discussion so far the conclusion seems to be unavoidable 

6 Report of Committee on Trust Policies on Effect of Present Economic Conditions on Trust Investment Policy, Trust Bulletin, October 1941, p. 13 at p. 24.

that the grant of broad discretionary powers to trust institutions is growing in favor with testators and settlors and with draftsmen and that the possession and exercise of such powers is growing in favor with trust institutions.

There are two basic reasons why trust institutions as executors and trustees are granted, and why they encourage the grant of, broad discretionary powers of administration, investment, and distribution. 

One reason is that, according to the present-day conception of trusteeship, the trustee of a personal (as distinguished from a corporate) trust is a manager of the trust property. The second reason is that in most cases the primary purpose of a personal trust is the use of the property (income and principal) for the benefit of the present beneficiaries. 

Managerial Aspect of Trusteeship

At the present time a trust institution as trustee of a personal trust is expected to be an active manager and not a mere safekeeper of the trust property. The textwriters of the law of trusts recognize the managerial function of modern trusteeship. 

Augustus Peabody Loring in the fourth edition of his book, A 

Trustee's Handbook, said: The original theory of the trust did not contemplate a trustee's doing anything but holding and taking care of the property. At the present day the usual object of a trust is to settle property in the hands of persons of good business ability to manage it for the benefit of others not possessed of such ability." 

Mayo Shattuck in his revision of Loring's Handbook sums up the matter and brings it up to date by saying that whatever a careful man would do to safeguard and manage his own property the trustee should do for the trust property. Professor Bogert says that a trustee is required to manifest in all his management of the trust the care, skill, prudence, and diligence of an ordinarily prudent man engaged in similar business affairs." 

Professor Scott calls attention to the fact that the modern trust is an outgrowth of the ancient use. The use was a passive trust. The 

7 P. 76; Little, Brown & Company, Boston, 1928. 

8 Shattuck Revision, sec. 17, p. 59. 

Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 541, p. 1705.

duty of the feoffee to use was not to interfere with the use of the land by the beneficiary. He had no power of management. Professor 

Scott goes on to say that it is a far cry from the ancient use to the modern trust when the trustee is under a duty in administering the trust to exercise such care and skill as a man of ordinary prudence would exercise in dealing with his own property, both to preserve the property and to make it productive.10 

Thus three of the leading American textwriters on the law of trusts, amply fortified by cases on the point, emphasize the managerial function of modern trusteeship.

Trust institutions also realize this aspect of trusteeship. In an address before the annual meeting of the Trust Division of the 

American Bankers Association in 1938, Lindsay Bradford, President of the City Bank Farmers Trust Company, New York City, said that the trust business is the handling of accumulated wealth in such a way that the principal is maintained and an appropriate income secured, and that the management of property entrusted to trust institutions is the important aspect of the personal trust business.¹ 11 If, under the modern conception of trusteeship, trust institutions as trustees of personal trusts have the duties and responsibilities of management, they must have corresponding powers of management. 

Where there is a duty, there must be a power; or, to state it the other way, where there is no power, there can be no duty. So, if there is the duty of management, there must be the power of management. 

The principle is stated thus in the Restatement of Trusts: 

The powers of a trustee are at least as extensive as his duties. If the trustee is under a duty to the beneficiary to do an act, he can properly do the act; and if the trustee is under a duty to the beneficiary not to do an act, he can properly refrain from doing the act.12

However, if the terms of the trust impose duties of management and if the law in one way or another gives adequate powers of management, what is the need for spelling out in wills and trust 10 Scott on Trusts, sec. 164, p. 826; sec. 174, p. 924; sec. 176, p. 932; and sec. 

181, p. 966. 

11 Bradford, Lindsay, "The Future of Trust Business," Trust Bulletin, December 

1938, p. 3 at p. 5; Trust Companies, November 1938, p. 603 at p. 604. 

12 Restatement of Trusts, sec. 186, p. 478.

agreements broad discretionary powers of management? Professor 

Scott gives a conclusive answer to this question in these two passages: one who reads the cases involving modern trusts cannot fail to notice the reluctance with which many courts are willing to hold that the trustee has powers which are not expressed in the trust instrument. Even today the courts have not fully accepted the principle that the trustee has such powers as are appropriate for the effective administration of the trust. The result of this attitude of the courts is to make it advisable, if not essential, to enumerate in some detail in the trust instrument the powers which the settlor desires that the trustee should have to enable him properly to administer the trust. The settlor cannot safely rely upon a hope that without such express provisions the court will find that powers necessary or appropriate for the efficient administration of the trust are to be implied. For this reason it is common to insert in the trust instrument express provisions conferring powers upon the trustee, even though some of the powers might be held to have been created without such provisions.18

Because of the reluctance of many courts to find that the trustee has powers which are not clearly expressed in the trust instrument, and because of the resulting doubts which arise as to the existence of certain powers, it is customary in well-drawn trust instruments to make provisions in express words conferring upon the trustee powers which are or may become necessary or appropriate for the efficient administration of the trust. The administration of the trust may be seriously impeded not only by the lack of such powers but also by doubts, even though the doubts are not well-founded, as to the existence of the powers.14 

Personal Aspect of Trusteeship

Personal trusts are created for the benefit of persons who aptly are known as beneficiaries. If such trusts are to be of real benefit to the beneficiaries, the administration of the trust must be adapted from time to time to the changing needs of the beneficiaries. Human needs are everchanging and never predictable. Thus human nature itself creates the need for broad discretionary powers of distribution to match the broad discretionary powers of administration. 

As has been brought out already,15 most testators and settlors in creating trusts have in mind primarily the present beneficiaries who usually are members of the immediate family (wife, children, parents, brothers or sisters) or other kinsmen or associates for whom the testator or settlor feels a special obligation or affection. If the changing and unpredictable needs of these beneficiaries are to be served continuously throughout the whole period of the trust, which may 

13 Scott on Trusts, sec. 164, p. 826-827. 

14 Ibid., sec. 186, p. 985. 

15 Supra, p. 78.

extend over many years, it stands to reason that the trustee should have broad powers with regard to handling principal as well as income.

In the administration of trusts, trust institutions find it advantageous for the trustee to have, in addition to broad powers of distribution, some expression or suggestion from the testator or settlor himself as to which of the beneficiaries present or ultimate — is to be preferred in case there may not be enough for the needs of both. 

It is indicative of the spirit of the time that in an increasing number of wills and trust agreements one finds expressions of preference such as the following:

Alabama. The primary purpose of the creation of this trust is to provide for the comfort, maintenance, support, and protection of my wife and of my children. Any interest of any other person or persons therein shall be subject and subservient to the primary purpose herein expressed. 

Colorado. It is not the settlor's intention necessarily to conserve the trust estate for the use and benefit of the beneficiaries, or any of them, who ultimately may be entitled thereto by virtue of the foregoing provisions of this instrument. On the contrary, his primary purpose is to provide for and protect his immediate lineal descendants.

New York. This trust is created for the primary benefit of the first party and, in making any decision hereunder, the trustee shall consider this purpose even though such decision may be to the possible detriment of the remaindermen. 

Present-Day Conditions 

It remains now only to consider wherein present-day conditions meaning the war and all that goes with it economically and socially - should make the grant of broad discretionary powers to trust institutions more advisable or less advisable than was the case under previous conditions or even now but for the war. 

The war has not created the need for broad powers of administration, of investment, or of distribution. It has not lessened the duties of management; it has only made the discharge of these duties more difficult. It has not reduced the problems of trust investment; it has only intensified them. It has not changed human needs; it has only made them less calculable or predictable. If there was need for broad powers in peacetime, there is all the more need for them in wartime. 

The war should serve, not to erase but to underscore, the provisions of wills and trust agreements granting these broad powers.

As business and economic conditions change, trust administration changes also. The Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court has said that the standard of the trustee is susceptible of being adapted to whatever conditions may arise in the evolution of society and the progress of civilization.16 Is it any wonder that Professor Scott speaks of the trust as "one of the most flexible of the instruments in the armory of our jurisprudence," or that he closes his treatise on 

Trusts with these words:

The evolution of the trust has been a great adventure in the field of jurisprudence. 

It has not ended. As long as the owner of property can dispose of it in accordance with his legitimate wishes, the great adventure will go on. The law of trusts is living law.18

CONCLUSIONS 

Broad discretionary powers of general administration, of investment, and of distribution, which trust institutions need for the settlement of estates and the administration of trusts, are being incorporated in an increasing number of statutes and of trust instruments. 

There is no conflict between these two sources of powers: statutes and trust instruments. In states, such as Louisiana, Florida, and 

Oklahoma, which have trust administration acts granting broad powers to trustees, the draftsman may deprive the trustee of powers so granted by incorporating in the instrument a provision expressly limiting the trustee's power. On the other hand, the draftsman may clothe the trustee with other powers. In states that do not have such acts, the draftsman, if he desires that the trust institution have broad powers, must spell them out in the instrument. So, it becomes largely a matter of draftsmanship. Is it better to grant the powers by statute and let the draftsman add to or take from them as he sees fit, or is it better to have each draftsman spell out in his own way and in his own words the powers he and his client think the trust institution should have? This still is an unsettled and somewhat local issue, which does not come within the scope of this Study. 

16 Kimball v. Whitney, 233 Mass. 321; 123 N.E. 665 (1919). 

17 Scott, Austin Wakeman, Fifty Years of Trusts, Harvard Law Review, November 1936, p. 60 at p. 76. 

18 Scott on Trusts, p. 2604.

Another question should be raised, lest it be overlooked, although it does not come directly within the scope of this Study. To what extent should a trust institution, as executor or trustee, have to look to the court, instead of looking to a statute or to the trust instrument, for additional or emergency powers? Court action involves expense and delay. The court may decide that it lacks authority or jurisdiction to grant the power sought. Consequently, for an experienced executor or trustee at any rate, it would seem better to grant by statute or by trust instrument all the powers likely ever to be needed than to rely solely on the power-granting authority or jurisdiction of the court.

Whatever the source of the powers-whether they are granted by statute or by the trust instrument — settlors and testators, draftsmen, and trust institutions seem more and more to be in favor of trust institutions' having broad discretionary powers of administration, investment, and distribution.

The reasons why trust institutions are in favor of broad powers are (1) that, under the modern conception of personal trusteeship, the trustee is expected to be an active manager and not merely a titleholder or safekeeper of the property and (2) that in most cases it is the desire of the creator of the trust that it be administered primarily for the benefit of the present beneficiaries. If the trustee is to manage the property wisely, it must have ample powers of management. If it is to administer the trust primarily for the present beneficiaries, it must have ample powers of distribution.

Although the trust institution is, for the reasons stated, in favor of having broad powers, it, nevertheless, welcomes the setting up of certain guideposts by statute or trust instrument, especially those in trust instruments in the form of expressions as to the main objective of the trust or as to any preference to be shown certain beneficiaries over other beneficiaries. The trust institution feels that these guideposts enable it to exercise its broad powers more intelligently and effectively for the accomplishment of the real purpose of the trust as it originally took shape in the mind and heart of the testator or settlor.

War, war conditions, or present-day preparations for post-war conditions are not creating the need for broad discretionary powers. 

They are only accentuating needs that exist at all times. The distinc-

tive merit of the trust is that it is adaptable to all sorts of conditions -to those of peace time, of wartime, and of reconstruction alike. It is in order to be able to make these adaptations that trust institutions need and welcome broad discretionary powers.
