HAROLD STONIER 

.

Study Number 5 HOW TRUST INSTITUTIONS EXERCISE DISCRETIONARY POWERS 

IN 

the preceding Study (Should Trust Institutions Have Broad 

Discretionary Powers?) the conclusions reached were that most testators and settlors and draftsmen of wills and trust agreements favor the grant of broad discretionary powers to trust institutions as executor and trustee and that most trust institutions welcome the grant of such powers.

The present Study is devoted to a discussion of the question, How do trust institutions exercise discretionary powers? From the point of view of the settlor or testator this question is one of vast importance, for, if he grants his executor or trustee broad powers, he wants every possible assurance that they will be exercised properly. From the point of view of the trust institution it is equally important, for, the greater the powers possessed, the greater the responsibility assumed in exercising them. 

In the preceding Study the discretionary powers of trust institutions were classified under three heads: administration powers, investment powers, and distribution powers. Each of these three groups was subdivided, making a total of 68 different discretionary powers that a trust institution may be called upon to exercise in the settlement of an estate or in the administration of a trust. Each of these 68 powers easily might have been subdivided still further. It is not practicable to discuss separately how trust institutions exercise each of these 68 or more powers.

In this Study, as in the previous one, it has seemed advisable to select for special treatment two of the powers most frequently granted the power to invest trust funds in non-legals and the power to use principal for income beneficiaries — and then to group all the other discretionary powers for general treatment only. 89

GUIDEPOSTS IN EXERCISING DISCRETIONARY POWERS 

In the exercise of discretionary powers trust institutions have available three guideposts. One is the law of trusts and trustees; another is government regulation of trust business; and the third is 

A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions. 

The Law of Trusts and Trustees 

The law lays down certain general principles that serve as guideposts for the exercise of all discretionary powers. These principles are applicable to all executors and trustees individual and corporate alike. One principle is that, unless it is provided otherwise by the terms of the trust, an executor or trustee in exercising a discretionary power must stay within the bounds of a reasonable judgment.¹ Another is that the court will not interfere with the exercise of a discretionary power unless the executor or trustee in exercising or failing to exercise the power (1) acts dishonestly, or (2) acts with an improper even though not a dishonest motive, or (3) fails to use his judgment, or (4) acts beyond the bounds of a reasonable judgment.2

Applied to the investment of trust funds, guiding principles of law are that the trustee is under a duty to the beneficiary (1) in administering the trust to exercise such care and skill as a man of ordinary prudence would exercise in dealing with his own property and, if he has greater skill than that of a man of ordinary prudence, to exercise such skill as he has; and (2) in making trust investments, in the absence of provisions in the terms of the trust or of a statute otherwise providing, to make such investments and only such investments as a prudent man would make of his own property, having primarily in view the preservation of the estate and the amount and regularity of the income to be derived.* 

When the executor or trustee is a trust institution, it, being a corporation, can act only through natural persons; hence discretionary powers granted to a trust institution can be exercised only 

1 Scott on Trusts, sec. 187, p. 987. 

2 Restatement of Trusts, sec. 187(e), p. 481. 

3 Ibid., sec. 174, p. 448. 

4 Ibid., sec. 227, p. 645.

by its employees, officers, directors, or stockholders. Professor Bogert says:

A great percentage of modern trusts are vested in corporate trustees, and the administration of a trust by a bank or trust company necessarily implies that all the powers of the trustee must be committed to the care of a trust officer or other agent or servant of the corporate trustee. The artificial legal entity which is the corporation cannot act personally. It can proceed only through its officers and employees or other intermediaries. There can be no question of the power to delegate, but rather only whether, in choosing the employees to whom delegation has been made, reasonable care has been used.5

Professor Bogert does not say whether in a given case the trustee should proceed through officers, or through employees, or through other intermediaries.

Professor Scott goes no further than to say that there are acts in the administration of a trust which it is improper for a corporate trustee to commit to its employees, where they are acts which involve such discretion that it should be exercised by the board of directors or a responsible committee or officer. But he does not specify what acts are proper ones, respectively, for employees, for officers, for committees, and for the board of directors. 

Neither did Augustus Peabody Loring in any of the first four editions of his A Trustee's Handbook nor did Mayo Shattuck in his Revision (which is the fifth edition of the Handbook) discuss what persons in the trust institution should exercise discretionary powers. 

Only one case has been found in which the court even approached, and then only incidentally, a discussion of the exercise of discretionary powers by trust institutions. In Chicago Title and 

Trust Company v. Zinser et al.,' the court took judicial notice of the fact that, when a person names a corporation his executor or trustee, he cannot expect the judgment or discretion of any particular individual in the institution to be exercised; that in making the appointment of a corporation, he must realize that as time goes on individual officers and employees will change.

However, the courts time and again have laid down the general principles governing trustees in exercising discretionary powers. 

The Supreme Judicial Court of Massachusetts has spoken as follows on the discretionary power to use principal: 

5 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 555, p. 1764. 

6 Scott on Trusts, sec. 171.4, p. 918. 

7 264 Ill. 31; 105 N. E. 718; Am. Cas. 1915D 931 (1914).

1 The power conferred upon the trustee was the exercise of reasonably sound judgment. No arbitrary or capricious power was conferred even though honestly exercised. A trustee vested with discretionary power to distribute a fund in whole or in part is bound to use reasonable prudence. The possession of full power or wide discretion by a trustee means the kind of power and discretion which inheres in a fiduciary relation, and not that illimitable potentiality which an unrestrained individual possesses respecting his own property. There is an implication, when even broad powers are conferred, that they are to be exercised with that soundness of judgment which follows from a due appreciation of trust responsibility. Prudence and reasonableness, not caprice or careless good nature, much less a desire on the part of the trustee to be relieved from trouble or from the possibility of making a foolish investment, furnish the standard of conduct! 8

In setting guideposts in exercising discretionary powers the law merely says (1) that a trust institution must live up to the standard of the prudent person under the circumstances; (2) that it must act within the bounds of reasonable judgment; and (3) that in decid-. ing what persons--whether employees, officers, or directors-shall exercise discretionary powers, and whether they shall act individually or jointly, and in what manner they shall exercise such powers, it shall live up to the standard of the prudent trustee under the circumstances.

Government Regulation 

Government regulation furnishes the second guidepost in exercising discretionary powers. The best known of these regulations is 

Regulation F-Trust Powers of National Banks—promulgated by the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System. Some state banking departments-that of the state of North Carolina, for example-issue similar regulations.

Regulation F provides that all investments of trust funds shall be made, retained, or disposed of only with the approval of the trust investment committee composed of at least three members who shall be capable and experienced officers or directors of the bank. 

The regulation also provides that, when the instrument creating the trust contains provisions expressly authorizing the bank, its officers, or its directors to exercise a discretion in the matter of investments, funds received or held in trust shall be invested only with the approval of the trust investment committee.10 

8 Corkery v. Dorsey, 223 Mass. 97, 111 N. E. 795 (1916). • Sec. 6(c). 

10 Sec. 10(a).

The North Carolina regulation provides that all investments of funds received or held by a bank as fiduciary shall be made, retained, or disposed of only with the approval of the trust committee, which shall be composed of at least three capable and experienced directors of the bank, at least one of whom shall be a director who is not an officer of the bank." 11 

Regarding the exercise of other discretionary powers, particularly that of using principal for income beneficiaries, the regulations are not so specific. Regulation F provides that the trust investment committee may have such additional duties relating to the trust department as may be prescribed by the board of directors¹2 and that the duties of the trust officers shall be evidenced by the by-laws of the bank or by a resolution of the board of directors.13 Further, the board of directors is responsible for the determination of the policies of the trust department." The North Carolina regulation provides that the duties of the trust officers shall be prescribed by the board of directors in a resolution duly adopted and recorded.15 

These regulations imply that the board of directors has the responsibility for designating the officer or the committee that is to exercise discretionary powers.

A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions contains two statements—one general and the other specific-relating to the exercise of discretionary powers.

The general statement is that it is the duty of a trustee, in administering the trust, to exercise the care a prudent man familiar with such matters would exercise as trustee of the property of others.16 This applies to the exercise of discretionary powers the standard of the prudent trustee.

The specific statement is that the responsibility for the investment of trust funds should not be reposed in an individual officer 

11 Regulations, 2(C) and 4(G). 

12 Sec. 6(c). 

13 Sec. 6(d). 

14 Sec. 6(h). 

15 Regulations, 2(B). 

16 Art. HI, sec. 3.

or employee but that all investments should be made, retained, or sold only upon the authority of an investment committee composed of capable and experienced officers or directors of the institution.¹7 

POLICIES AND PRACTICES IN EXERCISING DISCRETIONARY POWERS 

The policies and practices of trust institutions in exercising discretionary powers are discussed under these three heads: (1) those relating to the investment of trust funds, (2) those relating to the use of principal for income beneficiaries, and (3) those relating to other powers. 

Investment of Trust Funds

The policies and practices of trust institutions regarding the exercise of the discretionary power to invest trust funds in non-legals (in states that have a legal list) do not call for extended or detailed discussion, for these policies and practices are, more or less, standardized. Already there is an abundance of literature on this subject. 

In 1930 the Trust Division of the American Bankers Association published a Handbook for the Review and Survey of Trust Investments, which still is substantially up to date. The American Institute of Banking devotes two chapters of its textbook on Trust Business to the investment of trust funds—one chapter to principles and policies of trust investment and the other to procedure for the investment of trust funds. The Graduate School of Banking now offers once every three years an advanced course in trust investment. 

Ultimate responsibility for general supervision of the investment of trust funds rests upon the board of directors. Regulation F declares that the board of directors is responsible for the investment of trust funds and for the disposition of trust investments.18 This does not mean that the board of directors must approve formally every purchase, sale, exchange, or conversion of a trust investment.

It means that the board must inform itself as to the manner in which the trust investment committee is performing its duties and the investment policies which are being pursued by the committee,¹9 

17 Art. IV, sec. 2. 

18 Sec. 6(b). 

19 Ruling, Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, Trust Bulletin, July 

1938, pp. 27-28.

The following is a brief summary of approved steps taken by trust institutions in exercising discretionary investment powers: 

1. A study of the trust instrument and of the known or ascertainable facts about the beneficiaries and the account from the practical point of view to determine the terms of the trust and the needs of the beneficiaries. 

2. A study of the trust instrument from the legal point of view to determine, among other things, the extent of the investment powers of the trustee. 

3. A factual study of investments already in the account and of investments being considered for the account by one or more statistical and investment specialists, to find and present the facts about each investment-this without reference to its fitness for any particular account. 

4. An application of the facts found by the statistical and investment specialists to the investment requirements of the particular account by a group or committee of officers (sometimes called the trust investment group or the officers' committee, and usually composed of trust officers, the trust investment officer, and the head of the securities analysis division) to make recommendations as to investments. 

5. A decision by the trust investment committee, composed of three or more capable and experienced officers or directors, based upon the facts found by the specialists (step 3) and the recommendations made by the group or committee of officers (step 4). 

6. A general approval or ratification by the board of directors of the policies and decisions of the trust investment committee. 

7. Execution by appropriate members of the staff of the orders of the trust investment committee.

How far trust institutions at the present time go in placing the responsibility for exercising discretionary investment powers upon the trust investment committee, subject to general approval or ratification by the board of directors, is shown by a study recently made by Walter Kennedy of Montgomery, Alabama. Preparatory for an address at the 1942 Mid-Winter Trust Conference on suggested procedure for the review and analysis of trust investments for a small trust department, Mr. Kennedy submitted a questionnaire to 50 trust institutions. He received 38 replies from 22 states. 

Of these 38 trust institutions, 9 were classified as small, 18 as medium sized, and 11 as large. Mr. Kennedy found that each of these trust institutions, without exception, has a trust investment committee of officers or directors (although the committee is known by different names); that the number of members of the committee ranges from 3 to 12, from 5 to 7 being the usual number; that over four-fifths of the committees are composed of both officers and directors who are not officers, the remaining one-fifth being com-

posed of officers some of whom are not directors; that over twothirds of the committees meet weekly or semi-weekly and the remaining one-third, monthly or semi-monthly; that the length of the committee meetings ranges from one to three hours, averaging one and one-half hours; and that one-half of these committees review each trust account annually, nearly one-third semi-annually, and the remainder quarterly.20

To discuss whether it is the recommending group or committee of officers or the deciding committee of officers or directors or the approving or ratifying board of directors that exercises the power would be as futile as to discuss which of the spokes of a turning wheel bears the weight of the load. Suffice it to say that in the exercise of its discretionary investment powers the trust institution looks to its statisticians and investment specialists for facts, to its trust officers for recommendations, to its committee of officers or directors for decisions, and to its board of directors for general approval or ratification of investment policies and decisions. 

Use of Principal

In 1940 these two questions were submitted to the head of the trust department of each of 27 of the larger trust institutions: (1) Where your institution as trustee is authorized, in its discretion, to use principal for income beneficiaries for support or education or illness or emergency or the like, who exercises the discretion and makes the decision-the head of the trust department, the officer in charge of the account, a committee of officers, a committee of the board, or the board itself? (2) What minutes or memorandums are kept of the facts and considerations inducing and justifying the decision?

Later the same questions were submitted to the students majoring in Trusts in The Graduate School of Banking. 

Through these two sources replies were received from 58 trust institutions in 42 cities in 25 states and the District of Columbia, representing practically every size and type of trust institution in every section of the country. The discussion that follows is based upon these 58 replies.

Wherever in this Study it is stated that in exercising the power to use principal a trust institution does or does not take a certain 

20 Kennedy, Walter, Review and Analysis of Trust Investments, Trust Bulletin, April 1942, p. 16; Trusts and Estates, February 1942, p. 195.

step, the statement is based upon what appears to be the prevailing policy or practice in these 58 trust institutions. Wherever the opinion is expressed that a trust institution should or should not take a given step, it too is based upon what appears to be the weight of opinion in these institutions. Neither the policies nor the practices of trust institutions with respect to the use of principal for income beneficiaries are standardized to the point where one is justified in making many general statements.

The exercise by a trust institution of the discretionary power to use principal for income beneficiaries usually involves six steps. 

These steps are: (1) the application, (2) the investigation, (3) the presentation, (4) the decision, (5) the record, and (6) the follow-up.

The Application 

Where the trust institution has the power to use principal for an income beneficiary, the person who makes the application for the exercise of the power and the way he makes it depend upon (1) the characteristics of the beneficiary, (2) the terms of the trust, and (3) the terms of the grant of the power. In some cases the trust institution on its own initiative would consider the exercise of the power; in other cases it would look to the beneficiary to make the application and would not make any move toward exercising the power unless or until the beneficiary made the application; and in still other cases it would wait for some third person to make the application.

Trust institution acting on its own initiative. If the beneficiary is an infant or an incompetent, aged, or infirm person who has no one to speak for him, the trust institution proceeds to make the necessary investigation without waiting for an application. It acts upon its own independent knowledge of the needs of the beneficiary. 

Application by beneficiary. If the beneficiary is a competent person, the trust institution usually waits for the beneficiary to make the application. This would be so in case of a normal woman who needs or thinks she needs principal, in addition to the income, for the support of herself and her children. It would be true also in case of a high school student or college student who thinks the income insufficient for his school expenses. 

Application by third person. If the beneficiary is unable or unwill-

ing to make his own application and if there is some person-such as parent, attendant, teacher, or friend-who is in a position to know the needs of the beneficiary, the trust institution accepts for consideration the application of such a person on behalf of the beneficiary.

Form of application. The trust institution does not and cannot have any fixed or unalterable policy or practice with respect to the form of application. No one form would be appropriate for every case. In some cases it would accept for consideration an informal application made in the course of a conversation, by telephone, by telegram, or by letter. In other cases, according to the circumstances, it would require a formal written application, signed, witnessed, and possibly sworn to before a notary public. In fact, the application or the form of application is not the all-important step. The application itself is not the determining factor. At most, it only sets the procedure in motion for the exercise of the power. 

The Investigation 

Although the trust officer handling the account might think that the trust institution should exercise its power to use principal, the trust institution would not do so until the case had been investigated—that is, unless all essential facts were known already. Although the trust institution had received an application-formal or informal, from the beneficiary or from a third person to use principal, it would not act without investigation—that is, again, unless it already was in possession of all the essential facts. 

The method and extent of investigation would depend largely upon the characteristics of the beneficiary and the terms of the grant of power. 

Characteristics of the beneficiary. If the beneficiary or the third person making the application (such as a parent or other member of the immediate family or a teacher) is known to be a dependable, trustworthy, provident person, sincerely interested in the welfare of the beneficiary, the trust institution is influenced greatly by such person's application and supporting statements. If, on the other hand, the applicant is known to be irresponsible, extravagant, impetuous, or perhaps motivated by some selfish purpose, the trust institution makes its own, independent investigation.

In the case of a trust under will or of a living trust after the settlor's death, the investigation should begin with conversations with persons in the trust institution itself who had known or had had dealings with the testator or settlor. The testator or settlor may have discussed with some person in the trust institution certain characteristics of beneficiaries which he preferred not to put in writing. For instance, a husband, leaving his estate in trust for his wife, may have spoken to some officer of her extravagant tendencies. Thus the best information for the guidance of the trustee in exercising its powers frequently may be found inside the institution. 

Terms of grant of power. If the trust institution is authorized to use principal for the support of the income beneficiary, or, as sometimes is the case, for the support of a group of income beneficiaries, such as the members of a family, the trust institution investigates (1) the current cost of living in the community, (2) the standard of living of the beneficiary or the family, (3) their saving or spending habits, (4) their other sources of income, (5) the probable duration of the trust and future needs of the beneficiaries, and (6) past experiences with the beneficiary with respect to the trust (for example, the reasonableness or unreasonableness of previous requests). 

It might require the beneficiary or the head of the family to present a budget. It might help him to prepare such a budget. Always it must face these two questions: (1) In fairness to the principal beneficiaries, how much of the principal can be used for the income beneficiaries? (2) How much principal can be used without risking the exhaustion of the trust before its purpose has been served? 

If the trust institution is authorized to use principal for education, it investigates (1) the prior school records (grades, conduct, and general standing) of the beneficiary, (2) the business or profession he is preparing for, (3) the expensiveness of the school, and (4) the general attitude of the beneficiary toward education. It might help to select the school — perhaps selecting one less expensive or better adapted to the needs of the beneficiary. Through correspondence or personal visits, it would establish and maintain contact with the school. If the beneficiary already was in school and asked for more money than the income amounted to, the trust institution might require him to submit an expense account and a budget of school expenses for the remainder of the school year.

In short, the trust institution would make the same sort of investigation as a prudent and provident parent would make and in much the same way, looking into everything that might throw light upon the beneficiary's need for additional money for school expenses. 

If the trust institution is authorized to use principal for illness or accident, it investigates (1) the nature of the illness or accident, (2) the standing of the doctor or hospital (if not known already), (3) the urgency of the need, and (4) the probable future needs of the beneficiary. It might request a statement from the physician or surgeon in charge as to the nature of the illness or accident and the need for hospitalization or for an operation. It might call for a budget of the medical, surgical, hospital, or institutional expenses. 

On the one hand, it must not let imaginary ailments be employed as an excuse for using principal needlessly, but, on the other hand, it cannot afford to let needed medical, surgical, or institutional care go unprovided if the funds are available and the power to use principal exists.

If, as so often is the case, the trust institution is authorized to use principal for the income beneficiary or beneficiaries without any specification as to the purposes for which it is to be used, the trust institution investigates (1) the characteristics of each beneficiary, (2) his needs, considering his standard of living, (3) the real purpose for which the trust was created, (4) the extent to which principal may be used for the income beneficiary without being unfair, in the light of all the circumstances, to the principal beneficiaries, and (5) the extent to which principal can be used without depleting the trust property before the purpose has been accomplished. 

A general power, without specification, to use principal for an income beneficiary or a power that goes no further than to say that the trustee may use principal for the beneficiary's "happiness" or "welfare" or for the "maintenance of the standard of living to which he is accustomed" imposes upon the trust institution the duty to make whatever investigation a prudent man would make under the circumstances. Indefinite and, therefore, unsatisfactory as this standard is, it is as definite as the law can be expected to make it. 

Wherever possible the testator or settlor should set some guideposts for the trustee by specifying, at least in a general way, for what purposes (such as for support, education, illness, accident, diminution

IOI of income, or decreased purchasing power of income due to economic conditions) principal is to be used. He should not stop with saying simply that principal may be used for the income beneficiary or even stop with saying that it may be used for the happiness or general welfare of the beneficiary. 

The Presentation 

If the trust officer who received the application and who made the investigation or who already possesses all the needed information, is, himself, to make the decision, no presentation is necessary. 

He acts upon his own knowledge or investigation. 

If, as usually is the case, the decision is made by some one other than the one who received the application or made the investigation, an appropriate presentation is made. 

As far as is practicable, the presentation should be made in writing, with all the supporting evidence in writing. If in an emergency the trust officer acts without waiting for an application, he should make a memorandum setting forth the facts and the reasons for his decision. If the application is made in course of a conversation or by telephone, the substance of the conversation should be reduced to writing. If practicable, it should be signed by the appli cant; if not, by the person in the trust institution who receives the application. If it is made by letter or telegram, the letter or telegram should be included in the presentation. If an invesigation is made, a full memorandum of the findings should be included. The presentation should be accompanied and supported by every writingmemorandum, letter, note, affidavit, bill, report, and the likethat throws any light upon the matter. The inclusion of all such writings is important for the trust institution's own protection. 

Should a principal beneficiary ever claim that the trustee abused its discretion, the trust institution should be able to show by documentary evidence that it acted on knowledge and investigation and not upon the importunity of the beneficiary or its own generous impulses.

If the decision is made by some individual, group, or committee other than the person who received the application or who made the investigation, the trust officer who received the application and made the investigation or had it made should himself, wherever

possible, present in person the application and all the supporting writings. His presence while the application is being considered enables him to supplement the writings and give the group or committee the benefit of his personal knowledge of the account and of the beneficiary.

The Decision 

What person or persons in the trust institution should make the decision to use principal-the trust administrator, a trust officer, the head of the trust department, two or more trust officers, a committee of trust officers, a committee of officers and directors, a committee of directors, or the board of directors? The answer to this question would depend upon the terms of the trust, the terms of the application, and the plan of organization of the trust institution. 

Small amounts. Some trust institutions, through trust committee or board action, specify the amount of principal a trust administrator or trust officer may use without reference to a committee. For instance, the rule may be that, if the application is for $100 or less, the decision may be made by the trust administrator or trust officer; if for not over $1,000, by the head of the trust department or by two senior trust officers; if for more than $1,000, by a trust committee.

Continuing orders. In the use of principal for support or for education, the committee may specify the amount that may be used during a calendar year or school year and let the trust administrator or trust officer use it in such instalments as he thinks best. 

Emergency. If principal is used to meet an emergency due to illness or accident, the trust administrator or trust officer may make the decision, order the expenditure, and obtain the ratification of the committee or of the board afterward.

The prevailing practice among trust institutions is to have the decision to use principal made or ratified by a committee of officers or directors. It is the general practice, except in a clear emergency, not to leave the decision, other than for a small amount, to any individual, not even to the head of the trust department. 

The Record 

The record up to the time of presentation has been discussed al-

ready. From this point on it is a matter of preserving this record and adding to it the minutes of the committee or board and memorandums of any additional information, if any, brought out during the discussion leading up to the decision. The files of the trust account should contain (1) the written application or a memorandum of an oral application, (2) the memorandums or writings connected with the investigation, (3) the memorandum of the trust officer making the recommendation, and (4) the minutes on the decision.

However, the record should not be so long or tedious or detailed as to lose the essentials in a mass of non-essentials. The test is this: 

Should a principal beneficiary ever question the use of principal for the income beneficiary, will the trust institution have in its file all the information that may be needed to explain and justify its decision?

Follow-Up In its follow-up of the use of principal by or for an income beneficiary, a trust institution must take a middle course, dictated by prudence and common sense. On the one hand, it must not close its eyes to the misuse of principal; on the other, it need not, in every case, supervise or check up on the way every cent is spent. 

If, for instance, it uses principal for support of a family, it need not audit every grocery bill, but it must not close its ears to reports that money intended for living expenses actually is being used for speculation or extravagance. If it uses principal for education, it need not require the beneficiary to account for the way he uses every penny of spending money; but it must not disregard the fact that he may be wasting time at school. It may require that tuition bills, board bills, bills for books and supplies be submitted to it for audit. If it uses principal for illness or accident, it may not require an accounting for every penny spent, but it must not overlook the fact that abuses may be perpetrated in the name of illness or ill health or even that people may over-influence and take advantage of beneficiaries who are weak-minded, neurotic, or who have imaginary ailments. It must not let the power to use principal for general welfare or happiness be used as a cloak for permitting the income beneficiary to get hasty, not to say unclean, hands upon

what rightfully belongs to the principal beneficiary. The trust instituition should follow up the use of principal in the way and to the extent and for the same purpose that a prudent man under similar circumstances would follow it up. 

Consultation with Principal Beneficiary 

Consultation with a principal beneficiary as to use of principal for an income beneficiary is not listed or discussed as one of the steps because it cannot be regarded as a step generally adopted or approved by trust institutions. In some cases it is a highly desirable step; in other cases, it is one of questionable propriety; and in still others it would be downright improper. Suppose the trustee is to pay the income for life to the mother and after her death to deliver the principal, discharged of the trust, to the children; that it has power to use principal for the mother's support; that the children are of legal age; and that there is complete harmony in the family. In such a case, the trust institution would do well to consult with the children, as well as the mother, as to the use of principal and, together with them, work out the plan of distribution under the terms of the trust best calculated to serve her needs both at the present time and during her later years. Suppose, on the other hand, there is not complete harmony in the family, that one reason the husband-and-father left the estate in trust was to protect his wife against the effects of such disharmony. In this case, to consult the principal beneficiaries might be to add fuel to the flame of family discord. Some testators and settlors who give their trustee power to use principal do so, partly at least, in order to relieve the members of the family of having to discuss such matters with one another.

Also, in consulting the principal beneficiary there is the possible danger of setting a precedent. To consult the principal beneficiary one time and not do so another time may be difficult to explain and may upset the smooth running of the trust administration. 

Generally speaking, it may be said that, when a testator or settlor gives a trust institution power to use principal for an incomebeneficiary, he means to have the trustee, and not the principal beneficiary or even the court, exercise the power. A trust institution must exercise rare tact and discrimination in consulting the prin-

cipal beneficiary, else the consultation may do more harm than good.

Typical Procedures 

The foregoing discussion of the exercise of the discretionary power to use principal has been general and somewhat indefinite. So many variables must be taken into account. Among these variables are the terms of the trust, the characteristics of the beneficiaries, the extent of the power, and the plan of organization of the trust institution. In addition to discussing policies and practices in general terms, it has been thought well to select several typical trust institutions in different parts of the country and describe their procedure for using principal for income beneficiaries. 

New York City. The procedure starts when an application in writing, without regard to the form of the writing, is made to the trust officer in charge of the account. If the request is made orally it must be reduced to writing. The trust officer checks the trust instrument to see if it authorizes such use of principal. In the case of the first exercise of discretion with respect to a trust, the trust officer obtains an opinion of counsel for the trust as to the authority of the trustee to pay over principal for the purpose requested. 

The trust officer then makes or supervises the making of the investigation. If principal is to be used for living expenses, he usually calls for a budget; if for medical or surgical services, he usually calls for a letter from the physician in charge.

A special form is then prepared in triplicate which gives certain essential facts with respect to the trust as well as a resumé of the investigation of the trust officer. This form then is submitted, together with the written application and the opinion of counsel for the trust, if any, to the general counsel for the trust institution, who checks as to the legality of the payment. The form next is submitted to two vice presidents for their approval of the payment. One of the approving vice-presidents, if available, should be the vice-president in charge of the personal trust division. The form then is returned to general counsel for the trust institution who prepares the resolution for presentation to the rust committee. The vice-president in charge of the personal trust division is a member of the committee and usually is present for questioning about the application.

The committee makes the decision, which is final. The committee minutes record the resolution, and the original application, together with a copy of the form, is filed with the other documents relating to the trust. A second copy of the form is filed with the general counsel's records and the third copy is placed in the trust officer's docket relating to the trust. 

Delaware. The application is either presented in writing or is reduced to writing, together with a recital of any facts throwing light upon or supporting the application. If the principal beneficiaries are adult, competent persons, they sometimes are asked to join in the request. In appropriate cases supporting letters are obtained from the family physician or the minister or some other disinterested person in a position to know the needs and characteristics of the beneficiary. The officer in charge of the account first considers and passes upon the application. He submits it to the senior trust officer who is the administrative head of the trust department. 

If it is a simple, clear case, with no doubt as to the authority for or the propriety of the application, these two make the decision. If either of them has any doubt about the matter, it is submitted to the executive head of the trust department. If he has any doubt, he submits it to the president, together with any additional information he is able to obtain from his associates or other sources. If doubt still lingers in the mind of any of them, the application and all the supporting facts are submitted to the trust committee, whose decision is final.

Thus, three methods are employed in passing upon applications. 

Some are passed upon by the officer in charge of the account and the administrative head of the trust department; others, by the executive head of the trust department after consulting with his associates, including the president; and still others, by the trust committee or, in extreme cases, by the board. The records upon which the officers or the committee or the board acts are (1) the application in writing with the reasons therefor, (2) the information brought to light in the course of investigation and, (3) if practicable, the approval of the principal beneficiaries. The records kept in the permanent files are the foregoing papers and, in addition, (1) a memorandum of the discussion of the officers, (2) a memorandum of the action of the committee, and (3) a complete record of the dates and amounts of all payments.

Georgia. The trust investment committee has prescribed the following procedure in exercising the power to use principal: If the cumulative total of principal is not over $500, the administrative officer's approval, based upon the proper application and investigation, is sufficient. If the total payments are over $500 but under $3,500, the head of the trust department must add his approval to that of the administrative officer. If the amount is over $3,500, the application must be submitted in writing and approved by the administrative officer, the head of the trust department and by the chairman of the board, the president, or the trust investment committee. If the amount is over $3,500, not only must the application be in writing, but, in addition, the administrative officer must see that proper evidence is furnished to support the use of principal. 

The terms of the trust instrument and the circumstances determine the extent and type of evidence required. Often the evidence is in the form of one or more affidavits, sometimes a letter, again a doctor's certificate, and in still other instances a letter or certificate from an educational institution or from some other appropriate source. There is an approved form of application which, together with the supporting letters, memorandums, certificates, and minutes of the trust investment committee, constitute the permanent record of the exercise of the power. 

Illinois. All applications are considered first by the trust officer and, if he is of the opinion that such application merits consideration, the data he has gathered is reduced to writing on a form prescribed by the trust administrative committee. This form shows the name, class, number, nature, and value of the trust; the relationship of the applicant to the account, the nature and amount of the payment, to whom and in what manner the payment shall be made; an excerpt from the instrument showing trustee's discretionary powers, and how, when, and for what uses such powers may be exercised; counsel's opinion, if one is obtained (if none, it is so stated); supporting papers or other data with a resumé of all factors considered; and the reasons for the payment and approval of the officer in charge of the account.

This officer obtains and assembles this information on the prescribed form (in duplicate) with all the supporting papers attached. 

This application is discussed first with the vice-president in charge

of the personal trust division and, in turn, is submitted by him to the committee for its consideration, and its decision is final. The trust administrative committee consists of the senior officers of the trust department and the minutes of this committee show a record of whatever action was taken. These minutes are later reviewed and made a part of the minutes of the directors' committee. 

California. A discretionary power to use principal is exercised by the trust officer in charge of the particular account, after consultation with and approval by his superior in charge of his division. This is generally true in those instances where the amount involved is of minor importance from the point of view of the size of the account. 

Where the amount in question is considered comparatively large, these officers consult with and procure the approval of the executive officer in charge of the department. In all instances, where there is any doubt whatever concerning the power to act, the trust counsel is called in to pass upon the legal aspects of the contemplated action. 

In every case, the trust officer in charge of the account makes a complete memorandum of his investigation, setting forth all pertinent facts, his recommendation and the reasons therefor. This is signed by all officers participating in the decision and placed in the files of the account.

Other Discretionary Powers 

With respect to the exercise of any discretionary power, whatever it may be, there must be (1) a clear understanding of the existence and of the extent of the power, (2) an investigation into the propriety of exercising the power in the given case, (3) a presentation of all the pertinent facts, and (4) a decision by the individual, the group, the committee, or the board of directors that has been charged by the by-laws, by a resolution of the board, by a resolution of the trust committee, or by the accepted practice of the institution with responsibility for making the decision. 

If the power relates to real property, the individual, group, or committee charged with responsibility for the management of real property in estates and trusts naturally would have the responsibility of finding the facts and making the recommendation or perhaps even making the decision about exercising the power. The exercise of a power relating to mortgages would be referred to the

individual, group, or committee handling mortgages to find and present the facts and make the recommendation or decision. The exercise of a power relating to taxes would be referred to the tax specialist or the tax division for facts, recommendation, or decision.

Thus the exercise of every discretionary power would, according to the plan of organization of the institution, be routed to and through the individual, group, division, section, or committee specializing in the phase of trust administration to which the particular power related.

CONCLUSIONS 

In face of the evident trend on the part of testators and settlors to grant trust institutions broad discretionary powers and on the part of trust institutions to welcome such a grant, the policies and practices of trust institutions in exercising these powers become matters of increasing importance alike to trust customers, to trust beneficiaries, and to trust institutions.

The policies and practices in exercising investment powers are, more or less, standardized. Those with respect to the exercise of distribution powers—particularly the power to use principal for income beneficiaries—are receiving the special attention of trust institutions and already are on their way toward standardization, so far as standardization of a matter involving so many variables is possible or desirable. With respect to the exercise of other discretionary powers, it is not practicable at the present time to make any more definite statement than that the trust institution must live up to the standard of the prudent man under the circumstances and that each trust institution, according to its own plan of organization, must develop policies and practices that will enable it to live up to this standard.

For the guidance of trust institutions, testators and settlors should state as definitely as possible the general purposes to be served by the exercise of the powers granted.

In general, the exercise of discretionary powers by a trust institution should be founded upon an existing knowledge or an impartial finding of the facts, a definite recommendation based upon collective information, and a deliberate decision representing and reflecting group judgment. In the exercise of discretionary powers

IIO  

specialization, collective information, and group judgment, as described in a previous Study (Why Trust Institutions Exist.), come into full play and characterize the service of trust institutions.

Scott on Trusts, sec. 187, pp. 986-1000.

SECOND SERIES, NUMBER SIX 

August Trust Institutions in Wartime by 

The Graduate School of Banking 

American Bankers Association 1942 

SUPPLEMENT TO THE TRUST BULLETIN 

Published by 

The Trust Division 

AMERICAN 

BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

22 East 40 Street. New York, N. Y.

HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 6  

TRUST institutions are engaged in the business of caring for and managing the property of others. In wartime, even more than in peacetime, people—especially men and women in military service or otherwise engaged in the war effort-need to have property cared for and managed for themselves and their dependents because they are unable to give it their personal attention. Consequently, in wartime trust institutions have an increasingly important rôle to play. 

Throughout this Study the term, men in military service, includes not only men and women on active duty in the Army, the Navy, or the Air Forces but also men and women diverted from their daily occupations to engage in the war effort on a wholetime basis. 

EXPERIENCES OF TRUST INSTITUTIONS IN THE FIRST WORLD WAR 

The experiences of American trust institutions during the first World War, 1914-1918, throw little light upon the present situation; those of English trust institutions are somewhat more illuminating. 

In the United States

Although there have been trust institutions in the United States operating throughout the period of every war in which this country has been engaged since, but not including,¹ the War of 1812, most of the development of the trust business in this country has taken place since the close of the first World War. The first trust department of a national bank was opened in 1915, and Section 11(k) of 

1 The Pennsylvania Company for Insurances on Lives and Granting Annuities of

Philadelphia was chartered in 1812, but it did not receive trust authority unil 1836. 

III

the Federal Reserve Act, abolishing the right of a state to prohibit a national bank within its borders from engaging in the trust business, was passed in 1918. Yet today more than half of the trust institutions in the United States are national banks. 

The proceedings of the annual meetings of the Trust Company Section of the American Bankers Association in 1917 and 1918 and the editorials and articles in Trust Companies during these two years do not show very clearly how the first World War affected the trust business, except that it produced administrative and operative problems similar to those of trust institutions during the present war.2 

In England

In 1914, when the first World War began, the oldest trust department in England—that of the Royal Exchange Assurance, a life insurance company-was only ten years of age, the office of the Public Trustee had been open only six and a half years, and the oldest bank trust department was only five years old. At that time the trust business of English institutions - mostly banks, life insurance companies, and the office of Public Trustee was a new venture. Yet it was the first World War that gave English banks their start in the trust business. On this point C. H. Barker in his book, Banks as Trustees,³ says

During the War years a greater need for finding new trustees for existing trusts, as well as new executors, unfortunately arose, when so many of those most competent to act individually lost their lives. For the same regrettable reason more wills, of which the banks had been appointed executors, came forward for probating and administration. At such a time, when individual life was more than ever uncertain, it was natural that more and more people turned to the banks as executors and trustees, in the sure and certain knowledge that the corporation, at all events, would survive to carry out the trust imposed upon it. . . . The War, therefore, had a very powerful effect on the growth of the bank trustee business. ...

During the first two and a half years of the first World War the Public Trustee received as many new trust accounts and will nomi- 

2 Wartime Readjustments of the Clerical Forces of Trust Companies, Trust Com panies, October 1917, p. 343. Investment of Trust Funds as Affected by Wartime 

Financing and Taxation, Ibid., July 1918, p. 9. 

3 Pp. 20-21; Sir Isaac Pitman & Sons, Ltd., London, 1933.

nations as he had received during the whole pre-war existence of his office.*

EXPERIENCES OF CANADIAN AND ENGLISH TRUST INSTITUTIONS IN THIS WAR 

In December 1941, more than two years after England and Canada had entered this war, six outstanding trustmen in Canada and six in England replied to the following inquiry: 

1. Have you noticed any increase in the number of wills in which your institution is named executor? 

2. Has there been a noticeable increase in the number of trusts created? 

3. Do you discern any difference in the terms of wills and trusts that you think. may be attributed to war conditions? 

4. In general, what would you say has been the effect so far of this war upon the trust business? 

In Canada

The six Canadian trustmen answered these four questions, in substance, as follows:

Number of wills and will nominations. Each of four of the six replying said that his company had not, as yet, experienced any substantial increase in the number of wills of persons still living, in which his company had been named. One of the four said that he was inclined to believe that up to the time of writing, the war had created in the minds of many people an outlook of day-to-day existence, which had made planning for the future difficult and generally neglected for problems of more immediate importance. Another spoke of a certain defeatist attitude of people at that time with respect to their estate plans. 

Two replied that their companies had experienced a substantial increase in the number of nominations under will. These two attributed the increase to the emphasis that war puts upon the uncertainty of human life (which affects both the testator and the exe- 

4 Allen, E. K., Stress of War Emphasizes Value of Trust Administration in England, Trust Companies, August 1917, p. 129.

cutor alike), the desire of people to put their houses in order before entering military service, the likelihood that an individual executor may be called into military service, the multiplicity of duties (mostly growing out of taxes and war regulations) now required of an executor, and the growing feeling on the part of the public that a trust company can give a greater degree of security, permanence, experience, and efficiency than an individual can give. 

Number of trusts. Many Canadian trust companies reported a considerable increase in the number of living trusts, particularly of men entering military service. On the other hand, some of the larger trust companies said that up to December 1941 they had not noticed any marked change in the number of trusts created. 

During 1941 most trust institutions in Canada experienced a considerable increase in the number and volume of executorships and administratorships, but it is impossible to tell how much of this increase should be attributed to the war. 5 Terms of wills and trusts. At the time of these replies there was some tendency in Canada, but apparently not a very marked one, in some cases to shorten the period of trusts under will and in other cases to eliminate them entirely and have the estate pass directly and outright to the family. This tendency was attributed to increased taxes and to the possibility of changed economic and social conditions after the war.

General effect of war upon trust business. The most noticeable effect of the war upon trust business in Canada has been greatly to increase the work required for the proper discharge of the operative and administrative details under wartime measures and the depletion of trust department staffs. (a) Wartime measures. Such wartime measures as foreign exchange control, trading-with-the-enemy regulations, custody of enemy property, price control, rental control, wages and salaries control, mail censorship, priorities, national defense tax, new heavy income and other taxes, the new Dominion succession duty, and 

5 Burroughs Clearing House, June 1942, p. 26.

the like, have presented to trust companies a maze of new duties and responsibilities.® (b) Depletion of staffs. These new duties and responsibilities have come at a time when trust department staffs are being depleted by calls to military service. The depleted ranks are being filled in, at best, by inexperienced or less experienced persons. This means that a decreasing and less experienced staff is having to do an everincreasing amount of operative and administrative work. 

In England

When the inquiry was made in December 1941, England already had endured and survived the severe bombings of the summer of 1940. The following is a summary of the replies of the English trustmen on the effect up to that time of the war upon the different phases of trust business under discussion.

Number of wills and will nominations. One trust institution reported that, since the commencement of this war, it had had a marked increase in the number of wills under which it was named. 

This refers to nominations under the wills of persons still alive and not to appointments under the wills of deceased persons. The number of these nominations increased almost violently, it said, from the period of enemy bombings and reached something like 500 nominations per week. As the danger from enemy bombing receded, it continued, the weekly number of nominations receded also and gradually returned to normal and by the first of 1942 were almost on the same level as before the war. "The proximity of danger from enemy action," it concluded, "thus appears to make our people will conscious to a remarkable extent." 

Another trust institution reported that ever since the Munich crisis in 1938 it had been experiencing a marked increase in the number of will nominations. It attributed the increase to the desire of people to arrange their affairs before entering military service and 

6 Gibson, John J., Trust Service in Canada under War Conditions, Trust Bulletin, March 1941, p. 36; Trusts and Estates, February 1941, p. 180; Baker, G. R. G.,

Trust Company War Work, The Monetary Times, Toronto, Canada, May 2, 1942, p. 16.

to the possibility of individual executors being called into military service.

The third institution reported that in the first few months of the war there had been a sudden, heavy increase in the number of wills naming the bank executor and trustee, that the war made people realize more keenly the uncertainty of life, but that this abnormal increase was not so noticeable now, if in fact it existed, the probability being that those prompted only by war conditions to make a will had by this time made one.

The fourth said the principal effect which the war had had upon the public so far as trust work is concerned had been to make them realize that they ought to make wills, and for this reason it had had a very large increase in the number of wills of which it had been notified in which the bank was nominated to be executor and trustee. No doubt, it added, there were many other cases in which it had not been notified of its nomination. 

A fifth English trust institution said that most people did not have sufficient time or incentive to think of such mundane things as executors and trustees. Yet even this one sensed an increasing will consciousness and a tendency, on account of the uncertainties of human life, to name trust institutions. 

The following table' also indicates a growing tendency in England to name trust institutions.
