7 This table should be read in the light of the following explanation: In England, executors or administrators are not required to publish notices to creditors; trust institutions habitually publish them; individuals do so less frequently. The figures, therefore, may be unduly favorable to trust institutions; yet, they may be accepted as reasonably trustworthy for showing the general trend toward trust institutions over this 12-year period. 

8 Hough, Stanley C., Trust Service under Fire, Trusts and Estates, February 1942, p. 174 at p. 175,

Number of administratorships. In England there has been a notable increase in the number of appointments of trust institutions to administratorships. Administratorships are more numerous now than formerly because many of those entitled to apply for letters of administration are unable through war service to apply. In some cases the executor named in the will is, for the same reason or for some other reason not present in normal times, unable to qualify. 

Number of trusts. In England up to December 1941 there had not been any noticeable increase in the number of trusts created, and some trust institutions had experienced a decrease. The reason ascribed for this decrease is that property owners, who otherwise would be disposed to make settlements upon their relatives or dependents, feel obliged now to keep control of their property so as to be able to meet tax requirements and personal and family needs. 

One English trustman said that voluntary settlements had largely come to an end and that the making of marriage settlements by young persons had become the exception rather than the rule. 

There appears to be some tendency on the part of testators,to leave their estates outright instead of leaving them in trust or subject to life estates. The nature of wills generally is tending toward simplicity. One of the reasons for this tendency toward simplicity is not connected with the war; it is an outgrowth of the Lord Birkenhead Acts of 1925, making it possible to incorporate by reference many provisions that it had been necessary to spell out in the will. 

Transferred trusts. In transferred trusts that is, trusts transferred from an individual trustee to a corporate trustee- there has been, and apparently there will continue to be, a marked increase. 

Transferred trusts are said now to be a substantial part of the new business of English trust institutions. "Private trustees who enlist in the army, navy, or air force," said one English trustman, "naturally wish to be relieved of their responsibilities and feel they cannot do better than to appoint their own bank trustee company." 

Terms of wills and trusts. In England wills are being modified so as to avoid double death duties. If a man leaves everything to his wife, and if she is the younger, then, if they are both killed in 9 In England, in case of two persons dying at the same time, the older is presumed to have died first-Law of Property Act, 1925, sec. 184.

the same air raid or other enemy action, she is presumed to have survived. One estate duty on land or a business, but not on other kinds of property, is payable on the husband's death and a further estate duty, but at a lower rate, is payable on the wife's death. To avoid these double death duties, the husband now provides that his wife shall have all his property if she survives him for one calendar month; and makes an alternative disposition in the event that she predeceases him or fails to survive him for a month. 

Crossover wills (the English term) or mutual wills (the American term) are being employed to an increasing degree. The husband and wife each wills everything to the other, with gifts over in case the other should not survive to inherit the estate. Where there are young children their interests will be protected by such wills, as they will take under the will of the surviving parent. 

General effect of war upon trust business. In England the most noticeable effects of the war upon trust business have been decentralization of trust work, duplication of records, depletion of staffs, provisions to meet governmental requirements, and drain on earnings. (a) Decentralization of trust work. Decentralization means the handling of trust accounts at several branch offices rather than at a central point. The process of decentralization began before the war started and, partly at any rate, as a protective measure in the event of war. For ten years or more prior to the outbreak of the war, trust institutions with offices in London had been establishing branches in different parts of England and transferring documents and records to these branches. With one exception, just before the outbreak of the present war all the London bank trust departments transferred their chief offices from the City of London to places less vulnerable to enemy air attack. This was no mean undertaking, they said, when such transfer meant for each the removal of tons of furniture, machines, and equipment and the housing, at first temporarily and then on a permanent footing, of the members of the staff. At the present time, each bank trust department is represented in the City by a small staff for the convenience of customers and to deal with matters that cannot conveniently be dealt with elsewhere, such as stock exchange transactions. (b) Duplication of records. The decentralization of trust work and further precaution against fire and destruction caused by air

raids or other enemy action made it necessary for English trust institutions to duplicate all their important, irreplaceable records. 

Vital records and original deeds have been duplicated by photography, and this duplication is maintained as fresh records are made and new deeds are received. Accountancy records too have to be duplicated. (c) Depletion of staffs. The depletion of staffs has been much more pronounced in England than in Canada. One English trustman spoke as though his institution soon might enter a second stage of filling in its ranks. First, when men were called into military service, young women were employed to take their places; then, as young women also were called, older women had to take their places. In another English trust institution, up to December 1941, about 40 per cent of the staff had joined the armed forces. Yet this institution then was dealing with an increased volume of business over and above that dealt with prior to the war. The carrying of this increased load had been greatly facilitated, it said, by the mechanization of operations and by the fact that quite a number of the women had become highly skilled in handling the bookkeeping and routine work. 

Some of the English trustmen said that, with their depleted staffs and with the increase in operations due to wartime requirements, they had had to reduce to the minimum the detail work in handling accounts. They reported that they had been able to do this without impairing efficiency and that their customers had been most helpful in meeting any suggestions which they had made. (d) Provisions to meet governmental requirements. English trust institutions have had to make special provisions for meeting governmental requirements. One of them had met the situation by establishing a new department whose duty it is to see that all transfers of securities and movements of money of every description conform in every respect to the defense regulations. Customers naturally are anxious to be assured that whatever transactions are undertaken shall comply with all governmental requirements. Through this new department this institution had been able to assist the Treasury, acting through the Bank of England, in meeting these requirements. (e) Drain on earnings. The depletion of the staffs and the increase in the wartime requirements has affected the earnings of English

trust institutions. Yet, as far as is known, only one of them so far has increased its charges since this war commenced. This one has raised its minimum acceptance charge and the management charges for collecting rent and managing property. Also, in its published schedules it has provided for additional charges at its discretion, principally in connection with managing businesses, holding interviews outside the office, and handling foreign assets. 

EXPERIENCES OF AMERICAN TRUST INSTITUTIONS IN THIS WAR 

In June 1942, after the United States had been at war six months, twenty-four trustmen, representing every section of the country, replied to the following questionnaire: 

1. What, so far, has been the discernible effect of the war upon your trust business that is, in what types of trust accounts are you experiencing a noticeable (a) increase or (b) decrease in number or volume received which is, in your opinion, directly attributable to the war? 

2. What trust services are you finding to be especially adapted to the needs of men in military service (a) new services, (b) existing services? 

3. What new or special services should trust institutions offer the general public during this war? 

4. What is the present policy of your institution in advertising trust services to men in military service that is, what points is it emphasizing in presenting the needs these men have for trust service? 

5. How is war affecting your trust service 

that is, what special. (a) executive, (b) administrative, and (c) operative problems is the war creating or accentuating and how are you solving these problems? 

6. How can trust institutions best help to win this war?

Effect of War upon New Trust Business 

Six months is too short a period for trust institutions to experience or for trustmen to discern the full impact of war upon new trust business. Even where there has been a marked increase or decrease in the number of trust accounts of a given type received during this period, it is not easy to tell whether or not this increase or decrease should be attributed to the war. For example, one trust institution reported that during this period it had received an unusually large number of executorships and that this phenomenon might be attributed to deaths resulting from anxieties produced by the war. But even in peacetime executorships frequently come to

trust institutions in waves. Who then can say that this trust institution would not have received the same number of executorships had there not been a war?

Several of the larger trust institutions reported a decrease in the number of corporate trust and corporate agency accounts. There are few new issues of stocks and bonds calling for the services of a corporate trustee, transfer agent, or registrar. This condition, however, obtained before this war and it may continue for the duration of the war. Some trust institutions reported a decrease in the number of new irrevocable trusts. This decrease is attributable partly to uncertainties as to taxes and partly to other war conditions. While a majority of the trust institutions replying reported an increase in the number of will nominations, a few have experienced some decrease, which was attributable, they thought, to the war. 

Services Especially Adapted to Needs of Men in Military Service 

Four types of trust service were suggested as being especially adapted to the needs of men in military service. They are will nominations, revocable funded or instalment insurance trusts, revocable living trusts, and agencies.

Will nominations. As older men are selected or volunteer, an increasing number of property owners will enter military service and be assigned to active duty. Each of these property owners will need a will. Many of them will be disposed to name their own banks executor and trustee under their wills. 

Funded and instalment insurance trusts. If the property owner entering military service has enough stocks or bonds to produce income sufficient to pay the premiums on his life insurance, he may create a funded insurance trust of his stocks and bonds as well as his life insurance policies and direct the trustee to pay the premiums on his insurance out of the income from his securities. If he owns some stocks or bonds but not enough for the income from them to pay all his insurance premiums, he may, nevertheless, create an instalment insurance trust and arrange with the trustee for his making up in cash what he lacks in income from his securities to meet the premium requirements.

1 Revocable living trusts. In most cases the living trust of a man in military service should be revocable so as to leave the settlor, after he returns from war, free to make any change in the arrangement he then may desire to make. The trust should be irrevocable only in those exceptional cases in which it should be irrevocable for reasons not connected with this war. 

The advantages of a revocable living trust for the man in military service are: (1) it enables him to make a complete and yet flexible arrangement for caring for and managing his property while he is in military service; (2) it enables him to make a complete and flexible arrangement for supporting his family and dependents and educating his children during this period; (3) it enables him to provide for the continuation of these business and personal services during any period of uncertainty as to his own fate in the war; (4) in the event of his death the trustee may continue to carry on for his beneficiaries; and (5), if he returns, he will be free to continue, to change, or to terminate the trust as his judgment then dictates.

Agencies. Some agencies have been established by property owners for the care and management of their property while they are in military service. However, there is one feature of an agency to which attention should be called. The death of the principal ordinarily terminates an agency.¹º In peacetime this characteristic of an agency seldom gives trouble. The trust institution as agent learns promptly of the death of the principal and in due time turns the property over to the executor or administrator. In wartime, on the contrary, there may be an extended or even an indefinite period of uncertainty. The principal in overseas service may not be heard from for weeks or months; he may be reported missing; by mistake he may be reported dead. During this period of uncertainty the trust institution as agent is in an anomalous position. If the principal in fact is alive, although reported missing or dead, the agency legally continues in full force and effect. If, however, the principal is actually not alive, although he has not been heard from or has only 

10 But see Umsted, John R., Powers of Attorney by Men in Service, The Legal 

Intelligencer, Philadelphia, July 3, 1942, p. 1.

been reported missing, the agency no longer exists and the agent is without authority to act further.

Because of this characteristic of an agency, many trust institutions are discouraging men in military service from creating agencies and encouraging them, instead, to create revocable living trusts. 

One trustman sums up the matter in these words:

It seems to us that the trust service best adapted to the needs of the service men in this war is the revocable living trust. Because of the fact that in this war men are stationed all over the world, where it is much more difficult to communicate with them than it was when they were in France in the last war, and because of the expected greater percentage of men who will be reported missing, with no certainty as to whether they are living or dead, it seems to us that if a managing agency is established, with or without a power of attorney to the trust institution or to an individual accessible to the trust institution, and at the same time a will is filed with the trust institution, there is a danger that an important step may have to be taken at a time when no one can be sure whether the attorney-in-fact or the executor named in the will has power to act. 

Trust Services Especially Needed by People in Wartime 

In wartime people need, not new services, but special adaptations of old, established, well-known services, among which are the following: 

Substitute will nominations. Many older persons- both men and women have named a younger person a son, brother, business associate, or friend executor and trustee under will. The person so named already may be in military service or may be subject to call or may be ready to volunteer. In any such case, the testator should name another executor and trustee. He may be disposed to name his bank or trust company substitute executor and trustee under will.

Successor trustees. Men who themselves are trustees under wills or agreements or court orders have entered or are about to enter the military service. It will be necessary to have either successor trustees or representatives of the trustee to carry on while they are in military service. There may be a disposition to name a trust institution successor trustee either for the duration of the war or for the duration of the trust.

Administrators. Some men entering military service, at the time

[

they volunteer or are called, will be serving as executors under will or as administrators and be in the midst of settling estates. Upon entering military service, they should turn the estate over to an administrator appointed by the court. The members of the family or the other beneficiaries may ask the court to appoint a bank or trust company to accept appointment as administrator to complete the settlement of the estate. 

Advertising Policies of Trust Institutions in Wartime 

This war has so far made few major changes in the trust advertising policy or program of trust institutions. Some institutions, through newspaper advertisements, booklets, and letters (especially through letters to professional men, such as doctors and dentists) are directing the attention of men in military service to the importance of their arranging for the care and management of their property. 11 At the present time, there would seem to be a special need for trust advertising of an informative, more than promotional, character designed to inform men in military service and other people, in general terms, about trust services especially adapted to wartime requirements. However, trust advertising should not play upon wartime emotions. A study of recent trust advertisements and booklets gives assurance that trust institutions generally are presenting their case positively and informatively without appealing to fears, apprehensions, or other emotions characteristic of wartime. 

Trust Problems Produced by This War 

The wartime problems of trust institutions fall into three groups: (1) general problems arising out of this war which trust institutions have in common with other business organizations, (2) standing trust problems which existed before this war and still exist, and (3) special trust problems growing directly out of this war. 

General problems. In the first group belong the problems of depleted staffs, of inexperienced staff members, of need for closer 

11 Journeay, Albert, Opportunities in Trust Advertising, Burroughs Clearing House,

April 1942, p. 22. For a discussion of the wartime advertising policy of English trust institutions, see Trusts and Estates, January 1942, p. 54.

supervision of the staff, of training new members of the staff, of meeting governmental regulations, of obtaining adequate mechanical equipment, of increasing costs of operation, and, in general, the problems of shifting rapidly, even precipitately, from a peacetime economy to a wartime economy. With respect to this group of problems, as members of their staff leave for the service, trust institutions are re-allocating duties; employing older men, young women, and older women, training them, and supervising them; eliminating non-essential operations; and, as far as possible, putting into effect all economies that do not impair the quality of the service.

Standing problems. In the second group belong the standing trust problems of meeting income requirements of beneficiaries out of low-yield investments, of inadequate trust compensation, and of the selection and supervision of trust investments. These standing trust problems are continuing to receive attention and some progress is being made toward solving them.

Special problems. In the third group belong the special trust problems of protecting trust property from war damage; of preserving and protecting trust records; of absent co-trustees, advisers, and consultants; and of missing principals, testators, and beneficiaries. (a) Protection of trust property from war damage. In reaching their decisions as to war damage insurance on trust property and property covered by mortgages held in trust accounts, trust institutions are applying the standard of the prudent man under the circumstances. If and to the extent that the prudent man would effect such insurance, the trust institution should insure trust property and should require mortgagors to insure mortgaged property. A prudent man in an exposed coastal area would effect war damage insurance on his property in cases in which an equally prudent man in an inland, less exposed area might not do so unless or until danger of enemy attack became more imminent. It should be noted, however, that even now in some of the inland cities trust institutions are covering all the real property in their estates and trusts and having mortgagors cover all mortgaged property with war damage insurance. (b) Preservation and protection of trust records. Likewise the

preservation of trust records may, for the time being at any rate, be a somewhat different problem in the coastal areas from what it is in the inland areas. Trust institutions in the coastal areas already are providing by means of photography and other duplicating processes for duplication and preservation of essential trust records. They are placing these duplicate records in safekeeping in inland institutions or in special vaults. The Dominion and several of the provinces of 

Canada already have enacted statutes making photographs of ledger entries, records, and documents admissible in evidence. It would seem that similar legislation should be enacted in the United States. (c) Absent co-trustees. Co-trusteeship with men absent in military service is raising some serious problems for trust institutions. 

In four states-Pennsylvania,12 Rhode Island,13 New York,¹ and

New Jersey¹5 — the matter is covered by statute. It is covered in the different statutes in one of three ways: (1) by empowering the court to authorize the remaining co-trustee alone to exercise all powers, both discretionary and ministerial, (2) by authorizing the co-trustee himself to delegate his functions to an attorney in fact, and (3) by empowering the court to appoint a substitute co-trustee. 

Unless or until legislation is enacted in the remaining states, trust institutions in those states soon will be facing a serious problem in their co-trusteeships with men in military service. So far, it is a comparatively new problem, and a satisfactory solution has not been found.

One trust institution reports that it is having each of its cotrustees, who is in military service and who is willing to do so, sign a letter authorizing the trust institution as the active co-trustee (1) to proceed with the administration of the trust, (2) to make such changes in investments under the terms of the trust as it thinks best, (3) to make advances from principal where the trustees have power to do so, and (4) to exercise all other discretionary powers, ratifying in advance the actions of the trust institution as 

12 Act of March 20, 1942, Acts 1942, Act. No. 3. 

13 Laws of 1942, S. B. No. 93, approved April 6, 1942. 

14 Sec. 103-a, Surrogate's Court Act, as added by Laws 1942, Chap. 377, approved and effective April 8, 1942. 

15 Laws of 1942, Chap. 160, approved May 6, 1942.

co-trustee. "Perhaps this will not be a full protection to us," says the head of this trust department, "but I am inclined to believe that, if any question arises in the years to come, the courts will be inclined to be considerate because of the emergency through which we are passing."

Another trustman says that, where there are three co-trustees and one of them is in military service, he thinks the other two may carry on with reasonable safety. Where there are only two co-trustees and one of them is in military service, he thinks that even in this case the remaining co-trustee should carry on and hope later to get the ratification of the co-trustee or the approval of the account by the court. He believes that because of the circumstances the court will be reasonably liberal in the allowance of accounts submitted by only one of the two co-trustees. On the other hand, there is the possibility that, after the war is over, some courts may be disposed to hold trustees of property in which men in military service are interested to even stricter accountability for technical correctness of administration than they would hold trustees for civilians either in wartime or in peacetime; and trust institutions, while they are grappling with the problems of absent co-trustees, should be mindful of this possibility. (d) Absent advisers and consultants. A somewhat similar problem will arise with respect to absent investment advisers and consultants. For example, under the terms of the trust, the trust institution is directed not to make or change any investment without the direction or approval, as the case may be, of a named adviser or consultant, who now is in military service and who cannot be reached for consultation, advice, approval, or direction. Or the settlor, now in military service, may have provided that the trustee should not make or change any investment without his approval or direction. 

If it is a revocable or amendable living trust and the settlor still may be reached, he may change the provisions about the adviser or consultant. For example, he may provide that, if the adviser or consultant, himself included, does not act within a stated number of days after notice is sent him, the trustee may act on its own judgment. 

Many trust instruments already contain this provision. But if the living trust is irrevocable or if it is a trust under will and the trust instrument does not contain this or a similar provision, the only

safe course for the trustee is to seek the advice or instruction of the court. (e) Missing principals. The dilemma that may be created by the principal of an agency being reported missing has been mentioned already.¹ 16 (f) Missing testators. Missing testators raise a distinct set of problems for trust institutions. For example, X Trust Company is executor under B's will. B is reported missing and for months nothing further is heard. The trust company would not be able to present proof of death that would entitle it to qualify as executor and obtain letters testamentary. Yet throughout this period of uncerainty B's property should be cared for and managed. The law provides for the appointment of a receiver or special administrator to take over and hold B's property until an executor or administrator is appointed. If the trust company, being the executor nominated in the will, is appointed receiver or special administrator, this partially solves the problem. However, the trust company acting in this temporary capacity would not have the powers of management that it probably would need, or would have, as executor. A revocable living trust would have obviated the need for a receiver or special administrator or even an executor for the property placed in trust. (g) Missing beneficiaries. Missing beneficiaries raise another set of problems. For example, A (father) leaves property by will to 

X Trust Company in trust to pay the income to his son B, who is in military service, for life and after B's death to pay over one-half of the principal, discharged of the trust, to C (B's wife) and hold the remaining one-half in trust to pay the income to or apply it for the benefit of D, E, and F (B's children) until the youngest of them shall be 21 years of age and then distribute the principal among B's children or their issue. Or B himself, before entering military service, creates a living trust with similar distribution provisions. 

Later B is reported missing. When should the trustee make the distribution of principal to C and begin making payments of income to or for D, E, and F? In most states, by statute or judicial decision, a presumption of death is raised by the lapse of seven years from the date of disappearance. Must the trustee withhold distribution or 

16 Supr, p. 122.

payments until B's death is confirmed or, failing this, until the lapse of seven years? If so, what is to become of the wife and children in the meantime? A partial solution of this problem is a provision in the will or trust agreement authorizing payments or distributions without proof of death or lapse of seven years. The following provisions of this character have been prepared by attorneys at law in the states indicated: 

Delaware. If the settlor, shall be a member of the armed services of the United States and the trustee shall receive notice in writing from an authorized official of the United States Government of the death of the settlor, or that he is missing, then the trustee shall forthwith, upon the receipt of notice of death of the settlor, or upon the expiration of two years after receipt of notice that the settlor is missing, hold, administer, and/or distribute the trust estate, principal and undistributed income thereof, in the same manner, and to the same extent as provided for upon the death of the settlor, irrespective of whether or not he shall then be living.

Georgia. Upon receipt of the usual notice directed to the family or next of kin by the Adjutant General in Washington or by any other official of the war department or other branch of the United States Government, that the life beneficiary under this trust who is in the services of the United States of America, is dead or missing and his whereabouts unknown, the trustee shall be authorized immediately to make whatever distributions of income or principal the said life beneficiary is entitled to receive under the trust, to the next succeeding beneficiary entitled to take, and the trustee shall be under no liability in making such payments to the next succeeding beneficiary. Should it later be established that the said life beneficiary be in life, the distribution being made to the next succeeding beneficiary shall cease in case the distributions have not been completed and the trustee shall again make distributions to the said life beneficiary in accordance with the terms and provisions of this trust and in the same manner and under the same conditions as existed prior to the time the said life beneficiary was reported dead or missing by the United States Government. However, the trustee shall be fully protected against any and all claims, demands, causes of action or losses arising from or growing out of such distributions made to the next succeeding beneficiary during the period in which the said life beneficiary was reported dead or missing by the governmental authorities. 

Illinois. (Assume that the trust instrument provides that the trustee shall pay the net income of the trust estate to A for his life and upon and after his death to B for his life.) If A shall become a member of the armed forces of the United States and if there shall thereafter be exhibited to the trustee notice from the United States Government or any department, branch, or agency thereof that A has been killed or is missing, the trustee shall pay to B the net income then on hand and thereafter accruing in the same manner and with the same effect as if A's death were conclusively established by competent evidence. If A shall thereafter appear in person or if there shall be exhibited to the trustee notice from the United States Government or any department, branch, or agency thereof that A is living, the trustee shall discontinue such payments to B and shall pay to A the net income then on hand and thereafter accruing. In no event shall the trustee or B be liable for any payments made to B pursuant to this paragraph. The provisions of this paragraph shall remain in effect for so long as A shall be in the said armed forces.

1 Massachusetts. If any beneficiary hereunder shall direct the trustee in writing to pay all or any part of the sums to which he or she might be entitled to persons reasonably dependent on such beneficiary or otherwise for the benefit of such beneficiary, the trustee may carry out such directions. If, in lack of such directions, the beneficiary is absent and the trustee cannot reasonably communicate with such beneficiary, or if the beneficiary is unable to do business from sickness or incompetence or any other reason, the trustee may, if it considers it advantageous to such beneficiary, pay all or any part of the sums to which such beneficiary might be entitled to persons reasonably dependent on such beneficiary, or otherwise for the benefit of such beneficiary. Payments under either of the two preceding paragraphs may be made until the beneficiary's death is established and the trustee shall be under no liability for any payments that shall prove to have been made in fact after such beneficiary's death or during any period when such beneficiary was incompetent. If the trustee shall believe that a beneficiary is dead, even though proof of such death cannot be produced, the trustee may carry out the provisions in this instrument specified to take effect on the death of the beneficiary without waiting for a period of seven years or any other period and without liability should the beneficiary in fact prove to be alive.

Missouri. In the event the settlor or life beneficiary, while in the military service of the United States, shall have been by the proper governmental agency officially reported as dead or missing, then sixty (60) days thereafter (unless such official report shall have been rescinded or otherwise modified to indicate that the settlor or such life beneficiary is or may be still living) the trust shall cease and terminate, and the trustee shall thereupon make distribution of the trust estate as herein provided, in like manner and under the same conditions as if the settlor or such life beneficiary had in fact departed this life; and the trustee shall be released and discharged of any and all liability or accountability hereunder to the settlor or such life beneficiary for such distribution; provided before any distribution shall be made to any beneficiary other than the wife or children of the settlor, such beneficiaries, respectively, shall enter into a bond to the estate, with security satisfactory to the trustee, conditioned that if such supposed decedent shall in fact be alive at the time of such distribution, then the distributees shall refund the amount receivable by them on demand, with interest thereon from the date of such demand. 

New York, Anything herein contained to the contrary notwithstanding, the death of any beneficiary currently entitled to receive income hereunder and/or annuity payments shall be conclusively presumed by the trustee hereunder, for the purposes of this trust, when the following conditions shall have occurred: (1) Such beneficiary is a member of the armed forces of the United States of America. (2) The trustee shall receive a report, which it believes to be genuine, that such person is dead or missing under such circumstances that the death of such person seems probable. The trustee shall incur no liability whatsoever for making any payments, whether of income or principal, hereunder, as if such beneficiary were in fact deceased, whether or not the report of the death of such beneficiary is subsequently determined to be erroneous, and such beneficiary shall have no right to recover from anyone payments of income or principal made by the trustee in reliance upon such evidence furnished to it that such beneficiary is deceased. 

Pennsylvania. When and so long as any income beneficiary is absent from the jurisdiction under such circumstances that the trustee is in doubt whether such income beneficiary is alive or dead, the trustee in its discretion may pay the income to which such absent beneficiary is or would be entitled to the next succeeding income bene-

ficiary entitled thereto. If the death of the absent income beneficiary would terminate the trust, in whole or in part, the trustee shall not distribute such principal until the fact of such death has been judicially determined but, in the meanwhile, shall hold such principal, with all of the investment powers herein conferred, and shall pay the income to the persons who would be entitled to such principal on the death of such absent income beneficiary. Payments of income made pursuant to any of the provisions of this paragraph shall be made by the trustee without liability to such absent income beneficiary and such payments shall not later be recouped from principal or from subsequently accruing income, or from the persons who have received such payments.

Tennessee. The word death as above used is defined to mean receipt by the trustee/ executor hereunder of official notice (defined to mean a notice in writing or in a telegram from a responsible department or official of the United States Government) that the settlor/testator is dead, missing, or otherwise unaccounted for; provided, however, if such notice does not specify the event of death, then the trustee/ executor shall seek such corroborative evidence as it may deem necessary or desirable, but failure to receive any further evidence for a period of- -after receipt of the first notice shall be deemed conclusive evidence of death for the purposes of this agreement/will, and shall be full protection to the trustee/executor in acting thereon: in the event such notice specifies the event of death no additional evidence or delay shall be necessary.

West Virginia. All dispositions and distributions of the trust estate herein expressly made contingent upon the death of the settlor may likewise be made and with the same effect and finality, after the settlor, if he be in any branch of the military service of the United States, shall have been officially reported missing and unaccounted for for a period of one year; and all persons dealing with the trustee shall be fully protected and saved harmless in accepting and relying upon the statement of the trustee in respect to such official report and its duration. (h) Other wartime problems of trust institutions. Two other sets of special wartime problems of trust institutions are: (1) problems of complying with governmental regulations connected with distributions or payments to trust beneficiaries in countries at war with the United States or to enemy aliens in this country and (2) problems growing out of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Civil Relief Act of 1940 and the proposed amendments of 1942 suspending for the duration of this war the enforcement of certain civil liabilities of certain persons in military service.

How Trust Institutions Can Help to Win the War 

Most of the answers mentioned ways in which banks and trust companies as financial institutions or bankers and trustmen as patriotic individuals can help to win the war. They mentioned such matters as (1) banks and trust companies buying war savings bonds

for trust and agency accounts and promoting the sale of war savings bonds, (2) bankers and trustmen participating actively in civilian defense and other war activities, and (3) bankers and trustmen explaining and interpreting the government's war program and helping the public to "understand war taxes, war restrictions, rationing, price controls, and other war measures, which depend for their success upon public cooperation and public morale." 17 

However, any direct part that the trust institution or the trustman as such can play in winning the war is restricted by the nature of the trust business itself. Since this business is caring for and managing the property of others, a trust institution cannot do with the property of others all that it could do with its own property. For example, in buying for a trust account war savings bonds or the bonds of a corporation producing war material, a trust institution is limited by the terms of the trust and by the needs of the beneficiaries. The trust institution as such can participate directly in financing the war effort only to the extent that it can invest trust funds within these two limitations.

The most effective contribution that trust institutions can make toward the winning of this war is in helping to keep up the morale of the people. This means helping to sustain the morale of many groups of people—for example: (1) men with property and dependents now or soon to be in military service, (2) men in wholetime civilian defense or war work, (3) beneficiaries of estates and trusts the individual executor, administrator, trustee, or guardian of which has been called to war or war work before his fiduciary responsibilities have been discharged, (4) men and women-parents, brothers and sisters, friends, and business associates—who have named individual executors and trustees and now face the need for naming substitutes for the duration of this war, and (5) of businessmen whose arrangements have been upset by the call of partners, associates, or keymen to war or war work, by the depletion of their staffs by the conversion of their plants to the production of war material, or by limitations upon their raw materials or their products. By caring for and managing property, by accepting substitute nominations or successor appointments to fiduciary capacities, 17 From the statement, The Banker in Wartime, prepared by the Economic Policy 

Commission of the American Bankers Association.

by accepting smaller accounts than they otherwise would be justified in accepting, by advising with husbands and fathers, businessmen, property owners, and members of their family on business, financial, and even personal problems connected with estates and trusts and helping to relieve their minds of worry and anxiety on these matters, trust institutions may have a vital part in winning the war. "Family morale is the basis of all morale," and it is in sustaining family morale that the trust institution is making its distinctive contribution toward winning this war.

CONCLUSIONS 

As far as a person at this time (August 1942) is warranted in drawing any conclusions about trust institutions in wartime, one seems to be justified in the light of the findings of the foregoing 

Study in venturing the following statements:

At the present time three groups of people seem to have special need for trust service. First, men in military service who own property and have dependents (members of their families or others) need the services of trust institutions to help them plan their estates so as best to meet the needs of their dependents; to accept nomination now as executor and trustee under their wills and eventually to settle and administer their estates; and, while they are on active duty, to care for and manage their property and use it for the benefit of their dependents. Second, the beneficiaries of estates and trusts which have been in the hands of individuals (as executors, administrators, trustees, or guardians) now in military service, need the services of trust institutions to complete the settlement of these estates and continue the administration of these trusts and guardianships. Third, persons who have named in fiduciary capacities men now in military service need the services of trust institutions to accept substitute nominations or successor appointments for the duration of the war.

Trust institutions may anticipate a considerable increase in the number of will nominations, of administratorships, of successor trusteeships, of revocable living trusts, and revocable funded or in-

stalment insurance trusts. On the other hand, they may expect some decrease in the number of irrevocable living trusts or irrevocable insurance trusts, and of corporate trusts and agencies. Revocable living trusts and revocable funded insurance trusts are likely to prove popular with and useful to men in military service. 

The vital part that the morale of a people has in winning a war and the distinctive part that trust institutions may have in sustaining this morale were expressed as follows by the late Francis H. Sisson in an address delivered in September 1918:

They (trust companies) have generated an unseen but powerfully effective and extremely vital force for the achieving of victory of the United States and its Allies in this war. And that force is exerting itself on the firing line wherever American troops are going into action. It is a psychological and moral force of incalculable value; for it is relieving countless thousands of our soldiers and sailors of anxiety regarding the welfare of their dependents, as well as concern about the proper care of business resources suddenly left behind at the imperative call of duty. This is the highest possible kind of trust service.18

Since the main objective of this war is the preservation for mankind of the democratic way of life, it should be remembered always that the trust institution itself is a product and expression of democracy. Although the idea of a corporation to settle estates was conceived in India as early as 1774, the first trust institution in the world was established in this country in 1822, when this nation was less than 50 years old. The trust institution is at home now whereever English is the mother tongue and English common law is the law of the land, but elsewhere it still is only an alien, existing by sufferance here and there in a few civil law countries, including Germany, Italy, and Japan. Its lifespan of 120 years has been contemporaneous with the period of greatest growth and development of the United States. Coming into being during the “era of good feeling" (1817-1824), it has survived and shared every vicissitude of this country's national life-tranquility and turmoil, peace and war, prosperity and depression. As running water first carves out a channel for itself and then adapts itself to the channel it has made, so the trust institution has made a place for itself in the economy this country and adapted its service to the needs of a democratic of 

18 Sisson, Francis H., Trust Service and the War, Trust Companies, October 1918, p. 386, at p. 390.

people. Its foundation stones are two of the distinctive freedoms of democracy: (1) the freedom of disposition of property by will and (2) the freedom of disposition of property in a trust. Its inspiration is the ethic of a free and self-reliant people-a man's duty to provide for his own and especially for those of his own household. In helping to win this war, the trust institution is fighting for its own right to carry on as one of the needed instrumentalities of a working democracy.

SUPPLEMENT TO THE TRUST BULLETIN

The Trust Division 

AMERICAN 

BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

22 East 40 Street New York, N. Y.

HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 7 HOW COMPENSATION FOR TRUST SERVICE IS DETERMINED 

IN N this country at the present time a trust institution is entitled to compensation for its service, although there is no provision for such compensation in the trust instrument.' This Study is a discussion of the factors that are taken into consideration in determining what is proper compensation for trust service. Each of the following groups plays a major rôle in this determination: (1) testators, settlors, and other persons who employ the service of trust institutions; (2) beneficiaries of trust service; (3) attorneys for executors, trustees, and other fiduciaries and for trust customers and beneficiaries; (4) courts having jurisdiction over trust accounts; and (5) trust institutions.

Recently, 21 outstanding trustmen of long experience, representing every section of the country, were asked to state what factors they thought settlors, testators, beneficiaries, attorneys, and trustmen take into consideration in arriving at what they regard as proper compensation for trust service. In a long line of reported cases it is revealed what courts take into consideration. The Trust Division of the American Bankers Association has stated what trust institutions take into consideration. This Study is an attempt to correlate and interpret the information received from these three sources. 

While this Study is largely a discussion of the compensation of trust institutions, it should be understood throughout that the compensation of trust institutions is the same as that of individual fiduciaries. 

1 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 974, p. 2850; Scott on Trusts, sec. 242, p. 1383; Shattuck Revision, sec. 91, p. 231. 137

There is a great deal of overlapping of the factors that these five groups take into consideration. In order to avoid repetition, each factor is discussed under the heading of the group which is thought to give that factor special consideration.

TESTATORS AND SETTLORS 

Unless the rate of compensation is to be specified in the will, a testator is not called upon very frequently to consider the factors that should be taken into consideration in determining compensation for the service of a trust institution as executor or trustee, or both, under his will. A recent analysis of several hundred wills shows (1) that a large majority of wills are silent as to the executor's or trustee's compensation and (2) that about one-third of the wills that mention compensation at all simply say that the executor and trustee shall receive reasonable compensation for their service. The reason for this is that in some states the compensation of executors and trustees under will is fixed by statute and that in other states tradition and custom as to compensation have acquired almost the force of a statute. It is mostly in cases of co-executorship or cotrusteeship and in cases of estates requiring unusual or extraordinary service that either the executor's or the trustee's compensation becomes a topic of serious discussion or arrangement among the testator, the trustman, and the draftsman. 3 With respect to a settlor, the situation usually is just the opposite. 

Out of a group of 256 living trust agreements, only 14, or one in 18, were silent as to the trustee's compensation. Since a trust agreement is a contract negotiated by the settlor and the trustee, the trustee's compensation naturally would be one of the topics of discussion and arrangement.

Unless the testator or settlor is a lawyer, or is connected with a trust institution, or has served as an executor or trustee, or has been the beneficiary of an estate or trust, he will have had little occasion to consider the factors which should be taken into consideration in determining an executor's or trustee's compensation. 

2 Stephenson, Gilbert T., Compensation Provisions of Wills and Trust Agreements, p. 3; The Graduate School of Banking, American. Bankers Association, New York, 1941. 

3 Ibid., p. 4.

In the opinion of these 21 trustmen, the average testator or settlor, when he is faced with the issue, is disposed to give special weight to these four factors: (1) value of service, (2) comparative rates, (3) standard rates, and (4) quality of service. Only if it is called to his attention will he consider a fifth factor- namely, provision for future adjustment of compensation. 

Value of Service

With respect to compensation, the basic question in the mind of the testator or settlor is, Will the service be worth what it will cost? If the settlor himself is the life beneficiary, he considers whether the saving to him in time, worry, and money and the probability of better management of his property will be worth more to him than the trustee's compensation. If the beneficiary is a member of his family, the settlor or testator considers the value in terms of personal service as well as of business or financial service to his beneficiary. He considers and gives much weight to the fact that in the trust institution there is a group of persons, who are solid, responsible, and experienced, to act as advisers and guides for his family. .

What a person pays for trust service should be regarded, not as a dead, out-of-pocket expense, but as an investment upon which he will receive returns in terms of valuable service to himself or his beneficiaries.

Comparative Rates 

In arriving at what he regards as proper compensation for trust service a testator or settlor frequently makes one or more comparisons. He sometimes estimates what it has been costing him in his own time and in the employment of clerical and other assistants to manage the property or to supervise the investments he is thinking of putting into a trust account. If he has been employing the services of outside agents—such as rental agents or investment counselors - he may compare their rates with those of the trust institution. If he or his family has had experience with other trust institutions or with individual fiduciaries, he may compare their rates with those of the trust institution with which he is dealing.

Standard Rates 

A testator or settlor is influenced greatly by standard or customary rates for trust service. If there are published rates, he is inclined to accept them as being proper, reserving for himself the question as to whether he can afford the service. 

The average testator or settlor is keenly conscious of his own lack of information on the subject. He casts around for a yardstick. 

The two yardsticks that first occur to him are comparative rates and standard rates. If he feels that the trust institution is offering him the service for substantially the same rates as the rates for which he can obtain somewhat similar service otherwise or elsewhere, he is satisfied. If he is assured that he is being charged the standard or customary rates for the service he is all the better satisfied.

Quality of Service 

Among trustmen the prevailing opinion is that as time passes there is less and less disposition on the part of people generally to shop around for rates. People are more influenced by the reputation of the institution and by the quality of the service than by preferential rates. The following expressions are from trustmen in widely separated states:

California. As between competitive fees, I can say from thirty years of experience that more trustors make their selection on the basis of quality of service, with the price a secondary and not deciding consideration, than upon the basis of price alone. 

Delaware. From observing the results when an institution for nearly 15 years charged higher rates than its competitors, I believe that settlors or testators quite definitely take into consideration the quality of trust service that they have reason to expect they will receive when they select a given trust institution. 

Future Adjustments 

Most testators and settlors appreciate the practical difficulty of presently fixing rates for future service. When the matter is called to their attention, usually they are willing to leave the compensation open, to some degree at any rate, for adjustment from time to time. 

For example, there appears to be an increasing number of trust

instruments containing a general compensation provision, in sub stance as follows: 

As compensation for its services the trustee shall receive the usual compensation according to the uniform schedules applied by the trustee at the time such compensation becomes payable and, in the absence of uniform schedules at that time, the trustee shall receive the usual compensation then received for such service. 

The following statement apparently reflects the prevailing opinion among trustmen:

Illinois. The average settlor or testator knows that there are standard rates which responsible trust companies in the community are charging, and he usually is willing to pay the current charges, with the understanding that the company is to receive reasonable compensation at all times and that future conditions may justify a change, and as to this he is willing to rely upon the integrity of the company. 

BENEFICIARIES 

The beneficiary often has a great deal to do, both before and after the opening of the trust account, with the determination of compensation.

Before the opening of the account the beneficiary may come upon the scene in either of these two ways: (1) A husband-and-father, for example, is thinking of making his will and naming a trust institution his executor and trustee. He talks over the matter with his wife or with his grown sons and daughters or with all of them together in a family conference. The first question of these prospective beneficiaries is, What will it cost? Or (2) the husband-andfather thinks that he should create a living trust or an insurance trust for his wife and children. When he mentions the matter to them, again their first question is, What will it cost? If he is able to give them an intelligent, even though a somewhat general, answer and if they are satisfied with the answer, the estate plan is carried out and the trust institution receives the will nomination and the trust accounts. If, on the other hand, the answer is not satisfactory and if the prospective beneficiaries object to the arrangement because they regard it as too costly, the husband-and-father is likely to abandon the plan altogether. 

After the account has been opened the beneficiary may come upon the scene again in determining trust compensation. For example, the will or trust agreement is silent as to the trustee's compensation,

or it provides for "reasonable" compensation, or it stipulates an amount or percentage of the trust or estate. The trust institution enters upon the discharge of its duties and carries on for a number of years. In the course of time it finds that for the compensation it is receiving it is administering the account at a substantial loss. The trustee reaches the point where it feels that, in justice to itself, it should make some effort to have its compensation adjusted to current conditions. The beneficiaries may be the only persons with whom it can negotiate for such an adjustment. 

It becomes highly important, therefore, for a trust institution to know what factors beneficiaries take into consideration in arriving at what they regard as proper compensation. Before a trust account is opened, prospective beneficiaries usually take into consideration the same factors as testators and settlors do. After the account has been opened and after it becomes a matter of adjusting existing and perhaps long-standing rates, in the minds of present beneficiaries two other factors come into play, namely (1) the effect upon income and (2) the apportionment between principal and income. 

Effect upon Income 

Usually it is the income beneficiary, not the principal beneficiary, with whom the trustee negotiates an adjustment of compensation in existing accounts. Unless the adjustment involves a shift in the basis of compensation-as from income to principal - the principal beneficiary is not affected. But any increase of compensation payable out of income will mean a corresponding decrease in payments to the income beneficiary. When any such adjustment of compensation is suggested, he considers at once the effect of the proposed adjustment on his income. In his mind such questions as these arise: 

What has been the record of the trustee as to income produced? In the past how stable have the payments been? How have they compared with returns from private investments or from other trusts? 

How will the proposed adjustment affect future payments? Will there still be a normal return from the estate? If the answers to these questions are satisfactory, the beneficiary usually approves an upward adjustment; otherwise, he is disposed to hold the trustee to the original rates.

Apportionment between Income and Principal 

Among income beneficiaries as a group there is a growing feeling that, although the total compensation is reasonable, they are paying too large a portion of it. If the annual compensation is based upon and taken out of income and the only charge against principal is the one taken at the termination of the trust, many income beneficiaries feel that they are bearing more than their share of the total compensation. They think that the principal beneficiaries should share the annual charge. As one of the 21 trustmen says: 

Massachusetts. Beneficiaries of income have more and more questioned the propriety of charging all the expense of operating the trust to income. Through their criticism, there has been a definite trend toward the apportionment of the costs of trust administration between principal and income, and thus a lightening of the burden thrown on the life beneficiary. This all seems both logical from the point of view of the nature of the service actually rendered by the trustee, obviously in part for the benefit of those interested in the principal, as well as more proper because the parties interested in income are usually more related to the settlor or testator and should not bear the entire expense of the compensation of the trustee; but, on the contrary, should, perhaps, be definitely favored in the apportionment of those costs. 

Beneficiaries also give consideration to the fact that in the trust institution they find a sound, solid, experienced person or group of persons with whom they can discuss their personal problems connected with the estate or trust as well as their purely business and financial problems. The quality of personal service they have been receiving may be a very influential factor with beneficiaries in their attitude toward adjusting compensation in existing accounts. 

LAWYERS 

Lawyers frequently are called upon to decide and state what they regard as proper compensation for trust service. For example, a testator or settlor asks his attorney what it would cost him to have his estate settled or administered by a bank or trust company. When the compensation for trust service becomes a topic of discussion between the trust institution and the customer or beneficiary, the latter advises with his attorney about the matter. When the allowance to an executor or trustee is up for consideration by the court, usually the attorney representing the executor or trustee and presenting the account is expected to explain and justify the amount

requested. In all such cases the attorney's word goes a long way in determining trust compensation.

Lawyers seem to give special weight to the two factors, responsibility assumed and intangible value of service. But it must be understood that they also take into consideration other factors, particularly the work done, the amount involved, and the results achieved. 

Responsibility Assumed

Lawyers know the responsibility assumed by a trust institution in rendering trust service. They realize the wide range of a fiduciary's duties, the limitations upon its powers, and the weight of its responsibility. They understand the high standard of care, skill, diligence, loyalty, and fidelity to which it is held. They appreciate the significance of these often quoted words of the late Justice 

Cardozo: 

A trustee is held to something stricter than the morals of the market place. Not honesty alone, but the punctilio of an honor the most sensitive, is then the standard of behavior. Uncompromising rigidity has been the attitude of courts of equity when petitioned to undermine the rule of undivided loyalty by the "disintegrating erosion" of particular exceptions.4

They know that a fiduciary may be held liable for acting or for failing to act when its motive is above reproach, when it thinks it is serving and when, as a matter of fact, it is serving the best interests of the beneficiary. Consequently, lawyers give special weight to the element of responsibility in arriving at what they regard as proper compensation for trust service. 

One trustman interprets the lawyer's point of view as follows:

Minnesota. An attorney, being himself a professional man and familiar with the legal implications of trusteeship, will give greater weight than any of the others to the responsibility assumed by an executor or trustee. He will be inclined to think of fees in round figures as he does of his own. While trust companies may disavow the professional nature of their work and while attorneys may protest the practice of a profession by a corporation, an attorney will more readily see the imponderable factors in the service rendered by a corporate fiduciary which enter into a fair fee than will a settlor or beneficiary, and ordinarily will be the most satisfactory judge of the value of the services of a corporate trustee. After responsibility assumed, an attorney will consider work done and results achieved. It is seldom that he will attempt a cost accounting as the basis for a fee. His mind does not run so much in that direction as it does to round figures for responsibility assumed. 

4 Meinhard v. Salmon, 249 N. Y. 458, 164 N. E. 545, 546 (1928).

Intangible Value of Service 

Lawyers are disposed to measure compensation for trust service by the same yardstick they apply to their own professional service namely, the intangible value of the service. Another trustman expresses this thought in these words:

Massachusetts. Attorneys often have a professional approach toward the basis of charge for trust service. The amount of time devoted to the work is not the only basis of charge for the attorney, nor is there a fixed measure in dollars per hour for every individual; the age, reputation, experience, and capability of the individual lawyer, as well as the amount he actually receives for his services in other instances, all enter into the rate of charge. The third measure is often the success that attends his efforts. Attorneys, therefore, find it difficult to limit themselves to a fixed percentage of compensation based only on the amount of income, perhaps, which they receive and disburse, or the amount of principal which they distribute from time to time or on the termination of the trust. It would seem, therefore, that the attorneys have found it difficult to consider a fixed rule of thumb for charges and have carried over into their trusteeships, as fair bases for compensation, some of the measures which have been the basis of their charges for professional service. 

Among trustmen the prevailing opinion is that attorneys, drawing upon their own experience as fiduciaries and as attorneys for fiduciaries and upon their own philosophy of compensation for service, are especially helpful in determining trust compensation. 

COURTS 

Courts approach the determination of trust compensation from many angles. They make allowances to executors, administrators, trustees under will, trustees of court trusts, and guardians. They construe statutes, provisions of instruments, and agreements as to compensation. On an issue properly joined they decide what is proper compensation for given trust service. In fact, they frequently have the final word on the subject.

The courts of original jurisdiction - the probate courts, the surrogates' courts, and the local courts of equity-rather than the appellate courts have the most to do with determining trust compensation. Time and again appellate courts have said that they would not set aside the orders or decrees of the lower court fixing compensation unless there was a clear violation of the law or an abuse of discretion. In a recent case the Supreme Court of Kansas said:

the amount of compensation to be allowed is for determination by the trier of the facts, and the action of the trial court will not be set aside unless arbitrary or capricious, unsupported by substantial evidence, and unless the allowances made are clearly shown to be unreasonable or unlawful.5

At the 1940 session of the Mid-Winter Trust Conference Mayo Shattuck emphasized the finality for all practical purposes of the decision of the trial court by saying that he had come to consider as the most important rule of procedure in the everyday practice of trust administration the rule that the findings of fact of the court of first instance are presumed to be correct and, practically speaking, are final unless they can be proved to be plainly wrong. 

Consequently, it is of the utmost importance that trust institutions know what factors the appellate courts think the trial courts should take into consideration and what factors the trial courts say they do take into consideration in arriving at what they regard as proper compensation for trust service. Trust institutions should take special pains to prepare their requests for allowances thoroughly and competently so as to give the court of original jurisdiction information on the factors it takes into consideration. The 

Supreme Court of Arizona has said,

Although, according to the evidence, the trustees here arrived at their compensation by computing a certain percentage of the corpus, claiming that was the practice among trust companies, the court examined into the nature, items, and actual extent of the services rendered and rendered its conclusion thereon." 

Compensation and Commissions 

Courts emphasize the distinction between compensation and commissions. The compensation of an executor or trustee is an allowance, which is the amount that the court regards as proper and not a commission (a percentage of the income or principal, or both), which the fiduciary may claim as a matter of right. As early as 

1833 the Supreme Court of North Carolina said:

Whether it (the trustee's compensation) shall be given as a commission or not, is hardly worth disputing about; that may be a convenient mode of computing in most 

5 Achenbach v. Baker, 154 Kan. 252, 118 P. (2d) 584, 585 (1941). 

6 Shattuck, Mayo A., The Trustee's Duty to Inquire into the Acts of His Predecessor, Trust Bulletin, March 1940, p. 19; Trusts and Estates, February 1940, p. 159. "Dunlap's Estate, 38 Ariz. 525, 2 P. (2d) 1045, 1047 (1931).

cases; but the true object is a just allowance for time, labor, services and expenses, under all the circumstances 

In 1878 the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania declared: 

The compensation of a trustee, of any character, may be arrived at, as a matter of convenience, by the way of a percentage on the amount of receipts and disbursements. 

But, after all, on all authority, it is a question, not of percentage, but of compensation. Whilst a percentage is constantly spoken of and used because of its convenience, yet, it is compensation, nothing more or less, that is steadily kept in view.9 

As Mr. Shattuck says:

It seems important to bear in mind that in many jurisdictions the commission-like arrangements are merely measures of amount of compensation and should in no sense be considered as fixed percentages to which the trustee is entitled as a matter of right.10 

It might be well, then, if trustmen, remembering that courts use percentages of principal or income simply as convenient yardsticks, spoke of allowances and compensation rather than of charges and commissions.

The following excerpts from reported cases from the states indicated are typical expressions of both trial courts and appellate courts as to the factors they took into consideration. 

Usual Combination of Factors 

The following statements suggest the combination of factors. courts usually take into consideration:

Connecticut . . . "reasonable" means what is fair in view of the size of the estate, the responsibilities involved, the character of the work required, the special problems and difficulties met in doing the work, the results achieved, the knowledge, skill, and judgment required of and used by the executors, the manner and promptitude in which the estate has been settled, and the time and service required, and any other circumstances which may appear in the case and are relevant and material to this determination.11

Virginia.. The value of the estate, the character of the work, the difficulties encountered, and the results obtained must all be remembered in reaching a judgment.12 

8 Boyd v. Hawkins, 17 N. C. 329, 336 (1833). 

9 Montgomery's Appeal, 86 Pa. 230, 234-35 (1878). 

10 Shattuck Revision, sec. 91, p. 233. 

11 Hayward v. Plant, 98 Conn. 374, 119 Atl. 341, 345 (1923). 

12 Grandy v. Grandy, 177 Va. 601, 15 S. E. (2d) 66, 67 (1941), quoting Trotman v. Trotman, 148 Va. 860, 139 S. E. 490, 492 (1927).

Special Factors 

Among the special factors courts consider are the size of the estate, the responsibility assumed, the character of the work, the difficulties encountered, the results achieved, the skill displayed, the customary compensation, and the compensation in the contemplation of the parties. 

I. Size of estate. 

Massachusetts. The amount of property in his (the trustee's) charge, and the amount of income received from it, are to be taken into account in determining at what rate he shall be compensated for the care and thought and intelligence, as well as the manual service, which are given to the performance of his duties.¹ 13 Illinois. In arriving at an equitable conclusion as to the question of compensation, the amount fixed should bear a reasonable relation to the value of the trust estate.14 

2. Responsibility assumed.

New Jersey. In a letter to the attorneys on both sides, the Vice Chancellor said: "In my judgment both (the attorneys for the trustee and the trustee, Trust

Company) are entitled to compensation not only for the actual labor performed by them in the discharge of their duties but also for the responsibility which they assumed in this matter. This responsibility . involved . . . the practical management of the business.

It cannot be expected that responsible institutions will accept trusts of this nature (managing a manufacturing enterprise) and discharge them with fidelity unless reasonable compensation is made for their services."15 

Arizona. It might be possible to keep a record of the time and labor devoted to the management and control of the trust estate, and to approximate therefrom just and reasonable compensation, if those were the only elements to be considered. But there are the risk and responsibility mentioned by the text-writers and courts. These are not so easy of computation in figures. Like pain and suffering in damage cases, the amount allowed for them must of necessity be left largely to the sound discretion of the trier of the facts.16

It is interesting to note that some courts have made allowances at a fixed percentage for the responsibility involved in receiving and disbursing money. For example, in 1839 the Supreme Court of 

Pennsylvania, passing on an allowance to an executor, said: 

Pennsylvania. The responsibility which is incurred by the receipt and disbursement of money, is a legitimate subject of compensation, and an unvarying rate per cent, 13 Parker v. Hill, 185 Mass. 14, 69 N. E. 336, 337 (1904). 

14 Dunne v. Cooke, 197 Ill. App. 422, 443 (1916). 

15 Elfreth v. Allen, 106 N. J. Eq. 263, 150 Atl. 561, 562 (1930). 

16 Dunlap's Estate, 38 Ariz. 525, 2 P. (2d) 1045, 1047 (1931).

without regard to the magnitude of the sum, will always be a just measure of it, because the responsibility increases in proportion to the amount. It is consequently susceptible of a uniform measure, which we think may be reasonably put at two and a half per cent. Not so the compensation of trouble. The settlement of a very large estate may be the business of a few days, while that of a very small one may occupy as many years; and the compensation for all beyond the responsibility ought to be graduated to circumstances.17

In 1861 the same court, applying the same rule to a trusteeship, said:

Pennsylvania. The compensation allowed to trustees is determinable by no fixed rule, not because the responsibility arising from the receipt and disbursement of the money which passes through their hands is not susceptible of a uniform measure, but because the labour and skill required in executing the trust must vary with its subject-matter.¹ 18 

3. Character of work.

California. The services rendered by the trustees included cares and responsibilities far greater than the mere collection of the (oil) royalties. . . the nature and the amount of the services rendered seem to justify the allowance as made.19 

4. Difficulties encountered.

Alabama. The nature and character of the trust and trust property show that, in executing the trust, rather unusual trouble and annoyance were to be encountered.20 

District of Columbia. The court quoted with approval the auditor's report: "These collections and their disbursement form but a part of the service imposed upon these trustees and a much smaller part of their responsibilities. I have taken into consideration the magnitude of the principal, personal estate, and its shifty character . . ." 21 

5. Results achieved

Kentucky. The fact that the trustee made the property much more valuable than it was before and brought into the trust a large sum of money should be taken into consideration, for if the results of his operations had been otherwise, this fact would have been considered on behalf of the owners of the trust property to render the property productive was the first work he was to do as trustee, and the most important thing for the owners. This he had done, and this fact must be considered in fixing his compensation . 22

6. Skill displayed.

California. The duties of the trustee were onerous. They consisted of the overseeing of the (farm) properties, the keeping up of the buildings and fences, borrowing and advancing money for the payment of taxes and the support of the beneficiaries and other expenses, keeping accounts, procuring tenants, looking out for the grain when it was threshed, the placing of insurance, the building of roads, digging of wells, and other active duties incident to the proper management of an estate of this character.24 

7. Customary compensation. Courts are influenced by the customary compensation of fiduciaries and, in rare instances, of nonfiduciaries. a. Usual and customary compensation.

Missouri. The evidence is to the effect that the usual and customary charge or compensation paid to trustees for similar services is 5 per cent. upon the income of the trust distributed to the beneficiaries, and 5 per cent. upon the corpus, or principal, of the trust fund disbursed upon the termination of the trust.25 

b. Other types of fiduciaries. 

New York... where the deed or instrument creating a trust contains no provision on the subject of compensation to the trustee for his personal services in the execution of the trust and where there is no agreement on the subject for a different allowance, the trustee, upon the settlement of his accounts, will be allowed the same fixed compensation for his services, by way of commissions, as are allowed by law to executors and guardians, and to be computed in the same manner.26 c. Analogous service by non-fiduciaries. In the early days, before customary allowances to fiduciaries had been established, the courts sometimes went outside the field of fiduciaries altogether and made comparisons with the rates of non-fiduciaries for somewhat analogous service.
