Massachusetts. We know of no better rule to guide our discretion in this particular than the usage which exists among merchants, factors, and others, who undertake to manage the interests and concerns of others; and we think the highest rate, at which these services are usually paid for, ought to be allowed to (the trustee).27 

8. Contemplation of parties. The court is influenced by what it thinks was in the mind of the testator or settlor. This is illustrated by a recent case in Connecticut. In this case the trustee undertook to charge a portion of its annual compensation to principal. The court accepted the reasonableness of the compensation as a whole and the fairness of the proposed apportionment between principal and income. Nevertheless, it declined to allow the trustee to charge any of its compensation to principal because, it said, the trust had been "created in contemplation of the general rule hitherto prevailing and with an imputed intent and a justified expectation that trustees' compensation for ordinary administration, during the continuance of the trust, would be paid out of income, without diminution thereby of the principal fund." "128 Kentucky. The usual price paid for such services, or what was reasonably within the contemplation of the parties when the trust was created, may also be considered.29 

Professor Scott sums up the factors that courts take into consideration under six heads as follows: The amount of service rendered; the difficulty of the service rendered; the risks run by the trustee; the responsibilities imposed upon the trustee; the skill in administering the trust; and the success in administering the trust.* 

Mr. Shattuck groups them under these four heads: 

1. The amount of service rendered. 

2. The skill required. 

3. The risk and responsibility assumed. 

4. The success achieved.³¹

TRUST INSTITUTIONS 

The factors which trust institutions take into consideration in arriving at what they regard as proper compensation for their service are grouped under four heads: (1) work done, (2) responsibility assumed, (3) amount involved, and (4) results achieved. A fifth factor, which applies to future service, is adjustment of compensation to conditions as they exist at the time the service is rendered.

Work Done 

In arriving at what is proper compensation for trust service an essential factor to be taken into consideration by trust institutions is the cost of the work done. Cost accounting systems have been devised by the use of which trust institutions can tell with a reasonable degree of accuracy what it costs them to administer a trust account of a given size and character.32 A description or discussion of cost accounting systems does not come within the scope of this 

Study. 

In determining trust compensation the need for considering the cost of the work done would seem to be self-evident. A trust institution cannot continue as a business enterprise unless it receives as compensation for service rendered more than it pays out in expense incurred. It cannot know what is proper compensation for service unless it knows what it costs to render the service. 

Although the cost of the work done is an essential factor in determining trust compensation, it is not the only factor and in many cases it is not the controlling factor. The cost figure is at best only the starting point. It serves as a measure of the profit or loss of a trust account. It serves as the floor upon which the structure of proper trust compensation is built. 

Responsibility Assumed

The inadequacy of the factor, cost of work done, and the neces- 

32 Guide to Trust Fees with Recommended Cost Accounting System, Revised 1940, pp. 41-65; Trust Division, American Bankers Association, New York. (hereafter cited by the shorter form, Guide to Trust Fees).

sity of adding the factor, responsibility assumed, are illustrated as follows in the Guide to Trust Fees: 

A trust institution once held in escrow a formula for a profitable product internationally sold. This formula was contained in a small envelope. It is apparent that the cost of holding this envelope, even though under two separate double vault controls, was nominal. But suppose the envelope had been tampered with or disappeared. One can imagine the possible money liability to the trust institution, or if no money liability, the injury to its reputation.33

As a factor in determining trust compensation, the term responsibility is used in a broad sense. First, it means legal responsibility, which is the obligation to answer for an act done and to repair any injury caused by it. In this illustration, if through negligence the formula were lost or destroyed or even if its contents were divulged, the trust institution as escrow agent would be liable to repair the injury caused by such loss, destruction, or divulgement. 

With long and broad experience in trust administration, it is possible for a trust institution to devise a yardstick by which compensation for legal responsibility assumed may be measured in a given type of account, but the same yardstick cannot be applied to all types of trust accounts. That is to say, the law of averages may apply to the legal responsibility involved in handling each type of account. But the amount of compensation or the method of calculating proper compensation for responsibility assumed does not come within the scope of this Study. Suffice it to say that it is a calculable amount.

A second, less tangible or calculable type of responsibility assumed may be called moral responsibility. A trust institution has a moral responsibility both to its stockholders and to its trust customers and beneficiaries. In the illustration, suppose it became known that the formula had been lost or destroyed or that the secret had been divulged. The trust institution would suffer in reputation and in future patronage fully as much as it would suffer in direct damage. 

The trust institution is morally responsible to its stockholders to save them from such indirect, as well as direct, losses. Again, when a person places his estate in the care and management of a trust institution, he holds it morally responsible for rendering good personal service, as well as good business or financial service to his 

33 Ibid., p. 8.

beneficiaries. In considering how much weight should be given to the factor, responsibility assumed, in determining trust compensation, trust institutions take into account not only their legal responsibility to beneficiaries and third parties but also their moral responsibility to stockholders and to trust customers and beneficiaries. 

Amount Involved 

The amount involved that is, the size of the estate or trust has a direct bearing upon the two factors, work done and responsibility assumed. As a rule, the cost of work done does not increase in direct ratio to the volume of the trust property. Usually it does not cost a trust institution so much for $1,000 handled to administer a $100,000 trust account as it does a $25,000 account. On the contrary, the legal responsibility involved increases in direct ratio to the volume of property. A $100,000 account usually imposes upon a trustee more and different duties than a $25,000 account does, and each new duty involves corresponding responsibility. Thus by giving due consideration to the amount involved, the trust institution is able intelligently to adjust the work-done factor downward and the responsibility-assumed factor upward. 

Results Achieved

The factor, results achieved, means much more than a statistical record of material accomplishment. It means more than accepting a $100,000 account and, by prudent investment and management, making the principal show a substantial increase and the income a satisfactory yield. In the determination of trust compensation the material results are a part of but they are only a part of the true valuation and appraisal of the trust service.

To tangible, calculable achievement must be added wholly intangible, incalculable achievement which, after all, is the real value of trust service. One of the intangible elements to be considered is the value to a settlor or testator in terms of his relief from anxiety, his satisfaction over the provision he has made for his family, and his consciousness that he has done the best he knew. Another element is the value to a beneficiary (wife, mother, daughter, or son) in having some one to care for and manage property and make

it productive, to provide funds for living expenses, school expenses and other necessities or comforts of life, and to give sympathetic, tactful, and understanding advice on personal matters connected with the estate or trust. Still another intangible element is the value to the community measured in terms of having an institution that (1) brings together and organizes a group of specialists-accountants, investment men, tax men, real property managers — and a group of men of affairs—the officers and directors and (2) integrates the specialized knowledge and skill of the first group and the composite judgment of the second and makes this knowledge, skill, and judgment available to people of modest as well as large means. 

Čertain business enterprises are engaged largely in rendering service that calls for the employment of specialized knowledge and skill and the exercise of judgment based upon training and experience. In this respect they parallel the professions. In valuing and appraising the service of such businesses many of the factors of professions apply. To this special group of business enterprises trust institutions belong. Accordingly, after they have taken into account the work done, the responsibility assumed, the amount involved, and the tangible results achieved, they take into account also the intangible value of their service.

Adjustment to Existing Conditions 

In determining their compensation trust institutions have to deal with two fundamentally different situations. The first is where the service already has been rendered; the second is where it still is to be rendered.

A typical illustration of the first situation is that of a trust institution, having settled an estate as executor, requesting the court for an allowance for service already rendered. It presents facts and figures showing the work it has done, the responsibility it has assumed, the amount that has been involved, and the tangible results it has achieved. Thus the court is furnished data to guide it in making the allowance. An illustration of the second situation is that of a trust institution accepting a living trust and endeavoring to determine before the service is rendered what its compensation should be.

Although in both situations the same four factors are to be taken into consideration, it is impossible in the latter case to tell beforehand what work will be done, what responsibility will be assumed, what amount will be involved, or what results will be achieved. 

With respect to the present determination of compensation for future trust service a fifth factor is taken into considerationnamely, provision for adjustment of compensation to conditions existing at the time the service is rendered. Such adjustment of compensation to existing conditions is possible (1) when the instrument is silent as to the fiduciary's compensation, or (2) when it provides that the fiduciary shall receive "fair," "reasonable," or "customary" compensation, or words to this effect, or (3) when it provides that the fiduciary shall be compensated according to the uniform or customary rates in force at the time such compensation becomes payable. But when the amount or rate of compensation is fixed in the instrument, without any provision for variation, adjustment becomes much more difficult. Trust institutions, taking into consideration this need for adjustment as a fifth factor, endeavor to have some provision for flexibility and adjustment made in trust instruments, to the end that rates may be adjusted to conditions as they exist when the service is rendered. 

CONCLUSIONS 

It is natural and proper that each of these five groups (settlors and testators, beneficiaries, lawyers, courts, and trust institutions) should see compensation for trust service from its own point of view. Yet when all the different factors that the five groups take into consideration are brought together, they do not, necessarily, clash with one another. One of the distinct impressions that a student gets is that all five of the groups sincerely desire that those who serve in fiduciary capacities shall receive proper compensation. If recent studies as to the attitude of people toward trust compensation reveal any one set of facts more clearly than another, it is that testators and settlors are willing to pay, that beneficiaries are willing to have paid or allowed, that courts are willing to allow, and that lawyers are willing to recommend fair and reasonable compensation for trust service. But sometimes in a given case they do not know

what is fair or reasonable because they do not possess all the essential facts.

Trust institutions are in an especially advantageous position to furnish the essential information about the basic factors of trust compensation. For nearly a quarter of a century they have been evolving a science of trust compensation. Two committees of the 

Trust Division of the American Bankers Association have taken the lead. Since 1918 the Committee on Costs and Charges (originally the Committee on Standardization of Forms and Charges) has been developing a workable cost-accounting system for trust institutions and has suggested schedules of compensation for the guidance of trust institutions in working out their own schedules.34 These schedules, together with suggested uniform schedules worked out by trust. institutions for their own guidance, are proving to be especially helpful in determining fair and reasonable compensation for normal trust service. Since 1936 the Committee on Trust Policies has centered its attention upon economies in trust administration and upon the bases and sources of trust compensation.35 

Trust compensation is not and perhaps never can be an exact science because there are so many variable and unpredictable elements. Nevertheless, progress is being made. To an increasing degree trust institutions are prepared to take all essential factors into consideration in valuing and appraising trust service. More and · more they are able to explain their charges and requested allowances on the basis of these factors. Because trust institutions are taking the lead in this field, they will share largely in the opportunity and the responsibility of determining what is proper compensation for trust service.

SECOND SERIES, NUMBER EIGHT 

December 

Wartime Provisions of 

Trust Instruments by 

The Graduate School of Banking 

American Bankers Association 1942 

SUPPLEMENT TO THE TRUST BULLETIN 

Published by 

The Trust Division AMERICAN BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

22 East 40 Street New York, N. Y.

HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 8  

HE war is causing draftsmen to incorporate in trust instruments many new provisions designed to cover special wartime situations. Also, the war is bringing into play many old provisions originally designed to cover peacetime situations. Most of these new provisions will not appear in instruments drawn after the war; but, no doubt, some of them, having proved their usefulness, will continue in general use.

The term trust instruments is used, in a broad sense, to include agency agreements and wills without trusts as well as living trust agreements, insurance trust agreements, and wills with trusts. It does not include corporate trust indentures or corporate agency agreements. In a word, it includes all the instruments upon which the personal, as distinguished from the corporate, accounts of a trust institution are founded.

Among the old provisions being brought into play by the war are those which give the agent or trustee broad discretionary powers. 

Draftsmen report that, wherever it had been customary before the war to grant fiduciaries broad powers, they have not found it necessary to incorporate many new provisions to cover wartime situations. 

The advisability of granting broad powers has been discussed in two earlier Studies of this series-Should Trust Institutions Have Broad Discretionary Powers?¹ and How Trust Institutions Exercise Dis cretionary Powers. Another pre-war provision which is proving to be especially useful under wartime conditions is the one authorizing the trust institution to carry securities in the name of its nominee without disclosing the fiduciary relationship. 

1 Study Number 4, supra, pp. 67-87. 

2 Study Number 5, supra, pp. 89-110. 159

This Study is based upon material furnished by 61 trustmen representing 5 leading trust corporations of London, England; 5 trust companies of Montreal and Toronto, Canada; and 51 trust institutions in 34 cities in 23 states of the United States. 

English Trust Instruments 

The English trustmen report that the war is affecting trust instruments in the following ways: (1) that there is an increasing number of alternate nominations of executors (if the individual executor cannot serve, a trust corporation is named to serve in his stead); (2) that there is an increasing number of alternate residuary devisees and legatees (if the ones named first do not survive to take the devises or legacies, others are named, so as to reduce the danger of partial intestacy); (3) that provision is being made for the lapse of gifts to devisees and legatees who cannot be located within a stated time after the death of the testator or the end of the war; (4) that trusts for beneficiaries in an enemy or enemy-occupied country shall be administered as discretionary trusts;³ (5) that provision is being made that, if devised buildings are destroyed by air attacks, the insurance thereon or compensation therefor shall belong to the devisee; (6) that trust corporations are being given broader discretionary powers; (7) that the possibility of the simultaneous death of all the members of the family is being covered in wills; and (8) that the original and two or more copies of wills are being signed and witnessed and the original and each executed copy kept in different buildings. 

Canadian Trust Instruments

The Canadian trustmen say that the war is affecting trust instruments as follows: (1) that the control of foreign exchange is making it necessary for draftsmen to incorporate special provisions relating to foreign assets and foreign beneficiaries; (2) that, on account of the heavy and increasing income taxes, which now are being imposed upon annuities also, trustees are being authorized to use principal, in addition to the income, for immediate beneficiaries; (3) that trustees are being given broader investment powers; (4) that 

3 This term is used in a technical sense. See Scott on Trusts, sec. 155, p. 774.

trust companies are being named sole trustees for the duration of the war while individuals now in military service are being given the right to become co-trustees after their return from the war; (5) that provision is being made that distributees now residing in foreign jurisdictions shall receive only the income of their share until the end of the war or until the Foreign Exchange Control Board shall permit the transfer of principal to such persons; (6) that provision is being made that trust companies as co-executors or co-trustees shall have authority to carry on alone while individual co-executors or co-trustees are in military service; (7) that special provision is being made with respect to War Risk Insurance on trust property; (8) that members of the immediate family are being provided for partially through specific legacies or devises rather than wholly through the residue of the estate, or else by giving them preference should abatement become necessary; and (9) that special provision is being made for persons who may be incapacitated while in military service and for their families, including the families of persons married after entering the service.

Some of the foregoing provisions are peculiar to English and Canadian trust instruments; others of them appear also in American instruments and these are illustrated in later sections of this Study. 

American Trust Instruments 

The American trustmen, with the aid of their legal counsel, have supplied numerous wartime provisions which either have been taken from trust instruments drawn since the war began or have been prepared especially by counsel. It is thought that this collection constitutes a fair cross-section of wartime provisions as far as they have been developed to the present time (December 1942). 

The provisions which follow are presented as illustrations and not as models of draftsmanship. Different draftsmen are meeting similar wartime situations in various ways. This Study presents and illustrates some of the ways without undertaking to pass judgment upon the legal or practical effectiveness of any of the provisions. 4 As was brought out in still another Study of this series-Trust Institutions in Wartime *—the principal wartime problems of drafts- 

4 Study Number 6, supra, p. 111 at pp. 126-133.

manship are those caused by absent trustees or co-trustees, absent advisers and consultants, absent or missing principals, missing testators, and absent or missing beneficiaries. The term absent means absent in military service, and the term missing means officially reported missing while in military service. In the previous Study a few illustrative provisions concerning missing beneficiaries were given. This Study, pursuing the subject further and in the light of later developments, covers absent or missing executors, trustees, co-trustees, advisers, consultants, principals, testators, and settlors, as well as absent or missing beneficiaries. 

AGENCY AGREEMENTS 

The two main groups of wartime provisions of agency agreements are those relating (1) to the absent principal and (2) to the missing principal. 

Absent Principal

In the usual type of personal agency the agent makes the recommendation but the principal makes the decision. If the principal is absent, it may be impracticable or impossible for the agent to reach him with its recommendation or for him to reach the agent with his decision. Draftsmen are attempting to meet this situation (1) by making it as easy as possible for the principal and agent to communicate with each other, (2) by having the principal appoint an attorney in fact to act for him while he is absent, and (3) by having the principal authorize the agent itself to make the decision. 

1. Making communication between principal and agent easy. This is accomplished by authorizing the agent to act upon communications which in peacetime might not be regarded as sufficient evidence of its authority.

Ohio. The agent shall be protected in taking any action hereunder as may be directed in any letter, cable, telephone or telegraph or radiograph message, or other communication, written or oral, which it in good faith believes shall have been sent to it by me or upon my order or by or upon the order of my attorney in fact. 

5 Ibid., pp. 129-131.

2. Having principal appoint an attorney in fact. The principal appoints an attorney in fact to serve during his absence and gives him broad powers. 

Ohio. I hereby appoint......... as my attorney in fact and empower him to exercise each and every power and authority hereinbefore granted to my attorney in fact. I reserve the right by written notice delivered to the agent to revoke the appointment of said attorney in fact or of any successor to him and to appoint a successor attorney in fact by like written notice at any time or times while this agreement is in operation. The agent is authorized to rely upon and follow any direction or authorization of my attorney in fact as herein authorized unless and until I shall have revoked the appointment of such attorney in fact by a writing 

Idelivered to the agent. 

3. Authorizing agent to make its own decision. The agent is authorized not only to make its own decision when the principal is inaccessible but also to decide whether or not he is accessible. 

Ohio. If at any time in the reasonable judgment of the agent neither my attorney in fact nor I shall be accessible or if, in the agent's reasonable judgment, both of us shall be unable for any reason to give directions to the agent as aforesaid, I hereby authorize and empower the agent to make such sales, exchanges, leases, investments, and reinvestments and in general to take such action with regard to the exercise of voting and other rights in connection with the property held hereunder or any part thereof as it in its discretion shall deem wise and the agent shall not be limited in making investments or reinvestments to any classes of property prescribed by law or rule of court for the investment of fiduciary funds. 

It is my desire that my attorney in fact and the agent shall in general make investments and reinvestments in the types of securities and property initially deposited under this agreement but this expression of desire shall not be deemed to be mandatory upon them.

I am now in the armed forces of the United States and it is possible that circumstances may arise which will render it difficult for the agent to ascertain where I am located and whether I am still living, or, if living, able or competent to act in matters of business. Accordingly in any case in which the action of the agent hereunder depends upon its determination as to whether or not I am accessible, or competent or able to act, or living, it shall have a broad discretion in making such determination, without being under any duty to make any inquiry other than as it may deem advisable. The determination of the agent with respect to any such matter shall be deemed to be a proper determination, and the agent shall be under no liability or responsibility on account thereof or on account of any acts or things done or omitted to be done pursuant thereto, to any person whomsoever, so long as it shall have acted in good faith. 

Missing Principal

As pointed out in the earlier Study, Trust Institutions in Wartime, the most troublesome problem of draftsmanship connected 

6 Supra, pp. 122-123.

. 

with agency agreements relates to the continuance of the agency after the principal has been reported dead or missing and before his fate is known. The same problem arises in connection with the attorneyship in fact mentioned in the preceding section. If the principal is dead, does the agency continue in force? If he is not dead, is the agent liable for its failure to carry on during the period of uncertainty? If the principal appoints an attorney in fact to act for him during his absence and later the principal is reported dead or missing, does the attorneyship continue if the principal is, in fact, dead?

The following provisions are designed to meet these situations: 

1. Making acts of agent binding upon principal's estate. 

Minnesota. In case of the principal's death, this authority, as to all matters done after his death by the agent but without knowledge by it of his death, shall be as binding upon the principal's executors and administrators as upon the principal if living, and the principal hereby upon behalf of himself, his heirs, executors and assigns, releases the agent from all liability which it might otherwise have as the result of acts done in good faith under this power, but after his death. 

2. Declaring powers of agent to be testamentary.

Pennsylvania. Inasmuch as I am about to go into military service and question may hereafter be raised as to the effectiveness of this power of attorney, depending on whether I am then living, I hereby direct that this instrument shall be irrevocable except by another instrument under my hand and seal, duly acknowledged and recorded, and that in the event that I shall be dead when any act is done by my attorney, the powers herein given shall be regarded as testamentary powers given to my attorney as trustee, and when my death is ascertained this instrument, unless revoked as aforesaid, may be read into any will that I may leave, whether prior or subsequent to this instrument, thereby giving full force and effect to a contractual obligation by me with those dealing with my attorney in fact whether I be living or dead. Nothing herein contained to relieve my said attorney from a proper accounting to me or my estate as the case may be but persons dealing with my said attorney shall be under no duty to see that such is done. 

3. Statutory provision for survival of agent's powers. On account of the difference of opinion among draftsmen and the lingering doubt in their minds as to whether even a specific provision in the agency agreement or the letter of attorney will cause the agency or attorneyship in fact to survive the death of the principal, some draftsmen are urging the enactment of a statute on the point. The state of Mississippi has had such a statute since 1880, the validity of which has been accepted without challenge in the courts. The pertinent sections of the Mississippi statute are as follows:

The death of the principal shall not operate as a revocation of an agency created by him, as to one who, without notice of such death, in good faith and under circumstances repelling the imputation to him of fraud or negligence, deals with such agent, believing upon good reason that he is still such; and this shall apply whether the agency were created by writing or not. 

Until revoked in writing or by death, letters of attorney duly recorded shall continue in force as to all persons who may deal with the attorney in good faith without notice."

WILLS WITHOUT TRUSTS 

The wartime provisions of wills without trusts relate mainly to executors and co-executors in military service. Most of the provisions relating to the distributees of estates apply equally to beneficiaries of trusts, and some of them are discussed under later sections. For many possible or even probable wartime situations no appropriate provisions for wills without trusts have, as yet, been suggested. 

Absent Executor

The usual way of covering the possibility that the individual who has been named executor may be in military service when the testator dies is by naming an alternate executor. 

Bank and 

I name my son, executor of my will; but if he shall be in military service at the time of my death, I name, in his stead, the 

Trust Company of and clothe it with all the duties, powers, and discretions herein conferred upon my son 

Absent Co-Executor

The usual way of covering the possibility that the individual who has been named co-executor may be in military service is by providing that in such a case the remaining co-executor shall become, in effect, sole executor. In some cases it is provided also that, if the person in military service should return after the will has been proved but before the estate has been settled, he may assume his duties as co-executor. 

Connecticut. I appoint my said son, and The 

Bank of to be executors of this will. 

If my said son should be in the military service of the United States at the time of my death, direct that said 

7 Mississippi Code, 1930, Vol. 1, Chap. 56, secs. 2952 and 2953.

The 

Bank of shall act as sole executor; provided, however, that, if my said son should be discharged from such service before the administration of my estate is completed, he shall have the right, if he so desires, to be appointed and to qualify as co-executor and to act as such with respect to the remainder of the administration of my estate. 

Missing Testator 

So far no provision has been found which is designed to accelerate the probate of the will of a testator reported dead or missing.

It is problematical whether such a provision would be effective. 

Unless proof of death satisfactory to the court is received, it seems as though the will might not be admissible to probate until the lapse of the period (usually seven years) which raises the presumption of death.

Absent or Missing Claimant or Distributee Nor has any provision been found which is designed to shorten the period within which persons in military service may make claims against an estate either as creditors or as distributees. The 

Soldiers' and Sailors' Civil Relief Acts provides, in effect, that no statute of limitation shall run against a person in military service. 

If such a person, who is a beneficiary or creditor of an estate, has been reported dead or missing, the executor or administrator may be delayed in closing the estate; that is, unless the distributees of the estate are able and willing to execute a bond of indemnity acceptable to the executor or administrator.

TRUST AGREEMENTS AND WILLS WITH TRUSTS 

Trust agreements and wills with trusts now being drawn contain provisions designed to cover situations created by settlors, trustees, co-trustees, consultants, advisers, and beneficiaries being absent in military service or reported dead or missing, and by beneficiaries in enemy or enemy-occupied countries. Provisions with respect to alienenemy beneficiaries, whether resident in this country or abroad, are to be found, for the most part, in statutes and governmental regulations rather than in trust instruments. 

Sec. 205.

Absent Settlor

Where the settlor provides that the trustee shall not make or change any investment without his direction or approval, if he is in military service, it may be a practical impossibility for him to give his trustee the required direction or approval. This situation is being met in the same three ways as in the case of an absent principalnamely, by making it as easy as possible for the settlor and trustee to communicate with each other, by the settlor's designating an attorney in fact to act for him during his absence, and by his authorizing the trustee to make its own decisions. A slight modification of the third way (which would apply equally to an agency) is by authorizing the trustee to make its own decisions upon the lapse of a stated time after notice had been mailed to the last known address of the settlor.

Illinois. So long as the settlor is living and legally competent all sales and investments shall be subject to his written approval. Failure to notify the trustee in writing of the disapproval of any proposed sale or investment within 10 days after notice thereof has been mailed to the settlor's last address known to the trustee shall constitute an approval thereof.

Provisions covering the situation where the settlor is also the immediate beneficiary are discussed and illustrated in later sections. 

Absent Trustee

Where an individual sole trustee is likely to be called for military service, he sometimes is authorized in the trust agreement to delegate his duties and powers to a substitute trustee named in the instrument. 

Delaware. The said or any of my said sons, after qualifying as a trustee of any trust hereunder, may delegate to the said 

Trust Company, or its successor, the exercise of any or all functions vested in him as such trustee during the whole or any part of any period during which he is engaged in war service or any period during which he is outside of the continental United States. Any functions delegated pursuant to the authority of this section shall be delegated by written power of attorney, signed before at least one witness, and such power of attorney may be acknowledged and recorded where my will is recorded. A power of attorney executed under the authority of this section may be made either before or after the commencement of the period to which such power relates, to become operative after the beginning of such period. Notwithstanding the revocation of any such power, any act done or instrument executed by the donee of the power shall be as valid and effectual in favor of any person dealing with such donce as if such power had remained unrevoked at the time when the

act was done or the instrument was executed, unless the person dealing with such donee had at that time actual notice of the revocation of such power. The provisions of this section of my will shall be controlling notwithstanding any rule of law or equity or any provisions of any statute of this state. 

Absent Co-Trustee

Although they are worded in many different ways, the main objective of all of the provisions with respect to absent co-trustees seems to be substantially the same—namely, to authorize the remaining co-trustee to carry on during the war as if it were sole trustee. 

The following are the principal ways that have been developed up to the present time for dealing with the problem of the absent cotrustee. 

1. Having the absent co-trustee preapprove in writing and agree to be bound by the acts of the remaining co-trustee. 

Missouri. I am, acting with you as co-trustee under various trusts created under the will of deceased. I am now in the Army Air Corps and as time goes on it may be difficult for you to receive prompt replies to communication addressed to me, or it may even be impossible to communicate with me at all. It, therefore, seems advisable to me that arrangements should be made so that the administration of these trusts may not be hampered because of delay in communicating with me, or because of your inability to do so. I, therefore, now provide and direct that during the period I shall be in the service of my country, you are to proceed with the administration of these trusts, acting as your best judgment and discretion dictate. In consideration of your doing so, I do hereby approve any and all acts and things done by you in the administration of these trusts as fully and effectively as if I had participated therein. If you find it possible to communicate with me from time to time, I should be pleased to have you keep me advised of your actions as trustees, but it is to be distinctly understood that your decision as to the advisability of communicating with me before taking action as trustees shall rest with you alone. 

I hereby ratify and approve in advance all actions which you may take from time to time in the future as trustees of these trusts. It is distinctly understood that the foregoing authorization shall remain in full force and effect only during the period I may be in the service of my country, and it may be further understood that I reserve the right to revoke the foregoing authorization by a written notice to you at any time.

This preapproval, given in a letter by the absent co-trustee to the remaining co-trustee, is not a part of the trust instrument. Nor is the settlor or beneficiary a party to the arrangement. 

2. Giving the individual co-trustee authority to delegate his duties and powers to the remaining co-trustee. 

New York. Any trustee acting hereunder is authorized to delegate, by revocable power of attorney in writing, to any other trustee hereunder any duty or power conferred upon him or it hereunder.

3. Declaring that the absent co-trustee shall be an inactive or passive trustee while in military service, with resumption of his duties upon his return, meanwhile casting all the duties and powers of administration upon the remaining co-trustee. 

New York. In case any trustee herein named or any trustee qualified and acting under this will shall enter the naval or military service of the United States of America (such trustee being sometimes hereinafter referred to as a passive trustee), I direct that during the period of such service, all powers, authority, and discretion hereby vested in my trustees shall be vested in and exercised exclusively by the trustee remaining in office other than the passive trustee; provided that during the period of such service, such passive trustee shall not be liable or accountable for the consequences of any action or omission of the trustee remaining in office; provided further that upon the termination of such service, such passive trustee shall resume the exercise of all powers, authority, and discretion vested in him by this will. 

4. Authorizing the individual co-trustee to become an inactive trustee and himself to name in his place an appointee trustee. 

Missouri. If any person upon becoming trustee of this trust or while a trustee of this trust shall be or become a member of the armed forces of the United States of America or any other country, then in such event the said trustee shall cause written notice of said fact to be delivered to his then co-trustee or co-trustees of this trust; and upon receipt of said notice the said trustee-member of the armed forces as aforesaid shall become an inactive trustee of this trust, and as such inactive trustee he shall be and he is hereby relieved from engaging in the business of administering this trust. Said inactive trustee may, however, in his discretion appoint an individual to the position of co-trustee of this trust, and each person serving hereunder by appointment by the inactive trustee shall be known as "appointee trustee." (Then follow provisions for a succession of appointee trustees and for the resumption of active duties by the original trustee.) 

5. Authorizing the individual co-trustee to suspend his duties and powers while he is in military service. 

California. The said is hereby authorized to suspend his powers and duties as co-trustee from time to time for such period or periods as he shall elect by filing with

Trust Company a written statement stating that he desires to suspend his powers and duties as such co-trustee for such period of time as he shall specify hereunder. Any third party dealing with the trust estate may rely conclusively upon the written statement of 

Trust Company as to the fact of suspension of the powers and duties of hereunder at any time. as co-trustee 6. Making the decision of the remaining co-trustee binding upon the absent co-trustee, with provision for conclusive presumption of notice to the absent co-trustee.

New Jersey.

In the event that both individual trustees, and are beyond the seas, the decision of the corporate trustee shall be binding on all the trustees. The consent and approval of any trustee to any act, action or actions proposed by one or more of the trustees shall be conclusively presumed to have been given, provided that notice of any proposed act, action or actions is sent by registered mail to the other trustee, or trustees, at his, or their, last known address within the United States of America, as shall be advised by each trustee to the others in writing, and provided that no answer thereto is received by the sender of such notice within fourteen (14) days of the date of mailing such notice, or notices. 

7. Authorizing the beneficiaries to name a substitute co-trustee. 

Pennsylvania. In the event that any one or more of the individual trustees shall be unable to act by reason of death or any other disqualification, the surviving and remaining trustee or trustees shall have and exercise all the powers herein conferred upon the trustees herein named unless the majority of the settlor's children and the descendants who are sui juris of any deceased child shall, by instrument in writing executed by the said majority of children or descendants, and delivered to the corporate trustee, appoint another person or persons to act as substituted trustee or trustees hereunder, in which case the surviving and remaining trustee or trustees and such substituted trustee or trustees shall have and exercise all such powers, provided that the total number of trustees shall never be more than four. 

8. Authorizing the remaining co-trustees to act as if they were sole trustees.

Pennsylvania. During the period when any of the individual trustees may be absent from in the armed services of the United States, the remaining trustees or trustee shall be authorized to administer the trusts which are herein established in the same manner as though they or it were herein named as the sole trustees or trustee. 

Absent Consultant or Adviser

The trust instrument may provide that, in case the consultant or adviser to the trustee is absent in military service, the trustee may proceed with the administration of the trust without the aid of the consultant or adviser. Or it may provide, the same as in the case in which the settlor himself is to be consulted, that the trustee may act alone upon the lapse of a stated number of days after a notice has been mailed to the last known address of the consultant or adviser. Since there is no basic difference between provisions for an absent consultant or adviser and those for an absent settlor or principal who must be consulted, no illustrative provisions under this section are given.

Statutory Provisions 

In the states which already have enacted (New Jersey, New York, Pennsylvania, and Rhode Island) or which hereafter enact statutes designed to cover situations created by absent fiduciaries, the foregoing provisions will be of less importance and many of them may not appear in instruments drawn after the legislation takes effect. 

Absent Beneficiary

While the beneficiary of a trust is absent in military service, it may be a practical impossibility for the trustee to get payments or distributions to him certainly, promptly, or regularly. In some cases the settlor himself is the immediate beneficiary; in other cases, he is a third person; but, in any case, the problems are similar and are being dealt with in the same way. This situation is being met in several ways of which the following are typical: 

1. Having the payments accumulate during the beneficiary's absence.

Georgia. The settlor is now in the service of the United States of America, and should he not be available to receive said income payments, due to the exigencies of the war or for any other reason, though he be alive, the trustee, during the period the settlor is unavailable shall accumulate the income and hold the same until called for by the settlor, whereupon all of the income from the trust, accumulated or otherwise, shall be paid to him. The trustee shall have the power and authority in its sole discretion to determine the question of whether or not the settlor is available. 

2. Authorizing the beneficiary to designate who is to receive payments during his absence. 

Illinois. Provided, however, that during the period that any beneficiary shall be in war service or in a service allied to war service (the interpretation of these terms by the trustee to be conclusive), such beneficiary shall have the right to direct the payment of any or all benefits accruing to him or her under this will to one or more persons related to him by blood, marriage, or adoption or to be applied in any manner for their benefit, and thereafter to revoke such appointment at any time. This power shall not be exhausted by one exercise thereof but shall be a continuing power for the period of such service. The trustee may continue to make payments under such power without liability, until it shall have received actual notice of a change of the circumstances which shall alter the benefits provided for such beneficiary.

3. Authorizing the trustee in its discretion to make payments to or application for the beneficiary's dependents.

California. In the absence of any such directions, and if the location of such settlor or beneficiary should be unknown to the trustee or should be such as to make it impossible for such settlor or beneficiary to receive the net income and/or principal of the trust estate to which he is then entitled under the provisions of this trust agreement, the trustee in its absolute discretion may pay to or apply for the benefit of any person or persons reasonably dependent upon such settlor or beneficiary for support and maintenance, such amounts from the net income and/or principal of the trust estate to which such settlor or beneficiary is then entitled under the provisions of this trust agreement, up to the whole thereof, as the trustee in its absolute discretion may deem necessary or advisable for the proper care, maintenance and support of such dependent person or persons. The said payments to or for the benefit of such dependent person or persons may continue, in the absence of contrary directions from such settlor or beneficiary, until the location of such settlor or beneficiary is known to the trustee and is such as to permit such settlor or beneficiary to receive the net income and/or principal of the trust estate to which he is then entitled under the provisions of this trust agreement, or until the death of such settlor or beneficiary, whichever event shall first occur. 

Connecticut. If shall hereafter marry, it is contemplated that he will give further instructions to the trustee in connection with providing for his wife, and any child or children he may have; but in the event of his being a member of the armed forces of the United States and located so that it is difficult or impossible to communicate with him, or he is reported missing, or his whereabouts is unknown, the trustee is authorized in its discretion to pay such sums as it may deem advisable from the principal or from the income of this fund from time to time to provide for the suitable and necessary support of said and any child or children he may have. 's wife

The foregoing provision shows that draftsmen in the United States, which was pointed out earlier in the Study as being the case in Canada, are taking cognizance of the fact that many persons are marrying on the eve of entering or soon after entering military service and may leave dependent families who should be provided for.

Missing Beneficiary 

When the beneficiary of a trust is reported dead or missing two questions arise: (1) What is to be done with the trust property until it is proved that he is dead or alive? and (2) What rights have the next successive beneficiaries to the trust property? In most cases the 

9 Supra, p. 161.

problems are substantially similar whether the immediate beneficiary is the settlor or some third person; and in some cases the same provision in the instrument covers both the missing settlor and the missing beneficiary. The following are some of the ways of dealing with this situation: 

1. Declaring that the immediate beneficiary is conclusively presumed to be alive until affirmative proof of his death has been received by the trustee.

Illinois. If any beneficiary who shall be in active service in the armed forces of the United States or any of its allies shall be reported missing, for the purpose of this (will-agreement), he shall conclusively be presumed to have survived until the trustee shall actually receive affirmative proof of his death and for the purpose of making distribution hereunder he shall be presumed to have died upon the date such proof is received by the trustee, and the trustee's decision in such case shall be conclusive and binding upon all beneficiaries hereunder and upon all persons claiming through such beneficiary as an heir at law or otherwise. 

North Carolina. Any beneficiary named or described herein who is now or may hereafter be in the armed service of the United States in time of war shall, for the purpose of calculating distributable income and making distribution as provided in this agreement, be deemed to be living until the trustee shall have actual knowledge of notice from the Government of the United States that such beneficiary is dead. 

2. Declaring that the beneficiary is presumed to be dead after the lapse of a stated period from the time he was reported dead or missing, provided he has not been heard from in the meantime. 

Connecticut. I direct that if my son enters the armed forces of the United States and shall be officially listed as missing for three years, any share which he would otherwise receive shall be distributed in the same manner as if he were, at the end of such time, known to be deceased.

North Carolina. If such beneficiary has been reported missing or if he or she cannot be located within six months after the end of the war, then upon the expiration of such period of six months the trustee shall thereafter distribute the income as if such person had been officially reported dead until such time as he or she shall prove to the satisfaction of the trustee that he or she is alive and entitled to such income, in which event the trustee shall resume payments to such beneficiary. 

Ohio. In recognition of the fact that my son, is now in active military service in the Army of the United States, and in recognition of the fact that my son, may be undertaking active training and duty in a branch of the armed forces of the United States, I authorize and direct my executor to presume the death of either of my said sons and to make distribution as provided in this my last will and testament, if in the course of the present world conflict the War or Navy Department of the United States has reported either of

my said sons killed, missing in action, wounded, captured, interned, missing, or otherwise lost or unaccounted for in the service of his country, provided neither I nor my daughter nor the wife of either of my said sons has heard directly and personally from the son about whom the report is made for a period of one year following such report, if at the expiration of said period I survive, or between the date of such report and the first anniversary of my death, should I die within the one year period prescribed.

Missing Settlor 

The following provisions are designed to cover payments and distributions if the settlor is reported dead or missing. 

1. Declaring that the settlor is conclusively presumed to be dead upon receipt by the trustee of official notice that he is dead or missing, with a provision to cover the situation should he later be found to be alive.

California. Should the United States Government or any department, agency, or official thereof, report that the settlor is dead, missing, or unaccounted for and such report comes to the knowledge of the trustee at the office where it is administering this trust, then upon receipt of such information by said trustee at such office, the trustee shall thereupon and thereafter hold, administer and/or distribute this trust as though the settlor were then deceased; and the trustee and all persons dealing with the trustee shall be fully protected and saved and kept harmless in accepting and relying upon such report.

Illinois. The settlor is about to enter the armed forces of the United States. Anything herein to the contrary notwithstanding, in the event the settlor is officially declared dead or missing at any time during the period of such service the trustee, without waiting for any period required by law before death may be presumed, shall thereafter hold and administer the trust estate and pay the net income therefrom and distribute the principal thereof in the same manner as herein provided for the payment of income and principal and the administration of the trust estate following the death of the settlor. In the event the settlor is officially located at any time thereafter, the trustee (except as hereinafter provided in the event the settlor is a prisoner of war) shall thereafter hold and administer the trust estate as herein provided for the administration of the trust estate during the lifetime of the settlor. 

2. Declaring that the settlor shall be conclusively presumed to be dead if not heard from within a stated time after he was reported dead or missing.

California. If at any time the settlor of this trust should be a member of the armed forces of the United States, and the trustee should receive satisfactory proof that the settlor has been reported as dead or missing by an authorized official of the United States Government, then upon receipt of such proof that the settlor has been

reported as dead, or upon the expiration of one year after the receipt of such proof that the settlor has been reported as dead, or upon the expiration of one year after the receipt of such proof that the settlor has been reported as missing, it shall be presumed by the trustee for the purpose of this trust that the settlor is deceased, and the trustee shall thereafter apply and distribute the income and principal of the trust estate in the manner and to the extent provided for in this trust agreement upon the death of the settlor, unless and until the trustee shall thereafter receive satisfactory proof that the settlor is still living, in which event the trustee shall resume payments of net income and/or principal to the settlor in accordance with the provisions of this trust agreement. 

Maryland. This trust shall terminate upon receipt of satisfactory proof by the trustee of the death of the settlor. The fact that the settlor is reported or recorded as missing by the United States War Department, by the United States Navy Departmnt, or by any other agency of the United States Government maintaining records of the personnel of any of the armed forces of the United States, shall not in itself be deemed evidence of the settlor's death. In the event the settlor is so reported or recorded as missing (as distinguished from killed or captured) then this trust shall continue for a period of two (2) years from the date of such official report or record, and no longer, unless before the expiration of said two (2) years, satisfactory proof of the death or survival of the settlor shall have been received by the trustee.

It should be noted that this instrument makes a distinction between a report of dead and one of missing. The lapse of two years after receipt of the official report of missing shall be accepted as satisfactory proof of death (in the absence of proof to the contrary). 

3. Authorizing the trustee to continue the trust and to make the payments and distributions in the usual way until an executor or administrator has been appointed to take over and settle the estate of the settlor. 

Connecticut. If said (the settlor) shall be a member of the armed forces of the United States and is reported missing, but there is not sufficient proof of death, so that the will of said is probated or administration taken out on his estate, the trustee shall continue to administer this trust in accordance with the terms hereof as if said were known to be alive, until such time as his will is probated or administration is taken out on his estate. 

Ohio. The trust hereby established shall continue in effect until such time as there shall have been furnished to the reasonable satisfaction of the trustee evidence of the appointment of an executor or administrator of my estate and thereupon the trust hereby established shall terminate and any portion of the trust estate in the trustee's possession at such time shall be paid over and distributed to the duly appointed executor or administrator of my estate to be treated and disposed of as a part thereof. In no event, however, shall the trust hereby established continue beyond the period prescribed for the vesting of estates by any law or laws of the 

State of Ohio relative to restraints on alienation. 1

4. Giving the trustee the right to terminate the trust and distribute the trust property.

Maryland. And full power is hereby conferred upon the trustee to terminate this trust at any time it believes such termination to be for the best interest of the settlor and/or his family, and upon such termination by the trustee the trust estate shall be transferred and delivered to the settlor or his legal representative. 

Settlor a Prisoner of War 

The draftsmen of the instrument from which the following provision is taken took cognizance of the fact that the settlor might become a prisoner of war and remain so for the duration of the war. 

The trustee is directed to pay the income to the beneficiary who would be entitled to the income if the settlor were dead. 

Illinois. The trustee is further directed in the event the settlor is at any time officially declared a prisoner of war to pay the net income to the beneficiary or beneficiaries who would be entitled thereto if the settlor were then deceased and to administer said trust in the same manner as if the settlor were deceased, provided, no part of the principal of said trust shall be paid to any such beneficiary, except such part of the principal as the trustee may be authorized to pay to any such beneficiary for the comfort, welfare, maintenance, and education of any such beneficiary. It is understood, however, that the settlor shall have no right to recover any net income paid during the period he was officially declared to be missing, deceased, or a prisoner of war, or to recover any part of the principal of the trust estate theretofore distributed as herein provided. 

Foreign Beneficiaries 

The term foreign beneficiaries includes (1) citizens of foreign countries who are resident in this country, (2) citizens of this country who are residents of foreign countries, and (3) citizens and residents of foreign countries. A distinction must be made between citizens of any one of the United Nations and of any one of those constituting the Axis powers. A distinction must be made also between residents of one of the United Nations and residents of one of the Axis nations. Alien-enemy beneficiaries do not come within the scope of this Study.

From the point of view of practical trust administration, the main problem of the draftsman is to provide that the foreign beneficiary actually will receive the benefits of the trust. The following are some of the provisions being made for foreign beneficiaries.

I. Authorizing the trustee to withhold payments and distributions until it is assured that they will reach the beneficiary. 

Delaware. Because of the unsettled conditions now existing in Europe, I direct that, if the trustee has cause to believe that payments of income or distributions of principal, in cash or in kind, intended for my mother or for my brothers or either of them, or for their issue living at the time of my death, would not reach them safely, I hereby authorize the trustee to withhold such income or principal for such periods of time not to exceed the lifetime of the last to die of those of my mother, my brothers, and the issue of my brothers, living at the time of my death, as the trustee shall deem advisable and, if the trustee deems it feasible, to apply such income and principal for the benefit of my mother or for the benefit of my said brothers or either of them, or their issue, in such manner as the trustee deems best until the trustee, in its absolute and uncontrolled discretion, deems it advisable to make payments or distributions thereof.

California. If at any time during the continuance of this trust it should be impossible for the trustee to distribute net income and/or principal of the trust estate to any beneficiary residing in a foreign country, as provided in this trust agreement, because of any restrictions or limitations imposed thereon by the laws of the United States or of the State of California, or by any orders, regulations, or rulings issued thereunder, then the trustee shall withhold the net income and/or principal of the trust estate to which such beneficiary is entitled, and continue to hold it in the trust, until the happening of any one of the following events, whichever shall first occur: (1) Until the trustee is able to make distribution to such beneficiary of the net income and/or principal of the trust estate so withheld; (2) until the death of such beneficiary; (3) until the date or the happening of the event upon which this trust shall in any event cease and terminate, as provided in this trust agreement. If the trustee should not be able to distribute the net income and/or principal of the trust estate so withheld to such beneficiary prior to the happening of either of the events designated in sections (2) and (3) above, then upon the happening of either of such two events, whichever shall first occur, the trustee shall distribute and deliver all net income and/or principal of the trust estate so withheld to or if he should be deceased then to the executor or administrator of the estate of the settlor, to be held, administered, and distributed as a part thereof. 

2. Authorizing the trustee to administer the trust as a discretionary trust.

Illinois. If in the judgment of my said trustee the full and free use and enjoyment of said benefits would be denied to said beneficiaries or any of them by the laws or acts of the foreign nation in which they reside, or if in the judgment of my said trustee the laws of such nation impose excessive taxes upon said property, or upon said beneficiaries in the event said property is paid over and delivered to them, or if such payment would be contrary to the laws of the United States of America, then and in any of such events, my trustee is authorized and instructed and empowered to retain said property in trust for said beneficiaries until said onerous conditions have been removed, and during such retention to accumulate the income arising from said trust estate, but with permission to pay over any of said income or principal or said trust to said beneficiaries in the proportions hereinabove set forth, from time to time as it may deem advisable, or use and apply the same for their use and benefit.

3. If the legatee is living in a foreign country, directing the executor to hold the legacy in trust until such time as distribution can be made.

California. If at the time of the distribution of my estate any legatee (including in that term, devisee) is living in any foreign country and it shall appear to the satisfaction of my executor and the court that due to political or other conditions existing there, such legatee would not have the substantial benefit or use or control of money or other property coming to him from my estate, I direct that distribution to any such person shall be withheld and the legacy held and disposed of as follows: The property shall be held in trust by my executor, hereby appointed trustee for the purpose, with all the powers of an owner as to management, control, and disposition. If not in the form of cash, the property shall be converted into cash as soon as practicable, and the proceeds deposited by the trustee in a demand or time deposit in a bank, the earnings, if any, on the fund to be added to the principal. When and if such legatee shall become a resident of the United States of America, or of some other country where the conditions above referred to do not exist, or when those conditions are removed, the fund shall be distributed to the legatee, and the trust shall cease. If the legatee shall decease prior to distribution of the fund to him, the fund shall be distributed to his legal heirs, their identity and their respective shares to be determined in accordance with the laws of succession of the State of California then in force relating to separate estate, but excluding those heirs who are residents of any country where the conditions above described exist. If there shall be no person qualified under the foregoing provisions to receive distribution of the fund, it shall go to my legal heirs, their identity and their respective shares to be determined as of the date of my death according to the laws of succession of the State of California then in force, excluding all persons disqualified under the above provisions of this paragraph. The determination by my executor or trustee of the facts which necessitate the withholding or the making of payments under the foregoing directions and as to the qualification of legatees to receive distribution, shall be conclusive upon all parties in interest, subject, while my estate is in probate, to the approval of the probate court. 

MISCELLANEOUS PROVISIONS 

Among other wartime provisions of trust instruments, illustrations of which space does not permit, are the following: Provisions relating to (1) the exoneration or indemnification of the trustee exercising broad powers designed to meet wartime situations; (2) the determination of the date of the official end of the war so far as this date affects distributions; (3) the limited endorsement on checks to payees other than the named beneficiary; (4) the payment of taxes (property and income) and insurance premiums (property and life); and (5) the reason for creating the agency or trust and giving the agent or trustee broad powers.

Trust Accounting 

The special attention of draftsmen and trustmen is called to the need for provisions about trust accountings under wartime situations. In many states the giving of legal notice to absent and inaccesssible beneficiaries for trust accountings may be a formal and expensive proceeding. By appropriate provisions in instruments these accountings may be made less expensive without impairing or jeopardizing the rights either of absent beneficiaries or of other parties at interest.

CONCLUSIONS 

At the present time the wartime provisions of trust instruments are in a process of evolution. Many new and hitherto unused provisions are being worked out and incorporated in trust instruments. 

As the war continues, still other situations will arise and these will be covered by provisions that have not yet been formulated. Because wartime provisions are in a formative state, this Study gives more than one illustrative provision to cover similar situations. In course of time draftsmen may approach some degree of uniformity as to the principal wartime provisions.

Experience already has shown that the grant of broad discretionary powers reduces the need for special wartime provisions. 

While the emergency legislation will do away largely with the need for special provision in the instrument for absent or missing fiduciaries, these statutes should not be relied upon to cover all the wartime situations.

The drafting of workable, elastic, farsighted wartime provisions of trust instruments is a distinct contribution to the morale of the persons in military service and to their families and dependents as well. Such provisions add to the economic and social stability of the country and by so doing they help to win the war. Thus the drafting of wartime provisions of trust instruments takes on a new significance. The draftsman of trust instruments is a definite participant and he may be an effective participant in the war effort. 

Atkinson, Thomas E., Soldiers' and Sailors' Wills, American Bar

Association Journal, November 1942, p. 753; Trusts and Estates, September 1942, p. 284.

Baldwin, Hanson W., Legal Effects of Military Service; Hanson W. 

Baldwin, New York, 1942. 

Griffin, Everett P., Drafting in Wartime, Trusts and Estates, September 1942, p. 249.

McGarey, Francis D., Interim Provisions for Dependents of Missing 

Persons, Trusts and Estates, September 1942, p. 281.

Montgomery, J. Seymour Jr., War's Effects on Estates and Trusts Suggested Will Clauses and Statutory Changes, Trusts and Estates, 

October 1942, p. 377.
