What and How a Trust Institution Should Tell People About Itself and Its Trust Business by 

GILBERT THOMAS STEPHENSON, A. M., LL.B. 

 The Graduate School of Banking 

American Bankers Association c+ 1943 

SUPPLEMENT TO THE TRUST BULLETIN 

Published by 

The Trust Division AMERICAN BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

22 East 40 Street New York, N. Y.

COPYRIGHT 1943 BY AMERICAN BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 9 WHAT AND HOW A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL PEOPLE ABOUT ITSELF AND ITS TRUST BUSINESS 

FOR OR á trust institution the problem, what it should tell people about itself and its trust business, is much more delicate than it is for most other business organizations. This is so because of the nature of trust business. All information about trust accounts or about the affairs of trust customers or beneficiaries is strictly confidential. Yet much information about the trust institution itself and about its trust business is affected with a public interest and, therefore, should be given willingly to the general public or to especially interested groups of people. It is not always easy to distinguish between information which is confidential and that which is not. This problem may be complicated still further by the fact that sometimes separate, apparently dissociated items of information, any one of which standing by itself would not be confidential, may be put together into an item of information that is highly confidential. 

Also the problems, how and when a trust institution should tell people about itself or its trust business, sometimes are difficult ones. 

In their effect the medium and the timing may be as important as the telling.

This is a study of what and how the individual trust institution should tell people about itself and its trust business and not a study of what or how trust institutions as a group should tell people about trust institutions or trust business in general. The research upon which it is based has been exploratory rather than directed 

¹ A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, Art. III, sec. 2. 

181

toward the establishment of a preconceived theory. It has been found that at the present time there is very little uniformity of policy or practice as to what or how a trust institution tells people about itself or its trust business. It is hoped that this Study may be the forerunner of a concert of effort by trust institutions to bring about some measure of uniformity on these two points. 

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL PEOPLE ABOUT ITSELF AND ITS TRUST BUSINESS A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions sets forth three things a trust institution should tell people about itself—namely, its financial responsibility, its staff and equipment, and the safeguards thrown around trust business." This brief statement is meant to be illustrative rather than exhaustive. It is obvious that a trust institution should tell people many other things about itself and its trust business. For example, it should tell them, among other things, about its background and experience as a trust institution, its services, its general policies, and its charges. What else it should tell people depends upon circumstances. 

Financial Responsibility 

3 As was brought out in a previous Study, assurance of financial responsibility is one of the things a person looks for in his executor, his trustee, or the guardian of the property of his children. Since this is so, a trust institution should give people full information about its own financial responsibility. Moreover, it should present this information in a form that makes it understandable by any intelligent person even though he is not a bookkeeper or accountant. 

Staff and Equipment 

A trust institution should tell people as much as they are interested in knowing about its staff and equipment. 

2 Art. VII, séc. I. 

3 Study Number 2, Why Trust Institutions Exist, supra, p. 23 at p. 33.

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 183 

1. Staff. The complete staff of a trust institution consists of its operative employees, its specialists, its administrative and its executive officers, and its committees of officers or directors or both. 

People generally are not interested in knowing in detail about the operative staff. Some of them are interested in knowing about its specialists the trust officers, investment men, tax men, real property and mortgage men, and other specialists engaged in the service activities of the trust department. Still more are interested in knowing about the officers and directors, particularly those who have direct or immediate responsibility for the trust business. 

It is the general practice of trust institutions to publish not only the names of their officers and directors, specifying the officers attached to the trust department, but also the professions and main business connections of their directors. An increasing, although still a comparatively small, number of trust institutions are publishing the names and connections of the members of their senior trust committees, particularly their trust investment committee. As was brought out also in the Study, Why Trust Institutions Exist, a person who needs an executor or trustee frequently wants, not the unsupported judgment of one person no matter how sound it ordinarily may be, but the group judgment of several persons, based on their varied knowledge and experience derived from many sources.

The publication of the names and connections of the trust investment committee should be particularly helpful in the development of living trust accounts and other types of accounts that become operative immediately. While the present members of a trust investment committee or other trust committee may not and probably will not be serving when the time comes for the settlement of a particular estate or the administration of a particular trust under will, nevertheless the present composition of the committee may be indicative of the type of people the trust institution habitually selects to exercise its group judgment. 

2. Equipment. The technical equipment and mechanical devices of a trust institution are matters of little interest or concern to the 

4 Ibid., p. 36.

general public. It even may be a mistake for a trust institution to volunteer and tell people in detail about the technique of its trust bookkeeping, accounting, or office management or try to explain to them the operation of its mechanical devices. To do so may tend to accentuate an impression already existing in the minds of some people that trust service by a corporation must, from the very fact that it is a corporation, be mechanical and impersonal. However, there are many people who are genuinely interested in seeing how the wheels of a trust department go round. To the extent that they are interested, a trust institution should show to them through personally conducted tours and explain to them orally its equipment, including such items as its physical layout; its bookkeeping, accounting, and filing systems; and its vault equipment and control. 

Safeguards Thrown around Trust Business

A trust institution provides many different kinds of safeguards for the property that is committed to its care and management. 

These safeguards have been classified under five heads as follows: statutory and regulatory, auditing and examining, insurance, physical, and management. Among the statutory and regulatory safeguards are minimum capital requirements, segregation of capital funds to protect trust property, special protection for uninvested trust funds, and the separation of trust property and trust business from other property and business. Auditing and examining safeguards include audits by both internal and outside auditors and examinations by directors and by supervisory authorities. Insurance safeguards include property, liability, and fidelity insurance of all appropriate kinds and amounts. Physical safeguards include guards, watchmen, and mechanical devices for the protection of real property; vault and storage places for tangible personal property; and vaults and protective methods (including joint control of two or more persons) of handling and accounting for intangible personal property. Management safeguards include the appropriate management of real property and of intangible personal property. 

A trust institution should tell people-the general public as well as its customers, beneficiaries, and prospective customers and their advisers-about these safeguards and, as opportunity offers, show people or explain to them how they work.

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 185 Background and Experience 

A bank or trust company should tell people about its background and experience as a trust institution. Its background and experience as a trust institution and as a bank may be quite different. 

These are some of the things people want to know: How long it has been a bank; how long it has been engaged in the trust business; in what kinds of trust business, if any, it has specialized; what special facilities it has for handling different kinds of property, investments, and tax work. Anything and everything that throws light upon its background and experience as a trust institution, which does not, directly or indirectly, divulge any confidential information about its trust accounts or the affairs of its trust customers or beneficiaries, is information which a trust institution in appropriate cases and under appropriate conditions should give people. 

Trust Services

A trust institution shold tell people about its trust services. 

There are, in all, at least 80 different types of trust services which the trust institutions of the United States are rendering." This list includes services to individuals, to business organizations, to public institutions, and to units of government. It includes services in settling estates, in administering trusts and guardianships, and in performing agencies. Perhaps not many trust institutions-except the largest ones-are rendering each of the 80 types of service; yet each of them-provided it has full trust authority-is authorized and, sooner or later, may be called upon to do so. For a trust institution not to tell people about or not to explain these services would be as shortsighted as it would be for a merchant not to display or tell people about his goods. This does not mean that a trust institution should try to inform all people as to all of these 80 services. 

Some of them are so highly specialized that the general public would not be interested in them. A trust institution on its own initiative should tell the different groups of people about the services in which these groups are especially interested. At the same time it should be ready to give every person who is interested full information about any or all of its services. 

5 Trust Business in Common Law Countries, pp. 552-557.

Trust Policies

The policies of a trust institution are the settled and definite courses of action adopted by it for the execution of its trust business. Certain of these policies are general; others are special. General policies are those that relate to the trust business or the trust department as a whole. Special policies are those that relate to specific phases or types of business or to specific activities of the trust department. People in general are not interested in these special policies. It may be inadvisable for a trust institution to say much about them. But people are interested in the general policies of a trust department, and their attitude toward a given trust institution is influenced greatly by its general policies. 

In September 1941 the Committee on Trust Policies of the Trust Division of the American Bankers Association suggested and the Executive Committee approved the suggestion that each trust institution take steps to determine definitely the policies of its trust department. The Committee on Trust Policies suggested the following 11 points on which a trust institution might wish to formulate and determine definite policies: (1) serving with individual co-executors and co-trustees; (2) dividing compensation with individual co-executors and co-trustees; (3) providing in the instrument itself for compensation; (4) reserving the right to decline appointments; (5) drafting trust instruments; (6) safekeeping wills; (7) calling attention to the importance of keeping wills up to date; (8) retaining original investments; (9) carrying on businesses; (10) retaining family lawyer and other professional representatives; and (11) self-dealing. While the Committee realized, 6 Trust Bulletin, January 1942, p. 5 at p. 6.

In the determination of its policy as to drafting trust instruments a trust institution would be governed by law or local procedure. See Joint Statement of Policies by American Bar Association and American Bankers Association Trust Division, Trust 

Bulletin, October 1941, p. 3. trust accounts 

8 While self-dealing - that is, dealing between a bank or trust company and its - is listed here as a point of policy, it should be understood that self-dealing, direct or indirect, is condemned on principle and prohibited by law and regulation. See A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, Article IV, sec. 2; 

Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 543, pp. 1722-1731; Scott on Trusts, secs. 170170.25, pp. 856-910, and sec. 206, pp. 1105-1107; and Regulation F-Trust Powers of National Banks, sec. 11.

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 187 

as it declared, that each trust institution itself must decide what it will do about publishing a statement of policies, it called attention to the fact that people who do business with a trust institution have an interest in all these points and wish to be informed of the trust department's policy regarding them. 

Trust Charges

A trust institution should tell people, as far as it is able to do so, about its charges for trust service. Many people who are receiving trust service want to know not only the amount of the charge but also how it is determined. People who are thinking of patronizing a trust institution want to know what the service will cost them or their estates or trusts. People—particularly lawyers, legislators, and leaders in the economic and financial life of the community-who are thinking of trust institutions in general as business organizations or as economic and financial institutions and considering whether or not they should be encouraged and supported naturally want to know about the charges and the value received for trust service. 

Most people (except lawyers engaged in probate and trust practice and trust customers and beneficiaries) do not know how a trust institution arrives at its charges for trust service. They do not know the factors that are taken into consideration in determining compensation for trust service-a topic which was discussed in detail in a previous Study, How Compensation for Trust Service is De termined. They do not know that in some cases the trust institution's compensation is fixed by statute; in other cases, by the court in its full discretion or by the court within certain limits fixed by statute; and in still other cases, by negotiation between the trust institution and the customer. They do not know that a large part of a trust institution's compensation is allowances made by the court or provided for by statute and not charges made by itself; that the allowances to and the charges of a trust institution are, in the main, similar in amount to the allowances to and charges made by an individual fiduciary; that a trust institution's whole compensation is the allowances, fees, and commissions it receives; that it keeps for itself none of the profits it may make out of profitable dealings with 

9 Number 8, supra, pp. 137-158.

the trust property but must account to the trust estate for all profits so made. These and many other things about trust charges are a closed book to people in general, many of whom would be glad to have trust service if they only were shown that they could afford it. 

A trust institution cannot tell a person now what the total charges or allowances should be for settling an estate or administering a trust at some uncertain time in the future, for there are so many variables and contingencies; and people, when they understand the situation, do not expect it to do so. But a trust institution can and should tell people, who wish to know, how its charges are arrived at; what factors it takes into consideration; what factors courts declare they take into consideration in making their allowances; what the statutes provide; what are the present customary or uniform or statutory charges for the different services. It can have minimum uniform charges for normal services and let people know that it adheres to these strictly and that for extra services it will expect reasonable additional compensation. In a word, a trust institution should be ready and willing to tell people whatever it can tell them about its charges that will be helpful to them in making up their minds whether or not they can afford the service. 

Trust Figures

A question much more difficult to answer than any of the foregoing is, what trust figures should a trust institution give people?

What figures should it give out either on its own initiative to the general public or upon request or inquiry to its customers and prospective customers and other interested parties such as lawyers, insurance men, and estate planners?

Because of the extreme difficulty at the present time of determining a sound policy as to giving out trust figures, more space in this Study is devoted to this than to any other single topic. It is hoped that the amount of space devoted to this topic will not give thè impression that trust figures are to be regarded as the most important thing a trust institution should tell people about itself or its trust business, for such is not the case. 

Should a trust institution tell people the total volume of its trust property or the total number of its trust accounts? Should it give people the breakdown of its trust accounts under such headings as 1

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 189 

estates, guardianships, trusts under will, living trusts (revocable and irrevocable stated separately), insurance trusts (funded and unfunded), charitable trusts, managing agencies, custodianships, corporate trusts, and corporate agencies, with the number of accounts and the volume of assets under each head? Should it give them the breakdown of its trust property under such heads as stocks (common and preferred), bonds (government and corporation), mortgages, real property, and miscellaneous assets (such as tangible personal assets and business interests)?

Such figures as these in the hands of an experienced person would be helpful in throwing light on such matters as the stage of development of the bank or trust company as a trust institution, the amount of emphasis it is placing on its trust business as a whole or upon certain types of accounts, and the experience it has had in the handling of different types of property. 

However, the difficulty with a trust institution's making figures of this character available to the general public is that at the present time there is little uniformity among trust institutions in their method of evaluating trust property for accounting purposes. Some trust institutions carry trust property at its cost or inventory value. 

Others carry it at some arbitrary control value-for example, real property at $1 per item, stock at par or $100 per share, or bonds at face value-regardless of current market value. Still others carry one part of their trust property at cost or inventory value and another part at some control value. Even in the same community trust institutions have different methods of carrying trust property on their books. So long as this lack of uniformity continues, the publication of comparable figures by these institutions would be confusing and misleading.

Many trust institutions, which otherwise would be favorable to publishing their trust figures, believe that, until there is more uniformity in the method of carrying trust property on their books and unless the method adopted substantially reflects actual values, it would be better not to publish them at all, but to have them ready and make them available to customers, beneficiaries, prospective customers and their representatives, and to other persons who have a genuine interest in such figures. In this way, they say, the trust institution would have an opportunity to explain its method of

carrying trust property and make the figures enlightening. With these trust institutions it is not a desire to withhold from people information they are entitled to have but to avoid the possibility of giving people figures which may confuse and mislead them. 

Further, it has been suggested that, in the light of this lack of uniformity in the method of evaluating trust property for accounting purposes, a trust institution which publishes its trust figures should explain briefly its own method of evaluation-for example, as to what types of trust accounts or trust property each it employs cost-or-inventory, par-or-face, or some other specified control value. 

Thus far, the discussion of trust figures has related wholly to the impersonal, material side of trust business-the number of trust accounts and the volume of trust property. It has been suggested that, from the point of view of the public, a much more interesting and helpful set of trust figures would be those that related to the personal, humanitarian, social side of trust business-the number and types of beneficiaries served and the characteristics of the service to each type.¹º Already a start in this direction has been made in some of the other common law countries;" and it is worthy of consideration by the trust institutions of this country. 

How A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL People about ITSELF AND ITS TRUST BUSINESS 

After a trust institution has decided what it should tell people about itself and its trust business, it must decide next how it should tell them—that is, what mediums it should employ. Different mediums are appropriate for the conveyance of different kinds of information. The principal mediums discussed in this Study are published statements of conditions of banks and trust companies, annual reports to stockholders, official pamphlets of trust departments, financial publications, and government reports. Among the common mediums are posters, window displays, billboards, motion pictures, radio, letters, leaflets, booklets, and newspaper advertise- 

10 See the author's article, A New Kind of Statement, Trusts and Estates, April 

1939, p. 486. 

11 Trust Business in Common Law Countries, p. 401.

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 191 

ments. A discussion of these mediums would lead one into the field of trust advertising which is beyond the scope of this Study. 

Statement of Condition 

The statement of condition of a bank or trust company, as called for by the federal or state authorities and as published in the newspapers and usually in leaflet or pamphlet form, contains much of the information, as discussed in the preceding sections, which a trust institution should tell people about itself and its trust business. 

The statement of condition always contains an enumeration under standard headings of the bank's or trust company's assets or resources and of its liabilities. This shows the bank's or trust company's financial responsibility as a corporation, but the statement ordinarily does not reveal anything about the trust property. 

The statement of condition as published in leaflet or pamphlet form and sometimes as it appears in the newspapers contains a list of the officers, usually indicating by name and title those attached to the trust department. It contains the names of the directors, usually with their professions and main business connections. It usually contains a general reference to the trust department - frequently limited, however, to a mere listing of the principal fiduciary capacities in which the bank or trust company serves. 

Nearly all statements of condition contain these four items of information - the assets and the deposit liabilities and other determinable liabilities of the corporation; the list and titles of officers; the list of directors and their connections; and the list of principal fiduciary capacities. In addition to these four, two other items of information are found in the statements of some trust institutions namely, the names and connections of the members of the trust committee and certain trust figures. 

1. Trust committee. Inclusion in the published statement of condition of the names and connections of the members of the trust committee or of the trust investment committee of the board of directors is a recent development. However, there seems now to be a growing tendency on the part of trust institutions to take this means of telling people the persons or the types of persons upon whom the institution relies for the exercise of group judgment in

connection with its trust business and particularly of its trust investments. One trust institution now carries the name of the members of its trust committee on its letterhead, opposite the names of its senior officers. 

2. Trust figures. A study of recent statements of condition of trust institutions shows that, while most of these statements contain no trust figures, some of them contain a wide variety of them. These figures range all the way from a statement of the grand total of trust property to a statement as detailed as that of the bank or trust company itself. Recent statements of condition of trust institutions contained one or more of the following types of trust figures: a. The amount of securities set aside and segregated to secure trust deposits. b. The total of personal trust assets. c. The totals of personal trust assets and of corporate trust assets. d. The totals of trust assets invested, of trust funds uninvested, of customers' securities, of corporate trusts, and of real estate trusts. e. The total dollar-volume of trust investments listed as an item on the asset side of the statement of condition and trust department or trust funds of the same dollar-amount listed as an item on the liability side. The listing of trust funds or trust investments in the statement of condition makes it appear as though trust property were a part of the assets or resources of the corporation which, of course, is not the case. f. A detailed statement of assets and liabilities of the trust department. The following are illustrations of such a statement, one from Massachusetts and the other from New York State. Massachusetts.

A statute of Massachusetts¹² requires a state-chartered bank or trust company to make to the Commissioner of Banks upon call a return containing, among other things, its trust funds and funds held for purposes of investment. The return must be in the form of a trial balance of its books and must specify the different kinds of its liabilities and assets, with the amount of each kind. While all of the trust institutions make these reports of the Commissioner as required, some of them publish a detailed statement of their trust department as illustrated above; others simply publish the total of their trust assets; and still others do not publish even their totals. 

As to the publication of trust figures in the statement of condition of the bank or trust company, there would, perhaps, be general agreement among trustmen on the following points: Trust property should not be included in the statement as resources or assets of the bank or trust company. When the totals of trust property are to be stated, the property in personal accounts and that in corporate accounts should be stated separately. Property held in custody and in safekeeping accounts, as to which the trust institution 

12 General Laws, ch. 172, sec. 26.

has no duties or responsibilities of management, should be stated separately from property in estate and in trust accounts. Unmatured life insurance policies held in trust should not be carried as trust assets except at some purely nominal control value. 

Annual Report to Stockholders 

In other English-speaking countries, more than in the United States, trust institutions use published annual reports to the stockholders as mediums for telling people about themselves and their trust business. Although these annual reports are addressed to stockholders and primarily are intended for them, nevertheless, when they are printed and mailed or handed to persons who are not stockholders, they reach and evidently are intended to reach the general public.

A recent annual report of an Australian trust institution is a pamphlet of 15 pages. It contains, among other things, a list of the officers and directors; the balance sheet; the profit and loss account of the company; a list of the fiduciary capacities in which the company may be appointed; a list of advantages of appointing the company in fiduciary capacities; and the scale of trust charges. 

A recent annual report of a South African trust institution is a 23-page pamphlet containing a list of officers and directors; a list of the trust capacities in which the company can serve; a list of the reasons why a person should make a will; the form of appointment of the company as executor and trustee; the balance sheet; the profit and loss account; a list of the advantages of naming the company in fiduciary capacities; and the schedules of charges. 

In this country, until recently, comparatively little use of annual reports has been made by trust institutions for telling people about themselves or their trust business. Even now most of the annual reports contain only brief, general references to the trust department. For example, one recent report of 3 pages devotes 5 lines to the trust department; another of 7 pages, 4 lines; and another of 

21 pages, 22 lines. By way of contrast, however, several of the reports for the year 1942 devote substantial space to detailed information about the trust department. One such report of 12.5 pages devotes 2.8 pages to the trust department; another of 23 pages, 14 pages; another of 7.5 pages, 3.25 pages; and one of 14 pages, 3.1

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 195 

pages. Compared with those of previous years, the reports for 1942 show a marked tendency on the part of the chairmen of the boards and the presidents of trust institutions to devote more space to the trust department and to give more detailed information about the trust business.

The annual reports that pay special attention to the trust business are of two general types. In one type the chairman or president makes a complete report of the bank or trust company as a whole, including the trust department. In the other type, he makes only a general reference to the trust department and leaves it to the head of the trust department to make the detailed report for the trust department.

Much of the trust information in these published reports, whether made by the chairman or president or by the head of the trust department, is of a general character. One such report raises and answers these general questions: (1) How is a trust formed? (2)

How is a trust evidenced? (3) After the trust instrument has been executed, what are the duties of the trustee? (4) Is a trustee entitled to compensation? (5) Is a trustee absolutely prohibited from dealing personally with the trust property? (6) Should the trustee make known to the beneficiary the manner in which he is handling the property? (7) Is a trustee liable for the acts of misconduct of his co-trustee? (8) In the management of a trust, what steps are first taken by a trustee? and is the trustee an insurer? Several of the reports of the year 1942 contain discussions of the prudent-man rule, of common trust funds, and of the propriety of increasing trust compensation by using principal as one of the bases and sources of compensation. However, information of this general character does not come within the scope of this Study. 

In the 1942 reports a trend is discernible toward incorporating or attaching specific, detailed information about the trust department and its trust business. Nearly all of these reports carry the year-end statement of condition of the corporation, the list and titles of the officers, the list and connections of the directors; and a considerable number of them carry the trust committee or trust investment committee. These reports also contain a wide variety of trust figures. 

1. Trust figures. The following is a composite list of trust figures taken from 16 recently published annual reports to stockholders for the year 1942:

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 197 

The annual report of one large trust institution gives the total trust assets at the year-end each 1932, 1941, and 1942, stating the percentage of increase during the 10-year period. The report of another trust institution contains a graph showing the increase or decrease of its trust assets, year by year, over the last 10-year period. 

While each of these 16 annual reports contained one or more of the foregoing figures, none of them contained all of them. Nor is this composite list a complete list of trust figures. For example, the breakdown of trust accounts is incomplete and there is, substantially, no breakdown of the trust property.

It must be apparent that some of the foregoing figures are of little or no interest, concern, or value to the stockholders, much less to the general public, while others convey highly important and significant information. On a given type of account one trust institution publishes detailed figures, while on the same type of account another publishes none at all. One institution, for example, publishes in a 24page pamphlet the report of the audit of one of its common trust funds, giving, among other things, a list of the investments; another trust institution treats its common trust fund with the same degree of confidentiality it applies to its private, personal trust accounts and publishes nothing at all. In view of this situation, would it not be advisable for trust institutions, working with one another or through their trust associations, (1) to decide among themselves what trust figures have real significance and (2) to adopt a consistent policy as to what figures to keep confidential and what figures to publish? 

Official Pamphlet of Trust Department 

The trust institutions of England, Scotland, Ireland, and of some other nations of the British Commonwealth use what has come to be known as an official pamphlet of their trust department as their principal medium for telling people about themselves and their trust business.13 

1. Contents of official pamphlet. An official pamphlet usually is made up of five parts as follows: (a) an introductory statement by 

18 For a detailed description and discussion of official pamphlets of trust departments, see the author's Suggestions for the Preparation and Use of the Official 

Pamphlet of the Trust Department, Trust Bulletin, January 1942, pp. 7-16.

14 the president or chairman of the board, (b) a statement of the historical background of the bank or trust company as a trust institution, (c) a list of the special qualifications of a trust institution," (d) a statement of the general policies of the institution with respect to its trust business,15 and (e) a statement as to its charges. In some official pamphlets there is a sixth part-suggested forms of appointment and grant of powers-possibly limited to a lawyers' edition. After the contents of the pamphlet have been passed upon and approved by counsel, the pamphlet as a whole is presented to, considered, approved, and ordered published by the board of directors. This is why it is called an official pamphlet. It should be issued in dated editions so that there never can be any doubt as to whether any statement in the pamphlet was or was not in force at a given time. 

2. Combination of annual report to stockholders and official pamphlet of trust department. In Australia and the Union of South 

Africa many trust institutions have made what is, in effect, a combination of their annual report to stockholders and the official pamphlet of their trust department. Each year, on the occasion of their annual meeting, they publish and distribute a rather elaborate pamphlet containing both the annual reports and the standard information about their trust department — such as its policies, its charges, and its preferred forms of appointment and grant of powers. The annual reports which keep the trust information up to date combined with the standing material which already has been approved by the board, serve, in effect, as annual editions of the official pamphlet. 

3. Combination of statement of condition and official pamphlet. 

A few American trust institutions are effecting a combination of their statement of condition and the official pamphlet of their trust department. On the inside back cover of the official pamphlet they are providing a pocket for the insertion of the current statement of condition. The statement of condition contains the latest financial statement, the list of officers, the names and connections of the directors, and, in some cases, of the members of the trust committee or trust 

14 See Study No. 2 of this series, Why Trust Institutions Exist, supra, pp. 30-42. 

15 See Trust Bulletin, January 1942, pp. 5-6.

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 199 

investment committee. Through such a combination a trust institution is enabled to convey to people authoritative and up-to-date information about itself and its trust business without the necessity of frequent revisions or new editions of its official pamphlet. 

Financial Publications 

In Canada financial publications are used by trust companies for telling people about themselves and their trust business. The Financial Post and The Monetary Times, both of Toronto, each publishes an annual issue or supplement devoted especially to trust companies. 

Practically all of the (about) 60 trust companies furnish for publication in these issues or supplements both narrative and statistical information about themselves and their trust business. They furnish such items of information as (1) the names of the officers and directors; (2) the capital account; (3) the total volume of trust assets for the current year and for each of from three to five years back; (4) the total assets of the company broken down into mortgages, collateral loans, and other investments; (5) the company funds and the guaranteed funds;16 (6) the net profits for the year; and (7) the dividend rate for the year.

The Australasian Insurance and Banking Record and Statistical 

Register publishes each December a summary of the balance sheets of the private trust institutions (as distinguished from the Public 

Trust Offices) of Australia and New Zealand. These summaries give only the total trust assets of each institution, with no breakdown either of type of trust accounts or kinds of trust property In this country, so far as known, there is no financial publication which carries regularly or periodically detailed trust information supplied by individual trust institutions. Some of the trust institutions which have common trust funds have been furnishing for publication certain figures. From time to time some trust institutions furnish other items of information about themselves or their trust business. But as yet there are no published figures of individual institutions comparable with those appearing in these Canadian and Australasian financial publications. 

16 Guaranteed funds means funds left in trust with the company and guaranteed by the company as to principal and a stipulated rate of interest. See Trust Business in 

Common Law Countries, pp. 274-278.

Government Reports 

In some countries trust institutions make available to the public information about themselves and their trust business indirectly through government reports.

The Canadian trust companies make an annual statistical report to governmental agencies, and these reports are published. The 

Dominion-incorporated trust companies report to the Dominion 

Superintendent of Insurance; trust companies registered in the

Province of Quebec, to the Superintendent of Insurance and Inspector of Trust Companies; and those doing business in Ontario, to the 

Registrar of Loan Corporations. 

The Public Trust Offices of England, Australia, New Zealand, and British India make elaborate reports, narrative and statistical, which go into great detail as to the year's activities of the office. 

These reports are published. 

In this country the published reports of the Comptroller of the Currency and the reports of some of the state banking departments contain figures of trust institutions as a group. The annual report of the Commissioner of Banks of Massachusetts contains figures on the trust department assets of the state banks and trust companies. These assets usually are classified under these heads: Government, state, and municipal bonds; other bonds; stocks; loans on real estate; other loans; real estate by foreclosure, etc.; real estate owned; deposits subject to check; other bank deposits; tangible personal property; and other assets. The trust department liabilities usually are classified under these three heads: as trustee, executor, administrator, etc.; income; and other liabilities.

While these government reports are public records and, therefore, are available to the public, it is doubtful that they have wide distribution among the general public.

CONCLUSIONS 

At the present time, in the absence of statute, regulation, or accepted policy, every trust institution must decide for itself what it will tell people about itself and its trust business and what, if any, difference it will make between what it tells on its own initiative to the general public and what it tells on inquiry or request to

WHAT A TRUST INSTITUTION SHOULD TELL 201 

its customers, beneficiaries, prospective customers, and their authorized representatives.

In reaching its decision as to what it will tell people about itself or its trust business, a trust institution must draw a sharp distinction between what and to whom it will tell about its trust accounts and what and to whom it will tell about itself or its trust business. It must, on the one hand, preserve inviolate the confidentiality of its trust accounts and the confidences of persons who deal or advise with it. It should, on the other hand, give people all the information about itself and its trust business that will enable them intelligently to decide for themselves, with such advice as they can obtain, whether or not they need its services and whether or not it can and will be likely to render them acceptably. A trust institution should, however, accompany the information it supplies with explanations and illustrations which, as far as possible, will keep it from being misinterpreted or misused.

A trust institution should tell people about its background and experience as a trust institution, its special qualifications to meet the needs of people for trust service, its general policies as a trust institution, and its charges for trust service. What else it should tell depends upon what else will be helpful to people in reaching the right decision about their need for trust service and their selection of a fiduciary.

When one comes to consider how a trust institution should tell people about itself and its trust business, one finds at the present time vast differences in policy and practice. In this country trust institutions have, at one time or another, employed many different mediums for telling people about themselves and their trust business but, as yet, have not made full use of any of the available mediums. 

In addition to and not in substitution for the prevailing customary ways of telling people about itself and its trust business-such as advertising and personal representation-a trust institution well may consider making greater use of its periodic published statement of condition, of its published annual report to stockholders, and of an official pamphlet of its trust department. The statement of condition is adapted to convey information as to the financial responsibility of the institution, and the officers, the directors, the members of the trust committee and the trust investment committee,

and their professions and main business connections. The annual report to the stockholders is adapted to make comparisons and to convey information about current developments of the trust business. 

The official pamphlet of the trust department is adapted to convey authoritative information as to the background and experience, special qualifications, general policies, charges and (possibly limited to a lawyers' edition) its approved forms of appointment and grant of powers.

A combination of the annual report and the official pamphlet, as in Australia and the Union of South Africa, or a combination of the statement of condition and the official pamphlet, as in some of the American trust institutions, might be even more effective than these three publications, received and read separately. 

The further growth and development of trust business depend, in large measure, upon the policies of trust institutions as to telling people about themselves and their trust business. If they confide in people, they will win their confidence and support; if they do not, they may risk arousing their suspicion. The more authoritative and understandable, and yet non-confidential, information people have about trust institutions and their trust business, the more readily and eagerly they will turn to them for trust service.
