HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 10 READJUSTING COMPENSATION IN TRUST ACCOUNTS 

TH HE present earnings of trust departments are far from satisfactory. 

This is an admitted and well-known fact.¹ The only way of remedying this situation promptly is by readjusting compensation in existing accounts. Reduction in costs will help some but not enough by itself to put trust business on a paying basis. Provision for adequate compensation in the future, essential as it is, does not increase present earnings.

This is a study of readjustment, not of adjustment, of compensation. It assumes that, when the account was opened, there was a satisfactory arrangement as to compensation, which since then has become unsatisfactory and now needs to be readjusted to current conditions.

Previous Research Studies 

The factual material for this Study has been supplied by three trust research studies. The first was made by Robert E. MacDougall of the Provident Trust Company of Philadelphia while he was a student majoring in Trusts in The Graduate School of Banking of the American Bankers Association. In the course of his research, Mr. MacDougall submitted to 112 trust institutions representing every section of the country a questionnaire on the adjustment of commissions on existing accounts. A summary of his findings was 

1 Theis, Henry A., Cost Analysis and Fees, Trust Bulletin, September 1940, p. 20; 

Wilson, Robert A., Pennsylvania's Third Trust-Cost Survey, Trust Bulletin, July 

August 1942, p. 17; special report of the Superintendent of Banks of State of New 

York Banking Department on Trust Department Earnings and Expenses, March 5, 1943. 203

published in Trusts and Estates.2 The second research study was made by the Trust Research Department of The Graduate School of Banking, in the course of which 72 trust institutions stated their experiences in efforts to readjust compensation through negotiation with customers. In the third study, also made by the Trust Research 

Department, 102 trust institutions told of their efforts to readjust compensation through negotiation with beneficiaries. Summaries of these two research studies were published in the Trust Bulletin and in Trusts and Estates.3

SITUATIONS TO BE DEALT WITH 

In any readjustment of compensation in trust accounts, three different situations have to be dealt with, namely: (1) type of account, (2) type of beneficiaries, and (3) provision for compensation.

Type of Account 

For the purposes of this discussion, personal trust accounts fall into two general classes as follows: (1) agencies and revocable living trusts and insurance trusts and (2) irrevocable living trusts and insurance trusts and trusts under will.

In a personal agency or in a revocable living trust or insurance trust, readjustment of compensation must be made by the principal and agent in the one case and by the settlor and trustee in the other. 

In an irrevocable living trust or insurance trust or trust under will, readjustments must be arranged with the beneficiaries or with the court.

Type of Beneficiaries 

In irrevocable living trusts and insurance trusts and in trusts under will, the type of beneficiary governs both the possibility and 

2 MacDougall, Robert E., Cure Your Own Trust Losses, Trusts and Estates, April 

1940, p. 349. 

3 Trust Bulletin, January 1941, p. 5, and December 1941, p. 21; Trusts and Estates, November 1940, p. 548, and November 1941, p. 536.

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

205 the process of readjustment. If the beneficiary is of legal age and competency, the trust institution can negotiate directly with him. 

But if the beneficiary is a minor or is under legal disability, the court alone has authority to make readjustments. 

- Provision for Compensation 

The compensation of the trust institution may have been fixed, determined, or provided for originally in any one of the following ways: (1) The compensation may have been fixed by statute; (2) the maximum and the minimum may have been fixed by statute and the court authorized to fix the compensation within these limits; (3) the amount or percentage may have been written into the trust instrument; (4) the amount or percentage may have been fixed in some collateral agreement or in an exchange of letters between the trust institution and the settlor or testator; (5) the instrument may have provided that the compensation shall be according to some named uniform schedule in force (a) at the time the instrument was drawn, (b) at the time the account became operative, (c) at the time the service was rendered, or (d) at the time the compensation was taken or received; (6) the instrument may have provided that the trust institution shall receive reasonable compensation for its services or words to this effect; or (7) the instrument may be silent on the matter, thus leaving the trust institution to rely upon the general principle of law in the United States that an executor, trustee, or other fiduciary is entitled to reasonable compensation although it is not provided for in the instrument.5 

SOME LEGAL CONSIDERATIONS 

The readjustment of compensation in trust accounts raises a number of legal problems, among which are the following: (1) Does a change in the statutory rates affect existing accounts? (2) Can the 

4 In only a few states is the minimum fixed by statute. 

5 Stephenson, Gilbert T., Compensation Provisions of Wills and Trust Agreements, 

The Graduate School of Banking, American Bankers Association, 1941; Bogert on 

Trusts and Trustees, sec. 974, p. 2850; Scott on Trusts, sec. 242, p. 1383; Shattuck 

Revision, sec. 91, p. 231.

court vary or disregard statutory rates? (3) Can the court vary or disregard the rates named in the instrument? (4) Can the testator or settlor vary or disregard the statutory rates? (5) Can the beneficiaries vary or disregard the rates named in the statute or in the instrument? (6) Can the executor or trustee itself, without the approval of the court or the agreement of the beneficiaries, vary or disregard the rates named in the statute or in the instrument? 

Effect of New Statutory Rates upon Existing Accounts 

Suppose, first, that when A made his will in 1940 the executor's commissions were fixed by statute at 4% of the appraised value of the estate; that in 1941 the statutory rate was raised to 5%; and that A died in 1942. Suppose, second, that in 1942 when A died the statutory rate for trustees under will was 5% of the income annually and 2% of the principal at the termination of the trust and that in 1943, while the trust was in process of administration, the rate was changed to 5% of income and 1/5 of 1% of the principal annually. In the first case would the executor and in the second would the trustee be entitled to the new rate or to the old one? 

In the executorship it is clear that the new rate would prevail. In the first place, A's will "speaks from the day of his death” in 1942 at which time the new rate was operative. In the second place, even if 

A had died in 1940, when the old rate still was in force, but the settlement of the estate had not been completed or the final account filed and allowed until 1941, after the new rate had gone into effect, the new rate would apply. This is in accordance with the general rule that, since an executor's compensation does not accrue or become vested until the court awards it, the compensation is calculated in accordance with the schedules and rules in force at the time when the award is made. 1 In the trusteeship under will also the new rates would apply, at least on regular accountings and awards made after the new rates went into effect. This would be so although part of the service was rendered while the old rates still were operative." The general rule is that the compensation of the trustee is governed by the statute in 

6 Prentice-Hall Trust Service, sec. 14,022 and cases cited. 

Ibid., and cases cited.

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

207 force at the time the compensation is awarded and not at the time of the testator's death. 

Right of Court to Vary or Disregard Statutory Rates 

Suppose, under the prevailing statutory rates, the executor's or the trustee's compensation in a given case is admittedly and grossly inadequate. Can the court vary or disregard the rate in order to do justice to the executor or trustee? This depends largely on the wording of the statute itself. The general rule is: Where a statute provides that the trustee is entitled to certain commissions as compensation, the statute usually is construed as not entitling the trustee to further compensation. In some states the statute gives the court some discretion in the matter. The new Maryland statute, for example, provides that the statutory rate is subject to be increased or diminished for sufficient cause by any court having jurisdiction over the administration of the trust and the allowance of special commissions and compensation for services of an unusual nature.10 

Right of Court to Vary or Disregard Rates Named in Instrument 

Suppose, for example, that the will or trust agreement or a collateral agreement or an exchange of letters provides that the trustee shall receive for its services 2% of the income annually and that the court finds that the 2% is grossly inadequate. Is the court powerless in the premises? Can it only let the trustee resign? Furthermore, will any successor trustee be bound by the same inadequate rate? 

1. Original executor or trustee. If the testator has named a compensation which is grossly inadequate, then for the purpose of procuring proper trust administration the court can increase the compensation to the point necessary to obtain a trustee of reasonable prudence and skill.11 Or where, owing to circumstances not antici- 

8 In re Donovan's Estate, 266 Mich. 362, 253 N.W. 552, 91 A.L.R. 1418 (1934). 

9 Scott on Trusts, sec. 242.1, p. 1388. 

10 Public General Laws, 1939, Art. 16, sec. 280. 

11 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 975, p. 2857.

pated at the time of the creation of the trust, the duties of the trustee have become more onerous, the court may award greater compensation than that named in the instrument.¹2 However, there are cases in which the courts have held, in substance, that the executor or trustee, having made a bad bargain, must stick to it.13 This suggests that there may be a difference in the attitude of the court in the case of a will as to which the executor and trustee was not consulted beforehand and in the case of a trust agreement to which the trustee was a party and to the compensation provisions of which it had agreed. 

2. Successor trustee. If the compensation named in the instrument is inadequate for the original trustee, then it would be equally so for any successor trustee. The same rule applies for the successor as for the original trustee-namely, that the court may allow greater compensation than that named in the instrument if to do so appears to be necessary to procure the proper administration of the trust and to prevent the defeat or substantial impairment of the accomplishment of the purposes of the trust.14 Furthermore, the successor trustee was not a party to and, therefore, is not bound by any original commitment as to compensation.

Right of Testator or Settlor to Vary or Disregard Statutory Rate 

Suppose in a given case the statutory rate is grossly inadequate. 

Can the testator or settlor, by agreement with the executor or trustee, vary or disregard the statutory rate by writing a higher or different rate into the instrument or into a collateral agreement?

The prevailing opinion is that the testator or settlor and the executor or trustee may agree upon rates different from those laid down by statute and that the rates they agree upon will control. It is generally admitted, says Professor Bogert, that the express stipulation of the settlor overrides the directions of the legislature as to the trustee's compensation; that the rates fixed by statute are, expressly or im- 

12 Scott on Trusts, sec. 242.4, p. 1391. 

13 See Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 975, p. 2856. 

14 Scott on Trusts, sec. 242.4, pp. 1391-1392.

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

209 pliedly, stated to be intended to govern only in case the settlor has not set up another standard of pay.15 

Right of Beneficiaries to Vary or Disregard Rates Named in Statute or Instrument

Suppose, in a given trust under will or living trust after the death of the settlor or in a given irrevocable living trust even during the lifetime of the settlor, the rates named in the statute or in the instrument are admitted to be inadequate. Suppose, further, that all the beneficiaries are of legal age and competency and that all the interests are vested. Can they agree among themselves and then agree with the trustee to a rate higher than that named in the instrument or statute?

If all the beneficiaries are of legal age and competency, they and the executor or trustee may, by agreement, reduce or increase the compensation named in the instrument or the statute. If some of the beneficiaries are of legal age and competency and others are minors or incompetent, the agreement would be binding only upon such of the beneficiaries as have capacity to contract.16 

Right of Executor or Trustee Alone to Vary or Disregard Rates Named in Instrument or Statute 

Suppose a trust institution, as executor or trustee, upon an accurate cost analysis, discovers that it is settling the estate or is administering the trust at a heavy loss to itself. Can it alone, without the approval of the court or the agreement of the distributees or beneficiaries, take compensation different from or more than that named in the instrument or statute?

The answer is that, once the trust institution has accepted the appointment and entered upon the settlement of the estate or the administration of the trust, it is bound to its commitment and can get relief only from the court or from the distributees or benefici- 

15 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 975, pp. 2855-2856; Shattuck Revision, sec. 

91, p. 232. 

16 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 975, pp. 2859-2860; Scott on Trusts, sec. 

242.7, pp. 1395-1396; Shattuck Revision, sec. 91, p. 232.

aries." However, the trust institution may decline to accept the appointment and may state that its reason for declining is that the compensation provided for it is inadequate; but, once it has accepted the appointment, it is bound by the compensation provisions of the instrument or the statute.18 This sometimes works a hardship on the trust institution in that frequently it cannot tell until it gets well along in the settlement of the estate whether or not the rates named in the statute or the instrument are adequate. In a few states it has been provided by statute that an executor or trustee may accept the appointment but renounce the compensation fixed in the will and claim that fixed by statute.19 

From the foregoing it must be apparent that the readjustment of compensation in trust accounts is not, inherently or primarily, a legal problem; it is, rather, a business problem. In one way or another from the purely legal point of view readjustment is possible in every type of account.

EXPERIENCES OF TRUST INSTITUTIONS IN READJUSTING COMPENSATION IN TRUST ACCOUNTS 

The readjustment of compensation in trust accounts can, in practice, be accomplished only through negotiation with customers (principals and settlors) or beneficiaries or through court action. 

All three of the research studies already mentioned have been confined to the experiences of trust institutions with readjustment through negotiation with customers and beneficiaries. Yet some mention should be made of readjustment through court action. 

Through Court Action 

Most of the court action with respect to trust compensation in existing accounts really is not readjustment; it is only adjustmentthat is, the making of a reasonable allowance for service rendered. 

Where the trust instrument is silent as to the trustee's compensation or where it simply provides that the trustee shall receive reasonable compensation or where the statute sets a maximum or a minimum 

17 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 975, p. 2855. 

18 Scott on Trusts, sec. 102, p. 542. 

19 Ibid., sec. 242.4, p. 1391.

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

211 or where, as in Maryland, it expressly authorizes the court to increase or diminish the compensation for sufficient cause and the court makes what it regards as a proper allowance, this is not a readjustment; it is only an adjustment. It is a readjustment only when the court varies or disregards the rates named in the statute or the instrument or previously agreed upon by the parties. While the court has authority to make readjustments in the true sense of the word, there are very few cases of record in which the court has disregarded and varied the rates named in the instrument, and in those cases it has done so only to prevent the defeat or substantial impairment of the accomplishment of the purposes of the trust. 20 In a previous Study2¹ there is a discussion of the factors that the courts have declared they take into consideration or that they would take into consideration in arriving at what they regard as fair and reasonable compensation. Presumably, once its jurisdiction is clear, a court would, in the main, take into consideration the same factors in readjusting as in adjusting compensation, with this important difference, however: The court would be influenced greatly by what it would regard as having been in the contemplation of the parties at the time the will was made or the trust agreement executed. This is well illustrated in the case of Bridgeport-City Trust Company, Trustee, v. The First National Bank and Trust Company of Bridgeport, Trustee, et als.,22 mentioned in the previous Study. In that case, the trustee petitioned the court to permit it to take part of its compensation out of principal instead of taking all of it out of income. The court admitted both the reasonableness of the amount and the fairness of the apportionment as between income and principal. Nevertheless, the court denied the petition on the ground, that, when the trust was created, on the basis of custom, it must have been in the contemplation of the creator of the trust that the annual compensation would be taken out of income. The court said that any departure from this general rule should be by legislative action and not by judicial fiat. 

20 See Kaiser v. Second National Bank, 123 Conn. 248, 193 Atl. 761 (1937); 

Smith v. Slover, 262 Ill. App. 440 (1931); Biscoe v. State, 23 Ark. 592; In re Battin, 104 Atl. 434, 89 N. J. Eq. 144 (1916). 

21 Study Number 7, How Compensation for Trust Service Is Determined, supra, p. 137 at pp. 145-151. 

22 124 Conn. 472, 200 Atl. 809 (1938).

Through Negotiation with Customers or Beneficiaries 

It has been found wholly impracticable to draw or to keep any distinction between the experiences of trust institutions with readjusting compensation through negotiation with customers, on the one hand, and with beneficiaries, on the other. In practice, when a trust institution undertakes in a systematic way to readjust its compensation in trust accounts, in order to succeed in the general effort it must negotiate in some cases with the customer and in others with the beneficiary.

The vast majority of the 102 trust institutions reporting to the Trust Research Department on their experiences in readjusting compensation through negotiations with customers and beneficiaries say that the results have been satisfactory and successful. In fact, only one of the 102 reports that its experience was definitely unsuccessful or disappointing. Mr. MacDougall received only three adverse reports from the 112 trust institutions he reached.23 

However, from the point of view of a research student, most of the replies are so general as to be difficult to classify. For example, to the question, What has been your experience in bringing substandard up to standard rates in living trusts and trusts under will?, the answers abounded in such phrases as "satisfactory," "most satisfactory," "successful," "very successful," "surprisingly successful" (this phrase recurred again and again), “favorable," and "most favorable."

The cities in which apparently the most systematic, consistent, and successful efforts have been made to readjust compensation through negotiation with customers and beneficiaries are Hartford; New 

Haven; Wilmington, Delaware; Philadelphia; Cleveland; Baltimore; Winston-Salem, North Carolina; Chicago; Minneapolis; St.

Paul; and Denver. The following are some of the definite results reported by trust institutions in the cities indicated:

Hartford. One trust institution reported that it has been successful in adjusting the rates in about 75% of the cases in which the compensation was fixed in the instrument and in 95% of the cases in which it was not fixed. 

New York. One trust institution reports that within the past four years it has tried to raise the rates in about 10% of its trusts under will and its living trusts and that it has been successful in over 80% of these cases. 

23 MacDougall, Robert E., Cure Your Own Trust Losses, Trusts and Estates, April 

1940, p. 349 at pp. 351-352.

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

213 Philadelphia. One Philadelphia trust institution reports that since the middle of 1939 it has increased its income commissions in existing accounts by about 16%. Another reports that it has increased its annual income commissions in existing accounts between $150,000 and $200,000 and that it has been successful in about 80% of the cases in which it had to negotiate with the beneficiaries. A third reports that it has increased its annual income commissions by about $108,000 and the principal commissions by $460,000 and that it has been successful in about 94% of the cases. 

Another trust institution reports that within two years time it has increased its commissions in 300 agency accounts by $36,000 a year. 

Cincinnati. One trust institution reports that it selected 25 of its most unprofitable accounts and undertook to readjust the compensation in them and that it succeeded in increasing the compensation in these accounts by about $5,000 a year. 

Winston-Salem. In 20 accounts the trust institution undertook to obtain the consent of the beneficiaries to increase the compensation and in every case it was successful. 

Chicago. One trust institution reports that it has increased its annual income by about 

10% by requesting additional compensation for extraordinary service that had not been contemplated. Another reports that of 590 living trusts and trusts under will it increased the rates in 355 or 60% and that in 17 of the 355 the rate later was reduced. A third reports that in 1934 it found that 60% of its living trusts were being handled at rates below those recommended by the Corporate Fiduciaries Association of Chicago; that 53% of its trusts under will were being handled at substandard rates; that it has been able to bring 80% of its living trusts and 84% of its trusts under will up to standard; and that at the present time only 10% of its trusts under will and 20% of its living trusts are substandard. Another reports that within the past five years it has increased its compensation in existing accounts by about 

25% of the original compensation.

Minneapolis. One trust institution reports that in 44 trusts, the principal value of which was about $8,000,000, it increased its compensation 79% through direct dealings with the beneficiaries; that of the 42 beneficiaries approached all but 5 approved the increase.

Denver. One trust institution reports that it was able to place nearly 100% of its accounts on a new schedule of rates with loss of the business or apparent dissatisfaction of probably less than 1% of the dollar volume of the business. 

Los Angeles. One trust institution reports that its success in readjusting compensation in court trusts has been negligible; that it has been about 75% in living trusts where the settlor is living; and about 25% successful in private trusts where the settlor is dead.

CONDITIONS PRECEDENT TO SATISFACTORY READJUSTMENT 

The foregoing reports are indeed gratifying and encouraging. 

Why were these trust institutions so successful? There seems to be, at least, three conditions precedent to the successful readjustment of compensation. They are (1) knowledge of costs, (2) pre-existing

good customer relations, and (3) careful selection of the person to conduct the negotiation. 

Knowledge of Costs

Several trustmen, whose trust institutions had been especially successful in this effort, stated that, in their opinion, their institutions would not have been so successful if they had not been in a position, by actual cost analysis, to show the customers and beneficiaries and their advisers that the present compensation under existing conditions is clearly inadequate. For example, a trustman from North

Carolina said that, had it not been for the accurate figures prepared on cost analysis, he did not believe that his institution would have been at all successful. Several other trustmen called attention to the fact that all readjustments had been based upon cost analysis. Mr. 

MacDougall's research led him to the conclusion that a prerequisite to any plan to increase compensation is accurate cost analysis.² 

Good Customer Relations 

24 Several trustmen attributed the success of their efforts to the preexisting good relations between the institution and its customers and beneficiaries. They felt that their institutions could not have been successful if they had not, at the beginning of the effort and throughout the period, possessed the respect, good will, and confidence of their customers and beneficiaries.

On this point, for example, a Connecticut trustman says, “Our experience goes to show that where they (the beneficiaries) are recipients of benefits from a trust fund set up by another, beneficiaries are disposed to have entire confidence in the bank. This may be due, in no small part, to the fact that we try to inspire that confidence by giving them the fullest information about the terms of the trust, the services we render, and the general basis of our charges for these services, etc." A Minnesota trustman says, "It was fortunate, indeed, that through the years the relations of our bank with the individuals concerned have been cordial and mutually satisfactory. . . . The beneficiaries, generally, have realized that a sincere and active personal interest has been maintained in the accounts 

24 Ibid., p. 419.

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

215 established for their benefit and that their welfare has been emphasized." 

Person Who Conducts Negotiation

Many trustmen emphasized the importance of the trust institution's exercising great care in the selection of the person to conduct the negotiation with the customer or the beneficiary. If the person selected is familiar with the account, if he knows the customers or beneficiaries to be dealt with, if his present relations with them are pleasant, and if he is tactful in presenting and following up the matter, then, as nearly all of the replies indicate, he stands a good chance of making a satisfactory readjustment. If, on the other hand, he is not familiar with the account or with the persons to be dealt with or if he is tactless, he is not likely to be successful. More than that, he is likely to impair the existing customer relations and possibly even the public relations of his institution. 

SUGGESTIONS AS TO PROCEDURE FOR READJUSTMENT 

The following suggestions regarding possible procedure for the readjustment of compensation have been gleaned from the experiences of the trust institutions which have been the most successful in their efforts. The suggestions are as follows: (1) Establish and apply a system for cost analyzing trust accounts; (2) based on cost analysis, make up a list of the trust accounts in which the present compensation is unsatisfactory; (3) name a committee of trust officers or of persons in the trust department and refer the list of accounts to this committee; (4) have this committee go over each account with the administrator of that account and with others familiar with the account and decide, from the business point of view, whether, in the light of all the circumstances, an effort should be made to readjust the compensation; (5) refer the accounts so selected for readjustment to counsel for its opinion as to the legal possibility or propriety of a readjustment; (6) for each account approved by both committee and counsel for readjustment select a definite person to conduct the negotiation; (7) leave the person so selected free to conduct the negotiation in the way his own judgment dictates; and (8) continue the committee until the efforts to readjust have been completed.

Establish System for Cost Analyzing Trust Accounts 

A small, although an increasing, number of trust institutions have established and are operating their own systems for cost analyzing trust accounts. In 1940 the Committee on Costs and Charges of the 

Trust Division worked out a system of cost analyzing trust accounts.25 More recently a simplified system, especially designed for the very small trust department, has been worked out and soon is to be published. By the use of one system or another, every trust department, small or large, should be able to tell, with reasonable accuracy, what it costs the trust institution per year to handle the given account and what the compensation must be to put the account on a paying basis.

Make up List of Unprofitable Accounts 

On the basis of cost analysis it is possible to arrive at a list of the unprofitable accounts. Knowing by cost analysis what compensation on different types of accounts must be received in order to make these accounts reasonably profitable, the trust department can prepare a list of unprofitable accounts of each type without having to cost analyze each account. Where the readjustment is to be based on new statutory rates, as was the case in Maryland, it is a simple matter to make up a list of accounts in which the compensation is below the new rates. 

Refer List to Committee on Readjustment

As soon as the trust institution decides that it will undertake in a systematic, thoroughgoing way to readjust compensation, it should name a committee on readjustment. The make-up of this committee may be the same as that of the standing committee on fees and commissions (if the trust institution has such a committee) or the same as that of the regular officers' committee, or it may be a special committee on readjustment. According to the circumstances, the committee may be named by the board, by the president, by the trust committee, or by the head of the trust department. In any 

25 Guide to Trust Fees, Revised 1940, pp. 45-52.

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

217 case, the committee should have definite responsibility for directing and supervising the effort. To this committee should be referred all accounts in which any attempt to readjust compensation is to be considered, and Mr. MacDougall already has suggested the form in which these accounts should be reported to the committee.26 

Have Committee Consider Each Account from Business Point of View 

The committee must decide for itself in what order it will take up the accounts for consideration—that is, whether it will take up all the agencies first, next the living trusts in which the settlors still are alive, and then the other trusts; whether it will take up the accounts in chronological order; whether it will try to complete the readjustment in a short period or spread it out over a longer period. 

One Pennsylvania trust institution points out the danger of trying to do too much too quickly. Over a period of six months it made an intensive effort to readjust compensation in trust accounts and thought it was accomplishing remarkable results. But it soon found that it was trying to do too much in too short a period of time. Now it is reviewing for the readjustment of compensation only one or two accounts at each meeting of the trust committee. "It may take us a longer time," it reports, "to get over all of our existing trust business properly, but I think we will do a more thorough job." 

The committee should approach the readjustment first from the business point of view. Granted that it is legally possible in one way or another to bring about the readjustment. Yet not all readjustments that would be lawful would be expedient. Many practical considerations need to be taken into account. For example, the account may be so near to termination that it would not be worthwhile to raise the issue; the amount involved may be too small; the background of the account-how it came to the trust institution in the first instance-may have a bearing on the matter; the characteristics of the customer or of the beneficiaries have to be taken into account. The committee cannot treat the accounts on a wholesale basis. For example, it cannot order that all substandard agencies shall be brought up to the standard or closed. It cannot treat all 

26 Trusts and Estates, April 1940, p. 349 at pp. 419-420.

trusts or types of trusts alike. It must consider each account on its merits and must reach its decision about readjustment in the light of all the information it can obtain from all available sources. 

Have Committee Refer List to Counsel 

Upon the basis of business considerations just described, the committee may decide that in certain accounts, although the rates are substandard or the accounts are unprofitable, no attempt should be made to readjust the compensation. Eliminating these accounts, the committee then should submit to counsel the list in which it thinks that from the business point of view a readjustment should be undertaken. Counsel may advise that as to certain accounts, on account of the terms of the trust or the provisions of the instrument or the legal status of the beneficiaries, no attempt should be made to change the present compensation arrangements. In some trust institutions it may be practicable to have the counsel as a member of the committee or to sit with the committee and, as each account comes up for consideration, then and there, to say whether or not from the legal point of view there are any barriers in the way of readjustment. 

Select Person to Conduct Negotiation

From the business point of view, there is no more important function of the committee than that of selecting the person who is to conduct the negotiation for readjustment. In many cases the readjustment will succeed or fail according as the right or the wrong person is selected. At this step in the procedure customer relations become all-important.

In most cases, the trust officer or the trust administrator, who has been handling the account and who knows and has had dealings with the customers and beneficiaries, is the logical person to conduct the negotiation. But in some cases he is temperamentally unfitted for this particular assignment and to impose it upon him might result in both failure to make the readjustment and in the impairment of his usefulness as administrator of the account.

In many cases the administrative head of the trust department would be the logical person. He, too, may be temperamentally

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

219 unfitted for this assignment, or as to a particular account there may be some other good reason why he should not conduct the negotiation. 

One trust institution had its president write the initial letter to the customer or beneficiary. On the one hand, the president's signature added prestige to the communication and obtained for it attention that it otherwise might not have received. 

But, on the other hand, if the president was not in the habit of writing letters on trust matters, if this was the first letter about the account the customer or beneficiary ever had received from him, or if he personally was unacquainted with the customer or beneficiary, the reaction might be just the opposite of that intended or hoped for. The person addressed might feel that there was something extraordinary in the wind, that a letter from the president indicated the gravity of the situation of the trust institution's uncertainty of its own position, and that he, the customer or beneficiary, had better be on his guard. A letter from the head of the trust department of a large institution, who never before had had direct contact or communication with the customer or beneficiary, might produce an unfavorable reaction and for the same reason. 

Exercise Discretion as to Approach and Negotiation 

1 Whoever is selected to make the approach and negotiate the readjustment should have broad discretion for, in order to succeed in his assignment, he must vary his medium of communication, his approach, and his negotiation according to circumstances. 

1. Medium of communication. All the usual mediums of communication-telephone or telegram, letter, or personal intervieware available to the person selected. a. Telephone. The telephone or telegram may be dismissed with a word. It is not an appropriate medium of communication for this purpose except to make an engagement for a personal interview. b. Letter. A letter may be used as the medium of communication in either of two ways: (a) As a means of obtaining a personal interview or (b) as a means of explaining in detail the desired readjustment. It is thought that the general use of the letter in the second way is inadvisable. In many cases it would be impossible to make a complete statement of the facts justifying the increase in

compensation without making the letter too long to be read or too technical to be understood by the lay customers or beneficiaries. It is all the more inadvisable to mail out a form letter on the subject, except perhaps to call attention to a change in the law affecting the compensation generally or in a particular type of accounts. c. Interview. The overwhelming weight of opinion among trustmen is that so far as possible the negotiation should be conducted by personal interview and that the interview should be in the trust institution and not in the office or home of the customer or beneficiary. In his own office the representative of the trust institution has at hand all the records and documents needed to answer the customer's or beneficiary's questions and to justify the trust institution's request for a readjustment of its compensation. In the customer's or beneficiary's home or office none of these things is available. Yet, under certain circumstances, it may be necessary to conduct the negotiation by mail or for the representative of the trust institution to call upon the customer or beneficiary in his home or office. 

2. Characteristics of customer or beneficiary. The person assigned to conduct the negotiation should have due regard for the characteristics of the person with whom he is to deal. Where he is dealing with an adult, competent person, thoroughly capable of taking care of himself in any business transaction, the negotiation between the two may be carried on as they would carry on any other business matter of importance. Where, on the other hand, the customer or beneficiary is unused to or is inexperienced in business negotiations, the representative of the trust institution must exercise great care not to put himself into a position where he or his institution can be accused of taking advantage of inexperience, credulity, or trustfulness. 

3. Provisions for compensation. The methods of approach and negotiation will be influenced also by the provisions for compensation. a. Instrument silent. If the will or trust agreement is silent as to the trustee's compensation and if there is no statute that fixes the rates, the rates in previous years will have been fixed by custom. In living trusts, for example, the rates may have been governed largely by what the courts have been allowing in trusts under will. In all such cases the representative of the trust institution must deal with

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

221 the appropriate person in the way his judgment dictates. While the trust institution under the living trust might raise the rate to the point which it regarded as reasonable and to what, in fact, was reasonable, without consulting beforehand with the customer or beneficiary, and then might await developments at or after the next accounting, it is thought that it would be bad policy for it to do so. b. Rate fixed by statute. If the compensation was fixed originally by statute and if a new statute raises the rate, the trust institution has the legal right, unless otherwise provided by the instrument, to raise the compensation to comply with the new statute, without consulting either customers or beneficiaries. Again, it is thought to be the better policy in most cases to notify the proper parties in person or by letter beforehand that the new rates would be put into effect than to put them into effect without calling attention to them until the next accounting or letting the beneficiaries discover the changes for themselves. c. According to uniform schedules. If the instrument provides that the trust institution shall be compensated according to the uniform schedules prevailing at the time the service is rendered or at the time the compensation is taken, then the trust institution has the legal right to apply the new schedules as soon as they become operative, without notifying the customer or beneficiary beforehand. 

Nevertheless, it would be courteous, to say the least, for the trust institution either before or along with the next accounting to tell the customer or beneficiary about the new schedules, together with a brief and simple explanation of the reasons for the changes in the rates. d. Fixed by instrument. If the rates were written into the will or trust agreement or were fixed by a collateral agreement or exchange of letters, a different and a much more difficult problem is presented. 

Suppose the old rates now are admitted to be grossly inadequate. 

On the one hand, is it fair to the stockholders of the bank or trust company to continue carrying the account at a substantial loss? On the other hand, can the trust institution afford to question the bargain which it had made and the customer (the testator or the settlor now dead) had accepted and relied upon? Further, how will later trust customers and beneficiaries feel about paying the higher rates, when they learn, by comparing notes, that other customers or beneficiaries are receiving similar trust service at much lower rates?

What becomes of the statement of principles as to uniformity and

impartiality of application of charges for trust service?

2 27 On this point there is a wide difference of opinion. One group of trustmen say that the trust institution, having made its bargain, should stick to it; that it runs the risk of losing more by putting itself into a position in which it may be charged with trying to go back on a bad bargain than it would gain by the increased rates. 

The other group argue with equal force that a trust institution should not be denied an effort to make needed readjustment even in these fixed-compensation accounts; that, fully recognizing and freely admitting its obligation to live up to the original agreement, it should be free, without forfeiting its self-respect or the respect of others; to lay all the facts before the customer or the beneficiary and leave it to him to say whether or not the old rates shall continue or the new ones be applied.

In the communities in which the trust institutions have been the most successful in readjusting compensation, the prevailing opinion is that a trust institution should not hesitate to seek readjustment to a fair and reasonable rate even in fixed-compensation accounts. 

For example, one Pennsylvania institution reports that, where the compensation was fixed in the instrument, in its negotiation with beneficiaries it is careful to call their attention to the agreement and to state that it is willing to live up to and will live up to the terms of the agreement but asks the beneficiaries, as a matter of justice and fairness, to consider the adequacy of the fixed compensation in the light of changed conditions; that its success is as great in the fixed-compensation cases as in others-that is, in about 80% of the cases; that, in its opinion, nothing can be lost and much can be gained by a discussion of the compensation provisions of the instrument with the beneficiaries; that, even though changes are not possible in all cases, the discussion educates its customers to the point where they will not expect to receive trust service in the future at the rates established in the past. 

Continue Committee until Readjustment Completed

Finally, it is highly important that the work of the committee on readjustment be continued until the assignment has been completed. 

27 A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, Art. V.

READJUSTING COMPENSATION 

223 In a large institution it may take months or even a year or so to finish the work. In this effort the best chance of success is in making haste slowly but in moving along steadily. 

CONCLUSIONS 

Should a trust institution undertake, through negotiation with its customers or beneficiaries, to readjust compensation in trust accounts? The following, by way of a general conclusion, is offered as the composite answer of the trust institutions to which the question has been submitted in the course of the three research studies. 

In those accounts in which presently inadequate compensation can be readjusted only by direct dealing with the customers or beneficiaries, there is no good reason why the trust institution should not deal with them and there are many good reasons why it should do so, provided it approaches them tactfully, through the proper person, and in a businesslike way, laying before them the facts, stated simply and understandably, that justify the readjustment. 

With respect to readjustment in fixed-compensation accounts the trust institution must, at all hazards, make it clear in the beginning and keep it clear throughout the negotiation that it is not trying to wriggle out of a bargain; that it is trying only to readjust compensation in the light of current conditions, which could not have been foreseen or anticipated at the time the account was opened; and further that it is trying to adjust compensation only to the point where it will be fair alike to the beneficiaries of the account and to the stockholders of the institution and promote uniformity and impartiality of application of trust charges. 

Scrupulously careful at all times and in every way not to take advantage of immaturity, inexperience, or trustfulness, the trust institution should, wherever possible, deal directly and in person with the customer or beneficiary.

Looking toward the future, the trust institution should avoid, so far as possible, the acceptance of long-term trust accounts that do not provide for the readjustment of compensation from time to time to meet current conditions. 

The wisdom of readjusting compensation through direct dealing with customers and beneficiaries is predicated upon their funda-

mental fairmindedness, on the one hand, and that of the trust institution, on the other. A fairminded customer or beneficiary wants the trust institution to make a reasonable profit on his account. A fairminded trust institution wants only a reasonable profit on its trust business. When fairmindedness prevails on both sides, nothing but good can come of the negotiation, no matter what may be the dollar-and-cents results. By such fairminded dealing they get each other's points of view and come to understand better each other's problems. Thus readjustment of compensation in trust accounts can be and should be made and in many institutions has been made both an opportunity and an occasion for actually improving, instead of impairing, customer relations.
