HAROLD STONIER

Study Number 11  HELD IN TRUST ACCOUNTS 

VERY trust institution already has or sooner or later will have in its trust accounts¹ shares of stock of corporations, either as original or as new investments. According to the annual reports of the Comptroller of the Currency over the last ten years, nearly onethird of the funds of both private trusts and court trusts in the trust departments of national banks has been invested in shares of stock. 

This is a fairly constant percentage, ranging only between 29.28% and 33.15%. The percentage of shares of stock in the trust departments of state-chartered trust institutions would be substantially the same.

The present trend toward giving trust institutions broader investment powers,2 through the adoption of the prudent-man rule by statute or by inclusion in the trust instrument, may mean for the country as a whole an increase in the investment of trust funds in shares of stock.

Not only do shares of stock already constitute nearly one-third of the trust assets of trust institutions, with the prospect of constituting still more, but, what is equally significant, a large and apparently an increasing percentage of the outstanding stock of many of the largest corporations is held by trust institutions in their trust ac- 1 The term trust accounts, unless otherwise indicated, embraces estates, guardianships, and agencies as well as trusts proper; and the term trustee embraces executor, administrator, guardian, and agent as well as trustee. 

2 See the previous Study Number 4, Should Trust Institutions Have Broad Dis cretionary Powers? 225

counts. Unless trust institutions, in common with other large corporate investors such as insurance companies and endowed institutions - have definite and sound policies as to voting shares of stock, both the corporations and their stockholders (including trustees) may suffer.

This is the third study from the business point of view of the voting of shares of stock held in trust accounts. The first was made by the late William J. Kieferdorf of the Bank of America National 

Trust & Savings Association, California, and was presented at the 

1938 Mid-Winter Trust Conference. None of Mr. Kieferdorf's address except his conclusions was published. The second study was made in 1939 by Ralph H. Spotts of the Title Insurance and 

Trust Company of Los Angeles, California, and this was published.* 

The factual material for the present Study has been supplied mainly by two nationwide surveys made in 1942-one by the Trust 

Research Department of The Graduate School of Banking to ascer tain the practices and the other by the students majoring in Trusts in The Graduate School of Banking to ascertain the policies of trust institutions as to voting shares of stock held in trust accounts. Each student was requested to prepare a statement of policies as to a trust institution's voting shares of stock by proxy, specifying the cases in which it should vote by proxy and those in which it should not do so, giving his reasons.

WAYS OF VOTING 

Trustees have two ways of voting shares of stock — namely, in person and by proxy.5 

In Person

A trust institution, being a corporation, can act only through natural persons. When one of its directors or officers or staff members, & Trust Companies, February 1938, pp. 146-147. 

4 Trust Bulletin, April 1939, p. 7; Trusts and Estates, February 1939, p. 209. 

5 Throughout this Study it seems unavoidable to use the term proxy in three different senses and only the context in each case will show the sense in which it is being used. First, it means the action or practice of voting shares of stock by means of an authorized agent; second, the paperwriting (technically, the letter of attorney) authorizing one person to vote the shares of stock of another; and, third, the person (technically, the attorney in fact) who votes the shares of stock of another.

duly authorized, attends the stockholders' meeting and votes as his judgment dictates the shares of stock it holds in trust accounts, it is, in contemplation of law, voting in person.* 

By Proxy 

There are four kinds of proxies used by trust institutions in voting shares of stock-namely (1) special proxy, (2) limited proxy, (3) general proxy, and (4) mixed proxy. 

1. Special Proxy. A trust institution votes shares of stock by special proxy when it authorizes some person whether a member of its staff or a third person, to attend the meeting and vote as instructed by the trust institution. The person attending the meeting and voting the shares of stock really is only registering the vote. which already has been cast by the trust institution. Yet, in deference to accepted terminology, this will be treated as one of the methods of voting by proxy. 

2. Limited proxy. A trust institution, in signing a proxy to vote shares of stock, may specify certain matters on which its representative, whether a member of its staff or a third person, is not authorized to vote. This is a limited proxy. 

3. General proxy. A trust institution votes shares of stock by general proxy when it authorizes a representative, other than one of its own directors or officers or staff members, to attend the meeting and vote the shares as his (the representative's) judgment dictates. 

The trust institution itself may name the representative, or it may accept the representative suggested by the corporation (by leaving the name or names as printed in the proxy), or it may sign a blank proxy in which the name of the representative is to be filled in by the corporation or by the person to whom the proxy is sent (known as giving a blank proxy). 

4. Mixed proxy. A trust institution may sign a proxy (a) instructing its representative how to vote on certain matters (special proxy), * Bogert, George Gleason, Handbook of the Law of Trusts, 2d edition, Ch. 9, sec. 96, p. 324; West Publishing Company, St. Paul, 1942 (hereafter cited by the shorter form, Bogert's Handbook on Trusts); Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 555, p. 1764.

(b) prohibiting him from voting on other matters (limited proxy), and (c) authorizing him to vote according to his own judgment on still other matters or on all other matters coming before the stockholders' meeting (general proxy). This is a mixed proxy.

In some cases it may be advisable for the trust institution to prepare its own proxy rather than to use the printed form. This course obviates the possibility of the trust institution's deleting too much or too little from the printed form and, in addition, serves to call special attention to the matters to be voted upon. 

POWERS AS TO VOTING 

What power has a trust institution to vote the shares of stock it holds in trust accounts?

Powers and Duties Distinguished 

By power is meant the legal power of a trust institution to vote shares of stock held in its trust accounts and the validity of the vote so far as it affects the corporation, its stockholders, and third persons. By duty is meant the equitable duty of the trust institution to its beneficiaries as regards the exercise of its legal power to vote the shares of stock."

The power of a trust institution to vote shares of stock held in trust accounts emanates from three sources - namely (1) the com- 

mon law, (2) the statutes, and (3) the trust instrument. 

Common Law

At common law, unless it is otherwise provided by the terms of the trust, the trustee of shares of stock has the power to vote them. 

The right to vote is one of the incidents of legal ownership of shares of stock. 

7 Scott on Trusts, sec. 193.1, p. 1049. 

8 Restatement of Trusts, sec. 193; Scott on Trusts, sec. 193, p. 1046; Shattuck 

Revision, sec. 44, p. 128.

Statutes 

Statutes on voting shares of stock held in trust accounts fall into two groups (1) those that give trustees the general power to vote and (2) those that give them the specific power to vote by proxy. 

1. Power to vote. The following states are among those in which the general power of a trustee to vote shares of stock is granted by statute: California, Colorado, Illinois, Iowa, Louisiana, Kansas, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, New Jersey, North 

Carolina, Oklahoma, Rhode Island, Virginia, and Washington.

These statutes confirm the power that already exists at common law. 

2. Power to vote by proxy. In the following states trustees are authorized to vote shares of stock by proxy as well as in person: 

California, Connecticut, Florida, Indiana, Maryland, Massachusetts, Nevada, North Carolina, Ohio, Oklahoma, Pennsylvania,

Rhode Island (permitting proxy to co-trustees), South Dakota, and Texas. In most of these states the statute authorizing a trustee to vote shares of stock by proxy is worded substantially as follows: A trustee owning corporate stock may vote it by proxy, but shall be liable for any loss resulting to the beneficiaries from a failure to use reasonable care in deciding how to vote the stock and in voting it.9 

According to Professor Bogert, the phrase, "use reasonable care in deciding how to vote the stock and in voting it," means "use reasonable care in deciding whether to vote the stock by a proxy or personally; whether to instruct the proxy how to vote and, if so, how to instruct him; and how to vote the stock personally, if that method of voting was used." Thus interpreted, the statute covers general as well as special and limited proxies. 

Trust Instrument

The testator or settlor has the power to authorize or instruct the trustee as to voting shares of stock held in the trust account. For example, the trust instrument may instruct the trustee how to vote 9 For the Uniform Trusts Act of which this is section 8, see Commerce Clearing 

House Trust and Estate Law Service, secs. 1312-1353, and Prentice-Hall Trust 

Service, sec. 3850.

on certain named matters; it may instruct the trustee to vote as directed by the settlor or a beneficiary or a third person; it even may take the power to vote from the trustee and give it to the settlor, beneficiary, or some third person; it may place the power to vote in one of several trustees.10 Likewise, the testator or settlor may authorize the trustee to vote shares of stock by proxy and may specify that it may vote by general proxy or by special or limited proxy. 

DUTIES AS TO VOTING 

The duties of a trust institution to the beneficiaries as regards voting shares of stock held in trust accounts are presented first as general principles and then as applications of these general principles to special situations.

General Principles 

A trust institution is under a duty to the beneficiaries to vote or not to vote, to vote in person or by proxy, to vote by special, limited, general, or mixed proxy according as one course or another is for the benefit of the trust account.' 11 If the proper administration of the trust requires that the trust institution exercise personal judgment at the stockholders' meeting, it is under a duty to the beneficiaries to attend the meeting and vote the shares of the stock in person. 12 Where the circumstances are such that the trust institution itself should determine how the shares of stock should be voted on a particular issue, it is under a duty to the beneficiaries to vote the shares of stock either in person or by special proxy and not by general proxy. 13 However, a trust institution is also under a duty to the beneficiaries not to put the trust account to the expense of its voting the 

10 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 556, p. 1778; Scott on Trusts, sec. 193.1, pp. 1047-1048. 

11 Restatement of Trusts, comment a under section 193; Scott on Trusts, secs. 193, 193.1, pp. 1046-1049. 

12 Restatement of Trusts, comment b under sec. 193. 

18 Ibid.

shares of stock in person where they properly can be voted by proxy and where the expense of voting in person would outweigh the advantage to the trust account of the shares' being voted in person.¹4 

Application to Special Situations 

The trust institution may be sole trustee or it may be one of several trustees. In either case it has to consider many different situations, among which are the following: (1) The character of the corporation, shares of stock in which are held in trust accounts, (2) the amount of the shares so held, (3) the matters coming before the stockholders' meeting, and (4) the ways of voting the shares of stock.

The corporation may be (a) the bank or trust company itself, stock in which is held in the trust account, (b) a family corporation, (c) a local corporation the stock in which is owned, wholly or largely, by the people of the community, (d) a small, locally owned and controlled corporation located in another community, or (e) a large corporation, stock in which is widely held. The trust institution may hold in one or more of its trust accounts (a) all the stock of the corporation except the qualifying shares, (b) the majority or a substantial minority of the stock, or (c) only a few shares out of a large number outstanding. The trust institution may hold the stock in only one acount or it may hold it in several and, conceivably, there may be a conflict of interest between or among the several accounts as to the voting of the shares of stock. The matters to come before the stockholders' meeting may be (a) routine — such as the annual election of directors—or (b) vital changes — such as a substantial amendment of the charter or the issuance of new securities. There may be complete unanimity among the stockholders or there may be contests. 

1. The trust institution one of several trustees. Where there are several trustees, they all must join in exercising a power unless it is provided otherwise by the terms of the trust or by statute. 15 This general principle applies to voting shares of stock. Where the trust 

14 See Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 556, p. 1778; Scott on Trusts, sec. 193.3. p.1052. 

15 Scott on Trusts, sec. 193.1, p. 1048.

institution is one of the several trustees, they all should join in voting the shares or in the decision not to vote them. If they cannot agree how to vote or whether to vote, then ordinarily they should not vote. But in extreme cases, where the proper administration of the trust requires the voting of the shares and where the voting of the shares held in the trust account is likely to be determinative of the issue (as in the case of a close corporation), the trustees should bring their disagreement to the attention of the court. 

Ordinarily the court will not tell the trustees how to vote. The most it will do is remove one or more of the trustees, and this is only in an extreme case, for abuse of discretion. Under the Pennsylvania and the Indiana statutes it is provided that, when stock is held in the name of more than two fiduciaries, the majority of them may vote it; if the fiduciaries are equally divided, the court will direct the voting of the stock as it may deem for the best interest of the beneficiaries.

In view of the joint responsibility of co-trustees, a trust institution should not, in the absence of a directing or authorizing provision in the instrument, make a general practice of leaving it altogether to the individual co-trustee to attend the stockholders' meeting and vote the shares of stock as his judgment dictates. In the case of a family corporation, there might be some temptation at times to do this. Nor, on the other hand, should the trust institution vote the shares of stock (even if permitted by the corporation to do so) without the express approval or joinder of its co-trustee. 

2. Stock in the bank or trust company itself. Under the Banking

Act of 1935, a national bank as sole trustee cannot vote its own shares of stock in the election of directors unless under the terms of the trust the manner in which such shares shall be voted may be determined by a donor or beneficiary of the trust and unless such donor or beneficiary actually directs how such shares shall be voted; but in a co-trusteeship, the individual co-trustee may vote the shares of stock as if it were sole trustee. Such shares of stock held in trust accounts and not voted are not counted in determining whether the requisite percentage of shares voted upon the matter at issue.16 It should be noted that this restriction applies only to the election of 

16 U.S.R.S., sec. 5144; 12 U.S.C.A., sec. 61.

directors. A number of state-chartered banks and trust companies indicate that in the election of directors they follow a similar policy as to voting their own shares of stock held in trust accounts. Apart from this restriction, it would seem that, so long as a bank or trust company holds its own stock in trust accounts, it may have a positive duty to vote the shares for the best interest of the beneficiaries. 

A trust institution would be presumed to have intimate knowledge of its own affairs, and it would not be justified in adopting a policy of not voting on an issue affecting the interests of the beneficiaries of its trust accounts.' 17 

3. Stock in a family corporation. Where the trust institution holds in one or more of its trust accounts all of or a majority of the shares of stock of a family corporation, it owes a duty to the beneficiaries to vote the shares and, furthermore, to vote them in person. Professor Scott, using as an illustration a business founded and incorporated by the settlor, says that, when the trustee holds sufficient shares to control actually or substantially the conduct of the corporation, it is under a duty to exercise that control for the benefit of the trust." 18 

If the testator or settlor expects and desires that some member of his family shall vote the shares of stock in the family corporation, the will or trust agreement should contain a provision authorizing or directing the desired member of the family to vote the shares of stock and relieving the trust institution of responsibility with respect thereto. In the absence of such a provision, a trust institution cannot relieve itself of responsibility by entering into an agreement with the beneficiaries as to the voting of the shares. It cannot delegate to a member of the family its own duty with respect to voting the shares of stock. 

4. Controlling stock in a small corporation in distant community. 

Suppose the trust institution holds in its trust accounts a majority or a substantial minority of the stock of a small, locally managed corporation in a distant community. In such case the trust institution has a duty to its beneficiaries to vote the shares of stock so long as it holds them as a trust investment. The stake of the trust 

17 See Spotts, Ralph H., Retention by a Trustee of Its Own and Other Stock, Trust Bulletin, December 1938, p. 12; Trust Companies, November 1938, p. 555. 

18 Scott on Trusts, sec. 193.2, p. 1049.

account in the corporation is too great for the shares not be be voted. 

In one case such corporations have been referred to as branches of the trusteeship.19 Whether they should be voted in person or by proxy depends upon the issues to the stockholders' meeting. If the matters to come up at the stockholders' meeting are specified in the notice, then the trust institution may be able to obtain from the corporation beforehand information sufficient to enable it to vote intelligently on the matters to be presented. In this case it should give a special proxy. It is seldom that a trust institution should give a general proxy to vote the shares of stock of a corporation in which the trust institution owns a controlling interest, for by giving a general proxy it delegates its duty to exercise its control of the corporation for the benefit of the trust. 

5. Minority stock. The really difficult decision as to voting comes when the trust institution is only a minority stockholder. 

No one trust institution owns even a substantial minority, much less a majority, of the stock of any of the nationally known corporations whose stock is listed on the exchanges. Yet the very fact that the stock of these corporations is so widely owned makes it all the more difficult for them to meet the quorum requirement usually a majority of the outstanding stock-for their stockholders' meetings.

The trust institution's retention or acquisition of shares of stock in the corporation is evidence that it has confidence in the management. Furthermore, it raises the presumption that the trust institution is familiar with the affairs of the corporation. While the stockholding of the trust institution may be very small compared with the total outstanding stock and the likelihood of its vote being determinative of any material issue may be remote, nevertheless, a trust institution should not adopt a policy of not voting in reliance upon the relative unimportance of its vote. If every stockholder took this view, the corporation could not obtain a quorum, the stockholders' meetings could not be held, and the stockholders' investments would be placed in jeopardy.

Assume, then, that the trust institution, even though a very small stockholder in a very large corporation, should, so far as practicable, vote the shares of stock it holds in trust accounts. 

19 In re Peabody's Estate, 260 N.W. 444, 99 A.L.R. 956, 218 Wis. 541 (1935).

a. Voting in person. If the trust institution is located in the same community as the one in which the main office of the corporation is located, it should, so far as practicable, vote the shares in person. 

The chances are that it will have in all its trust accounts combined a large and substantial holding of this stock either as original or as new investments; that it will be acquainted with the management; and that its director or officer attending the stockholders' meeting may be able to make a worthwhile contribution to the discussion. b. Voting by special proxy. Where the main office of the corporation is at a distance too great to justify attending the meeting, voting the shares of stock in a large corporation by special proxy is not nearly so much of a problem as is that of voting the shares of stock of a small corporation. In the first place, the basic facts about the large corporation already are known to the trust institution. In the second place, where the corporation is subject to the rules and regulations of the Securities and Exchange Commission, the corporation must submit, along with its solicitation of proxies, a proxy statement on the matters to be considered. This statement is designed to give stockholders the information they need for deciding how to vote.

In the rules and regulations of the Securities and Exchange Commission the following matters are specified on which the corporation soliciting proxies must furnish the stockholders detailed information: (1) The election of directors, (2) the election of officers other than directors, (3) the election of auditors, (4) any plan of compensation for officers, directors, or employees, (5) the amendment of the charter or by-laws, (6) the authorization or issuance of securities, (7) any plan involving the modification of any class of securities, (8) any plan of merger or consolidation, (9) the acquisition or disposition of property, (10) the restatement of any asset, capital, or surplus account of the corporation, (11) any report of the corporation or of its directors, officers, or committees, or any minutes or meetings of its directors or stockholders. If there is to be action on any matters not specifically referred to above, the proxy statement must describe briefly the substance of each matter in substantially the same degree of detail as that required for the matters specified. The practical effect of this requirement is that a trust

institution which holds in its trust accounts stock in corporations subject to the rules and regulations of the Securities and Exchange Commission usually is supplied with information that enables it intelligently to chart its course with respect to voting the shares of stock on matters contained in the notice. 

However, there are exceptions to the general rule that a trust institution should vote by special proxy even when information on the matter to be decided has been supplied by the corporation. There are instances in which the stockholder cannot vote intelligently on a point until he has heard the pros and cons presented at the stockholders' meeting. If it is important that the trust institution exercise its judgment at the time of the meeting, it should vote the shares in person; whereas, if it can exercisce its judgment in advance, it is proper for it to vote by special proxy.20 c. By general proxy. It is not practicable for the trust institution to attend all the stockholders' meetings of these nationally known corporations and vote the shares of stock in person. Nor is it practicable to vote by special proxy in all matters, particularly those of a routine nature, coming before the stockholders' meeting. It would not be reasonable to require the trustee to attend the meeting at a distant place when, in all probability, its influence would be negligible. It frequently is a practical question of voting by general proxy or not voting at all.

It is not surprising, therefore, that there has been a growing tendency to accept voting by general proxy in certain cases as a business necessity. The National Conference of Commissioners on Uniform 

State Laws, in drafting, approving, and recommending for enactment the Uniform Trusts Act giving trustees the power to vote by proxy, admits by the following official comment that in doing so it is yielding to business necessity:

Section 8 permits a trustee to vote corporate stock by proxy, if reasonable care is used. This is a delegation of trust powers and so technically illegal, but it is believed that such proxy voting is now actually done by trustees and is practically necessary in any reasonable plan of administration.21 

20 Scott on Trusts, sec. 193.3, pp. 1052-1053. 

21 Official comment as to Uniform Trusts Act, sec. 8; Prentice-Hall Trust Service. sec. 3850.

Similarly, the text writers are feeling their way toward approval of the voting of shares by general proxy. Professor Bogert says: 

In the absence of statute or controlling provision in the trust instrument, it would seem that the trustee should not be bound by a strict rule, either against or in favor of giving a proxy, but should be permitted to leave to another the voting of the stock when the circumstances are such that a normal owner would take like action, and required to vote the shares personally when the amount of the stock, place of the meeting, situation of the corporate affairs, and other features would lead one holding for his own benefit to go in person to the meeting to vote the stock.22

Professor Scott says: 

Although by the giving of a general proxy the trustee is entrusting to others the power to vote the shares, it is not ordinarily an improper delegation for him to do so. The trustee cannot be expected to attend all shareholders' meetings in person, and indeed would ordinarily not be justified in incurring the expense involved in such attendance. On the other hand, he should not be required to refrain from having the shares voted, since this might prevent the securing of a quorum at the meeting. In ordinary routine matters, therefore, where the trustee does not hold more than a small proportion of the shares of the corporation, it would seem that it is not improper for the trustee to execute a general proxy.23 

Mr. Shattuck in his revision of Loring's A Trustee's Handbook says quite frankly:

One of the duties of the trustee is to take active management and to protect the trust estate. The trustee must have the power, therefore, to exercise all of the ordinary rights of a stockholder. The giving of a general proxy would seem to constitute a violation of the duty not to delegate the administration of the trustee's office, but it is done every day and it seems entirely proper if the trustee is acquainted with the questions to be voted upon, has used due care in selecting the proxy, and has given suitable instructions with regard to the vote.24 

In reaching their decision as to voting comparatively small stockholdings, one of the problems that trust institutions face is, What is a small holding in a large corporation? What is a number so small as not to have any appreciable influence or as not to be a substantial portion of the whole number?

Several trust institutions have undertaken to apply mathematical standards such as 100 or 200 shares; 1/10 of 1% of the outstanding stock of the corporation; 5% of the productive property in the trust 

22 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 556, pp. 1778-1779. 

28 Scott on Trusts, sec. 193.3, p. 1052. 

24 Shattuck Revision, sec. 44, p. 128.

account and by these standards to plot their course as to voting shares of stock in person or by proxy or voting them at all. The prevailing opinion among trustmen seems to be that it is inadvisable to have any such mathematical standards if they are to be inflexible and applied in all cases; that, while such standards are convenient and may serve as guides, they easily may mislead a trust institution sometimes into voting by proxy or not voting shares of stock that for some valid reason should be voted in person. 

To what extent, then, should a trust institution vote by general proxy the shares of stock it holds in a large, nationally known corporation? Where the matters to come up at the stockholders' meeting are purely routine matters-such as the election of directorsthe giving of a general proxy would be, in effect, only a vote of confidence in the management and the trust institution would be justified in giving this vote of confidence; else it should get rid of the investment as soon as reasonably possible. As a matter of fact, a surprisingly large number of stockholders' meetings of corporations deal only with routine or, at least, non-controversial matters. d. Limited proxies. Out of an abundance of caution, it is thought that even in giving general proxies in cases in which only routine or non-controversial matters are expected to come up the trust institution should delete from the usual proxy form the provisions (a) relating to the ratification of the acts of the directors and officers and (b) authorizing the proxy to vote on other matters coming before the meeting. Despite the fact that the designated proxy usually is a responsible officer or employee of the corporation and naturally would vote in favor of the management, nevertheless the prevailing opinion among trustmen is that these two provisions should be stricken out, not for lack of confidence in the proxy or in the corporation but as a matter of general policy. 

RESPONSIBILITIES as to VotinG 

The responsibilities of a trust institution with respect to voting shares of stock held in trust accounts grow out of its duties and its powers. If a trust institution has the duty to vote or not to vote, it has the power to vote or not to vote; if it has the duty and the power, it has the responsibility to vote or not to vote; and, conse-

quently, it has the potential liability for losses to beneficiaries resulting from its voting or not voting. 

For Voting or Not Voting

If the trust institution is under a duty to its beneficiaries to vote the shares of stock it holds in their accounts, it has a responsibility to vote them and, if it does not do so, it will be liable for any determinable loss directly attributable to its failure to vote the shares.

For Way of Voting 

Likewise, if the trust institution is under a duty to vote the shares in person, it will be liable for any loss attributable to its delegating the duty of voting to a proxy. Also, if it is under a duty to vote by special or by limited proxy, it may be liable for voting by general proxy. 

For Substance of Voting 

In voting in person or by proxy the trust institution is responsible for exercising reasonable care to see that the vote cast is for the best interests of the beneficiaries.

Would a trustee, duly authorized by statute or by the trust instrument to vote by proxy and having exercised due care in selecting and informing its proxy, be liable for loss to the beneficiaries resulting from the proxy's negligent failure to vote or from his voting improperly? Although no case directly in point has been found, the application of this general principle of law would seem to relieve the trustee of liability: If in the administration of the trust the trustee properly employs an agent who negligently causes a loss to the trust estate, the trustee is not liable to the beneficiaries for the loss.25

The statement sometimes is made that, whereas a trustee will not incur any liability by not voting, it will incur a liability by voting wrong, and that, therefore, it had better not vote at all. All the foregoing discussion seems completely to negative the idea that a 

25 Scott on Trusts, sec. 225, p. 1190.

policy of not voting is the safe course to follow. Although in a given case it may be difficult for the beneficiaries to prove that they suffered direct damage from the trustee's failure to vote or to vote in a certain way, this fact does not lessen the trustee's responsibility or justify any lowering of the normal standard of care-that of the prudent man under the circumstances — required in every phase of trust administration.

POLICIES AS TO VOTING 

In line with the foregoing discussion and in the nature of a summarization of the points covered, the following is presented for the consideration of trust institutions as a partial statement of policies as to voting shares of stock held in trust accounts. 

As to Voting

It should be the general policy of a trust institution to vote rather than not to vote the shares of stock it holds in trust accounts. If it does not vote the shares, it should have a satisfactory, positive reason for not voting. 

Voting in Person

It should vote the shares in person- that is, through one of its directors, officers, or responsible staff members-when (1) it owns a majority of the stock of the corporation or (2), although it does not own a majority, it is important to the beneficiaries that the shares of stock be voted and it is impossible for the trust institution to know in advance of the stockholders' meeting what matters are to come up or before the meeting to obtain the necessary information upon which to base its decision how to vote. 

Voting by Special Proxy 

The trust institution should vote the shares of stock by special proxy when (1) it knows beforehand what matters are to come up and (2) it is supplied with information sufficient to act upon in reaching a decision as to voting on the issue presented.

Voting by General Proxy 

The trust institution should limit the use of the general proxy to the following combination of situations: (1) When it has clear authority to vote the given shares by general proxy, (2) when it is not practicable, (3) when it has full confidence in the present management, and (4) when only routine, non-controversial matters are to be voted upon.

Voting by Proxy Signed in Blank 

A trust institution should never under any circumstances sign a proxy in blank. Although it is a case in which it is proper for it to vote by general proxy and although only routine matters are to come up, it should fill in the name of a proxy-ordinarily the name of a director or officer of the corporation.

Right of Substitution 

A trust institution should not give its proxy the right of substitution, and if such right is given in the printed form it should be deleted. Although the abuse of this right may be remote, nevertheless it is the delegation of a duty to an unknown agent. So far as is known, Oklahoma is the only state which by statute gives trustees the power to vote shares of stock by proxy "with or without power of substitution." Texas expressly denies a proxy the power of substitution. 

Conflicts of Interest

If a conflict of interest between the trust institution and one of its trust accounts as to voting shares of stock should arise, the best interests of the trust accounts should control. If a conflict between two trust accounts should arise, the trust institution should, at all hazards, avoid partiality as between the two accounts. To take any other course would be to violate the fundamental duty of loyalty.26 

26 Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 543, p. 1722; Scott on Trusts, sec. 170, p. 856; Shattuck Revision, sec. 18, p. 62.

Contests 

In the case of a proxy fight, when one group of stockholders is trying to continue and the other group is trying to oust the present management, the trust institution should follow unhesitatingly and courageously the course that it regards as being for the best interest of the beneficiaries.

The easier course would be either to vote for the present management or not to vote at all. The trustee might defend its vote for the present management by saying that the fact that it was holding the stock as a trust investment was evidence that it was satisfied with present management; that, if it would not vote for the present management, it should dispose of the stock. It might defend its not voting at all by saying that it did not care to become party to a controversy and that its vote would not have any effect upon the final outcome of the contest. It is thought that neither of these is the proper course for a trust institution to take as a general policy. may be true that in a majority of cases the trust institution owes its beneficiaries a duty to support the present management. It may be true also that in some cases it owes its beneficiaries a duty not to take sides in the contest. But in every case the trust institution owes its beneficiaries a duty to study the points at issue in the contest and to base its decision as to voting upon what is for the best interest of the beneficiaries. It

PROCEDURES IN VOTING 

It is fully as important for a trust institution to have definite, established, well-understood procedures as to voting shares of stock held in trust accounts as it is for it to have sound policies. Its procedures are the expression of its policies. 

What follows is an attempt to present a procedure which is a composite of those brought to light by the Graduate School of Banking students participating in the nationwide survey mentioned earlier in this Study. 

Proxy Clerk

There should be some person in the trust department to whom all

notices of stockholders' meetings and all proxies are referred. This person, who may be known as the proxy clerk, should have direct responsibility for receiving and clearing notices of stockholders' meetings and proxies. 

Proxy Record

There should be a proxy record showing the name of the corporation, the date of the meeting, the disposition of the notice, and the proxy. With this record as a starting point, the trust institution will be able in the future to tell what it did with respect to voting every share of stock held in any trust account. 

Trust Investment Officer

After the initial record has been made, the trust investment officer is the logical person to receive from the proxy clerk the notice and proxy. This officer possesses or can acquire the necessary information about voting the stock. According to his authority, he makes the decision or the recommendation as to voting.

The following are a few of the points to which the trust investment officer should pay special attention: (1) The proxy must be executed in the exact name in which the stock stands on the books of the corporation. (2) If the shares can be voted only upon the direction of or with the approval of the settlor, beneficiary, or some third person, the trust investment officer should see that this direction or approval has been obtained. (3) If the trust institution holds shares of stock in the same corporation in several different capacities, in some of which it can and should vote and in others of which it cannot and should not vote, the proxy should indicate clearly which shares are being voted and which are not being voted. (4) If by the terms of the trust the trustee must obtain the direction or approval of the settlor, beneficiary, or a third party as to any investment changes, then the trustee should obtain the direction or approval of such person as to voting the shares on any issue that would be likely to affect in a material way the investment itself.

Trust Investment Committee

The trust investment committee should have responsibility for making the decisions as to voting shares of stock held in trust accounts. This does not mean that every notice of a stockholders' meeting and every proxy should be referred to and acted upon by this committee. It does mean that this committee has a prime responsibility in this respect. In certain cases and under certain circumstances it may authorize the trust investment officer or some other officer to make the decision as to voting; in other cases, the committee itself should make the decision; and in still other cases it should refer the matter to the board for its decision. 

Board of Directors

The voting of the shares of stock of a certain corporation or of the stock held in a certain trust account may be a matter of such importance as to require action by the board of directors rather than by any officer or committee. Suppose, for example, it is a question of liquidating or of selling or of reorganizing a corporation owned or controlled by the trust account or the stock in which is a large part of the trust property. The board, rather than the trust investment committee, might be the proper body to pass judgment upon voting the stock for or against such liquidation, sale, or reorganization.

Report of the Stockholders' Meeting 

A trust institution should keep as a part of its permanent records of the account a report of the stockholders' meeting in which shares of stock are voted. This is particularly true in the case of local or small corporations, the acts of whose stockholders may not become a matter of general knowledge. If the shares of stock are voted in person, the representative who attends the meeting should prepare a report on the main decisions arrived at in the meeting. A number of trust institutions send along with every signed proxy a letter requesting a report on the meeting. If the shares of stock should, as a matter of duty to the beneficiaries, be voted, it would seem that the trust institution, as a matter of its own protection, should keep a record of the vote.

Exact procedures in voting shares of stock held in trust accounts must be determined by the size and plan of organization of the trust institution. However, it is thought that each trust institution, whatever its size or plan of organization, should formulate its procedures in the light of these two principles which already have been discussed in a previous Study:27 (1) The exercise of discretionary powers by a trust institution should be founded upon an existing knowledge or an impartial finding of the facts, a definite recommendation based upon collective information, and a deliberate decision representing and reflecting group judgment; and (2) in deciding what persons-whether employees, officers, or directors-should exercise discretionary powers and whether they should act individually or jointly, and in what manner they should exercise such powers, the trust institution should live up to the standard of the prudent man under the circumstances.

CONCLUSIONS 

A trust institution has the power to vote shares of stock held in trust accounts, for the power to vote is one of the incidents of legal ownership of stock.

The test of the duty of a trust institution to the beneficiaries as to voting shares of stock seems to be what a person of ordinary prudence would do under simliar circumstances.

As to voting shares of stock by general proxy, the law seems to have advanced from the point where it generally was regarded as a delegation of duty-and, therefore, a breach of trust-for a trustee to give a general proxy and to be approaching the point at which the trust institution has the duty as well as the power, to vote by general proxy if an individual stockholder who as a man of ordinary prudence, having decided in the light of all the circumstances to vote the shares, would vote them by general proxy rather than in person or by special proxy.

In this whole field much of the law and, therefore, much of the practice, still is in process of development in response to business requirements. Trust institutions, by working together in the deter- 27 

Study Number 5, How Trust Institutions Exercise Discretionary Powers.

mination and formulation of definite and, more or less, uniform policies as to voting shares of stock in trust accounts, will aid substantially in this development. By so doing, they will make constructive contributions both to corporate management and to trust administration.
