Study Number 12 HOW DIRECTORS, OFFICERS, AND EMPLOYEES ARE  

HE satisfactory development of trust business—that is, the obtaining of new trust accounts-depends in no small degree upon the personal efforts of directors, officers, and employees of trust institutions. One metropolitan trust institution reports that over 50% of its new trust business comes from contacts developed by its directors, officers, and employees. Their personal efforts supplement or complete but do not take the place of other business development activities of trust institutions, such as advertising and personal representation.' Good service alone is not enough to assure a sustained and satisfactory development of trust business. For the best results good service, advertising, personal representation, and the personal efforts of directors, officers, and employees must go hand in hand.

While he was president of the American Bankers Association, Robert M. Hanes, president of the Wachovia Bank and Trust Company of North Carolina, said:

While it is true that the successful and faithful performance of a service is the best argument for new business, nevertheless, we must admit that the development of corporate fiduciary service will never reach the stage where all persons of means will ¹Throughout this Study the term personal representation, instead of solicitation, is used because it is thought that representation is more expressive of the true function of the officer or employee of a trust institution who is engaged in business development. For the same reason the person so engaged is referred to as trust representative and not as solicitor or new business man. See A Statement of Principles of Trust 

Institutions, Art. VI, sec. 2, and Study Number 1, Plain English for Trust Business, pp. 17-18. 247

naturally turn to a trust institution of their own accord. Continuous salesmanship is necessary to maintain an adequate volume of business in the department.* 

This Study is, in the main, a report of the ways in which the directors, officers, and employees of trust institutions are developing trust business. It is not a study of the theoretical ways in which they can or should develop trust business but of the practical ways in which they actually are doing so. It is based upon the experiences of 

57 trust institutions in 40 cities in 26 states. Each of these institutions was asked to state how its own directors, officers, and employees were developing its trust business. This Study is the composite answer of these 57 institutions.

Although analysis of these replies shows that directors, officers, and employees are developing trust business in the same ways, it is thought best, even at the cost of some repetition, to treat each of the three groups separately and make each of the three sections complete within itself.

HOW DIRECTORS ARE  

Directors of trust institutions are developing the trust business of their institutions (1) by placing their own trust business with it; (2) by letting people know that they have done so; (3) by saying a good word for the trust department to their family, friends, and associates; (4) by giving their institution the names of prospective customers; (5) by arranging for interviews between trust representatives and prospective customers; (6) by supplying information about prospective customers; (7) by accepting special assignments of business development; (8) by arranging for public presentations of trust topics; (9) by handling criticisms of trust service; and (10) by serving on the trust business development committee of their institution. 

Placing Their Own Trust Business

The most effective way directors are developing trust business is by placing their own trust business with their institution—that is, by naming it executor and trustee under their wills, trustee of their living trusts or insurance trusts, agent, and in other fiduciary 

2 Know Your Trust Department, Trust Bulletin, November 1939, p. 10 at p. 13.

capacities. A number of trust institutions report with gratification and appreciation that they have been named in the wills of every one of their directors or that every director is making use of their trust service.

When directors place their own trust business with their institutions they accomplish three things in business development. First, they bring trust business to the trust institution, for, as a rule, the trust accounts of directors are desirable. Second, in working out their own estate plans, they acquire a knowledge and appreciation of trust service which they use to good effect in talking with other people about their affairs. Third, having so placed their own trust business, they are more disposed to encourage their friends and associates to do the same. 

Second only in effectiveness to placing their own trust business is placing that of the members of their families and of their business enterprises-in fact, placing all the trust business they control. 

However, the placing of their own trust business and the trust business which they control is most effective and helpful in business development when it is so placed on normal terms and conditionsparticularly as to the trust institution's powers and compensation. 

The wills of directors (which after their death become recorded documents) set precedents, good or bad, which others follow. 

Channing H. Cox, president of the Old Colony Trust Company of Boston, Massachusetts, thus emphasizes the importance of a director's naming his own institution:

When a director has (named his institution under his will), he can, with much greater force, call the trust department to the favorable attention of others. A director by expressing and showing his belief in his own bank may be the greatest potential source of trust business that can be possible. 

Letting People Know 

Directors are developing trust business by letting people know that they have named their own institutions. In some cases they are doing this publicly, but in most cases privately. 

1. Publicly. For the past eight years or longer the St. Louis Union 

3 How the Board of Directors Can Aid in the Development of Trust Business, Pro ceedings, Eleventh Mid-Winter Trust Conference, Trust Division, A.B.A., 1930, p. 38 at p. 53.

Trust Company of St. Louis, Missouri, has published at least twice a year an advertisement containing the statement, "I am making use of the services of the St. Louis Union Trust Company in connection with my estate and have named it as my executor or trustee," followed by the facsimile signature of each director. Each director of the company expressly consents to this use of his name; and the public reaction to this advertisement, it is said, has been very favorable. 

2. Privately. Directors are taking occasion in conversation or by letter to tell their friends and associates that they have named their institution executor or trustee under their wills. Since the directors of a bank or trust company usually are leaders in the community, a word from a director to the effect that he has named his bank or trust company carries a great deal of weight with other people. 

It is understood, of course, that the trust institution itself would not, without the express authority of the director, tell people that he had named it in a fiduciary capacity; for this is a confidential matter, and the confidentiality applies as strictly to the affairs of directors, officers, and employees as it does to the affairs of other people. 

On the importance of directors not only naming their own institution but letting people know that they have done so, the late W. J. 

Kieferdorf of the Bank of America National Trust and Savings 

Association, San Francisco, California, said:

Besides being generally men of wealth themselves and having estates that some day may be a source of profit to the bank, they can attract most desirable business. Naming their bank executor of their wills is evidence of complete confidence in the institution of which they are the responsible managers. How infectious is the statement of one director to another, "Well, I have made my will and named our bank as executor and I have filed it in our trust department.' " 5 Saying a Good Word for the Trust Department Directors are developing trust business by saying a good word for the trust department whenever the opportunity offers. This is being done on golf courses, in clubs, at dinner tables, and in social and recreational as well as in purely business contacts. The subject some- * Trust Companies, April 1935, p. 462. 

5 How to Increase the Earnings of the Trust Department During Its Early Years of 

Operation, Proceedings, Third Regional Trust Conference, 1925, p. 15 at p. 17.

times is introduced by the simple statement, "I have just made my will and named 

Bank and Trust Company my executor and trustee."

The following experiences reported by two of the 57 institutions are not unusual:

A director, while on a trip with a customer of the bank, started a discussion about his own plans for his estate and his family's future. 

The conversation led to an appointment for the director's traveling companion with a trust officer of his institution. As a direct result of the casual conversation the director shortly thereafter had the satisfaction of having his friend become a trust customer of his bank. "You need some sound advice from a good trustman. I shall ask one of the trust officers of the bank of which I am a director to call on you." This was the end of a conversation between two temporarily hospitalized fellow convalescents. The burden and bother of investment management under an individual trusteeship by one out of touch with things financial had been the topic of conversation. An appointment was made by telephone with a trust officer. An agency account was suggested. During a brief second call the plan was explored in detail. Thereafter an agency agreement was executed. 

Soon after this agency became operative, the director himself made an identical arrangement of his own estate affairs. Both these accounts are substantial. The first customer had had no previous contacts with the institution. He was influenced to turn to the trust institution wholly by the recommendation of the director.

The second experience also suggests how a director himself sometimes is helped with respect to his own affairs by discussing the subject with others.

In introducing the subject of wills and trusts and in saying a good word for the trust institution, directors usually are accepted as fellow businessmen or fellow professional men or friends or neighbors with whom one can talk freely, rather than as representatives of the bank or trust company.

Directors are proving helpful also in cultivating for their institutions the good will of their own lawyers--both their personal attorneys and those of their corporations or firms.

Giving Names of Prospective Customers 

Directors are developing trust business by giving their institution the names of prospective customers. Through business, professional, social, recreational, civic, and other contacts, they hear of new enterprises and of newcomers to the community who may need trust service. They hear also of new turns in the affairs of long-established businesses or of long-time residents, which create needs for trust service.

Directors are especially helpful in keeping mailing lists revised and up to date. They are suggesting new names to be added and old names to be left off. Thus, they are helping the employed staff to focus attention and effort on those who are desirable prospective customers, which concentration is resulting both in the acquisition of profitable business and in economy of effort and money in acquiring new business.

Arranging Interviews In developing trust business, directors are arranging for interviews between trust representatives and prospective customers. In some instances they are doing so in the course of casual conversations. For example, the person with whom the director is talking expresses an interest in the trust services of the director's bank or trust company. 

The director says, "Let me arrange with Mr. to talk over these matters with you." The person consents. Thereupon the director gets in touch with a trust representative, and the initial interview is arranged. In other cases the director makes the appointment by telephone. In still other cases he gives the trust representative a note or card of introduction. If the prospective customer resides in another community, the director may give the trust representative a formal letter of introduction, going into some detail as to the nature of the call and bespeaking a sympathetic hearing by the person to whom the letter is addressed. Trust representatives testify to the great value of such an introduction by a director. They say that it not only opens the door for the initial interview but also smooths the way for a complete and businesslike discussion of the subject. 

As to the part its directors are playing in business development, one southern trust institution says:

The directors of have always been most willing to cooperate in helping us to secure all kinds of business, and we find them frequently useful in influencing friends and associates to name in their wills and trust agreements. For example, recently one of our directors, who is a member of the trust committee, wrote a letter to a friend in another city which influenced the friend, a substantial businessman, to name the in his will.

In a similar vein, an eastern trust institution appraises the value of its directors in trust business development: 

During the past six months some of these directors (members of the trust committee) have not only referred us, with letters of introduction, to some very profitable business, but in a few cases they have actually gone out themselves to call on people who have subsequently named us in their wills. 

When directors arrange interviews for the trust representative, still another purpose is served. The directors themselves are relieved of any feeling or, more likely, of any dread of responsibility for discussing the details of trust business with prospective customers. If directors felt that, once they mentioned the subject, they would be expected to carry the discussion through to some conclusion, they might hesitate to mention the matter at all. But realizing that all they need to do is to introduce the subject and that a trust representative is available and competent to carry on to whatever extent is desired or is needed, directors do not hesitate to broach the subject and arrange the initial interview. 

Supplying Information

Directors are helping trust representatives to plan interviews with prospective customers. A director may know the proper approach for the trust representative to make the points to be emphasized, the points not to be mentioned unless the person himself brings them up for discussion, and the general objectives of the estate plan of the person to be interviewed. He may know things about the prospective customer, his family, or his affairs that the person himself will not be likely to mention.

As the trust representative goes further into the estate plan, he may need additional information-possibly about the prospective customer himself that he may not feel free to seek from any person other than a director or an officer of the bank or trust company itself. 

It may be information, not of a banking or financial but of a personal nature, that the director rather than the officer would be able to

supply. Thus the director, serving both his institution and his friend, the prospective customer, is a liaison, supplying the information essential to the working out of the most practicable estate plan and the rendering of the best trust service. 

Accepting Special Assignments 

Directors are accepting special assignments for business development. Mention has been made already of their arranging for the initial interview. But many directors are going further than this. At the request of the president or the head of the trust department or of the business development department, directors themselves are making initial calls upon prospective customers. They are accompanying the trust representative in making the initial call. They are discussing with the prospective customer certain phases of his estate plan as to which he thinks the advice of the director, as a fellow businessman or fellow professional man as one intimately acquainted with his affairs or his family, may be especially helpful. 

While directors are not expected to accept frequent, arduous, or time-consuming assignments for business development, they welcome the opportunity to accept occasional and definite assignments to do specific things. They appreciate being asked to do such things, even though in a particular case they may not be able to comply with the request.

Arranging Public Presentations 

One way of developing trust business is through public discussions of trust service by trustmen. From time to time trustmen are invited to speak on trust subjects at meetings of civic clubs, chambers of commerce, and women's clubs. Directors, who are members of these men's organizations and whose wives and daughters are members of these women's organizations, are arranging for representatives of their banks or trust companies to speak to these groups on different phases of trust service.

Handling Criticisms 

In a negative but none the less effective way directors are developing trust business by handling criticisms of trust service. Some-

times they are approached with criticisms that might not reach an officer. A particular criticism may be just, or it may be unjust due to misinformation or lack of information. The director approached may not know the answer to the criticism. But by bringing it to the attention of the management he gives the latter an opportunity to make the needed correction or to supply the lacking information. In this way directors are removing barriers and, indirectly, helping to develop trust business.

Serving on Business Development Committees 

Practically every trust institution makes some provision for the consideration by the directors of the business development activities of the institution. In most institutions these matters are brought to the board or to the executive committee or to the trust committee. 

A very few trust institutions have a special business development committee of directors and officers known by different names--such as Business Development Committee, New Business Committee, or 

Public Relations Committee. As members of these committees, directors are having definite and effective parts in business development by approving adequate advertising and business development budgets, by passing judgment upon the business development plans and activities, and by giving business development the standing it deserves among the organized activities of the institution. 

HOW OFFICERS ARE DEVELOPING TRUST BUSINESS 

It seems advisable to classify officers into executive officers, trust officers, and banking department officers. Executive officers include the chairman of the board, the president, the executive vice president, and the head of the trust department. Trust officers include the administrative officers of the trust department, including the specialists such as the investment men and the tax men. Banking department officers include all the departmental officers outside the trust department.

While all officers have many ways in common of developing trust business and while most of these ways are the same as those already ascribed to directors, nevertheless, different groups of officers have distinctive means and opportunities for developing trust business.

This Study contains no discussion of the ways in which officers and employees in the business development department or division of the bank or trust company-trust representatives, as they are called-develop trust business. A discussion of the technique of developing trust business through personal representation is beyond the scope of this Study. Rather, this Study is a discussion of the ways by which the other officers and employees are helping trust representatives to develop trust business.

Creating Friendly Atmosphere 

Executive officers are developing trust business by creating and maintaining an atmosphere friendly to such effort. They are setting the sail of their institution for business development. One institution goes forward in the development of its trust business while another stands still or goes backward, although to all outward appearances one is putting as much energy into the effort as the other. Frequently the difference in results is attributable largely to the difference in attitude of the executive officers toward business development. 

In their replies many of the trust institutions attribute their success in developing trust business largely to the friendly, stimulating attitude of their presidents. 

When he was president of the Washington Bankers Association in 1936, W. M. Jenkins of the Everett Trust & Savings Bank, Everett, Washington, said:

The attitude of the ordinary bank executive toward his trust department is almost sure to be decidedly influenced by the attitude of the head of the bank. I am sure, in our own case, we would never have gotten very far if it had not been for the constant support and encouragement given us by our president."

Executive officers are developing trust business in two other ways. 

First, they are assuming full responsibility for seeing that the trust business development plans of their institution are executed. Once the plans have been adopted, they have as much executive responsibility to make a success of them as they have to make a success of other plans adopted by their institution. Second, they are dealing 

See the chapter on Personal Representation of the American Institute of Banking textbook on Trust Business. 

The Bank Executive's Viewpoint, Trust Bulletin, June 1936, p. 3 at p. 7.

direct with the directors with respect to placing their own trust business with their institution. In many cases the president or the chairman of the board is the only officer who feels free to taik with directors about their own affairs.

Giving Technical Advice and Guidance 

In developing trust business trust officers are supplying the technical advice and guidance needed in estate planning. This refers, of course, to business and financial, not to legal, advice and guidance. 

In the largest institutions specialists in estate planning may be found in the business development department itself; but in other trust institutions, the administrative trust officers supply the technical advice and guidance. 

Placing Their Own Trust Business 

Executive officers, trust officers, and banking department officers alike are developing trust business by placing their own trust business with their institution. Trust institutions are making special efforts to have every officer in every department name the institution executor or trustee under his will. This means desirable trust business. But, what perhaps is even more important from the point of view of business development, it means that the officer, in working out his own plan, has his interest in trust service stimulated and, in naming his own institution, sets an example which his friends and associates follow. 

In 1929, A. C. Robinson, then president of the Peoples-Pittsburgh 

Trust Company of Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, speaking as one president to other presidents, said:

The first obligation of the president to his trust department is that he should have his will made in its favor and that he should commit to it any trust business which he may have. He owes this as a matter of good faith to his company and to his customers, and as showing his faith in his own institution, but beyond that, his action should be based not only on faith but on actual knowledge of the safety and skill with which his interests will be protected. He is on the inside and knows or ought to know in regard to these things. If he does not have confidence enough in his own institution to make it his executor and trustee, or if he does not commit to it any trust business he has, he has no place as president of the institution or right to solicit trusts from others. Every trust officer has a right to demand of his president this proof of interest

and sincerity. His next obligation is to see that as many as possible of his directors, officers, and employees do the same thing. Officers, as well as directors, are placing their trust business with their institution on normal terms, particularly as to compensation. 

The late Colonel Francis H. Fries, of Winston Salem, North Caro lina, after naming his daughter and the institution of which he was president, co-executors, added this paragraph to his will: 

I realize that the Wachovia Bank & Trust Company will give much time and attention to the settlement of my estate and the administration of the trust funds, that they will keep the books and make the investments and that my daughter will keep posted through their efforts, so that with her agreement, I direct said trust company shall receive the commissions charged in its schedule at the time of my death." 

Other presidents of trust institutions have incorporated in their wills testimonials of their faith and confidence in their institutions. 

The will of Festus J. Wade of St. Louis, Missouri, contained the following statement: 

I have had every opportunity, over a period of years, to know the manner in which the Mercantile Trust Company (now the Mercantile-Commerce Bank and Trust Company) manages estates and trusts for others. I have full confidence in the capacity and integrity of the company to manage my estate judiciously and capably, after my death. It is for that reason I have named the Mercantile Trust Company as my sole executor and trustee. I recommend that my wife and children defer to its judgment in all matters connected with the administration of my estate. 

Letting People Know 

Officers are developing trust business by letting people know that they have placed their own trust business with their institution. It is much easier for them than it is for directors to do this. There is a more or less standard way by which officers let people know that they have named their institution. It is by displaying on their desk a card containing the information. The card is either framed or is put under the glass top of the officer's desk in a position and at an angle naturally to be seen by any person at his desk. The wording usually is, in substance, as follows: "I have made my will and named the 

Bank and Trust Company my Executor. I recommend that you do the same," followed by the facsimile signature of the 

8 The President and His Trust Department, Proceedings, Seventh Regional Trust 

Conference, 1929, p. 107 at p. 109. 

Trust Companies, August 1931, p. 219 at p. 220.

officer.10 These cards attract the attention of people who come to the officers' desks and frequently lead to discussions of trust matters. 

The reaction of the public to the use of these cards seems to be favorable.

Officers have found that telling people their own estate plans is a very effective way of stimulating interest in trust matters. Mr. Kieferdorf, in the address already referred to," said that in some of his talks he had shown his insurance trust agreement and explained how he had provided for his dependents if he should predecease them. 

Such a disclosure, he said, had the additional virtue of indicating that he was practicing what he preached. 

Saying a Good Word for the Trust Department Banking department officers-particularly lending officers-are especially helpful in developing trust business by saying a good word for the trust department in the course of their daily work. As they carry on discussions and negotiations with the banking department customers in connection with loans, lines of credit, financial arrangements, and other business and banking affairs, they acquire useful knowledge as to customers' needs for trust service. It is natural and easy for them to mention trust service and to suggest to customers that they might like to talk over with a trust representative their estate plans or their possible needs for corporate trust or corporate agency service.

Merrel P. Callaway, while he was a vice president of the Guaranty 

Trust Company of New York, said,

No trust department can reach its full measure, or attain its highest accomplishments, unless it has the full and whole-hearted support of the bank as a whole, and particularly the high executive officials. . . . And the best trust business, both personal and corporate, comes from the depositors and customers who do business with the banking department, usually having their only contacts with the banking officials, and from the business and social affiliations of the top and leading officers of the institution, which business can in large part be obtained only if these officers cooperate with the trust department to that end. Your trustmen will do most of the work, but they must have you to work with them, and help them open up, and sometimes close the busi- 10 For samples of such cards and an account of the experience of trust institutions in the use of them, see Publicity Bulletin, Vol. 5, No. 9, Committee on Publicity, Trust Company Division, American Bankers Association, April 1926, pp. 6-10. 

11 Supra, p. 250.

ness. It is astonishing, at times, to see how effective a word from the head of the bank is in securing new and valuable trust business." 

Giving Names of Prospective Customers 

Banking department officers are, perhaps, the most fruitful source of names of desirable prospective trust customers. In the conduct of their banking business these prospective customers come to the officers instead of the officers going to them. This means that the banking department officers, in a day's time, see and establish contact with many more prospective customers than they could if they had to go to see people in their homes, offices, or places of business. They have unusual opportunities to find out who are desirable prospective trust customers. The names supplied by the banking department officers frequently are the most carefully selected names on the list of prospective customers. prospec- They are especially helpful also in keeping mailing lists of tive trust customers revised and up to date. As already indicated, they know, sometimes better and sooner than the trust officers themselves, the names that should be added to and those that should be taken off the lists.

Arranging Interviews Banking department officers are developing trust business by arranging interviews between prospective customers and trust representatives. In some cases they do so while the prospective customer still is at their desk, by having the trust representative come to the officer's desk and meet the prospective customer and then and there arrange for the interview. In other cases they take the prospective customer over to the trust department or the business development department. In still other cases they arrange for interviews by telephone calls or by notes of introduction.

Accepting Special Assignments 

These officers are developing trust business by accepting special assignments. They are too busy with their own work to devote a 

12 Trust Department Responsibilities of Bankers, Trust Bulletin, July 1938, p. 7 at p. 9.

great deal of time directly to trust business development. Yet experience teaches that few officers in any trust institution are too busy or too absorbed in their primary work to accept special assignments of business development. The special assignment may be writing a letter, making a call, or sitting in on or even participating in initial interviews with the prospective customers.

A few years ago the president of one large trust institution, which was very successful in developing trust business through the personal efforts of its officers and department heads, had a weekly meeting with them and had each officer or department head accept the name of just one person to see during the week, not to discuss the details of trust business but to arrange an interview with a trust representative. The distinctive features of this plan were (1) that the president of the institution conducted the meetings, (2) that each officer and department head was asked to see one-but only one-prospective customer a week, and (3) that the officers or department heads were not expected to discuss the details of trust business but only to arrange interviews for the trust representatives. 

Tom K. Smith, president of the Boatmen's National Bank of St. 

Louis, Missouri, at one time president of the American Bankers 

Association, said:

The president of a bank can take a very definite part in the field of securing new business. He should by all means be available for counsel when questions regarding newspaper advertising, direct-by-mail and other sales campaigns are under consideration. He can suggest that a call be made on a certain individual or corporation. In important situations, he should be available to write a letter, make a telephone call, or, if the situation warrants, he should count it a privilege to make a personal call. 

I welcome these opportunities in my own bank, as I like to know at first hand how the public regards our bank, and then I think it helps me to understand better the character and qualifications of the men who work in our new business department.13 

It has been estimated that 80% of the new trust business of a bank should come from the customers of the banking department.“

Officers and members of the staff of the banking department have closer association with people and more accurate knowledge of their affairs than anyone else in the institution. It is not surprising, therefore, that, according to so many of the replies received, the banking 

13 Trust Department Policies as Seen by the Bank President, Trust Division, A. B. A. 

Year Book, 1934-35, p. 223 at p. 230. 14 "Drew, J. E., Meeting Tomorrow's Challenge Today, Trust Bulletin, February 

1941, p. 12 at p. 14.

department officers are having a major part in the trust business development of their institution. 

HOW EMPLOYEES ARE  

The term employees includes both junior officers and employees. 

It is impossible to draw a clear line between them because in some institutions there are some persons who are designated as officers-as assistant cashiers, assistant treasurers, assistant secretaries, assistant trust officers—who if they were in other institutions and were clothed with the same amount of authority and responsibility would not have official titles and, therefore, would be classified as employees. 

The highly strategic position which the regular employees hold in business development lies in the fact that a very large percentage for some institutions it has been estimated as high as 80%-of the customers of the bank seldom or never come in direct contact with the senior officers. Their contacts are largely with the tellers and vault attendants. These employees, therefore, hold key positions in trust business development.

Making Special Appeal to Elderly People 

Employees, when younger people, make a special appeal to some elderly people. Timid, shy, hesitating, uncertain old people sometimes unburden themselves freely to a patient, sympathetic, understanding teller or vault attendant when they would not do so to the president or the head of the trust department or any executive officer. 

In such cases employees sometimes are able to obtain the trust business of these elderly people when no one else in the bank or trust company could do so.

Making Direct Approach to Young People 

Employees, also when they are younger people, have a direct approach to the other young people of the community. The young people in the bank or trust company meet the other young people of the community in social, recreational, civic, and other relationships. 

These other young people constitute the next generation of trust

customers. Many of them even now are prospective customers for living trusts, insurance trusts, and agencies.

Among the more specific ways in which employees are developing trust business are the following: (1) Placing their own trust business with their institution; (2) giving names of prospective customers; (3) saying a good word for the trust department; (4) accepting special assignments; (5) handling criticisms of trust service. These ways are, in the main, the same as those already ascribed to both the directors and officers. So they are, and the reported experience of trust institutions justifies the listing of so many of the same points for all three groups. However, with respect to employees, different considerations or, at any rate, different points of emphasis come into play. 

Placing Their Own Trust Business

Trustmen experienced in business development have said that, if they were charged with responsibility for developing the trust business of a bank or trust company, one of the first things they would do would be to try to obtain the will of every employee in every department of the institution, the settlement of whose estate would require the services of an executor or administrator. Such services would be required if the estate amounted to more than a few hundred dollars. These trustmen go on to explain that they would want the wills of these employees mainly because of the moral effect the obtaining of the will would have upon both the employees and the general public.

The effect upon the employees would be to arouse and stimulate their interest in trust service. The placing of their own estates and the making of their own wills would serve to introduce them to the actualities of trust business. Naming the institution executor and trustee would make them feel all the more that they are part and parcel of the institution in which they are employed. However, these trustmen warn that no pressure should be brought to bear upon employees to name the bank or trust company. Mr. Kiefendorf had this to say about the importance of employees naming their own institution: . . . your activity in this respect (obtaining the wills of the employees) should not cease until you have obtained the will of every member of your institution from the 1

president down to the humblest employee. Personal experience in making and signing a will is the most effective object lesson and demonstrates most clearly to an employee what a will is and how it should be executed. Having gone through the process himself he can speak familiarly and with some degree of assurance to an inquiring patron.15

The effect upon the general public of employees naming their own institution would be that it would become their testimonial of their own confidence. Members of the general public who were kinsmen, neighbors, and friends of a deceased employee, when they learned that he had named his own institution would accept and would be influenced by his act as a testimonial of confidence in the institution. 

Giving Names of Prospective Customers 

All of the employees of a trust institution do not have equal opportunities to give the names of prospective customers. Bookkeepers, accountants, clerks, file clerks, and others employed in operative activities seldom come in direct contact with customers. The banking and savings department tellers, the safe deposit vault custodians, the outside employees looking after real property and mortgages, and the other employees who, in the course of the day's work, come in direct contact with customers, have the best opportunities to hear of prospective customers and to pass their names on to the persons directly responsible for business development. Tellers and vault custodians, in the course of their daily work, see or hear of past-due coupons presented for payment, credit inquiries regarding applications for additional insurance, checks representing the proceeds of insurance or the distributive shares of estates, purchases of large blocks of securities, substantial increases in commercial accounts, or important real property transactions. In all such cases an alert employee at once senses the possibility of a trust customer. 

In some trust institutions the secretaries to executive officers are especially helpful in developing trust business by reminding the officers of prospective trust customers, and having the officers arrange with customers for introductions to and interviews with trust representatives. 

15 Op. cit., p. 15 at p. 17.

Saying a Good Word for the Trust Department Employees are stimulating prospective customers into action by saying a good word for the trust department.

The following incident illustrates how an employee was instrumental in obtaining a desirable trust account: The trust department was on the third floor. An elevator operator overheard an elderly woman remark to her companion, "Every time I come in the bank, I think how careless I am in not having my will prepared." He courteously asked the woman if she knew the trust officer of the bank. When she said that she did not, he suggested to her that he be permitted to introduce the trust officer to her, so that she would know where to go and whom to see when she was ready to discuss the planning of her will. She accepted his suggestion. The introduction was made. Within two weeks' time the woman had signed her will naming the bank as executor. 

Because of friendships that frequently develop between employees and customers, a good word for the trust department spoken by the employee may be received more readily and may stimulate action more quickly and effectively than the same word or even a better word spoken by a senior officer or a director.

Accepting Special Assignments 

Rarely are employees, unless they are engaged directly in business development activities, expected to make calls upon prospective customers or to discuss with them the details of trust business. They may not be prepared to do so or, even if they are prepared, they may not have the time to engage in such discussions. But employees frequently help to develop trust business by introducing the trust representative to the prospective customer and even by sitting in on initial interviews.

The officer in charge of business development of a trust institution in the Middle West says:

I have found it exceedingly helpful in the past, when an employee would suggest that John Doe might be worthy of cultivation, to arrange to take the employee along to make the introduction personally, instead of merely arranging it. Then the employee, after the preliminary remarks were over with, would sit in on the ensuing conversation with his prospective customer. He would, perforce, be a passive spectator, but from the sparring he would pick up helpful items. Such a procedure is excellent in building and maintaining employee morale and spurs the employee on

in an effort to suggest other worthwhile names or leads. Should the employee have failed to exercise the proper discrimination in his selection, it becomes apparent before his very eyes in a forceful but not unpleasant manner. Frequently the prospective customer is on close friendly terms with the employee and he is pleased and flattered to have his favorite teller, say, drop into his place of business and bring an officer along to become acquainted. The prospective customer expands and sometimes fairly glows with pride and pleasure, and the officer makes rapid progress in establishing the degree of confidence necessary to successful trust relationships. 

Handling Criticisms 

By the proper handling of criticisms of trust service, employees are removing barriers and thereby making substantial contributions to business development. Criticisms frequently come first to the ears of employees. People make to, or in the hearing of, employees critical statements about the trust institution or about one of its officers or about trust institutions, or trust business, or trust service in general, that they would not make to, or in the hearing of, senior officers or directors.

Seldom is an employee himself called upon to answer these criticisms. He may not know the answers. Even if he knows the answers, he may not be the proper one to give them. But it is within his province and his line of duty to bring these matters to the attention of the proper person in the institution. By doing this in the spirit, not of a talebearer but of loyalty to his institution, he gives the officers an opportunity to deal with the criticisms in the proper way. 

CONCLUSIONS 

The replies of the 57 trust institutions upon which this Study is based and the opinions of the outstanding bankers and trustmen quoted seem to justify the following conclusions:

The satisfactory development of trust business depends in no small measure upon the personal efforts of directors, officers, and employees. 

For the best results their efforts must be combined with good service, good advertising, and good personal representation.

Directors, officers, and employees alike are developing trust business by placing their own trust business with their institution, by letting people know they have done so, by saying a good word for their trust department whenever opportunity offers, by supplying the names of prospective customers, and by accepting special assignments.

. 

267 For their distinctive parts, executive officers are creating an atmosphere friendly to business development activities; trust officers are giving technical advice and guidance in estate planning; banking department officers are making bank customers trust conscious; and employees, when younger people, are making a special appeal to elderly people and are maintaining direct contact with other young people.

Thus the several groups have many ways in common, as well as distinctive ways, of developing trust business. 

If this study shows any one thing more clearly than any other, it is that where there is a will there is a way. Directors, officers, and employees who have the will to develop trust business have at hand effective ways of doing so. The replies of these 57 trust institutions prove conclusively that a very large part of the trust business development now going on in this country is directly attributable to the personal efforts of directors, officers, and employees and that each of the three groups has a full part to play.

Trust business development is not a thing apart, to be engaged in only by those especially employed for the purpose. While trust representatives intensively cultivate the crop, the active aid of directors, of officers, and of employees is needed both in preparing the ground and in reaping the harvest.

Directors, officers, and employees who are active in developing trust business are having a double satisfaction in realizing that they are helping their institution to be more profitable and useful and that they are rendering a needed service to the people whom they influence to become trust customers.
