Study Number 13  

trustee?" 

HAT qualifications, characteristics, or earmarks should I observe in the bank I might choose as my executor and This question was asked, first in person and later by letter, of the 

Director of the Trust Research Department of The Graduate School of Banking by a young engineer who recently had moved from a distant state into a community which had four well established banks engaged in the trust business. Although he already was convinced that he should name a bank as his executor and trustee, he did not know which one of the four to name or how to go about selecting it. At that time he may not have established a permanent banking connection; else, other things being equal, he probably would have been inclined to name his own bank. 

The Director of the Trust Research Department, in turn, submitted the question to the students then majoring in Trusts in The Graduate School of Banking and received 38 replies of students in 34 banks and trust companies in 31 communities of 17 states. 

This Study is largely a composite of these 38 replies, with this qualification: The technical earmarks-those which would have significance only or mainly for lawyers, accountants, or bank examiners-have been omitted or subordinated, and special emphasis has been put upon those earmarks which, it is thought, have the most significance for laymen.

Out of the large number of earmarks suggested by the students, only ten are selected for discussion, and these are selected because, 269

from the prospective customer's point of view, they seem to be the more basic ones. The earmarks selected are the following: (1) good general reputation, (2) sound financial condition, (3) present management that inspires confidence, (4) experience in the trust business, (5) adequate and competent staff, (6) adequate organization, (7) convenient physical layout and equipment, (8) good general attitude toward its trust business, (9) definite trust policies, and (10) good relations with persons with whom it has direct dealings in its trust business. No attempt is made to list or discuss these earmarks in the order of their importance or significance in the selection of a bank as one's executor and trustee. 

GENERAL REPUTATION 

One earmark of a good trust institution is that it has a good general reputation, both as a bank and as a trust institution, throughout the community in which it is located. 

As a Bank

The general reputation of a bank is a compound of many elements. Among these elements are the bank's historical background, its rate and consistency of growth, the way it has weathered financial or economic crises in the past, its earnings record, the standing of its former as well as that of its present directors and officers, and its pervading atmosphere, past and present, as to friendliness, alertness, accuracy, promptness, and soundness in banking transactions. 

As a Trust Institution

A bank engaged in the trust business has a general reputation as a trust institution distinct from its reputation as a bank. It may be well known as a bank and little known as a trust institution, or vice versa.

Although information about individual trust accounts, trust customers, and trust beneficiaries is confidential so far as the bank is concerned, these rules of confidentiality do not apply to trust customers or beneficiaries. They are free and, usually, willing to tell their experiences with and to reveal their general attitude toward the bank with which they have done or are doing trust business. If its

customers and beneficiaries generally have been pleased and satisfied with the trust service they received, this is a very good sign that the bank is a good trust institution. However, in reaching a conclusion as to the general reputation of a bank one should beware of being over-influenced by the opinions of one or two persons only. Also, in this connection, one should take into account the fact that dissatisfied persons usually are readier and freer than satisfied persons to recount their experiences and express their opinions.

It is easily possible even for a newcomer to a community within a reasonably short time by discreet inquiry to find out the general reputation of the different banks of the community both as banks and as trust institutions.

The importance of a person's looking for this earmark is that a bank which has a good general reputation is likely to have earned it by rendering good banking and trust service in the past and now to be zealous to keep its good reputation by maintaining its standards of service.

FINANCIAL CONDITION 

Another earmark of a good trust institution is that it is in a sound financial condition.

Statement of Condition 

Evidence of a bank's financial condition, which is immediately available to any inquirer, is its published statement of condition. The form of statement of banks is pretty well standardized, and this makes comparisons easy.

It will be enlightening and helpful for the inquirer to compare the statements of condition of the same bank over a period of years and also to compare the statements of the different banks of the community. In this way he will be able to discover trends and to find out whether trends discernible in the statement of one bank are confined to that bank or are common to all of the banks of the community.

A layman may need assistance in interpreting the bank's statement and in making these comparisons. Some banks now are having their statements of condition arranged and presented, with comments and explanations in non-technical language, in such a way as to 1

enable an inquirer, although unacquainted with banking, financial, or accounting terminology, better to understand the meaning and significance of the various items in the statement. It is not amiss for an inquirer to ask a representative of the bank to explain the statement. He will be impressed by the readiness, definiteness, and patience with which the representative complies with the request. 

Also, in some communities a local investment banker may be available, and he may be helpful in explaining the statement. 

The importance of a person's looking for this earmark is that he, naturally and properly, wants to leave his estate in the hands of a financially strong, stable, and responsible executor and trustee. Even though the financial condition of a bank may change for better or for worse between the time a person makes his will and the time of his death, present solvency and the presence of favorable and absence of unfavorable trends in financial condition offer the best prospect of continued solvency.

Although trust property must be kept separate from the bank's own property, so that the failure of the bank need not jeopardize the trust property, nevertheless, no right-thinking property owner, who wishes to name a bank as his executor and trustee, is content to rely upon this requirement as his main safeguard. Instead, he wishes to name a bank the soundness of whose financial condition at the present time is unquestioned.

PRESENT MANAGEMENT 

A third earmark of a good trust institution is that its directors, executive officers, and legal advisers are persons who give assurance that the present management of the bank is safe and sound. Directors

As regards the directors, one should inquire particularly into their general reputation in the community, the businesses and professions they represent, and their age and activity. 

1. General reputation. As to the general reputation of the directors, one may inquire: What is their business or professional standing? 

How have they handled their own affairs? How do they rate as citizens?

2. Businesses and professions represented. It is a good sign if the main types of businesses and professions of the trade area are represented on the board. The importance of this is that men of broad and varied business and professional experience are needed on the board and especially on the trust committee to deal with and pass judgment upon the problems that arise in settling estates and administering trusts. 

3. Age and activity. It is a good sign also if the board of directors is a combination of younger, middle-aged, and older men. A proper balancing of the three groups gives assurance of both present and continued well-balanced administration. 

Executive Officers

The general reputation and the age and activity of the executive officers also should be a subject of inquiry. 

1. General reputation. It is a good sign if the president and other executive officers are men of high standing in the community, honored and respected by their fellow citizens, known for their public spiritedness and their scrupulous fairness in all dealings with their fellowmen. 

2. Age and activity. Here, as in the case of the directors, there is need for a balancing of age and activity. The presence on the official staff of a proportionate number each of younger, of middle-aged, and of older men tends to give assurance of the existence of progressive and yet sound policies in the present administration and of their maintenance in the years to come. One of the students quoted a trust customer as saying: "What I liked about the men in the bank I selected for my trustee was the fact that there was a sprinkling of black hair among the gray." 

Legal Advisers

It is a good sign if the bank's legal advisers are lawyers of high standing in the community and in the profession. This refers to both the counsel for the bank and the counsel for the trust department, as in some institutions the two are different. The counsel for the bank and for its trust department are the direct choice of the bank and, therefore, the type of counsel selected throws light upon the management.

Lawyers who serve as legal advisers to the bank and its trust department exert a powerful influence both in the making of policies and in the maintenance of standards. 

The names and usually the business and professional connections of the directors, of the executive officers, and frequently of the legal advisers are published in the folder along with the statement of financial condition. So, it is not difficult for an inquirer to find out the essential facts about the members of these three groups. 

EXPERIENCE IN THE TRUST BUSINESS 

Another earmark of a good trust institution is that it has had broad experience in the trust business. Some of the signs of experience are the age of its trust department and the volume and the diversification of its trust business.

Age of Trust Department- 

It is, naturally, a very favorable sign if the bank has been in the trust business a long time. But age alone should not be given too much weight. It is possible for a young trust department to have an experienced staff of persons who have acquired their experience in other, older trust departments. 

Volume of Trust Business 

A large volume of trust business usually is a sign of experience. 

This refers primarily to personal trust business, although the volume of corporate trust and corporate agency business also throws some light upon the bank's experience through contacts with businessmen and its familiarity with business problems. However, volume must be judged in the light of the population and the wealth of the community and of the prevailing method of valuing trust property. 

While a bank with $100,000,000 of trust assets might be a comparatively small trust institution in a very large and wealthy city, it would be a large one in a smaller community. A person moving from a larger community into a smaller one, or vice versa, must beware of judging the size of the trust institutions in the two communities by the same standards.

The volume of trust business must be judged also in the light of the trust department's method of valuing trust property. Some trust departments carry their trust property at cost or inventory value; others, simply at some control value unrelated to actual value; others, at par or face value; and still others use different methods of valuation for different kinds of property and different types of trust accounts. So, any statement as to the volume of trust business should be coupled with an explanation of the bank's method of valuing trust property, else comparisons of the volume of trust business of different trust institutions may be misleading. 

Beyond a certain point the volume of trust business loses much of its significance as a sign of experience. For example, in a city of 

100,000 people, it may not make any material difference, so far as experience is concerned, whether it is a $50,000,000 or a $100,000,000 trust department or, in a city of 1,000,000 people, whether it is a $500,000,000 or a $1,000,000,000 department. 

Diversification of Trust Business

It is a good sign of broad experience if the trust business is well diversified both as to number and as to types of trust accounts. The possession of a large number of accounts is a sign that the trust department has had experience in the administration of different kinds of property and in dealing with different kinds of beneficiaries. 

Similarly, diversification within reasonable limits as to types of accounts indicates experience in the different phases of estate and trust administration. For example a $50,000,000 trust department with 1,000 estates, trusts, guardianships, and agencies, would have had more experience in general trust business than would a department of the same size with a very few large estates or trusts of the same general type.

While experience in trust business is an important earmark of a good trust institution and while age, volume, and diversification are the signs of experience most readily available to the inquirer, these signs must be read and appraised with discrimination. Among trust institutions generally there is little uniformity of practice as to the figures they publish or even give upon request.¹ Very rarely will an 1 

See Study Number 9, What and How a Trust Institution Should Tell People 

About Itself and Its Trust Business, supra, pp. 188-190. 1

inquirer encounter difficulty in obtaining by direct inquiry from a representative of the bank all of the statistical information that really throws light upon the bank's experience in the trust business. 

STAFF OF TRUST DEPARTMENT 

An indispensable earmark of a good trust institution is an adequate and competent trust department staff. The members of the staff to whom an inquirer should pay special attention are the members of the trust committee, the trust officer or officers, and the specialists in the trust department. 

Trust Committee An active trust committee is a very important part of the staff of good trust institution. These are some of the favorable signs: When the members of this committee represent the main types of businesses and professions in the community; when they meet in committee regularly; when they have time and take time to give serious thought and to reach well-considered decisions on trust matters presented to the committee; when the head or some responsible representative of the trust department attends the meetings of the committee regularly, participates in the discussions, and thereby gives the other members of the committee the benefit of his familiarity with the trust account or the trust problem under consideration. 

A person will do well to inquire into the make-up and actual functioning of the trust committee. Yet in making this inquiry and passing judgment on what he finds out, he must be mindful of the fact that the make-up and functioning of the trust committee are influenced by local conditions, that what would be practicable in a smaller trust department or a smaller community might not be practicable or even desirable in a larger department or larger community. In some banks, particularly in the smaller ones, the whole board serves as the trust committee; in other, larger banks, there may be several trust committees, each with definitely assigned functions, composed mainly of active officers. The test is whether the bank through its board or through one or more committees of officers or directors provides fully for the application of group judgment to estate and trust administration.

Trust Officer or Officers 

In smaller trust departments the head of the department is known as the trust officer; in some of the larger departments he is known as the senior trust officer, and in the largest trust departments there are numerous trust officers. In public estimation the trust officer is the embodiment of the department. It is, therefore, looked upon as a sign of a good trust institution that its trust officer or trust officers possess certain attributes.

These attributes have been listed as follows: Working knowledge of law, comprehension of accounting, understanding of investments, experience in business management, ability and disposition properly to handle his personal finances, healthy public spiritedness, sympathy, patience, ability to keep confidences, moral habits above reproach, business practices above criticism, spirit of cooperation, experience in trust work, and enthusiasm for trust work. While all of these qualifications are important, the inquirer cannot take the time to inquire into them all in detail, and he does not need to do so. The three things to which he should pay special attention are the trust officer's experience in trust work, the soundness of his judgment, and his personality. 

1. Experience in trust work. The settlement of an estate or administration of a trust is specialized work, calling for technical knowledge and skill that can be acquired only by experience. Consequently, it is a good sign if the trust officer has had several years of experience in estate and trust administration. However, much of this experience sometimes is acquired outside a trust department. For example, a lawyer-trust officer who had had an extensive probate practice before becoming a trust officer may have acquired in his practice much of the experience needed in his trust work. So, the inquirer cannot judge the trust officer's experience in trust work solely by the length of his employment in the trust department. 

2. Soundness of judgment. This refers to his business judgment and common sense as distinct from his special or technical knowledge. The proper settlement of an estate or administration of a trust calls for the exercise of the same practical approach to business and financial problems and the exercise of the same sort of business and 2 

Stephenson, Gilbert T., Trust Business As a Life Work, Trust Bulletin, May 

1940, p. 16.

financial judgment as are essential for the proper handling of one's private affairs.

Trust officers gradually acquire general reputations as to their business judgment and common sense. But, aside from general reputation, an inquirer, out of a comparatively brief conversation with a trust officer, may get a reasonably trustworthy impression of his approach, whether practical or theoretical, to the ordinary business and financial problems that arise in the settlement of an estate or administration of a trust. It is a favorable sign when he finds that the trust officer is endowed with good common sense as well as technical experience. 

3. Personality. Trust service is one of the most intimately personal services in the business world. It involves direct and continued dealings with customers and beneficiaries, many of whom are widows, children, and aged persons, with respect to personal and domestic as well as purely business and financial matters. Consequently, the personality of the trust officer-particularly his patience, sympathy, tact, understanding-counts for a great deal in the selection of a bank for executor and trustee. The test which many of the students say they would apply and which, they think, others should apply is this: Is the trust officer the sort of man I would want my wife, children, and parents to deal with? The recurrence of this test in the students' answers shows how large a place they think personal qualities occupy in the minds of people and what influential factors these qualities are in the selection of one's executor and trustee. 

A number of the students suggest also that, before a husband and father makes his final decision as to his executor and trustee, he should take his wife into the trust department, have her meet and talk with some of the trust officers, and then find out what is her general impression of the trust department and of the trust officers. 

It is a good sign if she liked the trust department and felt that she would be willing to transact her trust business and that of her children with the trust officers with whom she talked. 

Specialists 

Of the various specialists on the staff of the trust department the one to whom the layman well may pay special attention is the person in charge of trust investments. It is a very good sign if this person

has a good reputation in the community and particularly among the bank's own customers as a careful, conservative, level-headed, farsighted investment man-the sort of person with whom they like to discuss their own investment problem. 

In 1941, Edwin Bird Wilson, Incorporated, of New York City, received replies to this question from 614 persons representing every section of the country: "Which qualifications (specifying six) do you consider most desirable in an ideal executor and trustee for your estate?" Of this 614, 32% considered investment experience the most important qualification; 20%, continuing responsibility; 15%, specialization; 15%, impartiality; 10%, knowledge of family problems; and 5%, technical ability. Thus nearly one-third of the persons replying put investment experience first among the qualifications of an ideal executor and trustee. 3 

ORGANIZATION OF TRUST DEPARTMENT 

Still another earmark of a good trust institution is that its trust department organization is adequate for the complete performance of all the activities of estate and trust administration. The settlement of an estate or administration of a trust involves four sets of activities, namely, executive, administrative, service, and operative. While a specialist, such as a bank examiner, before passing judgment upon the adequacy of the trust department organization, would inquire specifically into the divisional or sectional organization for the performance of each of these four sets of activities, the layman would be interested mainly in only these two questions on organization: How is the trust department organized (1) generally to settle estates and administer trusts and (2) specifically to invest trust funds? 

Estate and Trust Administration

It will be well for a person trying to select the bank to be his executor and trustee to have a representative of the trust department explain to him, step by step, in general terms, not in tedious detail, how it actually would go about the settlement of his estate and the 3 

Madden, John C., Most Important Qualifications of Trust Institutions, Trust 

Bulletin, October 1941, p. 38.

administration of the trusts under his will. The directness, definiteness, and simplicity of this explanation will go a long way toward revealing the adequacy of the general organization of the trust department. 

Investing Trust Funds

The inquirer will do well also to have a representative of the bank explain in some detail how it handles trust investments-what organization it has for analyzing stocks, bonds, and other securities, for reviewing the investments of trust accounts, for making decisions as to investments, and for seeing that these decisions are carried out promptly and accurately.*

However, it is easy to misjudge and undervalue a trust department organization because it is simple and on paper would be unimpressive. The plan of organization should be in keeping with the volume and characteristics of the trust business. 

PHYSICAL LAYOUT AND EQUIPMENT OF TRUST DEPARTMENT 

One tangible and visible earmark of a good trust institution is a physical layout and equipment adequate for the proper handling of trust business. From the point of view of the layman, this has particular reference to the location of the trust department, its arrangement for the convenience of trust customers and beneficiaries, and the trust vaults.

Location of Trust Department 

It is a good sign if the location of the trust department is such as to indicate that the trust business is regarded as a coordinate branch of the business of the bank. In the growth and evolution of a trust department it may be expected that it will be located, first, on the banking floor, and later moved upstairs and given a part of a floor or a whole floor of its own. Whatever the stage of its growth and evolution may be, it is a good sign if the trust department has a location that in accessibility and prominence compares favorably 

*See Study Number 5, How Trust Institutions Exercise Discretionary Powers, supra, pp. 94-96.

with that of the banking department. However, due allowance should be made for the fact that the limitations of space and the architecture and arrangement of the bank building may make the trust department less accessible or prominent than the management otherwise would provide.

Arrangement of Trust Department In the arrangement of the trust department features which are of special significance are the provisions for privacy and for the uninterrupted transaction of trust business. It is a favorable sign if the desks are spaced so that conversations being carried on at one desk will not be overheard by persons at another. Another good sign is that there are one or more convenient conference rooms. 

People who do business with the trust department say that they appreciate arrangements that avoid interruptions by telephone calls or otherwise while they are transacting their business. They say also that the clatter of machines, which people working in the bank get used to, is especially annoying to them. It is easy to understand this, when one realizes that many beneficiaries of estates and trusts have their first direct contact with the trust department immediately following a death in the family, when they are nervously upset and easily annoyed. So, arrangement of the trust department that gives freedom from physical annoyance and disturbance is especially appreciated by these beneficiaries. 

Arrangement of Trust Vaults

From the inquirer's point of view, the most significant item of the equipment is the vault in which the trust securities and documents are kept and safeguarded. It is a good sign when the trust vault or vaults are conveniently located, are commodious, have good arrangement for keeping the securities and documents of the various accounts separated from one another and separate from those of the bank itself, and provide for the dual control and custody of trust securities and documents.5 Prospective customers are pleased to find 

See Regulation F-Trust Powers of National Banks, sec. 12. 1

representatives of the bank ready and willing to show them the trust vault equipment and to explain the system of control and custody. 

GENERAL ATTITUDE TOWARD THE TRUST BUSINESS 

Another earmark of a good trust institution is that the management takes an active interest in the trust business of the bank. On this point a person may pursue these inquiries: What has been the attitude of the directors and officers in the past? What is the attitude of the present directors and officers? How much interest do the general executive officers show in the trust department? How definite is their information about the trust business? 

Estates of Deceased Directors and Officers In the case of a bank that has had a trust department several years, it is a good sign if directors and officers who have died within the past few years named the bank in their wills. The patronage of the former directors and officers indicates that the trust department already has an established place in the estimation of the management. This test, of course, could not be applied to a bank that had opened its trust department within the term of office of most of the present directors and officers.

Wills of Present Directors and Officers 

The fact that most of the present directors and officers have named the bank in their wills is a highly favorable sign. However, inquiry on this point must be pursued with delicacy and discrimination; and unfavorable implications must not be drawn from the inquirer's inability readily to obtain this information. At the present time only a few banks publicize or divulge in any way what their directors have done as to naming the bank. An increasing number but still only a minority of banks publicize the fact that their officers have named the bank. 

* See Study Number 12, How Directors, Officers, and Employees Are Developing 

Trust Business, supra, pp. 249-250, 258-259.

References to Trust Department in Statement of Condition and Report of President to Stockholders 

Several students expressed the opinion that the place and the space devoted to the trust department in the pamphlet containing the statement of condition and in the president's published annual report to the stockholders are indicative of the general attitude of the management toward the trust business. Does the statement of condition carry the names of the members of the trust committee? Does it list and call special attention to the principal trust services offered? Does the president's annual report convey definite, specific (of course, not confidential) information about the year's progress and developments in the trust department?

Considerable space in the pamphlet containing the statement of condition and in the president's published report devoted to the trust department, with definite information about the trust business, indicate that the present management recognizes the importance and place of the trust department in the bank's affairs. However, in view of the present lack of uniformity of practice on this point, a bank's interest in or emphasis upon its trust business should not be judged solely by the amount of space devoted to the trust department in these two mediums of information.

Definiteness of Information on Trust Matters 

The readiness of the general officers of the bank to discuss trust matters and the definiteness of their information are indicative of their general attitude toward the trust business. Do they themselves bring up a trust subject for discussion? Do they respond readily to inquiries about trust service? Do they show a grasp of the basic facts about the trust department-such as the historical background of the bank as a trust institution, the basic needs of people for trust service, the principal trust services rendered by the bank, its general trust policies?

TRUST POLICIES 

Still another earmark of a good trust institution is that it has and. adheres to definite trust policies as to developing trust business, as to accepting trust accounts, as to charging for trust service, and as to

the general conduct of its trust business. While the layman, being inexperienced in trust work, may not be able to pass judgment upon the soundness of all these policies, he can pass judgment on their definiteness and on the readiness and consistency with which they are stated and explained by the representatives of the bank. 

Developing Trust Business 

The two main ways of developing trust business are through trust advertising and trust representation. 

1. Trust advertising. The propriety of a bank's advertising its trust services has been settled beyond question. But the amount, mediums, and character of trust advertising are matters of policy which each bank must decide for itself. It is a good sign if its trust advertisements are readable, informative, dignified, do not overstate or overemphasize the qualifications of the bank or cast any reflections upon other trust institutions or individuals, relying upon the strength of its own case rather than any weakness of its competitor's case." 

2. Trust representation. The primary function of trust representation in business development is that of explaining and advising with respect to trust service. Accordingly, it is a good sign if the trust representative remains consistently in the rôle of adviser and consultant on estate and trust matters without ever getting out of characer and becoming a high-pressure salesman. 

Accepting Trust Accounts 

It is a favorable sign of a good trust institution that it has definite policies, which its representatives readily state and explain, as to accepting trust accounts. A few trust institutions have reduced their acceptance policies to writing, but most of them have not done so." 

The important facts in this respect are the existence and the definiteness, not the form of statement, of these policies. It should be encour- 

17 See A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, Art. IV, sec. 1, and joint State ment of Policies of the American Bar Association and the American Bankers Association Trust Division, sec. 4.

A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, Art. VI, sec. 2. 

'See Trust Bulletin, November 1940, p. 3.

aging to an inquirer to learn that every proffered trust account, including his own, is subjected to these two basic tests: Is trust service needed? Can the service be rendered properly?10 Evidence of the bank's exercise of care and caution in the acceptance of trust accounts augurs well for the success of its trust business and, therefore, for the quality of its trust service to beneficiaries. The thoughtful and prudent inquirer is appreciative of the care and prudence the bank's representatives manifest in weighing and considering the acceptance of his own account. 

Charges for Trust Service

It is a sign of a good trust institution that it has definite policies with respect to its charges for trust services, is willing to state them, and adheres to them consistently.¹¹

The person trying to select his executor and trustee is favorably impressed when he finds that the bank knows its trust costs; fixes its charges to cover its costs and leave a fair margin of profit; applies its charges uniformly and impartially; and competes with other banks on quality of trust service rather than on rates.12 

General Conduct of Trust Business 

Another favorable sign is that the bank has definite general policies for the conduct of its trust business. From the inquirer's point of view, the significant facts are the existence of such general policies, their definiteness, the understanding of them by the management, and the willingness and ability of the management to discuss and explain them. The form of statement of the policies and whether or not they have been put in writing are matters of much less importance. In 1942 the Committee on Trust Policies of the Trust Division suggested that every trust institution takes steps to determine the general policies of its trust department and listed 11 points on which 10 

A Statement of Principles of Trust Institutions, Art. II, sec. 1. 

11 

See Study Number 7, How Compensation for Trust Service Is Determined, supra, p. 137. 12 

See Study Number 10, Readjusting Compensation in Trust Accounts, supra, p. 203. 1

it should consider making and publishing a statement of general policies. 13 

TRUST RELATIONSHIPS 

Finally, it is an earmark of a good trust institution that it maintains friendly and cooperative relationships with the members of those groups with whom it has direct dealings in connection with its trust business. These groups are, principally, lawyers, probate judges, investment bankers, accountants, tax specialists, specialists in estate planning, life insurance men, its own employed staff, and other trust institutions. 

Cooperating Groups

Of all these groups (other than the employed staff) lawyers representing the bank as executor and trustee and probate judges have the best opportunity to see the trust institution at work. In connection with larger estates trust accountants, tax specialists, investment bankers, and specialists in estate planning come into direct contact with the trust institution. Life insurance men have business with the trust department in connection with the proceeds of insurance in estates and trusts. It is a good sign when representatives of these groups express satisfaction over their dealings with the bank on trust matters. 

Employed Staff

It is a favorable sign of good relations with the employed staff if the bank encourages and assists them in all their efforts at further training and self-improvement. This refers to internal training courses, to American Institute of Banking chapter activities and courses, and to The Graduate School of Banking courses. It is a good sign also if the bank encourages or helps them to provide for their physical welfare and economic security. This refers to group insurance, group hospitalization, profit-sharing plans, and pension plans.

All such provisions and plans for the employed staff indicate not only a wholesome attitude on the part of the management toward 

18 Trust Bulletin, January 1942, p. 5.

the staff as individuals but also an awareness of and alertness to the need for continuing efficiency and standards of service. 

Other Trust Institutions 

The relations of the bank with other trust institutions are a matter of real significance to the inquirer. Is the bank a member of the local clearing house association; of the local trust association, if there is one; of the state trust association; of the Trust Division, American 

Bankers Association, which is the national trust association? Membership in these organizations is evidence not only of good relations with its associates in the trust business but also of alertness to current developments in the trust field.

CONCLUSIONS 

The selection of the executor and trustee under his will is one of the most important steps a property owner ever takes with respect to the material welfare of those who are dependent upon him. 

Whether he will select a bank rather than an individual or one bank rather than another will depend, not so much upon the actual superiority of one over the other, as upon his awareness of the superiority and of the special fitness of the one or the other to serve his purposes.

The young engineer, whose challenging question started this Study, is typical of the thousands of men and women in this country. who realize the advantages banks in general have to offer as executors and trustees and yet who need and want help in the selection of one bank out of many.

There is no standard set of earmarks of a good trust institution. 

So much depends upon circumstances. Yet it is thought that these ten earmarks are characteristic of good trust institutions in general and that this listing and brief discussion of them may be helpful both to prospective customers and to trust institutions themselves.
