Study Number 14 THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 

TRUST under will or under agreement seldom is created for one beneficiary only. Ordinarily there are one or more immediate beneficiaries as well as one or more ultimate beneficiaries. Frequently there are two or more immediate beneficiaries and, in addition, two or more ultimate beneficiaries. Some of these beneficiaries are entitled to their interests simultaneously and others, successively. 

Whenever there are two or more beneficiaries of a trust, the question of preference may arise. For example, if a man creates a trust for his wife for life, with the remainder to his son, a question of favoring his wife over his son, or vice versa, may arise. Similarly, if he creates a trust for his two sons for life, with the remainder to his two grandsons, questions of favoring one of his sons over the other or one of his grandsons over the other or of favoring his sons as a class over his grandsons as a class, or vice versa, may arise. 

Preference to one or another beneficiary or class of beneficiaries of a trust is a matter of practical concern to the trustee, to the court, and to the legislature, as well as to the beneficiaries and to the creator of the trust.

The research upon which this Study is based has been an attempt to find out who, in the minds of creators of trusts, are the preferred beneficiaries. 

THE TRUSTEE'S POSITION 

So far as the trustee is concerned, apart from the terms of the trust, there are no preferred beneficiaries. 289

Trustee's Impartiality Should a trustee, on its own initiative and without authority, prefer one beneficiary to another, it would be committing a breach of duty. Where there are two or more beneficiaries of a trust, a trustee is under a duty to deal impartially with them.¹ This is equally true whether the beneficiaries take simultaneously or successively. 

For example, if it is a trust to pay the income to A, B, and C, the trustee must divide the income among them equally. If it is a trust to pay the income to A for life and then to pay over the principal to B, the trustee is under a duty to administer the trust as to preserve a fair balance between the respective interests of A and of B.² 

Authority to Give Preference 

However, the trustee may be and frequently is authorized to give preference to one beneficiary or to one class of beneficiaries. Where such authority exists, it is the duty of the trustee to show preference according to the intent of the creator of the trust. The court will not control the exercise of such discretion except to prevent the trustee from abusing it.³ Professor Bogert makes the point that, if the options open to the trustee are such that it is immaterial to the principal beneficiary which course is chosen, the trustee should take that choice which will be the most advantageous to the income beneficiary. The exercise of this option does no violence to the principle of impartiality, for, as Professor Bogert states the case, in choosing the course most advantageous to the income beneficiaries the principal beneficiaries are not prejudiced.

Since a trustee is under a duty not to favor one beneficiary or class of beneficiaries over another unless it has clear authority to do so, one comes at once to the question: Who can authorize the trustee to favor one beneficiary over another? The four possible sources of authority are the court, the legislature, the beneficiary of the trust, 1 

Restatement of Trusts, secs. 183 and 232; Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 801, p. 2315; Scott on Trusts, secs. 183 and 232, pp. 970 and 1256; Shattuck's Revision, secs. 27 and 55, pp. 89 and 158. 

2 Scott on Trusts, sec. 232, p. 1257. 

3 Restatement of Trusts, sec. 183; Scott on Trusts, secs. 183 and 232, pp. 971 and 1258. * Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 801, p. 2316.

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 291 

1 and the creator of the trust. This Study does not discuss the ways in which a trustee exercises preference, for this is the subject matter of a previous Study.5

THE COURT'S POSITION 

A court of equity, the same as a trustee, is under a duty to deal impartially with beneficiaries. If the court ordered or authorized the trustee to show any preference, it would be varying the terms of the trust. It is justified in varying the terms of the trust only in cases of impossibility of performance, illegality of the terms of the trust, or impending defeat of the purposes of the trust. 

Discovering Intent of Creator of Trust 

The function of the court is to interpret the terms of the trust and in so doing to determine, so far as possible, the intent of the creator of the trust. It undertakes to arrive at this intent, not only from what the testator or settlor said in the trust instrument, but also from the circumstances of the case." 

Presuming Intent of Creator of Trust

The court recently has shown a tendency to presume an intent of the creator of the trust even where he has expressed none in fact or even where he may not have thought about the matter. In presuming this intent, the court has shown a disposition to presume it to be in favor of the income beneficiary. 

Professor Scott shows by the following illustration how the court, by presuming an intent, may, in effect, favor the immediate beneficiary over the ultimate beneficiary:

Thus where a trust is created for successive beneficiaries, and there is no express provision in the trust instrument as to what shall be treated as income and what as principal, the fact that the beneficiary entitled to income is closely related to the settlor and that the beneficiary entitled to the remainder is not so related is frequently of importance as showing that the testator presumably intended that certain receipts should be treated as income rather than as principal. 

Study Number 5, How Trust Institutions Exercise Discretionary Powers, supra, p. 89. 

Scott on Trusts, secs. 164-167, p. 825. 

7 Ibid., sec. 164.1, p. 827. 

9 Ibid., sec. 164.1, p. 829.

Presuming Intent to Favor Income Beneficiaries 

As early as 1897 the Appellate Division of the Supreme Court of New York State, on an issue involving the accumulation of dividends, said:

I am unable to appreciate the sentiment that occasionally crops out in judicial opinions, that the integrity of the trust fund has greater sanctity than the interest of the life tenant. ・ ・ ・ 

Why should the life tenant fast for 25 years that the remainderman may feast at the end of that period?"a

In 1927 the Supreme Court of Colorado held that in all doubtful cases the interests of life tenants are to be preferred to the interests of remaindermen, citing an English case of 182310 and a Massachusetts case of 1851.11 In the Massachusetts case the court had said that it is contrary to the presumed intent of the testator to narrow the benefit intended for the first object of his bounty for the benefit of an object more remote. 

In a recent case, In re Nirdlinger's Estate," the issue was the apportionment of the proceeds of the sale of foreclosed property. 

The Supreme Court of Pennsylvania held that there should be apportionment between the life beneficiary and remaindermen, saying: 

The foundational reason for this conclusion is that in nearly all instances of longcontinuing trusts, the life tenants are the primary objects of the bounty of the testator, and their incomes should be preserved to them, as far as it is possible to do so, even though it may result in ultimate demolishment of principal to be paid to far off remaindermen. Life tenants should not be required to starve in order that remaindermen may ultimately feast.

In a still more recent case, involving the apportionment of the proceeds of the sale of cumulative preferred stock among successive beneficiaries, the same court said:

Equitable principles are applied in all apportionment cases and the primary interest of the testatrix to provide for those nearest to her an income would seem to demand such a conclusion." 13 Although these recent expressions of the court show a disposition sa (Matter of Rogers, 22 App. Div. 428, 436, 48 N. Y. Supp. 175, 181 (2d Dept. 

1897), aff'd 161 N. Y. 108, 55 N.E. 393, 1899). 10 

Mulcahy v. Johnson et al., 90 Colo. 499, 252 P. 816. 

Angerstein v. Martin, 1 Turner and Russell Reports, pp. 231, 238. 

11 Lovering v. Minot, 9 Cush. (Mass.) 151. 12 

327 Pa. 171, 193 A. 30, 32 (1937). 13 

In re Fisher's Estate, 344 Pa. 607, 26 A. (2d) 192, 196 (1942).

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 293 

to favor the income beneficiaries, even at the expense of the principal beneficiaries, this preference is based upon the intent, expressed or presumed, of the creator of the trust and not upon the partiality of the court itself. Before the court prefers one beneficiary to another, it usually finds as a fact that such preference is in harmony with the intent of the creator of the trust, as expressed in the instrument or implied from the circumstances, or that it is in harmony with what would have been his intent had he thought about the matter.14 Thus, the court discovers the intent, not in its own mind, but in that of the creator of the trust. 

In the light of the foregoing, a testator or settlor should not look to the court, any more than he should look to the trustee, to favor one beneficiary over another.

THE ATTITUDE OF THE LEGISLATURE 

The legislature has shown a tendency recently to favor the immediate over the ultimate beneficiaries.

Uniform Principal and Income Act 

In two respects at least the Uniform Principal and Income Act, as originally drafted, shows a disposition on the part of the legislature to favor the income beneficiary over the principal beneficiary. 

The first relates to premiums on bonds and the other to unproductive property. 

1. Premium on bonds. The Uniform Principal and Income

Act, with modifications, has been adopted as a whole or in part in 13 states (Alabama, California, Connecticut, Florida, Illinois, Louisiana, Maryland, North Carolina, Oklahoma, Oregon, Texas,

Utah, and Virginia), and the section on amortization of premiums has been adopted in substance in at least one other state (Delaware). 

In the Act as originally drafted and as adopted in all the foregoing states, except Alabama and Illinois, a trustee does not amortize premiums on bonds unless it is directed to do so by the terms of the trust. This provision gives the income beneficiary a distinct preference as to all bonds selling at a premium. 

2. Unproductive estate. The Act as originally drafted and as adopted in most of the foregoing states provides that a trustee must 

14 Scott on Trusts, sec. 164.1, p. 831.

dispose of unproductive property (not earning so much as 1% net a year) as soon as possible without sacrifice of value, and prescribes a formula for apportioning the proceeds of the sale, when made, between the income beneficiary and the principal beneficiary. This provision for apportionment gives the income beneficiary a share in the proceeds of the sale which otherwise he might not have, and thus makes him a preferred beneficiary. 

Mortgage Salvaging Operations 

In two leading cases, In re Chapal's Wills and In re Otis' Will,10 the Court of Appeals of New York set out certain rules for the liquidation of defaulted mortgage investments. Subsequently, problems arose that had not been covered in these two cases. In 1940 the legislature enacted a new section¹ of the Personal Property Law to clarify and simplify the rules laid down in the Chapal and Otis cases. The legislature incorporated in the act the following declaration of purpose:

The purpose of the enactment of this subdivision is declared to be the simplification of the rules of procedure in mortgage salvage operations and the elimination of present complications which work to the disadvantage of the life tenant, who is usually the principal object of the testator's or settlor's bounty, by depriving him of a fixed right to the actual payment of any net income earned by the property. Such fixed right is granted in lieu of the discretion now given to the trustee to pay net income or any part thereof to the life tenant. 

This shows an intent on the part of the legislature to favor the income beneficiary in mortgage salvaging operations. 

Provision for Minors

In California,18 New York,10 and in several other states, the legislature has authorized the court to order the use for the income beneficiary of income directed to be accumulated, where the beneficiary of the accumulating income is destitute of other sufficient 15 

269 N. Y. 464, 199 N. E. 762 (1936). 

16 276 N. Y. 101, 11 N. E. (2d) 556 (1937). -17 

Sec. 17-C. 

18 Civil Code, sec. 726. 19 

Personal Property Law, sec. 17, and Real Property Law, sec. 62.

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 295 

means of support or education.20 Such statutes show the disposition of the legislature to favor the beneficiary during the period when he needs the income most, even at his own expense later on. 

Estates of Missing Testators and Intestates 

In 1943 five states (Arkansas, Idaho, Nebraska, Ohio, and Pennsylvania) enacted statutes providing for the appointment of a trustee to take over and manage the property of a missing testator or intestate and, upon the order of court, to use income or principal or both for the support and maintenance of his dependents. 

This shows a legislative intent to favor a person's dependentsusually his wife and children—even at the expense of others who might be his devisees or legatees.

While the legislature may, as in the above illustrations, lay down rules that usually will result in one beneficiary or one class of beneficiaries being favored over another, the legislature can deal only with general situations and not with specific cases. In all these cases the legislature is trying to provide for the carrying out of what, it presumes, would be the desire of the property owner. 

THE BENEFICIARY'S POSITION 

In some states and under certain circumstances two or more beneficiaries, if legally competent, may agree first with one another and then with the trustee for it to favor one beneficiary or one class of beneficiaries over another.21 For example, if a man leaves his estate in trust to pay one-half of the income each to his wife and to his adult son and at his wife's death to pay over the principal to his son and, if her half of the income proves to be insufficient for her needs, the son himself may turn over to her part or all of his share, or in some states he may have the trustee make payments direct to his mother out of his share of the income. In some states he even may authorize the trustee to pay over part of the principal to his mother. 

From the legal point of view, should one beneficiary authorize or 20 

See Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 216, p. 651; Scott on Trusts, sec. 168, p. 850. 21 

Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, secs. 218 and 1002, pp. 670 and 2928; Scott on 

Trusts, sec. 62.10, p. 390.

direct the trustee to favor another beneficiary or class of beneficiaries over himself, he would raise perplexing and, in many states, as yet unanswered questions of law as to spendthrift trusts and as to the destructibility of trusts. From the business point of view, it would be rare that the creator of a trust, without expressing a preference himself, would rely upon his beneficiaries by agreement to work out satisfactory preferences. To do so would be to invite dissensions which might defeat the purposes of the trust. 

THE TESTATOR'S OR SETTLOR'S POSITION 

It is to be seen, then, that, despite all the trustee, the court, the legislature, and the beneficiary can do properly in preferring one beneficiary to another, the ultimate and the inescapable responsibility with respect to preference rests squarely upon the shoulders of the creator of the trust. He has the right, by the terms of the trust, not only himself to favor one beneficiary over another, but also to authorize the trustee to show preference. As Professor Scott says:

By the terms of the trust the trustee may have discretion to favor one beneficiary over another, the extent of his discretion depending upon the provisions of the trust instrument. 22 Next come the questions: In the mind of the creator of the trust is there a preferred beneficiary? If so, which beneficiary or class of beneficiaries is preferred? If a preference is to be shown, how is it to be expressed and how is it to be exercised?

The Presumed Preference 

As the foregoing discussion shows, both the court and the legislature presume and act upon the presumption that normally the creator of a trust regards his immediate, income beneficiaries as his preferred beneficiaries—the first objects of his bounty, as it usually is expressed.

Likewise, lawyers and trustmen who have had long experience in dealings with creators of trusts express the opinion that in most cases the immediate, income beneficiaries are the preferred beneficiaries. 22 

Scott on Trusts, sec. 183, p. 971.

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 297 

In 1938, Judge Thomas C. Hennings of St. Louis, basing his observation upon long experience at the bar, upon the bench, and as the head of the trust department of the Mercantile-Commerce Bank and Trust Company of that city, said in an address before the American Bar Association: 

In the matter of the conservation of the trust property we have been taught that the rights of the remaindermen to have the property remain intact are superior to those of the life tenant. I question the absoluteness of that position, notwithstanding the failure by the trustor or testator to authorize encroachment on principal in case of need. 

Changed conditions and circumstances may call for the use of principal, in addition to income, for the support of a wife or child in whose welfare he had greater concern than in a remainderman, whom he may possibly have never seen. 

At the 1941 Mid-Winter Trust Conference Judge Carl E. Wahl strom, Judge of Probate of Worcester County, Massachusetts, said:

After all, the remaindermen are only just remaindermen. They are entitled only to what remains. What if the corpus is reduced or even consumed? If it has served the purpose for which it was created, namely to provide for the walfare, comfort, support, maintenance and happiness of the life beneficiary and this is the purpose of most trusts-then the trustee surely is not to be penalized." 

At the same conference Louis H. Roseberry, head of the trust department of the Security-First National Bank of Los Angeles, said: 

Living persons, usually those dependent upon the customer who creates a trust, are ordinarily his chief concern in providing benefits under his trust. Remaindermen are apt to be purely secondary in his objective-in many cases they are mentioned merely as a class to take the principal after it is supposed to have served the needs of the primary beneficiary.2 24 At the same conference, also, Harry M. Bardt, vice-president and trust officer of the Bank of America National Trust and Savings 

Association, Los Angeles, said

With few exceptions, the principle of perpetuation is no longer the motivating factor on the part of those creating trusts today. Economic changes have forced a new concept with respect to the accumulation and retention of property. The prime purpose of most trusts today is to assure adequate provision for those who may be the objects of the settlor's or testator's bounty.' 25 

23 Should the Needs of a Life Beneficiary Vary a Trustee's Investment Program? 

Trust Bulletin, February 1941, p. 5 at p. 10. 

24 Tomorrow's Trusts: The Annuity Type, Trust Bulletin, March 1941, p. 17. 

25 Why Principal and Income?, Trust Bulletin, March 1941, p. 24.

Eighty-six of 88 trust officers in 50 cities of 23 states, replying to a questionnaire, have stated that in their opinion the testator or settlor is more concerned about providing for his present beneficiaries than for his ultimate beneficiaries.26

Are these lawyers, judges, and trustmen warranted in their opinion that in most cases in the mind of the creator of the trust the immediate beneficiary is the preferred one? The only person who can give the definitive answer to this question is, of course, the creator of the trust himself.

The Expressed Preference 

Recently each of the students majoring in Trusts in The Graduate School of Banking was requested to ask 10 of his friends, who were property owners and married men with children, to state whether they would want the trustee under their will to give preference to the present beneficiaries or to the ultimate beneficiaries or to either class of beneficiaries, and to give the substance of their answers, with their reasons and observations.

Replies were received from 367 persons in 30 cities in 17 states. 

Of these 367 persons, 321 or 87.5% said they would want the trustee to give preference to the present beneficiaries; 15 or 4.2%, to the ultimate beneficiaries; and 31 or 8.3% said they would not want the trustee to give preference to either class of beneficiaries but to preserve a fair balance between them.

As might have been expected, the reasons and observations of these 367 persons were a great deal more illuminating than their bare statements as to which of the beneficiaries they would have the trustee to prefer.

Present and Ultimate Beneficiaries Distinguished 

These 367 persons had some difficulty in distinguishing between the terms, present beneficiaries and ultimate beneficiaries. The term, present beneficiaries, was meant to cover those who begin receiving benefits from the trust as soon as it is set up-usually but not always the income beneficiaries; and the term, ultimate beneficiaries, those 26 

'See Study Number 4, Should Trust Institutions Have Broad Discretionary 

Powers?, supra, p. 67 at p. 78.

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 299 

who receive benefits only after the present beneficiaries have been served-usually the principal beneficiaries but sometimes successive income beneficiaries. Fortunately most of the persons who replied gave their own interpretation of the terms. 

Present Beneficiaries Preferred 

Some of the 367 persons regarded their wives as the present beneficiary and their children as the ultimate beneficiaries; while others regarded their wife and children together as the present beneficiaries and their parents, brothers and sisters, distant kin, and charities as the ultimate beneficiaries. 

1. Wife. Where the wife and the children were thought of separately, the wife usually was the preferred beneficiary. Among the reasons ascribed for giving her the preference over their children were these: (1) She had had almost as large a part as the husband in the creation of the estate; (2) the children were hers as much as his, and she could be depended upon to care for them; (3) her needs would increase and her earning power wane as she grew older, while the earning power of their children would increase as they grew to maturity; (4) the children, as soon as they grew up, would be able to look after themselves; and (5) the younger children would have to look to their mother for support, and this would be conducive to their discipline and to happy family relationships. 

2. Wife and children. Where the wife and the children together were regarded as the present beneficiaries and other individuals or charities as the ultimate beneficiaries, the following were some of the reasons given for preferring the present beneficiaries: By making the wife and children the preferred beneficiaries, (1) it would be possible for them to maintain the standard of living to which they had been accustomed; (2) it would give better assurance that the children would have the proper schooling; (3) it would be caring for those nearest and dearest to the property owner; (4) it would make it possible to keep the family home intact; (5) it would provide for those who were dependent upon the property owner; and (6) it would enable the trustee in providing for the needs of the family to cope with the uncertainties of the future. 

Other less personal or sentimental reasons for making the wife and children as a class the preferred beneficiaries were these:

(1) indisposition to perpetuate the estate beyond this generation; (2) lack of interest in or responsibility for succeeding generations; (3) duty of each generation to provide for its own dependents; (4) lack of knowledge of the needs of ultimate, perhaps still unborn, beneficiaries; (5) dangers incident to inherited property; (6) legal, as well as moral, obligation to support immediate family; and (7) reliance upon social security legislation to provide for the dependents of succeeding generations. 

3. Minor children and adult children. Many of the persons, declaring their children to be the preferred beneficiaries, drew a distinction between their children as minors and as adults, saying that so long as they were minors they, along with their mother, should be the preferred beneficiaries but that, after they had grown up, their mother should be favored over them.

Ultimate Beneficiaries Preferred 

In most of the 15 cases in which the property owner said he would want his trustee to give preference to the ultimate beneficiaries his answer showed that he was thinking of his wife as the present beneficiary and of his children as the ultimate beneficiaries. 

The following reasons were given for preferring the ultimate to the present beneficiaries: (1) Present beneficiary extravagant; (2) present beneficiary already amply provided for; (3) lack of confidence that the present beneficiary would provide for the ultimate beneficiaries; (4) desire to hold the estate intact; (5) desire that sons feel independent of their mother; (6) children would take care of their mother in case of need; (7) present beneficiary already provided for and special desire to provide for certain charities; (8) desire to perpetuate a business; and (9) desire to perpetuate the family name and fortune for grandchildren.

No Preferred Beneficiaries 

A considerable portion of the 31 persons who replied that they would not want their trustee to show any preference as between the present and ultimate beneficiaries were lawyers and construed the question as if it meant the trustee's showing preference when it had no express authority to do so. Upon this interpretation they were, of course, right in saying that the trustee should not show any preference.

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 301 

The others gave the following reasons for their impartiality: (1) They were willing to trust the trustee to do what was best for the different beneficiaries; (2) they wanted to avoid any favoritism; (3) the present beneficiary was a second wife and the ultimate beneficiaries were children by a first wife, and the creator of the trust wanted them treated impartially; (4) the beneficiaries are all of the same class-children; (5) all the beneficiaries-present and ultimate—already were amply provided for; and (6) impartiality would promote good relations among all the beneficiaries. 

The weight of the evidence is to the effect (1) that the average man regards his wife and children as a group his preferred beneficiaries; (2) that he thinks of his wife and minor children as a group and as to them would not have one preferred to the other; (3) that, if he expresses a preference for his wife, it is because he has confidence that she can and will take care of their children; (4) that, if he expresses preference for his minor children, it is either because his wife has an independent estate or else, on account of her extravagant tendencies or lack of business or financial experience, he is afraid she will let the estate be dissipated; (5) that, as between his wife and his adult children, his preference is for her, that he is disposed to let his adult children, if normal mentally and physically, shift for themselves; and (6) that he is not greatly interested in passing property on to succeeding generations. 

From the foregoing the court, the legislature, or the trustee should not take for granted that the immediate beneficiary always is to be preferred. There still are some cases in which the creator of the trust wants his ultimate beneficiaries preferred and others in which he wants no preference shown. In these cases, no less than in the others, the intent of the testator or settlor must be respected. 

These two questions still remain to be discussed: (1) What should the property owner himself, as testator or settlor, do, and (2) what should the trust institution, as trustee, do to give effect to this preference?

GIVING EFFECT TO PREFERENCE 

The creator of the trust and the trustee must play the major rôles in giving effect to preference the former in expressing and the latter in executing the preference.

The Testator's or Settlor's Part 

The two main things that the testator or settlor can do to give effect to his desired preference are (1) to give the trustee broad discretionary powers and (2) to state in definite terms which beneficiaries or class of beneficiaries he desires preferred over others and, in general, to what extent he desires the preference to go. 

1. Granting broad powers. Among the broad powers of administration that enables the trustee to give preference to present beneficiaries are these: (1) To retain original investments where the yield would be greater than on legal investments; (2) to invest in non-legals where the yield may be greater than on legals; (3) to carry on (for a limited time and with proper safeguards) a business in which the profits are greater than would be the yield on securities; (4) not to amortize premiums on bonds; (5) to allocate or apportion expenses and dividends in a way that may be advantageous to the present beneficiaries.27

The main power of distribution that enables the trustee to favor present beneficiaries is the power to pay over or apply principal, in addition to income, for the present beneficiaries.2 28 

If the testator or settlor desires to prefer the ultimate beneficiaries, he, naturally, will not grant the power to use principal for immediate beneficiaries. 

2. Expression of preference. If the creator of the trust desires that the trustee shall favor one beneficiary over another, he should give the trustee information, advice, or guidance that will enable the trustee to carry out both the spirit and the letter of the trust. 

To provide in the will or trust agreement that the trustee may use principal for the maintenance and support of the wife and children and the education of the children is to give the trustee an ample grant of power, and, in the absence of abuse of discretion, the court 

27 For a discussion of powers of administration, see Stephenson, Gilbert T., Dis cretionary Powers of Trustees under Wills and Trust Agreements, The Graduate 

School of Banking, American Bankers Association, New York, 1937. 

23 For a discussion of powers of distribution, see Stephenson, Gilbert T., Distribu tion Provisions of Wills and Trust Agreements, The Graduate School of Banking, 

American Bankers Association, New York, 1939.

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 303 

will not interfere with the trustee's exercise of this power.29 But if the grant of power to use principal stops here, the trustee still will be in a quandary as to how liberal or how conservative it should be in using and consuming principal. For example, under such a broad grant of power such questions as these may arise: Does the testator or settlor want the entire estate consumed, if the trustee thinks best, in the maintenance of the family and the education of the children? 

Does education include university and professional or technical training as well as high school and college schooling? 

It is advantageous to the trustee to have, in addition to the grant of broad discretionary power of distribution, some amplification or explanation of the preference of the testator or settlor. For example, this statement from an Alabama trust instrument quoted in a previous Study³° was, no doubt, helpful to the trustee in carrying out the spirit of the terms of the trust and accomplishing the real desires of the creator of the trust: 30 The primary purpose of the creation of this trust is to provide for the comfort, maintenance, support, and protection of my wife and of my children. Any interest of any other person or persons therein shall be subject and subservient to the primary purpose herein expressed.

However, in spelling out the purposes and motives underlying these preferences, the draftsman must beware of creating unintended trusts or terms of trusts through the unguarded use of words and phrases intended to be only suggestions.

The Trustee's Part 

31 The trust institution as trustee may do, at least, three things in giving effect to desired preference. First, it may, within certain limitations, adapt its investment program to the need of the beneficiaries. Second, it may suggest plans of distribution that will accomplish the purpose. Third, it may employ the common trust fund as a means of facilitating the periodic distribution of principal. 

1. Adaptation of investment program. Judge Wahlstrom, in his 

1941 Mid-Winter Trust Conference address already referred to, said, 29 30 

Restatement of Trusts, secs. 183 and 187.

Study Number 4, Should Trust Institutions Have Broad Discretionary Powers?, supra, p. 67 at p. 84. 31 

Scott on Trusts, sec. 25, p. 152.

9932 "I declare with emphasis-the trustee should vary investments to meet the needs of the life beneficiary.' But, in the absence of express direction in the instrument, a trustee would not be justified in making investments for the sake of greater income which, with due consideration for the interests of the principal beneficiary, it would not be prudent for a trustee to make. 

Despite the trustee's eagerness, commendable though it is, to produce as much income as possible for the income beneficiary, it still is held to the principle of fair balance between safety and productivity, which principle is stated by Professor Scott as follows: 

Where the trustee is directed to pay the income to a beneficiary during his life and on his death to pay the principal to another beneficiary, the interests of the two beneficiaries are to a certain extent antagonistic, and the trustee is under a duty so to administer the trust as to preserve a fair balance between them. He is under a duty to the former beneficiary to take care not merely to preserve the trust property but to make it productive so that a reasonable income will be available for him. He is under a duty to the latter beneficiary to take care to preserve the principal of the trust property for him. He is not under a duty to the beneficiary entitled to the income to risk the safety of the principal in order to produce a larger income, but he is under a duty to him not to sacrifice income for the purpose of increasing the value of the principal."

For the trustee to shape its investment program to meet the income requirements of the immediate beneficiaries is not the most effective, nor is it, in the long run, the safest way of giving effect to the testator's or settlor's preference for these beneficiaries. 

2. Plans of distribution. Trust institutions may be especially helpful in working out, in collaboration with the testator's or settlor's attorney, plans of distribution that will enable the trustee to give the desired preference to one beneficiary or class of beneficiaries without jeopardizing the interests of other beneficiaries (other than depriving principal beneficiaries of the principal consumed by present beneficiaries). 

Louis H. Roseberry in his 1941 Mid-Winter Trust Conference address suggested five plans for effectuating the testator's or settlor's preference for his present beneficiaries.4 The St. Louis Union Trust 

32 Trust Bulletin, February 1941, p. 5 at p. 10. 33 

Scott on Trusts, sec. 232, p. 1257. 

34 Trust Bulletin, March 1941, p. 17 at pp. 18-19.

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 305 

Company of St. Louis issued in 1941 a pamphlet, Preferred Beneficiary Trusts, containing suggested plans of distribution designed to favor immediate beneficiaries. Other trust institutions over the country have worked out and made available to the legal profession plans of distribution involving the use of principal for immediate beneficiaries.

All plans of distribution involving grants of power to use principal must be considered in the light of current revenue acts taxing the exercise of powers of appointment and giving the term, power of appointment, a much broader meaning than it has had heretofore. 

3. Employment of common trust funds. Common trust funds are facilitating the administration of trusts in which the trustee is authorized or directed to make periodic payments out of principal. 

At the present time (December 1943) the establishment of common trust funds is authorized by statute or by judicial decision in 

23 states as follows: Alabama, Arizona, Connecticut, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Illinois, Indiana, Kentucky, Louisiana, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, New York, North Carolina, Ohio, Pennsylvania, South Dakota, Tennessee, Vermont, Washington, and Wisconsin. Common trust funds are known to the writer to exist in the following eight states: Delaware, Connecticut, Minnesota, Missouri, New York, North Carolina, Pennsylvania, and

Vermont. They may have been established in other states, and perhaps others soon will be established under the recent enabling acts adopted in Alabama, Connecticut, Georgia, Illinois, New York (vital amendment), Ohio, Washington, and Wisconsin. The common trust fund movement is gaining momentum at a rather noteworthy rate.

An illustration will show how the common trust fund facilitates payments out of principal to immediate beneficiaries. Take, for example, a trust under will or a living trust of $25,000 to pay the income to the testator's or settlor's wife for life and to pay over the principal or what remains of it to the testator's or settlor's brothers. 

The wife is the preferred beneficiary. Her husband wants her to have all the income and as much of the principal as may be necessary for her comfort and support. He authorizes the trustee to use principal, in addition to income, and sets forth in the will or trust

agreement that his primary purpose in creating the trust is to provide for the comfort and maintenance of his wife and that the interests of his brothers shall be subordinate to the accomplishment of his primary purpose. That is to say, he leaves no doubt whatever as to the primary purpose of the trust or as to the power of the trustee to consume principal. Suppose, further, it is agreed that at the present time the wife's requirements are $150 a month, that the trust fund can be invested to yield 3.5%, or $875, a year; and that on this basis it will be necessary to use $72.75 of principal a month or $925 of principal a year.

With a common trust fund the trustee can keep the funds of this $25,000 trust invested almost to the last dollar and without reference to the periodic payments out of principal. If, as is the case with many of the more recent common trust funds, additions to, and withdrawals from, the common trust fund are monthly and the original unit value of the participations is only $10, the trustee can withdraw from the common trust fund each month the number of units necessary to produce the $72.75 and leave the balance invested. 

Or, if, as is the case with some of the older common trust funds, the period for addition and withdrawal is quarterly and the original unit value $100, still it will be necessary to keep uninvested at any one time only the equivalent of two months' payments (those between the two quarterly periods) plus any small amount less than the current value of a unit. Thus the trustee can keep the trust fully invested, can have the cash available each pay day to make the necessary payments, and can carry out its investment program without reference to these payments out of principal. 

The common trust fund is equally well adapted to the requirements of large trusts calling for periodic payments out of principal. 

At the present time only $25,000 of any one trust can be invested in one common trust fund.35 In a $100,000 trust, for example, in which $100 per month must be paid out of principal, the trustee may invest through a common trust fund $25,000 or some less amount to provide for the monthly payments and invest the remaining $75,000 outside the common trust fund and without reference to these payments. 

35 Regulation F-Trust Powers of National Banks, sec. 17(c) (5).

THE PREFERRED BENEFICIARIES OF TRUSTS 307 CONCLUSIÓNS 

The weight of evidence is that in the vast majority of cases the testator's or settlor's wife and children are the preferred beneficiaries of his trusts and that he wants these beneficiaries amply provided for even though it takes all or most of the trust estate. 

The trustee itself must be impartial in its dealings with beneficiaries whether their interests in the trust are simultaneous or successive. It is under a duty to keep a fair balance between or among the interests of the different beneficiaries or classes of beneficiaries unless it is expressly authorized or directed by the terms of the trust to favor one beneficiary over another. 

The power and the propriety of the court in showing preference or in authorizing the trustee to show preference are severely limited in that the court is bound by the intent, expressed or implied, of the creator of the trust. The legislature may enact statutes that result in one beneficiary being favored over another, but it cannot deal with specific cases.

The creator of a trust is the proper person to provide for the exercise of such preference as he wants shown. Full responsibility for showing or for authorizing the showing of preference rests upon him and him alone. He should consider and decide whether the income will be sufficient to meet the needs of his immediate beneficiaries. Having decided that the income will not be or that it may not be sufficient, he should give the trustee the power to use principal and to invest in common trust funds (if it is in a state that authorizes such funds). Further, he should set forth in the trust instrument his primary purpose in creating the trust and give the trustee guidance in the exercise of the preference he desires shown. 

By so doing he helps the trustee to carry out both the spirit and the letter of the trust.

 For Legal Aspects 

801, p. 2315.
