Study Number 15  BY TRUST INSTITUTIONS 

A MONG trustmen and lawyers intermediate court accounting by trust institutions is receiving an increasing amount of attention at the present time. Since 1936 at least 16 states¹ have adopted statutes on the subject. It was a topic of discussion at the 1943 session of the 

American Bar Association.² In November 1943 the Trust Division of the American Bankers Association named a special Committee on Intermediate Court Accounting. 

Definition of Terms 

Accounting, as the term is used in this Study, means the rendering of an account by a trust institution as fiduciary, showing for the 

¹Indiana (Laws 1937, Ch. 166); Louisiana (Laws 1938, Act. No. 81, secs. 34, 95); 

Massachusetts (Act of 1938, Ch. 154); New Jersey (Laws 1938, Ch. 108; 1939, Ch. 140; 1941, Chs. 240-241); Connecticut (Laws 1939, Ch. 183; 1941, Ch. 91; 

1943, Ch. 100); Delaware (Laws 1939, Ch. 143); Illinois (Probate Act 1939, secs. 

289-308, 309-323, 337; Laws 1941, H.B. 558); Kansas (Laws 1939, Ch. 180); 

Tennessee (Public Acts 1939, Ch. 175, secs. 3, 7; 1941, Ch. 62); Colorado (Laws 

1941, H.B. 1376, sec. 27); Nevada (Laws 1941, Ch. 135); Rhode Island (Laws 1941, Ch. 978; 1942, Ch. 1144); Washington (Laws 1941, Ch. 229); Georgia (Laws 1943, No. 406); New York (Laws 1943, Ch. 611); Ohio (Laws 1943, H.B. 268). 2 

Brewer, Nelson J., Streamlining the Procedure for Fiduciary Accounting, Pro ceedings, Sections of Real Property, Probate and Trust Law, American Bar Associa tion, 1943; Trusts and Estates, September 1943, p. 219. 309

accounting period what the fiduciary has received and what it has expended and what receipts and expenditures have been allocated to principal and what have been allocated to income.³ 

An intermediate accounting is one rendered while the account still is in process of administration as distinct from the final accounting rendered at the time the account is being closed. In some statesin Georgia, for example-one finds the term intermediate final accounting, meaning that the account is final up to the time of this accounting but that later accounts will be rendered. The term intermediate always implies that there will be a later, final accounting. 

Court accounting means rendering an account to the court as distinct from private accounting to the creator or to the beneficiary of the trust.

This Study is limited to intermediate court accounting and, therefore, does not embrace final court accounting or either intermediate or final private accountnig. While, as stated above, there has been important recent legislation on intermediate court accounting in at least 16 states, it has been thought advisable to select for analysis and comparative study only four of the statutes—namely, the Uniform Trustees' Accounting Act which has been adopted in form or in substance in Indiana,* 

Kansas, Nevada, and Washington; the Georgia act; the New 

York act applying to accounting in the Supreme Court of that state; and the Ohio act.10 For the sake of brevity, the statutes will be referred to respectively as the Uniform Act, the Georgia act, the New York act, and the Ohio act. With respect to the New York act of 1943, it should be stated that the principal effect of this statute is to provide for the Supreme Court a simplified procedure for a hearing on an intermediate account similar in the main to the procedure already followed in the Surrogate's court. Thus the New 

Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 963, p. 2786; Scott on Trusts, sec. 172, p. 919; 

Shattuck Revision, sec. 21, p. 76. *Laws 1937, Ch. 166. 

Laws 1939, Ch. 180. 

6 'Laws 1941, Ch. 135. 

7 Laws 1941, Ch. 229. 

8 Laws 1943, No. 406. 

9 Laws 1943, Ch. 611. 10 

Laws 1943, H.B. 268.

York act relates more to the procedure than to the substance of intermediate accounting.

While special attention is centered upon these four acts, reference is made to some of the distinctive features of legislation on intermediate court accounting in other states. Furthermore, the fact that only these four recent acts have been selected for analysis and comparative study should not be construed as an implication that a study of the other recent acts or even of some of those enacted before 1937 would not be equally rewarding or, in fact, essential to a complete grasp of the subject.

TO WHAT TYPES OF ACCOUNTS Acts AppLICABLE 

The main types of accounts to which the recent intermediate court accounting acts apply are executorships and administratorships, trusteeships under will, guardianships, trusteeships under agreement or by declaration (living trusteeships), and trusteeships by order of court. Certain types of accounts are expressly excluded from the provisions of the acts. 

Executorships and Administratorships 

The Georgia and the Ohio acts apply to executorships and administratorships; the Uniform Act and the New York act do not. 

In the ordinary executorship or administratorship intermediate accounting is not a matter of so much concern as it is in trusteeships, for in most cases the estate is settled and distributed within a year or two and the executor or the administrator need make only a first-and-final accounting. It is only in connection with executorships and administratorships which continue year after year and involve the management of property and in some instances the investment of funds accounts partaking of the nature of trusteeships-that intermediate accounting becomes a matter for legislation. 

Trusteeships under Will

The Uniform Act, the Georgia act, and the Ohio act apply to trusteeships under will. The New York act applies in the main to a certain type of trusteeship under will. In New York the Surrogate's Court and the Supreme Court have concurrent jurisdiction

of the normal trust under will, although in most cases in a normal trust under will the trustee accounts to the Surrogate's Court; but the Supreme Court has exclusive jurisdiction of a trust under will the property in which is directed by the will to be added to and administered as a part of a living trust. The practical effect of the 

1943 New York act is to extend to this special type of trust under will in the Supreme Court the intermediate court accounting provisions that theretofore existed only for the usual type of trust under will in the Surrogate's Court.¹¹ Guardianships

The Georgia and the Ohio acts apply to guardianships of the property of minors and of incompetents. The New York act does not apply to guardianships, since they are covered by previous legislation. Nor does the Uniform Act apply to guardianships. 

Trusteeships under Agreement or by Declaration Under the Georgia act a trustee under agreement or by declaration may elect to have an intermediate court accounting. The Ohio act does not apply to this type of account. The New York act applies expressly to trusts under agreement or by declaration, but the court accounting is optional with the trustee or an interested party. The 

Uniform Act as adopted in Indiana, Kansas, and Washington applies to this type of account, but the trustee may be relieved of the requirements of the Act by the creator or beneficiary of the trust or by the court. The Uniform Act as adopted in Nevada does not apply to trusteeships under agreement or by declaration unless expressly so provided for in the trust instrument.

Under none of these acts is the trustee under agreement or by declaration required to make intermediate court accountings under the statute if the creator of the trust relieves the trustee from making such accountings. Of course, in all cases for cause shown, a court of equity has the right, apart from the statute, to order an accounting.¹2 

In Maine the settlor or trustee of a living trust may have the 11 

Surrogate's Court Act, sec. 171 as amended Laws 1934, Ch. 172; secs. 255-256 as amended Laws 1937, Ch. 272. 12 

Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 963, p. 2786; Scott on Trusts, sec. 260, p. 1467.

appointment of the trustee confirmed by the probate court, in which case the trustee thereafter must make a court accounting every three years unless excused or relieved therefrom by the settlor or beneficiary.13 Also in Rhode Island under a 1942 statute a trustee under will or under agreement may apply to the court for the allowance of its accounts. 14 

Trusteeships by Order of Court 

The Georgia act applies to all fiduciaries now required by law to make annual returns, which would include trusteeships under order of court. The Ohio act applies to all fiduciaries appointed by and accountable to the probate court. The New York act does not apply to this type of trusteeship. The Uniform Act applies to trustees appointed by "a court or other authority.”

Types of Accounts Expressly Excluded 

The New York act excludes voting trusts, mortgages, trusts for the benefit of creditors, and certain special types of trusts specified in the real property law and in the personal property law of the state. The Uniform Act excludes resulting trusts, constructive trusts, business trusts where certificates of beneficial interest are issued to the beneficiaries, investment trusts, voting trusts, insurance trusts prior to the death of the insured, trusts in the nature of mortgages or pledges, trusts created by judgment or decree of a federal court or a state court other than the probate court, liquidation trusts, and trusts for the sole purpose of paying dividends, interest or interest coupons, salaries, wages, or pensions. The Washington version of the Uniform Art expressly excludes executors, administrators, and guardians.

FREQUENCY OF INTERMEDIATE COURT ACCOUNTING 

Some of the acts specify the frequency of the intermediate accounting in different types of accounts, while others simply provide the procedure for accounting without specifying the frequency. Also, 13 

Revised Statutes, 1930, Ch. 82, secs. 14-15; Laws 1939, Ch. 24. 

14 Laws 1942, Ch. 1144.

some of the acts make a distinction as to frequency between the filing of and the hearing on the account. 

In Different Types of Accounts 

Under the Uniform Act the trustee, if required to file at all, must file an intermediate account annually. Likewise, under the 

Georgia act the fiduciary must file its accounts annually. Under the 

Ohio act an executor or administrator must file its first account within nine months of its qualification and each six months thereafter. Other fiduciaries, if required to render an account to the court, must render an account at least once every two years. The 

New York act provides the procedure for but does not specify the frequency of the accounting.

Filing of and Hearing on Account 

Filing or rendering the account simply means depositing it with the court; hearing on the account means consideration of and action upon the account by the court. 

Under the Uniform Act, upon the third intermediate accounting --that is, upon the filing of the third annual account-and every three years thereafter the trustee must apply to the court for a hearing on and approval of all unapproved accounts. Under the 

Georgia act at the end of the third year from its qualification and each three years thereafter the fiduciary may file a report known as an intermediate final report upon which there is a hearing. Thus, under the Uniform Act the court hearing on the account is compulsory, unless relieved by the terms of the instrument, the consent of the beneficiaries, or an order of court in the manner provided in the Act; whereas, under the Georgia act the hearing on the account is optional with the fiduciary.

Size of Account 

In some states the frequency of the accounting is determined by the size of the account. In Delaware, for example, a trustee under a will probated after April 5, 1909, must account every two years if the principal of the account is over $100,000, every three years it is between $25,000 and $100,000, and every five years if it is not over $25,000.15 In Virginia, if the account is not over $500, and in 15 if 

Revised Code 1935, sec. 4400; Laws 1939, Ch. 143; Rule 171, Court of Chancery.

West Virginia, if it is less than $300, the fiduciary is required to account only every three years. In Wisconsin, where the assets are less than $500, a trustee under will is required to account only upon the termination of the trust. In North Dakota, Oklahoma, and Utah a guardian must account semi-annually if the account is over $20,000; otherwise, annually. In many other communities by custom or by rule of court the frequency of the intermediate accounting is governed by the size of the account. 

CONTENTS OF INTERMEDIATE COURT ACCOUNTS 

Some of the acts specify with great particularity the contents of accounts; while others make only general requirements as to the contents, relying upon the established accounting practice of the jurisdiction to supply the details. The Uniform Act and the Georgia. act are specific; the Ohio and the New York acts, general. 

Contents Specified 

Under the Uniform Act the intermediate account is made up of eight parts as follows: Statement of period covered, list of interested parties, principal account, income account, disclaimer of self-dealing, statement as to unpaid claims, summary, and other facts required by the court. Only five of these parts call for comment. 

1. List of interested parties. The account must contain the names and addresses of the living beneficiaries known to the trustee, with a statement as to those known to be minors or incompetents, a description of unborn or unascertained beneficiaries, and name of the surety on the trustee's bond and the amount of the bond. 

2. Principal account. In a separate schedule the account must show (a) the principal on hand at the beginning of the period and the then status of its investments; (b) the investments received from the creator of the trust and still held; (c) additions to the principal during the period with the dates and sources of acquisition; (d) investments collected, sold, or charged off during the period with the loss or gain and whether credited to principal or to income; (e) investments made during the period, with the date, source, and cost of each; (f) deductions from principal during period with the date and purpose of each; and (g) principal on hand at the end of

the period, how invested, and the estimated market value of each investment. 

3. Income account. In another separate schedule the account must show (a) the income on hand at the beginning of the period and in what form held; (b) income received during the period, when, and from what source; (c) income paid out during the period, when, to whom, for what purpose; and (d) income on hand at end of period and how invested. 

4. Statement as to self-dealing. The account must contain a statement to the effect that the trustee neither bought trust property from nor sold trust property to any affiliate, officer, employee, nominee of the trustee, or nominee of an affiliate of the trustee. 

5. Statement as to unpaid claims. The account must contain a statement of unpaid claims with reason for failure to pay them, and a statement as to estate or inheritance taxes on the trust property. 

As to the contents of the account the Georgia act is practically the same as the Uniform Act, except that the Georgia act omits the section on self-dealing.

Although Kansas incorporated in its Probate Code of 1939 most of the Uniform Act, it omitted material parts of the section on contents of the intermediate accounts. Likewise, Indiana made certain changes in this section.

General Statement as to Contents 

While the New York act, being primarily a procedural act, does not specify the contents of the account, it is presumed that any account rendered to the Supreme Court under the act would, in general, contain the same items as an account rendered to the Surrogate's Court. To an account filed in the Surrogate's Court there must be appended an affidavit to the effect that the account contains, to the best of the accounting party's knowledge and belief, “a full and true statement of all receipts and disbursements on account of the estate or fund, and of all money and other property belonging to the estate or fund which have come into his hands or been received by any other person, by his order or authority, for his use. 

Under the Ohio act, an intermediate court account must contain an itemized statement of all funds, assets, and investments known 

16 Surrogate's Court Act, sec. 264. "16

to or in the possession of the fiduciary at the end of the accounting period, any changes in investments since the last accounting and receipts, disbursements, and distributions verified by voucher or proof. The accounts of trustees appointed by and accountable to the probate court must and those of other fiduciaries may show receipts and disbursements separately identified as to principal and income. 

The Massachusetts statute provides that the account shall consist of three schedules: one showing the amount of personal property according to the inventory or at the time of the last previous accounting and income and other property received and gains from sales or otherwise; another showing payments, charges, losses, and distributions; and a third schedule showing the investments and the changes in investments. The trustee must list the receipts, payments, and charges of principal and income separately.¹ 17 

PROCEDURE IN INTERMEDIATE ACCOUNTING 

The term procedure in this section refers rather to the hearing on the account than to the mere filing or rendering of the account. 

The principal steps in a hearing on an intermediate court account are (1) starting the proceeding, (2) notifying the interested parties, (3) having the parties represented, (4) hearing on the account, and (5) making the order of court with respect to the account. 

Starting the Proceeding

Under the Uniform Act, either the trustee or a beneficiary may start a proceeding by petitioning the court for a hearing on the account, and every three years the trustee must petition the court for a hearing on and an approval of all unapproved accounts. 

Under the Georgia act the fiduciary desiring "an intermediate final accounting" files with the court a petition and report setting forth all the information required for annual returns and, in addition, the information specified in the preceding section of this Study on the contents of the account.

Under the Ohio act, upon the filing of an intermediate account, either upon the request of the fiduciary or at the instance of the court or upon motion of any person having an interest in the estate 

17 General Laws 1932, Ch. 206, sec. 2.

or trust, the account must be set for hearing. When an account is set for hearing, the hearing is upon this account, upon all other accounts filed after the effective date of the act and not theretofore settled under the act, and upon any other account or accounts filed prior to the effective date of the act and specified in the notice. 

Under the New York act an intermediate account is brought on for hearing by an order of court to show cause signed by a justice of the Supreme Court based upon a verified petition setting forth the facts claimed, the names and addresses of the parties interested, and the relief sought. If the trustee itself petitions for a hearing on the account, it must file, along with its petition, a verified account. 

Notifying the Interested Parties

The interested parties, who must be notified of an intermediate accounting, fall into several categories, including resident adult and legally competent persons, non-resident adult and legally competent persons, minors and adults under legal disability, unborn or unascertained beneficiaries, and contingent beneficiaries.

Under the Uniform Act, notice may be served on any known beneficiary by delivering a copy of the notice and of the account to him personally or to his guardian or attorney of record, or by sending it by registered mail, with return receipt requested, to him or his guardian or attorney of record at his last known address. The Act further provides that, if the residence of any beneficiary is unknown or there is doubt as to the existence of one or more persons as benficiaries, the court shall make such provision as to service of notice as it believes proper. The notice by the trustee of the application for a hearing on and approval of the account must inform the beneficiaries of the amount of commissions or other compensation to be requested by the trustee on such hearing and the amount of other fees which the court then will be requested to allow. 

Under the Georgia act, upon a petition for a hearing being filed with the court, the court, upon written order, fixes the time and/ place of the hearing and names of the persons on whom service shall be made by personal service or by service by publication for nonresidents. Service on contingent beneficiaries need not be required by the order. Residents of the state must be served personally. Service on non-residents shall be perfected in the same manner that service is perfected on non-residents for the probate of wills in solemn form.

The practice of the court in this respect is to order notice by publication once a week for four weeks in the newspaper in which the sheriff's advertisements of that county are published. 

Under the Ohio act, the notice must set forth the time and place of the hearing, specify the account to be considered and acted upon, the period covered, and must state that the person notified is required to examine the account, inquire into all statements thereof and the manner in which the fiduciary has executed his trust, and make any objections he may have, or the account may be approved without further notice. The notice must be served either by personal service of a copy upon any interested person found in the state; or by registered mail to such a person at his last known address in the United States; or, as to non-residents of the United States, by publication in a newspaper and mailing a copy of the publication to any nonresident whose address is known to the fiduciary; or by a combination of two or more of these methods. Notice may be served personally on any person not under disability. Notice upon any person under disability must be served on his guardian or other legal representative in one of the ways specified. Also, a person not under disability may waive service of notice and consent to the approval of the account by the court.

Under the New York act the order to show cause, with a copy of the petition, must be served in the manner prescribed for the personal service of a summons, "except that the order to show cause may be served upon a defendant without the state without the necessity of an order of publication, and except that the justice signing the order to show cause may in his discretion prescribe such other manner and time of service as he deems appropriate.” In New York the hearing on the intermediate account is regarded as a "special proceeding" to be conducted according to the established practice with respect to special proceedings. This has the advantage of not introducing a new kind of proceeding in connection with hearings on intermediate accounts.

The special features of these acts-such as sending notices by registered mail, sending copies of newspaper notices to non-resident parties whose address is known, and waiver of notice are designed to make the giving of notice as easy as it is practicable to do without sacrificing the rights of the beneficiaries.

Representation of the Parties at the Hearing 

Another important point covered by the acts is the representation of the parties at the hearing-especially minors, adults under legal disability, unborn or unascertained beneficiaries, and contingent beneficiaries. Persons not under disability may appear in person or by attorney, or they may not appear at all at the hearing, and yet, if they have been notified of the hearing in the manner provided by the statute, they will be bound by the order of court. 

Under the Uniform Act, minors, incompetents, and unborn or unascertained beneficiaries may be represented by a competent living member of the class to which such beneficiaries would belong, or by a guardian ad litem as the court deems best. Thus, an adult competent brother may represent a minor or incompetent or unborn brother or sister who is or may be a beneficiary. In case of doubt as to the existence of a person as a beneficiary, the court will make such provision for the representation of such person as it believes proper. 

The Georgia act simply provides that guardians ad litem shall be appointed for minors and persons under disability. While the court must name the persons upon whom service shall be made, the act provides that service on contingent remaindermen need not be required by such order.

Under the Ohio act a person under disability must be represented by a guardian of his estate or by a guardian ad litem. An unborn or unascertained beneficiary must be represented by a guardian ad litem "unless the court finds that such person is fully or adequately represented by one or more competent living members of the class to which such person does or would belong, or by the guardian of the estate of a member of such class." Thus, the court may find that an unborn or unascertained beneficiary is fully and adequately represented not only by an adult, competent brother-if the unborn or unascertained beneficiary would belong to that class-but even by the guardian of the estate of a minor or incompetent brother belonging to that class. Contingent beneficiaries must be served with notice of the hearing if they are in being and ascertainable. The beneficiaries of charitable trusts, if not represented by existing and identifiable corporations and organizations, are represented by the Attorney General of the state.

Under the New York act a minor or incompetent must be repre-

sented either by a guardian ad litem or by a guardian or committee. 

The New York act makes special provision, as follows, for the representation of contingent beneficiaries: In the case of persons whose interest in the trust property is dependent upon the happening of a future event, it is sufficient to make parties to the proceeding the persons in being who would constitute such class if such event had happened immediately before the commencement of the proceeding, and the final order in the proceeding shall be binding and conclusive on all present and future members of the class. Suppose, for example, it is a trust to pay the income to A (wife and mother) for life and upon A's death to pay over the principal, discharged of the trust, to such of A's children as then shall be living. Each child's interest is contingent upon his surviving his mother. Under the New 

York act it is sufficient to serve notice upon A's children living at the time of the commencement of the accounting proceeding; the order of the court in the proceeding will be binding upon any children of A that may be born thereafter.

The New York act further provides that, where an interest in trust property has been limited to a person who is a party to the proceeding and the same interest has been further limited upon the happening of a future event to a person or persons who are or may be distributees, heirs at law, issue, or other kindred of such party, it shall not be necessary to make such distributees, heirs at law, issue, or other kindred parties to the proceeding, and the proceeding shall be binding and conclusive upon them. This provision is understood to mean that, if presumptive members of the class are parties to the proceeding, potential members of the same class need not be made parties.18 For example, in an intermediate accounting in a trust to pay the income to A for life and on A's death to pay over the principal to A's children and, if A has no children surviving A, to pay over the principal to A's grandchildren, it would not be necessary to make A's grandchildren (the potential members of the class) parties to the proceeding if A's children (the presumptive members of the class) were made parties. 

Thus, in theory, the presumptive members of a class represent the potential members of the same class. 18 

Brewer, Nelson J., Streamlining Fiduciary Accounting, Trusts and Estates, September 1943, p. 219 at p. 221.

One of the significant features of the Uniform Act, the Ohio act, and the New York act each is that it provides for the representation of unborn, unascertained, or contingent beneficiaries by the existing members of the class to which these beneficiaries, if they were born, in being, ascertained or their interest was vested at the time of the proceeding, would belong. This sometimes is referred to as virtual representation. Since there is a community of interest among all the beneficiaries of the same class, it would seem that the present beneficiaries, in protecting their own interests in connection with the intermediate accounting, would, for practical purposes, amply protect the interests of all other presumptive or potential members of their class.

Hearing on Account 

1 After the proceeding for the intermediate accounting has been started, the date and place of the hearing set, the interested parties notified, and representation provided for minors, incompetents, unborn or unascertained, contingent, and other classes of beneficiaries requiring special representation, the next step is the court hearing on the account.

Under the Uniform Act on the day of the hearing the trustee must file an affidavit proving the timely delivery to the known beneficiaries of the documents required by the statute or by the order of court. The procedure as to the filing of objections, examination of the trustee and other witnesses, inspection of the trust property, adjournment, references to a master or other representative of the court, amendment of the account, and similar matters is in the discretion of the court.

Under the Georgia act, if any objection to the fiduciary's “report, actings, and accounting" is filed by any interested party at or before the time fixed for the hearing, the hearing on the account is continued automatically until the next regular term of the court and then heard as are other causes pending in that court. If no objection is filed, the court approves the account, without a formal hearing, at the next regular term of the court. 

Under the New York act, after taking proof with respect to issues that may be raised by answer or objection, the court will enter a final order approving or disapproving the account. If the interested

parties file a written consent to the entry of a proposed final order, approving the account, it may be presented at special term for consideration as an uncontested matter, and the court may approve the account or make such other disposition as justice may require. Also the court may refer the account to a referee to hear and determine the issues unless questions of law exclusively have been raised or unless objection to the account has been filed. 

In Ohio, upon the filing of the account, any interested person shall make any objections which he may have to the account or any matters pertaining to the execution of the trust. Such objections must be specific and in writing. The court thereupon inquires into, considers, and determines all matters pertaining to the account and to the manner in which the fiduciary has executed the trust. 

Action of the Court 

Under the Uniform Act the court may approve or disapprove the account. The court may surcharge the trustee for any loss caused by a breach of trust committed by the trustee. If it finds that the trustee has failed to perform any of the duties with respect to intermediate accounting imposed by the act, it may, in its discretion, remove the trustee, reduce or disallow its compensation, or inflict any other civil penalty. 

In Georgia the court may approve the account or it may surcharge the trustee for any amount authorized under the law and evidence. 

In New York the court may approve or disapprove the account and may impose such further provisions and conditions as justice and equity may require under the circumstances. 

In Ohio the court shall, upon the hearing, inquire into, consider, and determine all matters relative to the account and to the manner in which the fiduciary has executed the trust. The court may approve the account or it may disapprove the account and surcharge the fiduciary for any loss caused by a breach of trust committed by the fiduciary. If the fiduciary fails to file his account as required by the act, the court, upon its own motion or upon complaint of any interested party, may issue a citation to the fiduciary to show cause why it has not filed the account; and if the court finds the delay was unreasonable, it shall make no allowance to the trustee for its services.

Thus, under all these acts the court has the power, either upon its own motion or on the petition of the trustee or of any interested party, to compel an intermediate accounting, to enforce its orders with respect to the accounting, and effectively to punish the fiduciary -by removal, by reduction or disallowance of compensation, or otherwise for failure to comply with the statute.

In all cases any order of court approving, disapproving, or otherwise disposing of the account is subject to the right of appeal by the trustee or by any interested party. 

FINALITY OF THE  

Assume now that the accounting statute has been complied with in every respect, that the court has approved the account, and that there has been no appeal from the decree approving the account. 

How much protection to the trustee is this court approval of the account? After the account has been approved, what right has any interested party to have the account reopened and reconsidered? 

Effect of Court Approval 

Under the Uniform Act, the effect of court approval is to relieve the trustee and its sureties from liability to all beneficiaries then known or in being or who thereafter become known or in being for all the trustee's acts and omissions which are fully and accurately described in the account, including the then investment of the trust funds.

The Georgia act declares that all parties in interest shall be bound by the order of the court in the intermediate accounting under the statute and that thereafter no such person shall be heard in any court, except upon appeal, to contest or question any matters or things covered in said accounting and the order thereon in the absence of fraud, accident, or mistake.

Under the Ohio act, upon the approval of a fiduciary's account, the fiduciary and his sureties shall, subject to the right of appeal, be relieved from liability to all interested persons properly before the court, for the fiduciary's administration of his trust during the accounting period, including the investment of trust funds.

Reopening the Account for Fraud

The acts provide for the reopening and reconsideration of the account for fraud or mistake, and some of the acts make a distinction between opening for fraud and opening for mistake.

The Uniform Act provides that on motion an account may be reopened by the court for fraud but provides further that a beneficiary may not move for the reopening because of fraud more than days after he discovers the existence of the fraud. Indiana filled the blank with "60," Nevada, "90," and Washington substituted "six months" for days." 

In Georgia, while the act provides for reopening for fraud, it does not specify the time within which the motion must be made after the discovery of the fraud.

The New York act makes no reference to reopening for fraud. 

In Ohio no order approving an intermediate account shall be vacated because of fraud unless a motion to vacate such order is filed within one year after discovery of the existence of the fraud, and the vacation of any such order shall not be effective against a bona fide purchaser for value.

In California the courts draw a distinction between extrinsic and intrinsic fraud, declaring that after time for appeal has expired an intermediate account, which has been approved by the court, can be reopened only for extrinsic fraud in obtaining the order of approval, in a proceeding in a court of general equity jurisdiction. 

Extrinsic fraud, the court declares, consists of such "fraud as prevents a real trial of the issues involved in the case, like conduct which prevents the injured party from receiving notice of the action or which causes the absence of necessary witnesses."19 By thus limiting the reopening to extrinsic fraud California gives court approval of intermediate accounts a greater degree of finality than is true of most other states. 

Reopening of Account for Mistake

Under the Uniform Act no account shall be reopened because of 19 

Clavey v. Loney, 80 Cal. App. 20, 251 Pac. 232; Security First National Bank v. 

Superior Court, 1 Cal. (2d) 749, 37 Pac. (2d) 69; Ringwalt v. Bank of America, 3 Cal. (2d) 680, 45 Pac. (2d) 967; Carr v. Bank of America, 11 Cal. (2d) 366; 

Estate of Lindauer, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 160.

a mistake more than a year after approval of the account. Motion to reopen for mistake may be made by either the trustee or a beneficiary.

The Georgia act provides for reopening for accident or mistake but does not specify the time within which it must be done after approval of the account or discovery of the mistake or after the accident.

The New York act makes no reference to reopening the account for mistake.

In Ohio no order approving the intermediate account shall be vacated because of a mistake unless a motion therefor is filed within a year after the date of such order. 

The Massachusetts statute of 1938 uses the term "manifest error," the precise meaning of which has not yet been determined.20 

From the foregoing it appears that an intermediate accounting under the statutes generally serves to relieve the trust institution and its surety (if it has one) from liability for its administration of the account during the accounting period, including the investment of the trust funds, except (a) as to interested parties not notified of the hearing or represented at the hearing in the manner provided by the statute, (b) as to acts or omissions not fully and accurately described in the account, (c) fraud, or (d) mistake. The account cannot be reopened for fraud unless the motion to reopen is made within a short time-six months is the longest period specified in a statute-after the discovery of the fraud. California allows a reopening of the account only for extrinsic fraud. An account cannot be reopened for mistake unless the motion to reopen is made within a short time-one year is the usual time-after the approval of the account or after the discovery of the mistake. In some states the court approval of the intermediate accounting is not binding upon minors or incompetents until the lapse of a stated period-180 days in Indiana-after their disabilities have been removed.

CONCLUSIONS 

The distinguishing characteristic of the recent court accounting statutes, as compared with the earlier ones, is that, whereas the earlier 20 'Laws 1938, ch. 154; Note by Guy Newhall, Trusts and Estates, May 1938, p. 574; Wilbur v. Hallett, 26 N.E. 322 (1940).

statutes were designed to bring reluctant fiduciaries to book, the recent statutes are designed to give an opportunity to account to fiduciaries ready and willing to account for their trust administration.21

Trust institutions and beneficiaries of trusts alike are conscious of the need for some provision for intermediate accountings in trust administration for the protection of both the trust institution and the beneficiary and for the guidance of the trust institution in its further administration of the trust. Where all the beneficiaries are of legal age and competence, it is possible to accomplish this by accounting directly to them. But where some of the beneficiaries, as so frequently is the case, are minors or adults under legal disability or are unborn, unascertained, or contingent, court accounting seems to be the only practical method of obtaining the desired protection and guidance.

In intermediate court accounting by trust institutions, these are some of the objectives to be striven for: (1) protection of the rights of all interested parties, (2) preservation of the confidentiality of trust relationships, (3) keeping the cost of the accounting within reasonable bounds, and (4) making the accounting final and conclusive as to all parties and as to all matters properly before the court. 

Protection of the rights of interested parties involves giving to them sufficient notice of the hearing and proper representation at the hearing on the account.

Preservation of the confidentiality of the trust relationships means that the testator or settlor should have the right to relieve the trustee of the duty to make intermediate court accountings if he thinks this course is necessary to preserve confidentiality. 

Both the direct and the indirect costs connected with the accounting should be kept within reasonable bounds so that the expenses of accounting will not be burdensome to the beneficiaries; for, after all, they, not the trustee, bear the expense of the court accounting. This objective of intermediate court accounting merits special emphasis. 

Except for mistake or fraud that prevents the account being fully heard and considered on its merits, the intermediate court accounting 

"Judd, Orin G., Some Phases of Practice in Inter Vivos Trust Accountings in New 

York, 42 Columbia Law Review, p. 207.

should relieve the trust institution from liability for its acts or omissions as to all matters fully and accurately described and presented in the account, including the trust investments. The accounting should be binding upon all parties properly notified of or represented at the hearing or who, being adult and competent, have approved or have waived notice of the hearing on the account. 

Gradually these objectives may be-in fact, are being attained through acts which, while not uniform in language, are nevertheless uniform in purpose and are adjusted to the varying laws and practices of the different states.

Estates, May 1942, p. 499; Graduate School of Banking thesis, 1941.

Accountings in New York, 42 Columbia Law Review, p. 207.

Companies, April 1937, p. 447.

Study Number 16 

E 

RIGHTS OF OUT-OF-STATE TRUST INSTITUTIONS 

VERY trust institution, regardless of its size or location, in carrying on its trust business is called upon from time to time to deal with property and with persons in other states than the one in which the trust institution itself is located. As executor or trustee under will, as administrator, or as guardian of the property of a minor or incompetent, it finds that a part of the property of the estate, trust, or guardianship is located in another state or in other states. It finds debtors, creditors, beneficiaries, distributees, and other interested persons living in other states. It finds itself under the will of a nonresident named trustee of property located in the decedent's home state. Consequently, the rights of a trust institution located in one state to deal with property and with persons in other states are matters of practical concern to all trust institutions. 

Any trust institution, whether a state-chartered bank or trust company or a national bank, whose principal office is located in another state is, for the purpose of this Study, an out-of-state trust institution. 

The term out-of-state, instead of foreign, is employed in order to avoid confusion when later in this Study the right of a trust institution to qualify as a "foreign corporation" to do business in a state is discussed.

Types of Accounts Covered 

It has seemed advisable to limit this Study mainly to the rights of out-of-state trust institutions as executor, administrator, trustee under 329

will, and guardian and not to devote much of the discussion to their rights as trustee of living trusts or insurance trusts, as personal agent, or as corporate trustee or corporate agent. 

Living trusts, insurance trusts, and personal agencies give rise to few problems of this nature. No matter how high or how strong a barrier a state may raise against out-of-state trust institutions, that state cannot prevent its own citizens from taking their cash, insurance policies, or securities over into another state and there establishing a living trust, an insurance trust, or an agency. As Professor Bogert says:

There would seem to be little doubt of the ability of a resident of State X to make a corporation of State Y trustee of a valid trust by inter vivos acts. The trust property, or symbols of its ownership, would be delivered to the foreign trustee, and the trust would procure a situs in the foreign state, so that the trustee would be administering a trust within the jurisdiction of its origin.¹ 

Types of Rights Covered 

In this Study the rights of out-of-state trust institutions are discussed under these three heads: (1) the right to exercise powers, (2) the right to serve in fiduciary capacities, and (3) the right to engage in trust business.

Capacity and Authority Distinguished 

The capacity and the authority of an out-of-state trust institution must be distinguished. A state-chartered bank or trust company receives its capacity to act through its charter from its home state but receives its authority to act in another state from that state. The state of incorporation, for example, says whether or not it can serve as executor; the other state says whether or not it may serve as executor under the will of one of its residents. A national bank receives its capacity from the Federal Government but its authority to act in a state, other than the one in which it is located, from the laws of that state. For the purposes of this Study, it is taken for granted that the trust institution has ample capacity; the discussion relates wholly to its authority to act in another state.2 1 

Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 132, pp. 398-399. 

2 Prentice-Hall Trust Service, secs. 16, 102-16, 104 and cases cited.

RIGHT TO EXERCISE POWERS 

Suppose a trust institution of one state, duly appointed and qualified in that state and serving as executor, administrator, trustee, or guardian of a resident of that state, finds a portion of the trust property located in another state. What powers has this trust institution with respect to this property, and what must it do to acquire the right to exercise these powers?

This section does not raise the question of the right of a trust institution to serve as executor, administrator, trustee, or guardian in another state but only of its right, while serving in any of these capacities under appointment in its home state, to exercise powers with respect to property located in another state. 

Executor or Administrator

Most of the states already have comparatively simple and inexpensive methods whereby an out-of-state executor or administrator may exercise all the powers needed for the proper handling of local property. This usually is accomplished in one of two ways: either by a grant of powers or by a grant of authority to qualify as ancillary administrator. 

1. Grant of powers. In some states the grant of powers to out-ofstate trust institutions to deal with local property is specific; in others, it is general. a. Specific. Among the specific powers usually granted out-ofstate executors and administrators are the following: to sue and be sued; to receive, collect, and receipt for debts due the estate, including bank deposits; to execute releases, discharges, and assignments; to satisfy mortgages, liens, and judgments of record; to foreclose mortgages; to obtain transfer of securities of local corporations; and to sell real property and personal property to pay debts or legacies or to remove the assets from the state. b. General. A few states make only a general grant of powers. 

Georgia, for example, provides that an out-of-state executor or administrator, “upon filing with the court a certified copy of such proceedings (for qualification in the other state), shall be entitled to use all the processes and remedies prescribed by the laws of this state, in 3 Infra, p. 335.

the same manner as if qualified under the laws of this state.

" 994 2. Grant of authority to qualify as ancillary administrator. A considerable number of states provide for the exercise of powers by outof-state executors or administrators by permitting them to qualify as ancillary administrators even when these states would not permit or would restrict the right of an out-of-state trust institution to qualify as executor or administrator of the estate of a resident. Having qualified as ancillary administrator, these out-of-state executors and administrators proceed to exercise all the powers requisite to the proper handling of the property located in that state. Ohio, for example, after specifying the conditions upon which an out-of-state trust institution may enter the state, either to serve in fiduciary capac ities or to engage in trust business generally, goes on to say that nothing in the act shall prevent a foreign corporation from qualifying as executor or administrator of property in Ohio, after appointment as executor or administrator by the courts of any other state as provided by law, when the decedent was a resident of such other state at the time of his death." 

3. Procedure for acquiring right. In most states the procedure whereby an out-of-state executor or administrator acquires the right either to exercise powers or to serve as ancillary administrator is brief, simple, and inexpensive. It involves only such steps as (a) filing a certified or authenticated copy of the letters testamentary or letters of administration and a statement that the letters have not been revoked, (b) naming a process agent and agreeing to be bound by service upon him, (c) filing a certified copy of the charter of the trust institution, and (d) executing a bond or offering satisfactory evidence that it already has given a bond ample for the protection of local creditors or claimants or that it is not required by its home state to give a bond with surety. 

Trustee under Will

The powers of an out-of-state trustee under will over local property and the procedure for obtaining the right to exercise these powers are not nearly so specific as they are for executors and administrators and, as will be seen later, for guardians. Yet it not infre- 

Code 1933, sec. 113-2402. 

5 General Code, sec. 710-154.

quently happens that a testator wants to name a local individual or a trust institution his executor and an out-of-state trust institution trustee under his will. Can the executor deliver the property to the trustee and, if so, what powers can the trustee exercise? 

1. Grant of powers. The following are, at best, only a few scattered references in the statutes to the exercise of powers by out-ofstate trustees under will. In the reciprocal states, if an out-of-state trust institution is authorized to serve as trustee under the will of a resident, then presumably it would be authorized also to deal with the local property of a non-resident decedent. The Connecticut reciprocal statute refers expressly to non-residents leaving property within the state, thus giving a trustee under will from another reciprocal state express power to deal with that property." Although Iowa is not a reciprocal state and does not admit out-of-state trust institutions to serve in fiduciary capacities, its statute nevertheless provides for the exercise of powers by out-of-state trustees under will. The 

Kansas statute includes trustees under will in the term fiduciary and expressly authorizes an out-of-state fiduciary, upon filing a certified copy of its letters or other record of authority and a certificate that the same still is in force, to assign, extend, release, satisfy, foreclose mortgages, judgments, or liens and to collect any debt secured thereby belonging to the estate represented by such fiduciary. A statute of Maine provides the procedure whereby the proceeds of the sale of local property may be turned over to the out-of-state trustee under will.10

From the point of view of trust administration, the right of an out-of-state trust institution as trustee to retain and manage local property may be of more practical concern than its right to sell the property and remove the proceeds. In many cases retention and management may be more in line with the testator's wishes and his beneficiaries' needs than sale and removal would be. Yet in many states the out-of-state trustee's right of retention and management of local property still is one of the unsettled points of law. 

2. Procedure for acquiring right. Where the point is covered by 

Infra, p. 339. 

7 General Statutes 1930, sec. 4903. 

Code 1939, sec. 11897. 

9 Probate Code 1939, secs. 136-137. 

10 Revised Statutes 1939, Ch. 76, sec. 68; Ch. 78, sec. 35.

statute, the procedure whereby the out-of-state trustee under will may obtain the right to exercise powers is, in the main, the same as that already described for out-of-state executors and administrators.¹¹ Guardian

In most states the statutes with respect to the exercise of powers by out-of-state guardians are even more specific than those for outof-state executors and administrators. Some states grant them the right to exercise powers; others, the right to qualify and serve as ancillary guardian. 

1. Grant of powers. The specified powers of out-of-state guardians are, in the main, the same as those of out-of-state executors and administrators. In both cases the statutes are designed to grant all the power needed for the proper handling of the property. But it is noticeable that in the case of guardianships the statutes are more specific as to the purposes for which the ward's property may be sold and the proceeds removed to the state of the ward's residence. 

The ward's property may be sold, not only to pay debts and make settlements, but also for reinvestment and to provide funds for the support and education of the ward. The power mentioned oftenest in the statutes is that of removal, with the corollary power of sale for removal. In some cases, however, retention and management will serve the ward's needs better than sale and removal would. 

2. Grant of authority to qualify as ancillary guardian. Some states permit an out-of-state guardian to qualify as ancillary guardian although they would not permit it to serve as domiciliary or principal guardian. 

3. Procedure for acquiring right. The procedure for out-of-state guardian to acquire the right to exercise powers is as simple and inexpensive as that for an out-of-state executor or administrator. It is required to take only such steps as filing a certified or authenticated copy of its letters of appointment, filing a bond or offering evidence that it has given a bond ample for the protection of local creditors or claimants, naming a process agent, giving notice of intention to remove the property, and showing that the contemplated removal will not conflict with the terms and limitations attending the right by which the ward owns the property. 

11 Supra, p. 331-332.

RIGHT TO SERVE IN FIDUCIARY CAPACITIES 

The right of a trust institution located in one state to serve as executor, administrator, trustee under will, or guardian for a resident of another state still is, for a large number of states, one of the most confused and obscure points of the law. Commerce Clearing 

House Trust and Estate Law Service says quite truly that in the majority of the states the answers to the questions on this topic are not very satisfactory, that in most states they are answered very sketchily, and that there has been very little judicial determination of the matter.12 Prentice-Hall Trust Service says with equal truth that the law giving the rights and powers of an out-of-state trust institution is in many states confused, if not obscure. It adds that clear legislative enactments by states whose laws do not now provide a definite solution to the problem would be of inestimable service to corporate fiduciaries generally.¹

Present State of Law 

13 The following summary gives but a faint idea of the present confusion and obscurity of the law on this point: Some states expressly prohibit out-of-state trust institutions from serving in any fiduciary capacity; others permit them to serve in only certain named capacities. Some states, known as the reciprocal states, provide that out-ofstate trust institutions may serve in any capacity in which the state in which those institutions are located permits trust institutions of the other state to serve; some of these states name the capacities as to which they reciprocate with other states. Some states say they admit out-of-state trust institutions to serve in all fiduciary capacities; others, in certain named capacities; but, then, these states forthwith restrict this privilege by prescribing as a condition precedent that out-of-state trust institutions first must qualify as foreign corporations to do business in the state. Some of these states make it easy and inexpensive, others difficult, if not impossible, for out-of-state trust institutions to qualify to do business. Some states draw a distinction between the right of out-of-state trust institutions to serve residents and their right to serve non-residents in fiduciary capacities. 

Still other states have no direct or express law on the subject, either

2. Reciprocal states. In the table 10 states are listed as reciprocal. 

This means, for example, that State A permits the trust institutions of State B to serve in the capacities in which State B permits the trust institutions of State A to serve. But the reciprocal statutes are not uniform. Some of them provide for reciprocity in any fiduciary capacity; others limit reciprocity to fiduciary capacities arising from appointments under will; and still others limit reciprocity to a few specified fiduciary capacities. None of these states, presumably, reciprocates except in the capacities named. New York, for example, limits its reciprocity to executors and trustees under will. Although 

Pennsylvania extends its reciprocity to guardians, it is doubtful that a New York trust institution could qualify or serve as guardian of North Carolina-Public Laws 1915, Ch. 196, sec. 1; Code 1939, sec. 1180. In 

1930 and again in 1932 the Attorney General gave an opinion that an out-of-state institution may serve as trustee of a living trust where property is situate in the state.

the property of a Pennsylvania minor, since New York does not extend reciprocity to guardians. Furthermore, these reciprocal states, presumably, do not admit out-of-state trust institutions from nonreciprocal states to serve in any fiduciary capacity, except possibly those from states such as Maine and Wyoming which may admit out-of-state trust institutions without requiring them to qualify as foreign corporations. Although the Pennsylvania statute extends to an out-of-state trust institution as trustee, guardian, executor, administrator, or “in any other similar fiduciary capacity" under appointment by will, deed of trust, or order of court, nevertheless Pennsylvania, presumably, would not admit a Virginia trust institution to serve in any capacity because Virginia is a non-reciprocal state. 

Procedure for Acquiring Right to Serve in Fiduciary Capacities In those states in which an out-of-state trust institution is not required to qualify as a foreign corporation to do business in the state as a condition precedent, the procedure for acquiring the right to serve in fiduciary capacities is simple and inexpensive. It usually involves only such steps as filing a certified or authenticated copy of its charter, naming a process agent, and giving a bond or satisfying the court that it has given a bond or that it is not required by its home state to give a bond with surety. In other words, if a state admits out-of-state trust institutions at all, without requiring them to qualify as foreign corporations, it ordinarily makes their entry easy and inexpensive in return for similar treatment for its own trust institutions.

RIGHT TO ENGAGE IN TRUST BUSINESS 

A discussion of the right of out-of-state trust institutions to engage in trust business logically begins with the question: What constitutes engaging in business? 

Engaging in Business

Under the general law relating to foreign business corporations it commonly is held that an isolated or occasional business transaction in another state is not doing business within the meaning of the statutes requiring foreign corporations to qualify as a condition precedent to doing business in the state.

Applied to out-of-state trust institutions, this principle usually has been interpreted to mean that the mere exercise of a power in connection with the property of a non-resident decedent or ward is not engaging in the trust business;¹7 whereas the complete settlement of a single estate or the complete administration of a single trust or guardianship is engaging in trust business.18 The reasoning in support of the latter application of the principle is that the settlement of a single estate or the administration of a single trust is not an isolated or occasional business transaction but is, instead, a more or less general exercise of the authority of the trust institution. As the Supreme 

Judicial Court of Massachusetts said of the administration by an outof-state trust institution of a single trust under will: The trustee was not called upon to perform a single isolated act of business, but was required to carry on a series of acts during the life of the beneficiary If the petitioner can be appointed a testamentary trustee, it will be engaged in doing business, not casual or merely incidental but as part of its usual occupation and in fulfilling one of the purposes of the corporation. It will be transacting business in violation of the statute . 19 

Limiting the Right to Engage in Trust Business 

The reciprocal states are the most hospitable of all the states toward admitting out-of-state trust institutions. Nevertheless, these states are reluctant to have out-of-state trust institutions, admitted occasionally to serve in fiduciary capacities, remain to engage continuously in the trust business. They show their reluctance mainly in these two ways: (1) by forbidding out-of-state trust institutions to establish 

17 American Loan & Trust Company v. East and West R. Co., (C.C.A., Ala.), 37 Fed. 242 (1889); Worth v. Knickerbocker Trust Co., 171 Ala. 621, 55 So. 144 (1911); Martin v. Bankers' Trust Co., 18 Ariz. 55, 156 P. 87 (1916); Moran v. 

Union Savings Bank & Trust Co. (Ark.) 97 S.W. (2d) 638 (1936); Equitable Trust 

Co. of N. Y. v. Western Land & Power Co., 38 Cal. App. 535, 176 P. 876 (1918); 

Farmers' Loan & Trust Co. v. Lake St. El. R. Co., 173 Ill. 439, 51 N.E. 556 (1898); 

Baden v. Washington Loan Trust Co., 133 Md. 602, 105 A. 860 (1919); Dime 

Savings Trust Co. v. Humphreys (Okla.) 53 P. (2d) 665 (1936); Colbert v. Toll (C.C.A. Tenn.) 31 Fed. (2d) 837 (1929); Nelson v. Detroit Security Trust Co. (Tex. App.) 38 S.W. (2d) 360 (1931). 

18 In re Wellings' Estate, 192 Cal. 506, 221 P. 628 (1923); Michigan Trust Co. v. 

Turney (Tex. App.) 36 S.W. (2d) 787 (1931); Petition of Guaranty Trust Co., 248 Mass. 319, 143 N.E. 46 (1924). 19 

Petition of Guaranty Trust Co., 248 Mass. 319, 143 N.E. 46 (1924).

or maintain offices or agencies and (2) by forbidding them to advertise for or solicit trust business. 

1. Establishing or maintaining office or agency. New York, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin prohibit an out-of-state trust institution, admitted by reciprocity to serve as executor or trustee under will, to establish or maintain directly or indirectly any branch office or agency in the state. The penalty for violating this act is loss by the out-of-state trust institution of the right to serve in these capacities. 

Massachusetts will not permit an out-of-state trust institution to maintain an office in the state unless its home state by affirmative authorization permits a Massachusetts trust institution to maintain an office in that state. 

2. Advertising or soliciting. New York and Wisconsin do not permit an out-of-state trust institution in any way, directly or indirectly, to solicit business as executor or trustee under will. The penalty for violating this act is loss by the out-of-state trust institution of the right to serve in these capacities. Massachusetts will permit an out-of-state trust institution to advertise or solicit only if its home state by affirmative authorization permits a Massachusetts trust institution to advertise and solicit trust business within its borders. 

Acting through Officers, Employees, or Agents

While some states do not permit either non-resident individuals²º or out-of-state trust institutions to qualify as executor, other states permit individuals21 but not trust institutions to do so. This has led in some cases to efforts to get around barriers to out-of-state trust institutions by naming an individual connected with an out-of-state trust institution instead of naming the institution itself. 

As soon as the states began to close their doors to out-of-state trust institutions, the draftsmen of wills began to have their clients name individuals connected with out-of-state trust institutions. Sometimes these individuals were designated, not by name, but by office. For example, the will read, "I name as executor of my will the person who shall be the officer in charge of the trust department of the X Trust Company at the time of my death and his successor or successors in office.” Doubtless it was understood by both the draftsman 20 

In re Petition of Mulford, 217 Ill. 242, 75 N.E. 345 (1905). 

21 Scott on Trusts, sec. 94, pp. 503-504.

and the testator that the trust company would serve as agent for the head of its trust department, that it would assume the responsibility, do the work, receive the allowance, and, to all intents and purposes, be the executor.

For this course of action there was ample precedent. In England until comparatively recently a corporation could qualify as executor or trustee only through or in the name of a designated officer, employee, or agent-called a syndic. This still is the only way in which some of the English trust institutions, which do not meet the technical requirements for being trust corporations, can serve in fiduciary capacities. 22 Some of the more recent statutes prohibit officers, employees, or agents of out-of-state trust institutions from serving in fiduciary capacities. For example, the Kansas statute provides that no officer, employee, or agent, whether resident or non-resident, of an out-ofstate trust institution shall be permitted to act as a fiduciary in Kansas when in fact such officer, employee, or agent is acting as such fiduciary in behalf of such out-of-state trust institution.23 The Ohio act of 193724 and the Nevada act of 194325 have similar provisions. 

Qualifying as Foreign Corporation to Do Business

If an out-of-state trust institution is permitted to qualify and does qualify as a foreign corporation to do business,26 then it may be assumed that it may engage in trust business generally; that, to all intents and purposes, it is a local trust institution. 

As already stated,27 the weight of authority, so far as the point has been adjudicated, is to the effect that settling a single estate or administering a single trust is doing business. Therefore, the requirements made of out-of-state trust institutions, being foreign corporations, as conditions precedent to their doing business in the state 

22 Trust Business in Common Law Countries, p. 172. 

23 Probate Code 1939, sec. 131. 24 

General Code, sec. 710-154. 

25 Laws 1943, Ch. 63. 

26 For a state-by-state digest of the statutes and cases and a collection of cases relating expressly to banks and trust companies, see What Constitutes Doing Business by a Corporation in States Foreign to the State of Its Creation, The Corporation 

Trust Company, 1943. 

27 Supra, p. 341.

become as important in determining their right to serve in fiduciary capacities even occasionally and in isolated cases as they do in determining their right to engage in trust business generally. 

The requirements made of out-of-state trust institutions desiring to qualify as foreign corporations to do business in Illinois, in Mississippi, in Ohio, and in Tennessee are perhaps typical and are sufficient to illustrate the point. 

1. Illinois. An out-of-state trust institution desiring to serve in fiduciary capacities or to engage in trust business in Illinois first must qualify to do business as a foreign corporation through the office of the secretary of state and then qualify to do a trust business through the office of the auditor of public accounts. To qualify to do business as a foreign corporation it must (a) pay a fee of $20, for filing the application; (b) pay the entrance fee or initial tax, based on the proportion of stated capital and paid-in surplus employed within the state; (c) pay in advance all or a portion of the annual franchise tax (depending on the month in which the application is made); and (d) file with the secretary of state an authenticated copy of its charter and two verified copies of its application for certificate of authority showing the location of its principal offices in Illinois, the name and address of its resident agent, and giving the data necessary for computing the fees. The entrance fee or initial tax and the annual franchise tax each is 1/20 of 1% of the Illinois proportion of the business and property. Having qualified through the office of secretary of state to do business in the state, it next must qualify, but only as a domestic corporation would have to do, through the office of the auditor of public accounts, to engage in the trust business.28 

2. Mississippi. In Mississippi an out-of-state trust institution desiring either to serve in a fiduciary capacity or to engage in trust business generally must pay either an annual privilège tax of $500 to serve in fiduciary capacities or to do trust business anywhere in the state or a $50 tax for each county in which it desires to serve or do 28 For recent discussion and adjudication of the right of an out-of-state national bank to qualify to do trust business in Illinois, see The Boatmen's National Bank of 

St. Louis v. Hughes, Illinois Supreme Court, Docket No. 27,094, January 18, 1944, 53 NE (2) 403. Rehearing denied March 16, 1944.

business. In addition to this privilege tax, it is required (a) to file a certified copy of its charter with the secretary of state, (b) to name a process agent, (c) to pay a filing or recording fee of from $2 to $500 based upon its authorized capital stock, and (d) to pay the fee (at the rate of 15 cents per 100 words, minimum $2.50) for recording the certificate issued by the secretary. The statute provides an alternative method whereby an out-of-state trust institution may qualify to do business by filing a certified copy of its charter with the Governor who, after advising with the Attorney General as to the legality or constitutionality of the charter, forwards the charter, with his approval, to the secretary of state, and the secretary of state, upon receipt of the recording fees, issues a certificate of authority to do business in the state. An out-of-state trust institution, upon meeting these requirements, becomes to all intents and purposes a 

Mississippi trust institution. 

3. Ohio. An out-of-state trust institution, as a condition precedent to qualifying to do trust business in Ohio, must (a) pay the superintendent of banks a fee of $500 for issuance of the certificate of authority to do business, (b) show that it has a paid-in capital of $100,000, (c) deposit with the state treasurer $100,000 in cash or approved securities, (d) pay a license fee of $100, (e) file with the superintendent of banks a certified copy of its last published statement of condition, (f) name a resident process agent, and (g) submit to examination as to any and all matters relating to Ohio trusts, estates, or property by the superintendent of banks or any deputy, examiner, or person especially appointed for the purpose. The fee of $500 to the superintendent of banks for the certificate of authority and the license fee of $100 to the state treasurer are payable annually. 

4. Tennessee. In order to qualify and to continue to do trust business in Tennessee, an out-of-state trust institution must (a) pay an entrance fee of $300, (b) file with the secretary of state a certified or authenticated copy of its charter and a declaration of the name and address of its process agent, (c) pay an annual privilege tax, accompanying the annual report and based either on business done in Tennessee (½ of 1% of the gross annual receipts from business done within the state, minimum $25, or on authorized capital stock ranging from $5 to $150), (d) pay an annual franchise tax (15 cents on each $100 of capital invested in Tennessee, minimum $25), and (e)

pay an annual excise tax of 3.75% of net earnings of business transacted wholly within Tennessee. 

It is not so much the other conditions of qualifying as it is the yearly taxes imposed upon the property, the business, or the earnings of an out-of-state trust institution that govern its decision as to doing business in another state. However, a discussion of the taxation of out-of-state trust institutions as foreign corporations is beyond the scope of this Study.

CONCLUSIONS 

Viewing the over-all picture of the rights of out-of-state trust institutions, one seems to be justified in making the following generalizations.

Right to Exercise Powers 

The present state of the law and practice as to the exercise of powers by out-of-state trust institutions as executor, administrator, or guardian is reasonably satisfactory. An out-of-state trust institution ordinarily does not have a great deal of difficulty or have to incur much expense in acquiring or exercising the right to sue or defend actions, to sell property and remove property or the proceeds thereof, to foreclose mortgages, to satisfy of record mortgages, liens, and judgments, or to do other things normally or usually required for the proper handling of property in an estate or guardianship. All but a few states have legislated as to the right of out-of-state trust institutions to exercise such powers. In some states the statutes, although definite and specific as to the rights of out-of-state executors, administrators, and guardians, still are indefinite or uncertain as to trustees under will and particularly with reference to their powers of management.

On the whole, it would seem that what remains to be done is not so much new legislation (except in the few remaining states) as it is the rounding out of existing legislation to make it more specific and definite and to make it expressly include trustees under will. 

Right to Serve in Fiduciary Capacities 

In 1940 the Executive Committee of the Trust Division of the 

American Bankers Association adopted the following statement of policy:

The right of any person to select as his executor or trustee any person or trust institution, wherever situated, should be regarded as inherent and should be protected and preserved." 29 In harmony with this policy, according to the table appearing earlier in this Study, 24 states admit, either generally or by reciprocity, an out-of-state trust institution to serve as executor and 27 states do so with respect to trustees under will. 

Through the reciprocal statutes already adopted by 10 states, a good start has been made toward giving nationwide application to this statement of policy. It would be helpful in carrying out this policy if reciprocal statutes were adopted by the remaining states and if there were more uniformity in the statutes. For example, it would seem that reciprocity well might be extended to administratorships. and guardianships. Although the occasions might be somewhat rare, still there would be times when the court for good reason would deem it advisable and, it would seem, should have the right to appoint an out-of-state trust institution administrator of the estate of a resident intestate or guardian of the property of a resident minor or incompetent.

It would seem practicable and desirable to draft a uniform reciprocal statute which as many of the presently non-reciprocal states as cared to do so might adopt and in line with which such of the 10. reciprocal states as cared to do so might amend their present statutes. 

The fact that these statutes are reciprocal would seem to make their substantial uniformity all the more desirable. 

Right to Engage in Trust Business 

It is thought that at the present time comparatively few trust institutions desire to engage in trust business generally in other states, in the sense of establishing and maintaining offices and advertising for and soliciting trust business. However, should a trust institution desire to do so, it would seem that it should, so far as practicable, meet the same requirements as those imposed upon local trust institutions. As the Illinois statute provides in effect, an out-of-state trust institution qualified to do business in that state shall enjoy the same, but no greater, rights and privileges as a local trust institution, and in general shall be subject to the same duties, restrictions, penalties, 29 Trust Bulletin, April 1940, p. 3 at p. 4.

and liabilities now or hereafter imposed upon a local trust institution.30

Generally, it would seem that the law should recognize some distinction between the status of an out-of-state trust institution entering a state to settle an estate or administer a trust or guardianship as an isolated or occasional act and that of an out-of-state trust institution entering a state to engage in trust business generally. It may be that in some of the more recent statutes this distinction is drawn sufficiently (1) by not allowing an out-of-state trust institution, desiring only to serve in fiduciary capacities occasionally or as isolated acts, to establish or maintain an office or to advertise or solicit and (2) by requiring an out-of-state trust institution, desiring to engage in trust business generally, to meet all the local requirements for qualifying as a foreign corporation to do business in the state. 

Whether the objective of further legislation be the completion or rounding out of the law as to the rights of out-of-state trust institutions to exercise powers with respect to the property of non-resident beneficiaries or wards, or the extension of reciprocity, or the formulation of terms and conditions upon which an out-of-state trust institution may engage in trust business generally, there appears to be urgent need in many states for not a little clarifying legislation. 

The urgency of this need offers at once a challenge for concerted effort and an opportunity for valuable service alike to lawyers, to legislators, and to trustmen.
