Study Number 17 

C 

PAY-AS-YOU-GO COMPENSATION FOR TRUSTEES 

REATORS and beneficiaries of trusts are fully as much concerned as are trustees themselves that the compensation of their trustees not only shall be proper in amount but also shall be taken out of the proper fund at the proper time. 

Interrelation of Amount, Source, and Timing of 

Trustee's Compensation

The amount, the source, and the timing of the trustee's compensation are interrelated, and all three have a direct bearing upon the accomplishment of the purposes of the trust. 

Because of the long duration of trusts the amount of the trustee's compensation should vary according to whether it is received before the service is rendered, is received periodically while it is being rendered, or is received after it has been rendered. 

Creators and beneficiaries of trusts are concerned that the trustee's compensation shall be taken out of the funds and at the times that will be most conducive to the accomplishment of the purposes of the trust. If too much compensation is taken out of income, the immediate purposes may be partially defeated and, if too much is taken out of principal, the ultimate purposes may suffer. 

Meaning of Pay-As-You-Go Applied to a Trustee's Compensation

The term pay-as-you-go, applied to a trustee's compensation, means that the trustee receives its compensation at the time the service is 351

rendered. For practical purposes it means during or at the end of the year or the period in which the service is rendered. If at the end of the year or at the end of the intermediate accounting period the trustee receives full compensation for services rendered up to that time, it properly may be said that pay-as-you-go applies. But if a substantial part of the trustee's compensation is paid when the trust is established for service to be rendered in the future or when the trust is terminated for service that has been rendered in the past, then pay-as-you-go is not wholly, even though it may be partially, in effect.

Pay-As-You-Go Plan for Other Types of Trust Service 

For most of the compensation of trust institutions pay-as-you-go already applies. For example, as executor or administrator, a trust institution receives its allowance from the court as soon as it has completed the settlement of the estate. If the estate must be kept open for an extended period of time, the executor or administrator usually receives partial allowances from time to time. As personal agent-safekeeping, custodian, managing, or escrow—a trust institution usually is paid by the year or at more frequent intervals. As corporate trustee and as corporate agent most of its compensation is received quarterly, semi-annually, or annually. 

It is mainly in personal trusts and in guardianships that pay-asyou-go is not yet in full force and effect.¹ Even in trusts it is partially in effect. In only a rare or extraordinary case does a trust institution, on the one hand, receive all its compensation at the time the trust is established or, on the other, wait until the account is closed before it receives any of its compensation. In the vast majority of cases even now as trustee it receives the major portion of its compensation annually, which means that to the extent of these annual charges pay-as-you-go already is applied.

Pay-as-you-go, then, is not a new or untried plan of compensation for trust service. With respect to most trust services it already is fully in effect and even with respect to personal trusts it is partially in effect. 

1 While this Study is limited to trusts, many of the statements apply equally to guardianships.

PAY-AS-YOU-GO COMPENSATION 

353 

Steps Already Taken Toward Applying Pay-As-You-Go to Trustees' Compensation

Until comparatively recently the prevailing practice was for the court to allow, or for the trustee by agreement or custom to take, a commission on income annually and a single commission on principal at the termination of the trust. In some states 5% of the income annually and 2% of the principal at the termination of the trust frequently was the rule-of-thumb way of stating the trustee's usual compensation. The theory was that 5% of the income was the income beneficiaries' share and 2% of the principal, the principal beneficiaries' share of the trustee's compensation. 

One departure from this method of allowing or taking compensation was when trust institutions began, by agreement with creators of trusts, to receive a smaller commission on principal if taken at the opening than if taken at the closing of the account. There were numerous instances in which the trustee gave the settlor the choice of a 1% commission on principal at the opening or 2% at the closing of the account.

Another departure was to take part of the trustee's commission on principal at the opening and the balance at the closing of the trust. It has not been uncommon for the trustee by agreement to take 1% of the principal at the opening and 1% at the closing of the account.

While both of these departures from the traditional method of postponing all commission on principal to the end of the trust were improvements, neither of them was wholly satisfactory in that the duration of the trust might be and in most cases would be indeterminate and, therefore, none of the parties at interest could tell how accurately to calculate the amount of the trustee's commission on principal according as it was allowed or taken at the beginning or at the end of the trust or part at the beginning and the balance at the end.

The next step in due course is for the court to allow or for the trustee by agreement to take compensation each year for the service rendered that year. The taking of this step means the reduction of the commission at the beginning to the amount necessary to cover fair compensation for the work and responsibility connected with opening the account and its reduction at the end to the amount

necessary to cover compensation for the work and responsibility connected with closing the account.

Any adequate consideration of this subject raises three basic questions: (1) Is pay-as-you-go compensation for trustees sound in principle? (2) Is it workable in practice? (3) Can it effectively be maintained throughout the duration of the trust?

PAY-AS-YOU-GO IN PRINCIPLE 

Whether a trustee's compensation should, as a matter of principle, be pay-as-you-go depends upon whether or not it is equally fair to the beneficiaries and to the trustee. Fundamentally, on this point there should be no conflict of interest between the beneficiaries as a group and the trustee.

From the Point of View of the Beneficiaries 

If the compensation of the trustee is to be adequate, then the amount of the compensation must be adjusted with reference to the time it is allowed or taken by the trustee. If it is allowed or taken when the trust is established, it will be proportionately less than if it is allowed or taken from time to time-annually, for exampleduring the period of the trust and less still than if it is allowed or taken at the termination of the trust. Or, to state it another way, if the trustee is not to receive its commission on principal until the termination of the trust 20 or 50 years hence, at that time it should receive enough more to make up for what it loses by not receiving it when the trust is established or in annual instalments. 

1. Amount and apportionment. From the point of view of the beneficiaries the application of pay-as-you-go to the trustee's compensation is not so much a problem of determining the right amount as it is one of arriving at the right apportionment of the amount between the income and the principal of the trust. 

It is the apportionment which may raise an issue between the income beneficiaries and the principal beneficiaries, in which issue the trustee itself should not be involved. Income beneficiaries, on the one hand, may think that pay-as-you-go compensation for trustees will be to their disadvantage because, if part of the commission on principal is allowed or taken yearly instead of all of its being taken at the termination of the trust, there will be a constantly decreasing

PAY-AS-YOU-GO COMPENSATION 

355 principal from which they are to receive income and they will consequently receive less and less income. 

Principal beneficiaries, on their part, may think that pay-as-you-go compensation for trustees will operate to their disadvantage in that annual commissions on principal may, in the long run, amount to more than a single commission on principal at the beginning or at the end of the trust.

However, the basic fact is that, if the aggregate compensation is adequate in amount, taking into consideration the time or times the compensation is received, then the amount is not at issue, and the only problem is one of fair and equitable apportionment of the trustee's total compensation between the income and the principal. 

2. Basis and source. The problem of apportionment itself may have been aggravated somewhat by confusion of thought as to the basis and the source of a trustee's compensation. Basis means the property, the service, or the responsibility upon which the amount of the compensation is calculated. Source means the fund or property out of which the compensation is allowed or taken. Too frequently laymen take for granted that a commission on principal must be allowed or taken out of principal and one based on income must be allowed or taken out of income; whereas, in fact, it is not uncommon for a trustee's commission to be based on principal (for instance, 1/5 of 1% of the principal annually) and yet allowed or taken out of income, in which case principal is the basis and income is the source of the compensation.

In general, the trustee's' compensation should be based on the property, the service, or the responsibility that will produce a fair amount for the trustee, and should be allowed or taken out of the fund or property in conformity with the purposes of the trust. Thus the basis of compensation is a matter of primary concern to the trustee; the source, to the beneficiaries.2 Basing substantially one-half of the trustee's compensation on principal and the other half on income tends to equalize and stabilize the compensation throughout the stages of any economic cycle and thus, in the long run, operates equally to the advantage of the trustee and of the beneficiaries. 2 

Stephenson's Studies, Chapter VII, Bases of Compensation for Trust Service, p. 107; Sources of Trust Compensation, Report of Committee on Trust Policies, Trust Bulletin, May 1938, p. 9.

From the Point of View of the Trustee 

From the point of view of trustees, pay-as-you-go has the advantages of providing the funds with which to meet current expenses and of giving the present management a return for the service it renders. 

1. Funds for current expenses. Pay-as-you-go fully applies to a bank's or trust company's own operating expenses. When it incurs a determinable expense of its own in connection with the principal of a trust account, it cannot postpone the payment of the bill for this expense until it terminates the trust and receives the trustee's commission on principal. It would seem then to be only an elementary principle of economics that, so long as the trust institution pays its own operating expenses as it goes, it should receive its compensation also as it goes. 

2. Returns for present management. The management of a trust institution should be compensated for service it renders. In a longestablished trust institution and over a long enough period inequalities tend to adjust themselves in that present management receives pay for past management's work and, in turn, future management will receive pay for present management's work. But in younger trust institutions with a smaller volume of trust business the law of averages does not come into play to equalize trust earnings. In any case it is more businesslike and, therefore, more satisfactory for management at any given time to be compensated for the service it renders, neither drawing upon the earnings of past management nor accumulating earnings for future management.

Uncertainty of Duration of Trust 

The traditional method of allowing or taking all the trustee's commission on principal at the opening or at the closing of the account or of dividing it between the opening and closing is likely to operate to the disadvantage either of the beneficiaries or of the trustee and it is impossible in a given case to tell to whose advantage or disadvantage it will work. This is so because of the unpredictability of the duration of the trust. Take, for example, a normal family trust of $100,000, yielding 3%, to pay the income to the widow, aged 50 years, for life and after her death, to pay over the principal to the testator's or settlor's children. Such a trust may come to its

PAY-AS-YOU-GO COMPENSATION 

357 end within the year of its creation or it may last 30 years or more. 

If the parties knew how long the trust would last and the amount and character of the labor and responsibility required of the trustee year after year throughout the duration of the trust, it would be possible in the beginning to calculate an annual commission on income and a single commission on principal that would produce fair compensation for the trustee. But they do not know any of these things. Any estimate of the duration of the trust or of the labor and responsibility involved year after year is guesswork. 

The uncertainty of the duration of the trust makes it impossible to calculate with any degree of accuracy the present value of an amount of compensation to be received in the future or the present compensation for service to be rendered in the future. On the basis of a 3% yield compounded annually, a $1,000 commission on principal allowed or paid the trustee today has a present value equivalent to $1,806 if allowed or paid 20 years from now; $3,262, 40 years from now; and $5,891, 60 years from now. Or, the other way round, $1,000 payable 60 years from now has a present value of only $169; 

40 years, $306; 20 years, $443. In his Special Report on Trust Department Earnings and Expenses in 1943, the Superintendent of Banks of the State of New York found that the average duration of the trusts included in his study was 27 years. On the basis of a 3% yield compounded annually, a $1,000 commission on principal payable at the opening of a trust to continue 27 years has a present value equivalent to that of $2,221 payable at the closing of the account, and a $1,000 commission payable at the closing of the account has a present value of only $450.

Thus, from the points of view both of the beneficiaries as a group and of the trustee, pay-as-you-go commends itself in principle as sound economics in that it takes guesswork out of the trustee's compensation and puts it on a businesslike basis. 

Reasons for Hesitancy in Applying Pay-As-You-Go

If pay-as-you-go is sound in principle, why has there been hesitancy in applying it to trustees' compensation? There are, perhaps, two reasons. The first is the tradition that the principal must be kept itract at all hazards and the second, which is a corollary of the first, the tradition that the ultimate beneficiaries are, in the minds of testators and settlors, the preferred beneficiaries.

Neither of these points will be elaborated, because in a previous Study³ it was found by research on a nationwide scale that the vast majority of testators and settlors prefer their immediate beneficiaries over their ultimate beneficiaries and want their immediate beneficiaries to have principal, as well as income, if their needs require it. The preservation of principal at the expense of the needs of immediate beneficiaries-usually members of the testator's or the settlor's family-no longer is in conformity with the purposes for which the vast majority of people are creating personal trusts at the present time.

Once these two traditions are abandoned, the application of payas-you-go to trustees' compensation becomes mainly a matter of bringing trustees' compensation into line with modern, approved business practice.

PAY-AS-YOU-GO IN PRACTICE 

In the discussion of the second basic question-Is pay-as-you-go workable in practice?-trusts under will and living trusts are treated separately. 

Trusts under Will

In some states, before pay-as-you-go can be applied to the compensation of trustees under will, provision must be made for the separate compensation of the executor and of the trustee where the same person is serving in both capacities. In other states provision must be made for the allowance or taking of part of the trustee's annual compensation out of principal. 

1. Separate compensation of executor and of trustee. In some states the law or practice long has been not to allow a principal commission to a person as trustee who already has received an allowance as executor under the same will. The theory evidently has been that the executor-trustee, in safeguarding and caring for the estate, is rendering but one service and that, having been compensated once as executor, he should not be compensated again as trustee. 

8 Study Number 14, The Preferred Beneficiaries of Trusts.

PAY-AS-YOU-GO COMPENSATION 

359 The unsoundness of this theory and yet the conflict of authority still existing are stated by Professor Scott as follows: 

In states in which executors and trustees are entitled to such compensation as the court deems reasonable, a person who acts both as executor and as trustee is entitled to such compensation as is reasonable in view of all the duties which he performs. 

To the extent to which his duties are increased because he acts in both capacities, he is entitled to greater compensation than that to which he would be entitled if he acted only in a single capacity. In states in which by statute executors and trustees are entitled to a fixed percentage on the principal of the estate, and the same person is named as executor and trustee under a will, there is a conflict of authority on the question whether he is entitled to commissions both as executor and as trustee. In some states it is held that he is not; in other states it is held that he is.* 

So far as is known, Pennsylvania now is the only state which by statute expressly prohibits the allowance of a separate commission as executor and as trustee to the same person serving in both сарасities. The Pennsylvania statute provides: 

In all cases where the same person shall, under a will, fulfill the duties of executor and trustee, it shall not be lawful for such person to receive or charge more than one commission upon any sum of money coming into or passing through his hands, or held by him for the benefit of other parties; and such single commission shall be deemed a full compensation for his services, in the double capacity of executor and trustee: Provided, That any such trustee shall be allowed to retain a reasonable commission on the income he may receive from any estate held by him in trust as aforesaid.

However, as Professor Scott points out, in some other states the courts, in the exercise of their discretion, still decline to allow two commissions on principal to the same person-one as executor and the other as trustee.

On principle it would seem clear that, where a person is appointed to render two separate and distinguishable services, one as executor and the other as trustee, he should be compensated for each service and should not be penalized because he is one person serving in two capacities. 

2. Allow part of trustee's annual compensation out of principal. 

In some states there is disinclination or, as the law in some of these states is construed, inability on the part of the courts to allow any * Scott on Trusts, sec. 242.9, p. 1398. See also Bogert on Trusts and Trustees, sec. 

978, pp. 2872-2877. 

1917, June 7, P.L. 447, sec. 45 (Purdon's Statutes, Title 20, sec. 813).

of the trustee's annual compensation to be taken out of principal.

3. Remedial legislation. Remedial legislation to cover both these situations is developing along these five lines: statutes (a) authorizing the court, in its discretion, to allow the trustee's compensation out of income or principal or both; (b) authorizing the court to fix the yearly compensation of the trustee; (c) fixing the trustee's annual compensation; (d) providing for the apportionment of the trustee's annual compensation between income and principal; and (e) giving the court broad discretionary powers as to the trustee's compensation. a. Giving court discretion to allow compensation out of income or principal or both. In the following states the court is authorized by statute to make the allowance to the trustee out of income or out of principal or partially out of each as to the court seems best: 

Georgia, Louisiana, Maine, Massachusetts,10 and Ohio.¹¹ The

Massachusetts statute, for example, simply says that a trustee's compensation may be apportioned between principal and income as the court may determine. b. Fixing the trustee's compensation on annual basis. Another group of states, all of them western states, authorize the court to fix the trustee's compensation on an annual basis. These states are 

Arizona,12 California,¹³ Idaho,¹ Montana,15 and Nevada.16 The

Arizona statute, which is typical, simply declares that the court, upon the accounting, may fix a yearly compensation for the trustee, to continue as long as the court may judge proper. These statutes do not say whether the trustee's compensation is to come out of principal or income.

361 c. Fixing trustee's annual compensation. Another group of states fix the trustee's annual compensation. These states are Kentucky, Maryland, and New York.

Kentucky. A Kentucky statute¹ of 1938 provides that trustees shall receive annually 5% of the income payable as collected and 1/5 of 1% of the principal or, at the trustee's option, a total of 5% of the principal payable at the time of distribution. In the absence of some provision, agreement, or direction to the contrary, the commission on income is payable out of income and that on principal, out of principal. 

Maryland. A Maryland statute,18 effective June 1, 1939, provides that trustees shall receive annually 5% of the income from real estate, ground rents, and mortgages; 5% of the first $10,000 of income from other sources, 4% of the next $10,000, 3% of the next $10,000, and 2% of any excess; and annually % of 1% of the first $500,000 of principal, 1/16 of 1% of any excess. At the termination of the trust, the trustee, in the absence of special circumstances, shall receive ½ of 1% of the principal. The commission on income is payable out of income and that on principal, out of principal. 

New York. New York statutes¹ of 1943 set forth separately the commissions allowed executors, administrators, and guardians; testamentary trustees and executors acting as trustee or acting as fiduciary for the benefit of a life tenant; and trustees of expressed trusts. 

Trustees are allowed to retain an annual commission of 6% on the first $2,000 of income, 3% on the next $10,000, and 2% on the balance and, in addition, an annual commission on principal equal in amount to 110% of the commission on income. The commission on income is payable out of income and that on principal, out of principal. If at the time of the distribution of principal the commissions on principal already received have been less than 5% of the first $2,000 of principal distributed, 2½% of the next $20,000, and 2% of the balance, the trustee may retain enough to make up the difference. However, if the trust has run less than 5 years, only one-half of the minimum rate is allowed. If there is one trustee, his total commissions on principal shall not exceed 6%; if two trustees, that of each one, 5%;

and if three or more trustees, that of each one shall not exceed 4%. d. Apportioning trustee's annual compensation between principal and income. In California the Uniform Principal and Income Act of 1941 provides that the trustee's compensation for both ordinary and extraordinary services, exclusive of compensation for acceptance or distribution, shall be paid one-half out of income, one-half out of principal, or in such other proportion as the court may direct." 21 e. Giving court broad powers as to trustee's compensation. Under the Maine statute, a trustee under will may be allowed an annual commission of not exceeding 5% of income, an annual "care and management fee" of not exceeding 1% of the trust fund (real and personal property), and at the termination of the trust a commission of not exceeding 5% of the personal assets. Under these ceilings, trustees under will usually are allowed 5% of the income annually and 4 of 1% of the principal annually until 5% of the principal has been taken provided the trust runs that long, and at the closing of the account from 1% to 5% of the principal according to circumstances. If by the termination of the trust the care and management fee has not amounted to as much as 5% of the principal, it is customary then to allow the balance of the 5%. 

4. Rule of court fixing trustee's commission. A rule of the Superior 

Court of the State of California in and for the County of San Diego, effective January 1, 1943, provides that for usual and ordinary services a trustee under will shall be allowed ¾4 of 1% of the principal or 15% of the net income, whichever is less and, in addition, an opening commission of 1/5 of 1% and a closing commission of 4/5 of 1% of the principal. The court may, in its discretion, increase or reduce the annual compensation specified, "depending upon the work performed, the responsibility assumed, and the results accomplished and other factors." The alternative of ¾4 of 1% of principal or 15% is a distinctive feature of this rule of court.

The Kentucky, Maryland, and New York statutes apply both to trusts under will and to living trusts. In some of the other states, where the commissions are not fixed by statute, trustees of living

PAY-AS-YOU-GO COMPENSATION 

367 2. Taking a part of trustee's annual compensation out of principal. In Kentucky, Maryland, and New York, under their recent statutes, as already mentioned, a substantial portion-in New York, over one-half of the trustee's annual compensation is taken out of principal even though, as in the case of New York, it is based on income. The table shows that in many other parts of the country part of the trustee's annual compensation is taken out of principal. 

There seem to be two sound reasons for doing this. The first is that considering the low yield of securities at the present time, to take all of the trustee's annual compensation out of income may work to the disadvantage of the income beneficiaries. The second is that a substantial portion of the trustee's labor and responsibility relates to the principal. In recent years the conservation of the principal has become an increasingly difficult responsibility for the trustee and for the assumption and discharge of this responsibility it would seem but fair that the principal beneficiaries should compensate the trustee. 

In his 1943 report to stockholders, James E. Gowen, president of the Girard Trust Company, Philadelphia, said:

In years gone by, when the administration of trust estates was far simpler than is the case today, the practice developed, sanctioned by the courts in the absence of statutory authority, of providing compensation for trustees through the medium of a commission upon income collected and at the termination of the trust, perhaps several generations later, through the allowance of a management fee, which might in itself not be adequate, based upon the value of the corpus of the trust. Such practice is economically unsound in the light of prevailing conditions, postponing, as it does in numerous cases, for many years the realization by the trustee of a fee which may or may not be commensurate with the services rendered and the risks assumed it is the principal value which measures the responsibility of the trustee aside from the duty to maintain a reasonable income yield.... 

3. Opening, annual, and closing charges. In some of the places named in the table, the published schedules of rates of trust institutions specify the services for which the opening, the annual, and the closing charges each are made. In Salt Lake City, for example, the opening charge-called the setting up fee-is to cover the cost of preliminary work, the checking of securities, the preparation of receipts, the making of original entries, the posting to ledgers, the making of necessary tickler or diary entries, card indexes, and the deposit of the securities in the vault. The annual charge is to cover the ordinary management of the trust property, the usual accounting

involved, together with the usual periodical statements to beneficiaries, consultations, correspondence, postage, ordinary supplies, returns for tax assessments, and all dealings with the public and public officials. The closing charge covers all the usual services involved in terminating the trust, including the distribution of the assets and the inheritance tax service. The Maryland statute²² specifies the labor and responsibility involved in closing a trust account, namely, making the division, ascertaining the parties entitled, ascertaining and paying taxes, and transferring assets. 

4. Additional charges for extra services. For any pay-as-you-go-plan for trustees' compensation to be effective, some provision must be made for additional compensation for extra service. Yet what is extra service must be considered and determined in the light of the facts of the given case. For example, in a trust composed of stocks, bonds, and mortgages, the management of real property, which might arise unexpectedly from the default and foreclosure of a mortgage, would be extra service; whereas, in a trust composed partly of real property from the beginning, the management of real property was in the contemplation of the parties at the time the trustee's compensation was arranged and would not be extra service. a. Statutory provisions. The Kentucky statute provides that if the trustee has performed additional services in the handling of the estate, unusual or extraordinary and not normally incident to the care and management of an estate, the court may allow the trustee additional compensation. The Maine statute authorizes the court in fixing the trustee's compensation to consider the nature, liability, and difficulty attending the trust. The Maryland statute provides that for sufficient cause the court may increase or decrease the specified commissions and may allow special commissions or compensation for services of an unusual nature. Under the New York statute adopted in 1943 the court might allow the trustee such additional compensation as under the circumstances may be just and reasonable, if the trustee's duties had been unusually difficult or burdensome, or if for any other reason the commissions provided did not adequately compensate him for services rendered. But this was stricken out in 1944 by an amendment.

In those states in which the statute simply provides that trustees 22 

Supra, p. 361. 

---

PAY-AS-YOU-GO COMPENSATION 

369 shall be entitled to reasonable compensation for their services, the courts are free to take into account all the labor and responsibility involved in the administration of the trust and to allow additional compensation for extra service. b. Composite list of extra services. The following is a composite list of extra services for which trustees in different parts of the country and in different types of living trusts have been allowed additional compensation: managing real property; making repairs in excess of a stipulated amount; preparing specifications, taking bids, and awarding contracts in connection with repairs or betterments; obtaining tenants; consummating long-term leases (more than one year); collecting rents; evicting tenants without court action; selling real property and establishing escrows in connection with sales; managing a controlling or substantial interest in a corporation or in a business requiring active supervision and management; depositing securities with bondholders or reorganization committees; liquidating a business or partnership; participating in reorganization and refinancing plans for corporations whose securities are held in the estate or trust; filing claims on assets of bankrupts and decedents; negotiating loans for estate or trust; acknowledging assignments of beneficial interest; foreclosing deeds of trust, including workouts with borrowers; servicing defaulted mortgages; furnishing extra copies of accounts and photostatic copies of wills and other documents; making special reports at the request of beneficiaries; determining, paying, and adjusting inheritance and estate taxes; determining heirship; rendering unusual service resulting from assignments or attachments; making difficult allocations between principal and income; assuming more than ordinary responsibility in exercising discretionary powers as to applying or paying over principal or income; and making special trips out of the city or state.

MAINTAINING PAY-AS-YOU-Go 

Once pay-as-you-go has been applied to the trustee's compensation, the remaining, and frequently the more difficult, problem is that of keeping the plan in effect throughout the duration of the trust. 

If pay-as-you-go is to be kept operative, some provision must be made for readjustment of the trustee's compensation from time to time, upward or downward, as current conditions require.

By Statute or Order of Court 

Where the trustee's compensation is fixed by statute, the legislature from time to time may change the statute to provide adequate compensation under current conditions. Likewise, where it is fixed by the court in its discretion, the court from time to time may readjust the compensation.

By Agreement 

Where the trustee's compensation is fixed by agreement, pay-asyou-go may be maintained (1) by authorizing the trustee itself from time to time to readjust the compensation to current rates and (2) by the court, the court may readjust the compensation. 

1. By trustee. As was brought out in a previous Study,23 the trust instrument may provide that the trustee shall be compensated according to the rates prevailing at the time the service is rendered or the compensation received. Such a provision makes it possible for the trustee to adjust its compensation to current conditions. If the trust institution has an official pamphlet of its trust department setting forth its current rates, this pamphlet may be offered as evidence of the prevailing rates at any given time. 

2. By trustee and beneficiaries. The trust instrument may provide that the trustee and the beneficiaries may negotiate readjustments of the trustee's compensation from time to time. This is being accomplished in two ways. One way is to have the legally competent beneficiaries represent and bind the legally incompetent or contingent beneficiaries in negotiating readjustments of rates with the trustee. The other way is to authorize the beneficiaries who are entitled to more than half of the income to represent and bind those entitled to less than half of the income. 

If the matter has the attention of the parties and the draftsman at the time the trust instrument is being prepared, it is as easy to provide for keeping pay-as-you-go effective throughout the duration of the trust as it is to provide for making it so in the beginning. 

23 Study Number 10, Readjusting Compensation in Trust Accounts, p. 203 at p. 221.

PAY-AS-YOU-GO COMPENSATION 

371 

CONCLUSIONS 

In all cases the first and main consideration is that the trustee's compensation is fair and reasonable, neither too much nor too little. 

It is equally to the best interest of testators and settlors, of beneficiaries, and of trustees that the trustee's compensation be adequate. 

Every consideration of good business administration points to the advisability of applying pay-as-you-go to the trustee's compensation. 

This involves an opening charge, an annual charge, and a closing charge adequate respectively to cover the labor and responsibility incident to opening, to administering, and to closing the account. 

Pay-as-you-go can be applied irrespective of the method of allocation or apportionment of the trustee's compensation as between income and principal. As to such allocation or apportionment the desire of the testator or settlor should control.

A nationwide survey reported in a previous Study²* shows that at the present time most creators of trusts want their immediate beneficiaries to be preferred over their ultimate beneficiaries even to the extent of making principal bear a substantial portion of the current carrying charges of the trust.

Throughout the country, as shown by the recent statutes and by the table of rates in this Study, there is an unmistakable trend toward applying pay-as-you-go to trustees' compensation. Experience already has proved that it is practicable, as well as desirable, not only to set up a pay-as-you-go plan in the beginning but to keep it effective throughout the duration of the trust.

The uncertainties of the present time, produced by the war and by the prospect of post-war economic readjustments, make it all the more advisable that, so far as practicable, every person's financial obligations be stated and settled at frequent intervals so that both debtors and creditors may know where they stand. This fact gives special timeliness to a pay-as-you-go plan for trustees' compensation.

Study Number 18 

 HELD IN TRUST ACCOUNTS 

T THE PRESENT TIME one-third or more of the trust funds in the of stock. A large percentage of the stock of corporations is held in trust accounts. Over 19% of the outstanding stock of one nationally known corporation, over 16% of that of another, and over 15% of that of two others is owned by fiduciaries. It is highly important and mutually advantageous to corporations and their transfer agents, to fiduciaries, and to the beneficiaries of estates and trusts that the transfer of shares of stock held in the trust accounts shall be as prompt, as easy, and as inexpensive as is consistent with safety and with the best interests of all the parties concerned. 

Within recent years the use of nominees has been employed to an increasing extent as one way of expediting the disposition of securities held in trust accounts. Instead of registering the security in the name of the fiduciary—for example, in the name of X Trust Company, Trustee under the Will of A, Deceased—it is registered in the name of some individual, partnership, or corporation designated as the nominee of the fiduciary. This form of registration is known as nominee registration.

The need for nominee registration was stated as follows by the Commissioners on Uniform State Laws in their prefatory note to the Uniform Trusts Act:

To enable the trustee to sell stock easily, and to avoid the requirements of stock exchanges that where stock is held in the name of a fiduciary elaborate proof of the power of the fiduciary to sell must be given. 373

Nominee registration is only one way of expediting the disposition of securities held in trust accounts. It is not the purpose of this Study to argue for or against the use of nominees or to discuss nominees of individual fiduciaries. Instead, it is to make a factual presentation of nominee registration for corporate fiduciaries and to report practical suggestions with regard to nominee registration in personal trust accounts (excluding custody and corporate trust accounts), which, upon request, have been made by corporations, by transfer agents, and by fiduciaries.

Since a security registered in the name of a fiduciary is not good delivery under the rules of the stock exchanges, nominee registration for the purposes of immediate sale long has been a fairly general practice. Recent developments in this field have related mainly to general use of nominees from the opening of the account rather than to use limited to immediate sales. This Study deals more with permanent than with immediate-sale nominee registration. 

PROPRIETY OF NOMINEE REGISTRATION 

The basic question is that of the propriety of nominee registration. 

Is it proper legally for a fiduciary to have trust securities registered in the name of a nominee or for a transfer agent, upon request of a fiduciary, so to register them?

At Common Law 

At common law it is not proper for a fiduciary to have securities registered in the name of a nominee, and in having them so registered it commits a breach of trust.¹ In the face of this principle of the common law, textbooks and judicial decisions³ warn against indiscriminate or unauthorized nominee registration.

By Statute 

At the present time 16 states in the following chronological order have adopted statutes authorizing nominee registration: Pennsyl- 2

Scott on Trusts, sec. 179.5, p. 956. 

Matter of Harris, 169 Misc. 943, 9 N.Y.S. (2d) 508 (1938); Munro v. Huber, 109 F. (2d) 97 (C.C.A. 2d, 1940).

4 5 vania, Florida, Louisiana, North Carolina, New York, California, New Hampshire,¹º Oklahoma,"¹ Oregon,12 Nevada,¹³ Wisconsin,¹ Minnesota,15 South Dakota,16 Texas,17 Kentucky,18 and 

New Jersey.19

However, simply to say that 16 states have adopted nomineeregistration statutes is not enough. The statutes differ from one another on major points. Therefore, to get a clear picture of the situation, each statute must be analyzed. 

1. Analysis of nominee-registration statutes. Analysis of these statutes covers the following points: (a) What types of fiduciaries are authorized to use nominees, (b) in what types of trust accounts and (c) for what types of trust property may they be used, (d) what must the fiduciary and (e) what must the nominee do with respect to nominee registration, (f) what provision is made for nominee registration in co-fiduciary accounts, (g) what liability does the fiduciary assume in nominee registration, (h) what provision is made for relief of the corporation and its transfer agent from liability, and (i) what is the penalty for violating the statute? a. Types of fiduciaries authorized to use nominees. In Florida, Louisiana, Nevada, Oklahoma, South Dakota, and Texas both individual and corporate fiduciaries may use nominees; in California, Kentucky, Minnesota, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, Oregon, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin the privilege is restricted to corporate fiduciaries. while individual fiduciaries may not use their own.nominees, they may deposit trust securities with a bank or trust company to be registered in the name of its nominee. b. Types of trust accounts in which nominees may be used. 

In Florida, Louisiana, Nevada, Oklahoma, South Dakota, and Texas nominee registration is limited to express trusts, which, of course, include trusts under will, living trusts, and trusts under order of court; in New York, to executorships, administratorships, guardianships, trusts under will, and other express trusts. The California list is the same as that of New York except that it substitutes private trusts for express trusts. Wisconsin names executors, administrators, guardians, and trustees under will; Kentucky, executors, administrators, guardians, committees, agents, and trustees. Minnesota, New 

Hampshire, New Jersey, Oregon, North Carolina, and Pennsylvania use the all-inclusive term fiduciary. c. Types of trust property for which nominees may be used. 

In Florida, Louisiana, Nevada, North Carolina, Oklahoma, and 

South Dakota nominee registration is restricted to shares of stock; in California, Kentucky, New Hampshire, New York, and Wisconsin, to stocks and other securities; in Pennsylvania, to investments, including fractional interests in investments; in Oregon, to bonds, stocks, mortgages, deeds or other securities or assets, real or personal; in Minnesota, to stocks, securities, or other property; in Texas, to stocks or other property; and in New Jersey, to shares of stock, bonds, debentures, notes, or other securities. d. Requirements of fiduciary. The following requirements are imposed upon a fiduciary by the statutes of the states indicated, as conditions precedent to having securities registered in the name of its nominee: (1) The trust records and all reports or accounts rendered by the fiduciary must show clearly the ownership of the property by the fiduciary and the facts regarding its holding-California, Kentucky, Louisiana, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, Oklahoma, Pennsylvania, South Dakota, Texas, and Wisconsin. (2) The nominee-registered securities must be kept separate and apart from the assets of the bank or trust company-California, 1

J 

Kentucky, Minnesota, New Jersey, New York, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin. e. Requirements of nominee. The following requirements are imposed upon the nominee in the states indicated: (1) Deposit with the fiduciary a signed statement showing the true ownership of the securities-Louisiana, Nevada, Oklahoma, South Dakota, and Texas. (2) Endorse the stock certificate in blank-Louisiana, Nevada, Oklahoma, South Dakota, and Texas. (3) Execute a stock power in blank (if the certificate is not endorsed in blank) and attach it to the certificate or execute a conveyance or assignment of the title to the nominee-registered property -Texas. (4) Not have possession of the security or access thereto except under the immediate supervision of the fiduciary—Louisiana, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, North Carolina, Oklahoma, South Dakota, and Texas. f. Provision for nominee registration in co-fiduciary accounts. In 

Kentucky, Minnesota, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, and Wisconsin, the bank or trust company may, with the consent of the individual fiduciary or fiduciaries (who are authorized by the statute to give such consent), cause the stock or other securities to be registered and held in the name of its nominee. g. Liability of fiduciary. In California, Florida, Kentucky, Louisiana, Minnesota, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, Oklahoma, South Dakota, Texas, and Wisconsin, the statutes declare that the fiduciary is liable for any loss to the trust resulting from the acts of its nominee in connection with the nominee-registered securities. If a bank's or trust company's nominees are composed exclusively of its officers or employees, they as nominees ordinarily are under the coverage of its standard-form blanket bond. h. Relief of corporation and transfer agent from liability. The 

Kentucky statute relieves the corporation and its transfer agent and registrar of liability for any loss occasioned by the acts of the fiduciary or its nominee with respect to stock or securities registered in the name of the fiduciary's nominee. The California statute

relieves the corporation and its transfer agent of any obligation to inquire into the propriety of the transfer unless the corporation or transfer agent has actual knowledge of a breach of trust or of the invalidity of the trust instrument or unless it has been enjoined by a court of competent jurisdiction from making the transfer.20 i. Penalty for violation of the statute. Kentucky and New York each prescribes a penalty for violation of its nominee-registration statute. Kentucky declares that any person violating any of the provisions of the act shall be guilty of a misdemeanor and fined not less than $50 nor more than $2,000. New York declares that any person violating any provision of the statute shall be guilty of a misdemeanor. Under the Uniform Trusts Act, as a penalty for the violation of any provision of the act, including the nominee-registration section, the trustee may be removed and denied compensation and, in addition, any beneficiary, co-trustee, or successor trustee may treat such violation as a breach of trust.2 21 Another analysis of the nominee-registration statutes in chart form is presented on pages 382-383 of this Study.

By Provision of Trust Instrument 

Even at common law, if the terms of the trust provide for nominee registration, the trustee has the power to have shares of stock so registered. Thus, the Restatement of the Law of Trusts declares: 

By the terms of the trust. the trustee may be authorized to hold trust property in the name of a third person nominated by him without designating the particular trust or without indicating that it is held upon trust." 

Professor Scott amplifies this statement by saying: 22 it is not uncommon to provide in the trust instrument that the trustee may hold stock in the name of a nominee without mention of the trust in the stock certificate. In such a case the trustee does not commit a breach of trust merely because he takes title to shares of stock in the name of a third person. The trustee would be liable, however, if he permitted the third person to have possession of the certificate and if the third person misappropriated it, since the trustee would not be using proper care to preserve the trust estate. Possibly the trustee would be liable at all events for the conduct of his nominee.28 20 

Civil Code, secs. 328b, c, and d. 

21 Sec. 20. 22 

Sec. 179, comment d. 23 

Scott on Trusts, sec. 179.5, p. 956.

In 1938, however, doubt was cast upon the efficacy of a provision in the trust instrument by a New York surrogate's court which held that a trustee cannot take title properly to securities in the name of a nominee even though by the terms of the trust he is permitted expressly to do so, in the face of a statute making it a misdemeanor for a trustee not to keep the funds and the property of the estate of a deceased person separate and distinct from his own property. At the time Professor Scott thought that this decision of the court was erroneous in that the statute on which the court relied was not intended to apply to cases in which nominee registration was authorized by the terms of the trust.25 In any event, so far as New York State is concerned, the matter was settled in 1939, as to executors, administrators, guardians, and trustees under will, and in 1944, as to trustees of other express trusts, by the adoption of statutes expressly authorizing nominee registration. 

1. Form of nominee-registration provision. The form of the nomi nee-registration provision in the trust instrument may be a matter of practical importance. For example, is it sufficient to grant power to use a nominee in only general terms such as these: 

To take and hold any or all stocks, bonds, notes, or other property in the name of a duly appointed nominee without disclosing the fiduciary relation. 

For trusts being administered in any of the 16 states having statutes authorizing nominee registration, it would seem that such a general provision is sufficient in that the provisions of the statute would be construed to complement those of the instrument. 

But suppose the trustee, located in State A which by statute authorizes nominee registration, is administering a trust under the laws of State B whose statutes are silent as to nominee registration. In view of such a combination of facts, which is not at all uncommon in connection with the transfer of securities, it might be advisable for draftsmen of trust instruments to amplify the nominee-registration provision to cover the main points now covered by the statutes. 

EXTENT OF NOMINEE REGISTRATION 

Long before there were nominee-registration statutes many trust instruments authorized the use of nominees and many trust institu- 

Matter of Harris, 169 Misc. 943, 9 N.Y.S. (2d) 508 (1938). 25 

Scott on Trusts, sec. 179.5, p. 956.

tions used nominees and, as already stated, there was widespread use of nominee registration for the purpose of immediate sale. In 

1939 it was estimated that 70% of the trust instruments then being drawn in New York City contained a nominee-registration provision.26 In February 1941, out of a group of 75 of the larger trust institutions of the country located in 35 cities in 24 states, 39 were using nominees regularly, 22 were using them to a limited extent, and only 14 were not using nominees at all. Since that time 11 other states have adopted nominee-registration statutes and numerous nominees have been established by trust institutions. It is reasonable, therefore, to conclude that the use of nominees is becoming more general. One large corporation recently reported that 22.5% of its outstanding stock was registered in the names of nominees. 

General Use

The general use of a nominee means that in appropriate cases the trust institution proceeds, without waiting until a sale is imminent, to have the certificates registered in the name of its nominee. However, it does so only in appropriate cases. For example, it would not request nominee registration of assessable stock or of stock subject to liability, or in co-fiduciary accounts without the consent of the co-fiduciary, or against the expressed wishes of the customer. Limited Use Some trust institutions restrict their use of nominees to certain situations or to certain types of accounts. 

1. Immediate sales. Many trust institutions still limit their use of nominees to cases where a sale is imminent, so as to be prepared to make good delivery as soon as the sale is effected. 

2. Living trusts and agencies. Other trust institutions limit their use of nominees mainly to living trusts and personal agencies. Some settlors prefer that the securities in their trusts be registered in the name of a nominee so as to keep their own name and their trust relationship out of the transaction. Some principals, instead of executing blank stock powers, prefer nominee registration. Some- 26 

Horn, John, and Dixon, Arthur, Nominee Registration to the Fore, Trusts and 

Estates, September 1939, p. 271 at pp. 274-275. ›

times nominee registration is used in co-fiduciary accounts, with the consent of the co-fiduciary, to avoid delays in effecting sales, due to the absence or illness of the co-fiduciary. 

ADVANTAGES AND DISADVANTAGES OF NOMINEE REGISTRATION 

A group of 100 larger trust institutions have been asked to state what they have found to be both the advantages and the disadvantages of nominee registration; and a group of 35 corporations and transfer agents have been asked to state what they have found to be the main problems of nominee registration. 

For the Beneficiary 

1. Advantages. From the point of view of the beneficiary of an estate or trust, the main advantages of nominee registration are said to be: (a) It expedites sales by having the nominee-registered security ready for immediate delivery. This, of course, is regarded as the main advantage of nominee registration. It is estimated that, with a 

New York transfer agent, on the Pacific Coast (even by the use of air mail) 5 days' time and in the Middle West (by regular mail) from 10 days' to 2 weeks' time frequently is saved by nominee registration. Such a saving of time may be greatly to the advantage of beneficiaries. (b) At the termination of the account it facilitates distribution in that the fiduciary does not have to furnish the transfer agents certified copies of distribution decrees or await approval of the transfer agent before making the distribution. (c) It may preserve the confidentiality of the trust relationship, first, by reducing the number of instances in which copies of the trust instrument must be furnished and, second, by keeping the name of the settlor or principal out of the record after the initial transfer to the nominee has been completed. This, it is said, applies with particular force to the securities of local corporations held by local trust institutions. (d) It reduces expenses incident to transfers which are legitimate charges against the estate or trust. It is thought that in most cases, from the point of view of the beneficiary, the first and the second advantages are the basic advantages of nominee registration. 

2. Disadvantages. The only disadvantage of nominee registration to the beneficiary that has been suggested is that it might be easier for a fiduciary improperly to dispose of a nominee-registered
