security. This has been the main argument against nominee-registration.27 However, in 1939, the Surrogates' Association of the State of New York, in recommending the adoption of a nominee-registration statute, reported to the General Assembly of that state that it was found that the safeguards actually employed by corporate fiduciaries which placed securities in the name of a nominee were such as to insure safety against misappropriation.28 

For the Fiduciary 

1. Advantages. From the point of view of the fiduciary, the main advantage of nominee registration is said to be that it reduces the costs of transfer that are not chargeable to the trust account. (a) It reduces the cost of preparing documents-such as copies of wills and other trust instruments, surrogate's certificates, certificates of residence, and court orders of one sort and another-incident to transfers subsequent to the nominee registration. For the original nominee registration the full quota of documents must be supplied by the fiduciary. (b) It reduces the miscellaneous correspondence incident to transfers. (c) It reduces postage and registration charges. (d) It reduces the amount of necessary physical handling of securities which, in turn, reduces clerical costs. (e) It reduces clerical errors in connection with transfers. The name of the nominee is simple and becomes well known to the clerks in the trust department. The repeated use of a simple name makes for speed and accuracy in setting up and maintaining the trust department records and permits the use of rubber stamps and other labor-saving devices. 

2. Disadvantages. To the fiduciary the main disadvantages of nominee registration are said to be: (a) It necessitates the setting up of special records to allocate dividends (particularly stock dividends, dividends payable in stock of another company), rights, proxies, and notices. (b) It increases the difficulty of discovering errors. One trust institution says that it has found that the use of the nominee puts a premium on the accurate keeping of collateral records, such as tickler files, etc., inasmuch as there is not the automatic check formerly available by having the name of the trust written on the face of each dividend check received. (c) Some con- 

27 Supra, p. 374 

28 Note to Laws 1939, Ch. 343.

fusion arises from transfers of nominee-registered securities made just before the date of the dividend. If the security has been sold or distributed just before but the transfer has not been completed by that date, there may be discrepancy between the amount of the dividend check as shown on the books of the transfer agent and that on the books of the fiduciary. This may occasion correspondence, explanation, and delays. (d) Some difficulties arise in connection with calls of preferred stocks and of bonds by lot. (e) Nominee registration sometimes necessitates explanations to customers and beneficiaries. Some trust customers do not like or do not understand the need for having their trust securities registered in the name of a third person; this objection applies especially to individual nominees. (f) Nominee registration raises the necessity of complying with additional governmental requirements, such as registering the nominee with the federal and with some state taxing authorities for transfer-tax exemption purposes, registering the nominee with some state authorities (as those of Massachusetts, North Carolina, and Pennsylvania) to do business under an assumed or fictitious name, and filing Form No. 1087 (Ownership Certificate) for federal income tax purposes.

Most of these are complaints, not of nominee registration as such, but of the mechanics of nominee registration. The prevailing opinion of trust institutions using nominees is that the substantial advantages of nominee registration outweigh these mechanical disadvantages. 

For the Transfer Agent 

1. Advantages. One very large corporation lists the following advantages of nominee registration: (a) It relieves the corporation of the necessity of examining the many legal papers connected with a fiduciary registration, and the attendant correspondence. (b) The name of the nominee is easy to inscribe on the stock certificates and on stencil plates, with less likelihood of error. (c) It reduces the number of accounts on the stock ledger, with the resultant saving in issuing dividend checks and stockholder literature. (d) 

Through the use of appropriate tax-exemption certificates it reduces the necessity of the corporation's scrutinizing each transfer for the required tax stamps.

2. Disadvantages. From the point of view of the transfer agent, the following are some of the reported disadvantages of nominee registration: (a) It imposes upon the transfer agent responsibility for seeing that, in addition to the other transfer requirements, the nominee-registration requirements also have been met. Unless the corporation and its transfer agent are relieved by statute or by the terms of the trust of liability for loss resulting from acts of the fiduciary or its nominee or unless they are indemnified by the fiduciary, many transfer agents feel that, under the law as it now stands, they must, as a matter of self-protection, pass judgment upon the propriety of nominee registration. (b) The fact that upon the stock transfer books the shares stand in the name of one stockholder-that of the nominee-may impose additional work upon the transfer agent, especially so when, for example, the fiduciary encounters difficulty in allocating stock dividends, dividends payable in stock of another company, or rights or, upon discovering errors, calls upon the transfer agent for assistance. (c) Nominee registration makes it more difficult for the transfer agent to furnish the corporation detailed information as to the real owners of its shares of stock or to verify the fiduciary's list of the numbers of certificates or shares outstanding in the name of its nominee.

From the point of view of the transfer agent, nominee registration appears, on the whole, to be satisfactory. None of the 14 corporations or 21 transfer agents consulted named any problems of nominee registration that would be regarded as very serious, and a large number of them took occasion to express themselves as being distinctly in favor of nominee registration in all appropriate cases.

SUGGESTIONS AS TO NOMINEE REGISTRATION 

The same groups of corporations, of transfer agents, and of trust institutions were asked for suggestions for the improvement of nominee registration. There are such striking parallels in the suggestions received from them that no attempt is made here to distinguish those offered by each of the three groups.

Nominee Partnerships 

If a trust institution decides to use a nominee, it should establish a nominee partnership. Generally it is thought to be inadvisable to

use individual nominees, because of complications that may result from their death, absence, or bankruptcy or from their severance of connection with the trust institution.

There seems to be little real advantage and there may be real disadvantage in using a nominee corporation. One disadvantage is that, in the event of a sale, a security registered in the name of a corporation is not good delivery and, if a resolution of the board of directors of the nominee corporation should be required to authorize a transfer, meeting this requirement might take as much time as supplying the papers required for a fiduciary registration. 

Or, if title to real property in another state should be taken in the name of a nominee corporation, some question might arise as to the right of a foreign corporation to take and hold title to real property in that state.

The partnership papers-that is, principally the partnership agreement and the agreement between the partnership and the fiduciaryare pretty much standardized and copies of them are easily obtainable from fiduciaries or transfer agents by trust institutions desiring to create nominee partnerships.29 The name of the partnership should be short, distinctive, and easy to write. The following are typical names now in use: Bull & Co., Cross & Co., How & Co., Map & Co., Nay & Co., North & Co., Olen & Co., Steere & Co., Unit & Co., Wall & Co., and White & Co.

Existence of Power of Nominee Registration 

Before a fiduciary requests nominee registration it should be assured of its power to take and hold securities in the name of its nominee. If both the statute of the state in which the trust is being administered and the trust instrument under which it is being administered are silent as to nominee registration, the power, it is thought, does not exist, and the trust institution should not request nominee registration, except for the purpose of immediate sale, and thus put the transfer agent to the necessity either of refusing the request or of assuming an undue risk. 

If the trust instrument is silent as to nominee registration and the 

29 For legal aspects of the partnership papers see Schumacher v. Davis, 1 F. Supp. 

959 (1932); Munro v. Huber, 109 F. (2d) 97 (1940); and Broderick v. Aaron, 151 Misc. 516, at 526 et seq. (1934).

trust institution relies wholly upon a statute, before the request is made by the fiduciary such questions as these should be considered: 

Does the statute cover this type of fiduciary, this type of security or property, and this type of trust account? Do the statutes of both the state in which the trust is being administered and the state in which the trust institution is located authorize nominee registration in this case? Was this account established before or after the adoption of the nominee-registration statutes being relied upon and, if it was before, do the statutes apply to pre-existing accounts? These are questions that transfer agents ask, and it would be well for fiduciaries seeking nominee registration to anticipate them. 

Information and Supporting Documents One of the main objectives of nominee registration is to relieve the fiduciary of the necessity of supplying information and supporting documents, with the resulting work and delay, each time a transfer is requested after the original nominee registration. With the original request for nominee registration, which usually is at a time when a sale is not imminent and, therefore, when immediateness of transfer is not a matter of prime importance, the trust institution should furnish the transfer agent all the information and documents needed by the transfer agent for passing judgment on the propriety of the request. It should be remembered that in the initial nominee registration that of a transfer from the name of a settlor, or decedent testator or intestate, or principal, or fiduciary into the name of the fiduciary's nominee-two sets of requirements must be met-namely, those requirements incident to any transfer where a fiduciary is involved and, in addition, those requirements incident to the nominee registration. 

1. Letter of information. Several of the larger transfer agents express approval of the practice of some trust institutions in attaching to the stock certificate a form letter, signed by an authorized officer, setting forth the pertinent information about the requested nominee registration. For example, one trust institution attaches to the certificate of stock to be registered in the name of its nominee such a letter containing the following information and commitments: (a) a quotation of the regulation of the State Banking Commissioner and a citation of the state statute authorizing nominee registration;

(b) a certificate that, after the certificate has been registered in the name of the nominee, as requested, it will be held in the same manner as if registered in the name of the estate, trust or fund in which the stock is owned, that the records of the trust institution and all reports and accounts rendered by it will show clearly the ownership of the stock and the facts regarding its holding, and that the nominee will not have possession of the certificate or access thereto except under the immediate supervision of the trust institution; (c) a commitment that, in consideration of the transfer agent's registering the stock in the name of its nominee, it will indemnify and save the transfer agent harmless for any loss incurred by the transfer agent by reason of such nominee registration; (d) a certificate that the transfer of the stock to the nominee is exempt from transfer tax under the provisions of Section 1802(b) of the Internal Revenue Code and Sections 270-270-a of the tax law of the State of New York, that evidence in proof of this exemption is maintained by the trust institution and is available for inspection by Internal Revenue and New York State Tax Commission officers; (e) the date of the letter; and (f) the name of the trust institution, the fiduciary capacity in which it is making the request, and the signature and title of the authorized officer. 

This letter anticipates the transfer agent's possible desire or request for information and for indemnification. After all, the nominee registration is being made at the request of the fiduciary and the nominee is the fiduciary's agent, so it would seem that the fiduciary should relieve the transfer agent of liability for the acts of the fiduciary's own nominee. 

2. Rubber stamps. The use of rubber stamps in connection with nominee registration is a growing practice among trust institutions. 

For one thing, they use rubber-stamp impressions to denote that the transfer is for the purpose of immediate sale. Some transfer agents feel justified in registering a transfer to a nominee if the certificate is stamped for the purpose of immediate sale even though both the trust instrument and the statute are silent as to nominee registration. They say that this involves only a minimum business risk which they are justified in taking in order to expedite the disposition of securities. When a security is registered in the name of a nominee for purpose of immediate sale, the transfer agent relies upon

the good faith of fiduciary to effect the sale promptly. If the terms of the trust or the order of court authorizing the sale named a minimum sale price, some transfer agents request a letter of indemnity from the fiduciary in connection with a transfer even for the purpose of immediate sale.

Also, rubber stamp impressions are used to denote that the nominee registration involves no change of ownership. The Internal 

Revenue Code³ provides that the federal transfer tax shall not be imposed upon transfers from a fiduciary to its nominee or from one nominee of such fiduciary to another, if such certificates are to continue to be held by such nominee for the same purpose for which they would be held if retained by such fiduciary or by the first nominee, but such transfer shall be accompanied by a certificate setting forth the facts. A certificate rubber-stamped on the stock certificate to the effect that the shares still are to be held for the same purpose generally is accepted as compliance with the requirement of the Code. Sometimes two certificates are combined in a statement (a) that the shares are to be held by the nominee for the same purpose as if held by the fiduciary and (b) that the transfer is for the purpose of immediate sale. 

3. Evidence of consent of co-fiduciary or third party. The statutes of 6 states-Kentucky, Minnesota, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, and Wisconsin-provide for nominee registration in co-fiduciary accounts, by authorizing the individual co-fiduciary to give his consent to registration in the name of the bank's or trust company's nominee. In the other states, if nominee registration is desired in co-fiduciary accounts, it should be authorized in the instrument. The co-fiduciary's signature to the certificate or other appropriate evidence of his consent should be furnished the transfer agent along with the request for nominee registration.

If the trust instrument provides that the trustee shall not make or change any investment without the consent or approval of a named third party, the trustee desiring nominee registration for the purpose of immediate sale should accompany the request with a certified copy of the consent or other acceptable evidence of the consent of the third party to the nominee registration. 

4. Evidence of successor trusteeships. In successor trusteeships it 

30 Sec. 1802(b).

expedites nominee registration where the successor trustee furnishes the transfer agent, along with the request for nominee registration, satisfactory evidence of its succession to the trusteeship. In the trust instrument, for example, A is the original trustee. In course of time 

A dies or resigns or is removed and X Trust Company, upon becoming successor trustee, requests nominee registration. X Trust Company should bridge the apparent gap in the office of trustee by showing the transfer agent how it became trustee and thereby became entitled to have the securities registered in the name of its nominee. 

5. Evidence of appointment of trustee under will. In some states a trustee under will takes over the property from the executor and carries on as trustee without any court appointment; in other states, the trustee must be appointed by the the court before it can take over the property. It expedites nominee registration when a trustee under will in one of the former group of states certifies to the transfer agent that court appointment was not necessary and when a trustee in one of the latter group sends along with the certificate of stock a certified copy of the court order of appointment. 

Once the certificate has been registered in the name of a nominee, thereafter the registration of the transfer out of the name of the nominee into that of a real owner, such as a purchaser or distributee, should be as easy and as expeditious as the registration of the transfer from one individual real owner to another. The main problems in nominee registration naturally come in the original registration of the transfer into the name of the nominee.

Registration out of Name of Nominee 

What many fiduciaries and transfer agents regard as the greatest single disadvantage or inconvenience connected with nominee registration can be avoided if trust institutions do not permit nomineeregistered certificates of stock to get out of their possession or control without provision for or assurance of prompt registration out of the name of their nominee. If the fiduciary delivers the nomineeregistered certificate to a distributee or purchaser and the latter, as too frequently is the case, lets considerable time elapse before having the certificate transferred out of the name of the nominee into his own name or into some other name, the books of the transfer agent do not show the change in ownership and the trust institution continues to

receive the dividends. This, of course, leads to confusion, to correspondence, to vexation, and to expense. One large trust institution, having had much experience with nominee registration both as fiduciary and as transfer agent, states that it does not deliver to any customer securities registered in the name of its nominee and that, where sales are involved, it enlists the cooperation of the broker in completing the transfer out of the name of the nominee at the earliest possible moment. This, it is thought, should be the general practice of all trust institutions.

Compliance with Request of Fiduciary as to Denominations Transfer agents aid materially in nominee registration by strict compliance, so far as possible, with the fiduciary's request as to the denominations in which nominee-registered certificates are to be issued. This relates especially to stock dividends, to dividends payable in the stock of another company, and to subscription rights. 

Suppose, for example, a corporation declares a dividend payable in the stock of another company, with fractions of shares payable in cash. If the transfer agent lists the nominee as one stockholder and issues the certificate and calculates the fraction on this basis, the number of shares and the amount of cash received by the fiduciary may not be the proper number or amount for distribution among the various trust accounts; whereas, if the transfer agent issues the certificates in the denominations requested, the certificates will be in the number, and the cash in the amount, proper for distribution. To issue the stock dividend or right first in a single certificate to be returned and re-registered in the desired denominations involves two transactions and still does not solve the problem of cash for distribution for fractions of shares. However, the refusal of the transfer agent to register the securities in the denominations requested is not due to the transfer agent's lack of accommodation or cooperation but rather to the necessity of its compliance with the regulations of the Securities and Exchange Commission. In order to reduce to the minimum the problems of the transfer agent in this regard, the fiduciary, on its part, should submit its request as to denominations as soon as it learns that the stock dividend, or the dividend payable in the stock of another company, or the stock right is to be declared.

CONCLUSIONS 

Judging by the number of enabling statutes that have been adopted recently and by the generally favorable attitude of both transfer agents and fiduciaries, it looks as though nominee registration is becoming more and more a general practice. 

In September 1937, when the Uniform Trusts Act containing a nominee-registration section was approved by the National Conference of Commissioners on Uniform State Laws, there were only two nominee-registration statutes then in existence-those of Pennsylvania and of Florida. Since 1937 such statutes have been adopted in 14 other states. Of these 14 states, 5-Louisiana, Nevada, North 

Carolina, South Dakota, and Texas-have adopted the Uniform Trusts Act in its entirety or have adopted the substance of the nominee-registration section of that act. The remaining 9 states— 

California, Kentucky, Minnesota, New Hampshire, New Jersey,

New York, Oklahoma, Oregon, and Wisconsin-have adopted independent nominee-registration statutes similar in some respects but different in other, material respects from the corresponding section of the Uniform Trusts Act.

Since 1937 fiduciaries and transfer agents have had much more practical experience with nominees than they had had before that time. It would seem timely, therefore, for the Commissioners now either to reconsider the nominee-registration section of the Uniform Trusts Act or to consider a separate Uniform Nominee-Registration Act. It would be advisable for them to consider provisions (a) for the use of nominees by fiduciaries other than trustees, (b) for the extension of nominee registration to trust property other than shares of stock; (c) for the use of nominees in co-fiduciary accounts, and (d) for the relief of the corporation and its transfer agent from liability for the acts of the fiduciary or its nominee. 

Until a satisfactory nominee-registration statute has been adopted in every state, it is desirable that, where nominee registration is desired, the trust instrument contains a definite and rather detailed nominee-registration clause, with provisions somewhat similar to those in the more recent nominee statutes. This is so because a trust institution frequently is called upon to administer trusts in a state other than the one in which it is located and, in the absence of a statute in the state whose law affects the transfer, the clause

in the instrument must be relied upon for the authority both of the fiduciary and of the transfer agent.

Since the nominee is the agent of the fiduciary, there is an implied, if not an express, understanding that the fiduciary will indemnify the corporation and its transfer agent against any and all loss from the nominee's acts or omissions connected with the nominee registration. 

Along with the original request for nominee registration, the fiduciary should furnish the transfer agent all the information and all the supporting documents needed by the transfer agent intelligently to pass judgment upon the propriety from its point of view of the nominee registration.

It is desirable that forms and procedures as nearly uniform as practicable be worked out by fiduciaries and by transfer agents in concert for their guidance in effecting nominee registration. 

Among corporations and their transfer agents, as well as among trust institutions as fiduciaries, the prevailing opinion, based on experience, is that nominee registration, while it does not, by any means, solve all the problems and, indeed, creates some new problems of its own, nevertheless has proved its usefulness as one way of expediting the sale and disposition of securities held in trust accounts.
